# Patterns

Composite patterns assembled from primitives. Each pattern solves a recurring task and inherits the state rules of its parts.

## Classification

- Category: patterns

When to use:

- Composing a screen for a recurring task such as finding, filtering, or editing information.
- Checking a flow for missing steps, exits, or recovery paths.

When to avoid:

- Inventing a one-off arrangement for a task a pattern already covers.

## Elements and components

| Pattern | Purpose | Core elements | Rules |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Primary navigation | Move between top-level sections | Nav list, current indicator, badge | Current section is marked in text or shape, not colour alone |
| Bypass link | Let keyboard users skip repeated navigation | First-focusable link, visible on focus, target landmark or heading | The link is the first focusable element on the page and stays hidden until focused; it moves focus to the target, not just the view — a link that only scrolls while focus stays in the header strands the next tab stops. Label the link for its destination ("Skip to main content", or "Skip to checkout form" where a task, not the page, is the destination); on multi-step flows, offer a task-specific target so later steps skip straight to the form. The target accepts programmatic focus and the focus indicator lands visibly on it |
| Back to top | Return to the top of a long page without scrolling back by hand | Floating button, scroll-distance threshold, top landmark or heading | Offer it only where the page is long enough that scrolling back is a chore, roughly more than two screens of content; on short pages omit it rather than showing a control that never helps. Keep it hidden while the person is near the top and show it only after they have scrolled past a stated distance, hiding it again when they return near the top, so it never sits on content that needs no scrolling. Fix it where it is easy to reach without covering content or other floating controls, commonly the bottom-right corner, clear of chat widgets, banners, and the primary action, and keep it at the standard touch-target size. On activation, scroll to the top and move keyboard focus to the top landmark or heading, not just the view, so the next tab stops follow from the top; label the control for its destination rather than a bare symbol. Scroll smoothly, but honour reduced-motion settings by jumping at once. Distinct from the Reading progress indicator row, which shows how far through reading the person has come without moving them, and from the long-document navigation row, which moves within a document rather than back to its start. Cross-check the Reading progress indicator row |
| Search | Find content by query | Search input, suggestions, results list, clear action | Show recent and suggested queries only where appropriate; results state the query. Typeahead updates on every keystroke with the strongest match keyboard-selectable; each row shows title, type, and one secondary detail so the target is confirmable without opening it; scope filters narrow the global box by project, type, or status when the result set is mixed. Keep the suggestion panel to a small scannable set, grouped and labelled where more than one content type returns, so the list stays readable at a glance; Escape closes the panel before it clears the query, and Enter commits the query as typed. Evaluate search health on a cadence: success rate (searches ending in a click or completed action), time-to-result, zero-result rate, top click position, and refinement rate. A rising zero-result or refinement rate points at the vocabulary or scope design, not the ranking; log zero-result queries as free research. Keep recent queries in their own labelled group, separate from completions and suggestions, so a past query is never mistaken for a recommendation. Each recent entry offers its own removal and the group offers a clear-all, since a history the person cannot prune becomes a record they did not choose. Show only queries that were actually run, newest first, and state plainly whether recents live on this device only or follow sign-in, clearing on sign-out on shared devices unless the person chose otherwise. Highlight the matched substring inside each suggestion so the match is scannable at a glance, and while new suggestions load, keep the previous rows on screen and swap them in one change so the panel never flashes empty. Where a suggestion is offered as a refinement rather than a result, let its refinement control append the suggestion to the query without running the search, so the query can be built up step by step; keep that control distinct from the row itself, which runs the search. For assistive technology, keep focus in the input while moving through suggestions, announcing each highlighted suggestion as the current option without moving focus to the panel, and announce the number of suggestions — or that there are no matches — as a status message when the list changes, without moving focus; expose whether the panel is open or closed so the presence of suggestions is known before moving through them. When a query returns nothing, state plainly that nothing matched, offer one spelling or scope correction, and keep the query editable in place with a route back to the full list; never present an empty list with no explanation and no way back. |
| Search history | Return to a query already run, change it, or remove it | History list, re-run control, edit control, per-item delete, clear-all control, retention statement | Keep only queries that were actually run, newest first, each entry naming the query and when it was run. A re-run control runs the same query again at once, while an edit control loads the query into the search box without running it, so it can be changed first. Offer a delete control on each entry and a clear-all control for the whole list, and state plainly how long entries are kept and whether they live on this device only or follow sign-in, clearing on sign-out on shared devices unless the person chose otherwise. Keep history in its own labelled list, never mixed with suggestions or recommendations, so a past query is never mistaken for a suggestion. Distinct from the recently viewed row, which lists opened items rather than queries, and from the saved-filter view row, which keeps a named filter combination rather than past queries. Cross-check the search row and the recently viewed row |
| Filtering and sorting | Narrow a list | Filter controls, active filter tags, sort menu, result count | Result count updates with filters; one action clears all filters. On compact screens, keep a sticky filter control above the results with sort as a separate control, showing the active count on the filter control; expand the most-used facets and collapse the rest, using a bottom sheet for a few facets and a full-screen panel for long facet lists. Let people choose several options before applying, with the apply control showing the live result count, and refresh the list once on apply; facet counts recalculate after each selection and count only items currently obtainable, so a count never promises an empty or unavailable result. Keep applied filters visible as removable chips above the results, and make whole labels tappable at the standard target size. For ranges, pair sliders with typed minimum and maximum inputs or preset ranges; for colour, use swatches rather than text lists. State how selections combine: within one facet, choosing several options widens the result, since any selected option matches; across facets, selections narrow the result, since every chosen facet must match — and where that combination is not obvious, say it plainly near the controls. Where a facet holds many options, add a search field inside the facet so a person can jump to an option instead of scrolling the whole list. For assistive technology, group each facet's options under a named group so the group is announced with its options; name each applied-filter chip's remove control for the filter it removes rather than a bare symbol, and announce the new result count as a status message when filters change, without moving focus. Cross-check the search results row |
| Data table | Compare structured rows | Header row, sort controls, pagination or load-more, row actions | Sticky header on long tables; density is a user choice where feasible; bulk selection uses a selection column with a select-all that states its scope; keep loading state per section (grouped headers, expanded rows) rather than whole-table only, and keep expanded-row content aligned with the visible viewport during horizontal scroll; cell-level actions combine one link with one or two adjacent button or link actions; sort state is explicit — an unsorted column shows a neutral indicator distinct from ascending and descending, the current direction is visible in the header and announced to assistive technology, multi-column sort states its column order and uses a unique field as the final tie-breaker so paging stays stable, sorting moves to the server once the set exceeds what the client can sort without lag, and the sort survives paging and filtering; column choice is a user control, not a fixed layout — people can show, hide, and reorder columns to fit the task at hand, and the choice persists across sessions where repeated use is likely; hiding and reordering never rely on drag alone, so keyboard and pointer routes both work; columns can be resized to expose a value in full rather than leaving it truncated; align numbers, currency, and percentages to the right with consistent decimals so digits line up by place value and magnitudes compare at a glance, while text stays left; where a column total matters, show a totals row in the footer that stays pinned while the table scrolls, and keep it out of the sort order so totals never sort as if they were a record |
| Data table density | Fit more rows without losing legibility | Compact, comfortable, and spacious density options; sticky column headers | Density is a per-user choice stored with preferences; row height never drops below the minimum target size |
| Expandable table row | Reveal detail for one row without leaving the table | Row toggle, detail row, collapse control | Each row that holds more detail offers a named toggle in its first cell stating expand or collapse in text as well as in shape, and expanding never moves focus away from the toggle; short detail appears in a detail row directly beneath its parent row spanning the full table width, while long detail opens as a separate detail view so the table stays scannable; only rows with detail carry a toggle, and a toggle never acts as the row's only action; the expanded state is announced to assistive technology and the detail content stays in reading order after its parent row; expand-all and collapse-all controls appear only where every row holds detail, and collapsing a row never discards entered input inside its detail — distinct from the accordion disclosure row, which organises sections under headings, and from the detail view row, which inspects one item without a sibling table. |
| Inline editing | Change one value in place inside a table or list | Editable cell, edit trigger, commit and cancel actions, validation message | Keep single-value, low-risk changes inline and move multi-field, cross-field-validated, or high-stakes edits to a dedicated editing surface; an editable cell states that it is editable and names the value it holds; entering edit mode is keyboard-reachable — Enter or a printable key starts editing, Enter commits, Escape cancels and restores the previous value, Tab moves to the next editable field, and arrow keys move between cells while navigating; while editing, navigation keys belong to the control inside the cell, and leaving edit mode returns them to cell navigation; a failed validation keeps the editor open, names the problem in text, and announces it to assistive technology; entering and leaving edit mode is announced, and the confirmed saved value is stated rather than assumed; the visible focus indicator always shows which cell is live |
| Bulk selection | Act on many rows at once | Selection checkboxes, selection count, action bar with the available actions | Selection survives sorting and paging; actions apply to the stated scope and confirm before destructive ones |
| Saved-item collection | Keep items for a later return, grouped by the person | Save action, saved-state indicator, named collections, collection controls, changed-item notice | Save an item with one action from a list or a detail view, and show its saved state wherever the item appears, in text or shape as well as colour. Offer named collections with rename and delete controls, and state the scope of each collection plainly — private or shared — before anything in it is shared. A saved item that the person opens again shows whether it is still current: where the original changed, state what changed or mark the saved copy as stale; where the original was removed, state that plainly and offer removal of the saved copy or the nearest available alternative, never silently dropping or silently swapping it. Unsaving is one action with an undo offer, and the saved view keeps the filtering and sorting controls of the list it came from. Cross-check the filtering and sorting row |
| Saved collection sharing | Share a saved collection with another person while keeping control of access | Share control, recipient choice, access-level choice, shared-collection list, revoke control, access-change notice | State the scope before sharing: whether the share covers the whole collection including items added later, or only the items in it now, so a new item is never shared by surprise. Offer two access levels in plain words: view-only, which lets the recipient see the collection without adding, removing, or renaming anything in it, and editable, which lets the recipient add and remove items but never change who else can see the collection — only the owner changes sharing. Where a link route is offered, state plainly whether anyone with the link can view the collection or only named recipients, and whether opening it needs sign-in, since a link passed on reaches people beyond the named recipients. Keep a shared-collection list where the owner reviews every recipient and access level, and offer a revoke control on each recipient that takes effect at once. State before sharing, and again at revoke time, what happens to items the revoked person added: they stay in the collection or leave with that person, never silently either way, and a copy a recipient made stays with the recipient after revocation. When the owner narrows a shared collection back to private, state that every recipient loses access at once and that the collection itself stays with the owner. Distinct from the saved-item collection row, which keeps items for one person, and from the shared cart row, which shares items for a single checkout. Cross-check the saved-item collection row and the shared cart row |
| Bulk action | Apply one decision to many selected items without losing track of scope or failures | Scope statement, action summary, confirmation control, per-item result list, retry and undo controls | State the selection scope before acting — the current page, the filtered set, or all matching items — and never treat a select-all on one page as all matching items unless that is stated and confirmed. A confirmation summarises the action, the count, and the scope, and names any items that will be skipped and why. Destructive batch actions need confirmation; reversible batch actions offer an undo for the whole batch instead of a confirmation for each item. While the batch runs, show progress and keep the selection visible; when it finishes, report successes separately from failures, naming each failed item with its cause and a retry path, and keep failed items selected for that retry. A partial success is never reported as a full success. Cross-check the confirmation row, the bulk selection row, and the undo and trash recovery row |
| Data visualisation | Show trends and comparisons in charts and graphs | Chart, takeaway title, labelled axes and data points, text alternative with a data table | Match the chart type to the story: bars for comparison, lines for change over time, simple types over complex ones; the title states the takeaway, not just the topic; label data directly instead of relying on a legend; never use colour alone — pair it with labels, patterns, or text; every chart carries a text alternative naming the type and the key insight; offer the underlying data as a table; never leave a key value only in a tooltip — a value that matters belongs on the chart or in the data table, since a hover-only value is unreachable by keyboard and touch; interactive charts are keyboard-operable with a logical tab order; for point-by-point exploration of a plot, offer an explorer mode entered from the plot area, where arrow keys move between neighbouring data points, legend selection, zoom, and search stay reachable by keyboard, and the focused region stays visible when zoomed; the movement geometry follows the chart type — free movement for scatter plots and maps, movement along one axis and within series for line, area, and stacked charts, and movement along the horizontal axis only for waterfall, small-multiple, and grouped-column charts; in right-to-left layouts charts are not mirrored; where an audio rendering is offered, it complements rather than replaces the text alternative and the data table, maps rising values to rising pitch so a trend can be heard, announces each value as it plays, and stays under the person's control with pause and stop, never starting on its own. For a single goal-progress read, such as daily activity against a target, prefer a bar over a ring where a quick accurate read matters: in controlled tests bars were read faster and with fewer errors than rings on a small screen, while rings suit a glanceable summary; whichever form is used, state the value and the target in words beside it, so progress never depends on judging an arc by eye |
| Onboarding | Teach the essential path on first use | Step indicator, one task per step, skip action, progress persistence | Steps focus on what the person must do, not on features; skipping is always allowed and returns later; progress resumes after interruption; detect and prefill language and region with a one-tap correction affordance; never pre-select sensitive fields, and question whether they are needed at all; prefill with the most likely value from location, common choice, or past behaviour; every default stays editable and skips validation until the person changes it; use standard autocomplete tokens so saved values can fill; never set defaults for decisions needing deliberation such as consent or legal acceptance; explain why each field is asked; show one question at a time. Teach in context rather than up front: inline help is the default, and coach marks appear only when the person first reaches the feature they explain, never more than three in a sequence, always dismissable, and never a substitute for a clear label or a first screen that teaches by doing |
| Identity verification onboarding | Prove identity before an account opens, without losing the person to the checks | Step count and time estimate, per-step purpose statement, document capture guidance, review status, save-and-return control, plain-language rejection reason, retry control | State the step count and expected time before the first check, and put low-friction steps first with document capture last, so the person knows the commitment before the hardest step. Explain beside each request why it is needed and what happens to the data, in plain words, never as a legal excerpt. Give framing and lighting guidance before a document photo, and where a capture fails state the exact cause in plain language with a retry, never a bare error code. Replace indefinite waiting with a review status, a realistic timing, and a save-and-return path, so a check that needs review never strands the person on a spinner. Where a document is rejected, state the reason plainly and offer the next workable step, and keep the full terms available on demand beside a short summary. Cross-check the onboarding row |
| Guided tour | Point out one element at a time inside the live interface | Dimmed surround, spotlight cut-out, anchored bubble, step counter, back, next, and skip controls | Highlight exactly one element at a time and dim the rest, with a short bubble anchored beside the target stating the benefit in one or two lines, never a paragraph. Show the step position (for example, 2 of 4) and offer back, next, and an always-visible skip or dismiss path; ending the tour early is always allowed and never blocks the task underneath. Use a tour only when a person first reaches a feature or when a change would otherwise disorient them, and prefer inline help or a single tooltip for a lone ambiguous element. Show each tour once per person per feature, never chain more than three steps in sequence, and keep the explained content findable afterwards through help or the element itself. The tour is keyboard-operable and dismissable at any step, and focus returns to a sensible place when it ends. Cross-check the onboarding row |
| Setup checklist | Track multi-step setup that can be left and returned to | Ordered task list, per-task state, resume control, skip control, progress count | List the setup tasks in order, each with a done, current, or remaining state stated in text as well as in shape, and show the count done of the total so progress is plain at a glance. Each item links directly to the task it names, so completing it happens in place rather than reading about it; items map to outcomes, not to a tour of features, and the list stays short — a handful of tasks toward one activation goal. A person can leave at any point and return later: a resume control takes them to the first unfinished task, and progress persists across sessions. Any item can be skipped, and skipped items stay reachable rather than vanishing, until the person dismisses them deliberately; dismissing the whole list never blocks the task underneath, and a finished list is marked complete rather than left dangling. Use a checklist where setup spans several separate actions; where there is only one thing to do, a single prompt replaces it. Cross-check the onboarding row and the guided tour row |
| Inline assistant actions | Apply automated help to the thing already selected | Contextual command on a selection or row, scope statement, inline preview, undo | Commands appear as verbs on the existing surface — selection menus, row actions, inline toolbars — not as a separate chat surface; inline wins when the target is visible, the task is short, and the result lands where the person already is, while chat wins for open-ended or multi-step exploration; every command states its scope before acting and is reversible, graded by consequence: trivial changes apply with a plain undo, costly ones preview the effect and wait for approval; capabilities are discovered in place, as a suggestion on the selection itself, never through a tour; loading names the running step and error keeps the selection intact |
| Form flow | Collect structured input | Grouped fields, inline validation, summary of errors, submit | Validate on submit and on correction; errors are listed and linked to fields. Do not validate while the person is still typing: check a field once on leaving it, then re-check on each change after it has been marked invalid, so the error clears as soon as the value is corrected. Keep each inline message beside its field, in text with an icon or label rather than colour alone, stating what is wrong and what fixes it. On submit failure, move focus to a summary at the top stating the count — except where only one field is invalid, where focus moves straight to that field — with each entry linking to and focusing its field, while keeping the inline messages in place — the summary alone is never the only indication, and a failure is never announced only through a disappearing notice. Keep every entered value after a failed submission; nothing the person typed is lost because the form failed. Decide how each field is collected: ask only for what is genuinely needed; infer what context already tells you and prefill it, always editable; defer non-essential fields to a secondary surface or a later step; confirm with a review step where a mistake has lasting consequences. Smart defaults are for low-risk choices only: a prefilled workspace name removes guesswork, but preselecting recipients or beneficiaries makes a consequential decision for the person — never default what needs deliberation, such as consent, money, or messages sent to others. Never move focus automatically to a field when the form loads: let the person choose where to start, since an unexpected focus jump disorients keyboard and screen-reader users. Where a control is non-standard, or a required field cannot be filled in from stored information, put a short explanation and a worked example beside the control itself, not only in help: state what the control does, show what a valid entry looks like, and keep that explanation reachable without leaving the field. Accept the value in the form the person gives it: accept the common formats for a field rather than one rigid shape — a phone number with or without spaces or dashes, a date in the locale's usual orders — and divide long numbers into chunks that autocomplete across fields, so a long reference is typed in readable groups. Where the system can correct a value reliably, offer the correction beside what was entered and let the person accept it or keep the original; never rewrite an entry silently. Word an error about what the system could not recognise, not about the person being wrong |
| Character count | Show how much text a limited field still accepts | Limit statement, count indicator, over-limit message | State the limit before typing starts, in words beside the field, never only as a silent cutoff: "up to 200 characters" beats a bare number. Show the count as characters used or remaining, updated as the person types, placed where it stays visible without covering the input, and keep it in text rather than colour alone. Announce the count to assistive technology on entry and at meaningful thresholds, not on every keystroke, and state plainly when the limit is reached or exceeded, including how many characters over the limit the text is. Never silently discard typed or pasted text beyond the limit: either stop entry with a plain statement, or accept the excess and mark the field invalid with a clear path to shorten it. Cross-check the Form flow row |
| Wizard | Complete a multi-step task | Step indicator, back and next, save and resume | Steps can be revisited; progress is preserved on interruption. Show the total step count up front; mark completed, current, and future steps distinctly, and keep future steps visible but non-interactive — a clickable future step implies a jump the process cannot honour. Step labels are short and specific, never bare numbers; on narrow screens prefer a vertical stepper over a cramped horizontal one. The forward action carries a contextual label where it reduces uncertainty; the back control preserves entered data. Flows longer than a few minutes offer save-and-resume; short simple forms of about five fields with no branching stay on a single page. Scope validation to the visible step: a required field on a hidden step must never block submission silently — either check each step on its own transition, or give each step its own form, so the submit action always reaches a field the person can see and fix. Mark the current step for assistive technology as well as in sight: the step list names the current step as the current step and states its position, such as step two of five, and announces the new step when the person moves to it, so a step list never reads as a row of identical labels; completed steps are named as completed in text, never by a check mark alone. Keep the indicator honest about the effort left, not just the screens left: a count or bar that treats every step as equal misleads where one step holds most of the work, and a bar that jumps from a quarter to nearly full teaches the person not to trust it. Where steps differ in size, weigh the indicator by the work in each step, or name the steps so the person can judge what remains; where the path branches, show the conditional route rather than a percentage that can move backwards when a new branch appears |
| Detail view | Inspect one item | Title, key facts, actions, related items | Primary action stays reachable while scrolling on compact screens |
| Notification list | Review updates | Grouped items, read state, done action, follow control, mark-all action, snooze and pause controls | Unread state uses shape or text in addition to colour. Offer a snooze on individual items and a pause for the whole list: a snoozed item leaves the list and returns at the chosen time as unread, never silently dropped, and the snooze choice states its return time in plain words rather than a vague interval. A pause states when it ends and what still breaks through, so time-sensitive items are never paused away unseen; when the pause ends, held items arrive together as a grouped summary, not as a flood of separate interruptions. Snoozing one item never silences the rest, and clearing a snoozed item cancels its return. Keep read state, triage state, and following as separate dimensions: reading an item says only that it has been seen, never that it has been dealt with, so opening an item never removes it from the active queue; a done action moves it out of the active queue without changing its read state, and following controls whether future updates arrive. State plainly what stopping updates does — whether it also removes the current item, and whether a direct mention or a new request can bring updates back — since separate controls do not always act independently. Count the badge in one stated unit: unread threads in the active queue, excluding items marked done, so several updates on one thread count once, and state the count beside the badge where the unit is not obvious, such as "12 active · 3 unread", so reading three items is never mistaken for emptying the queue. Cross-check the notification digest and notification escalation rows |
| Notification preferences | Choose which updates arrive, through which channel, and how often | Category groups, channel matrix, cadence control, quiet-hours control, per-source mute, reset control | Group settings by the topics the person cares about — messages, mentions, billing, activity — rather than by internal event names, and let each group be switched independently so turning one topic off never silences everything. Lay categories against channels in one matrix, so a person can choose one channel for billing and another for mentions at a glance, with the on-or-off state unmistakable in text or shape as well as colour. Offer a cadence per category — at once, as a scheduled summary, or off — and never let a summary be the only place an action-required item appears. Offer quiet hours that state plainly when they end and which urgent items still break through, and a per-source mute that silences one noisy source without touching the rest. Start quiet for promotional updates and let the person opt into more, and keep a single reset that returns every preference to its starting value. Apply a change at once and keep it saved across visits, and state plainly where a choice is governed by a system-level permission rather than this screen, so a switch that cannot take effect never looks as if it did. Cross-check the notification list row, the notification digest row, and the notification escalation row |
| Focus mode schedule | Silence interruptions on a schedule, by activity, with a stated allowlist and a stated end | Named modes, per-mode allowlist of people and apps, schedule trigger, active-state indicator, auto-reply, held-notifications summary | Offer named modes for distinct activities — work, personal, sleep, driving — each with its own allowlist of people and apps that may break through, so a mode that lets everything through is a label rather than a boundary. Let each mode run on a schedule set by time, place, or app launch, stating the days as well as the hours — a weekday-only schedule is the point — and prefer a time schedule where reliability matters. Show a visible state while a mode is on and state plainly when it ends, so the silence is never mistaken for a fault; when it ends, held notifications return together in one grouped summary, never as a flood of separate interruptions. Let the person choose an automatic reply that tells senders they are unavailable, and keep a narrow emergency path — such as a repeated call from the same person — that can still break through. Where the platform supports it, sync the schedule across the person's devices so one schedule silences every screen, and let each mode change what apps show while it is on, such as a calmer home screen, so the mode is visible as well as silent. Distinct from the notification preferences row, which sets per-topic delivery inside one product, and from the notification digest row, which groups low-urgency updates on a schedule without silencing anything; a focus mode changes delivery across the whole device. Cross-check the notification preferences row, the notification digest row, and the notification list row |
| Activity feed | Review a chronological history of events | Event list, timestamp, event-type cue, grouping label, load-more control, new-items control | Present the feed as a list so assistive technology reports item count and position; grouping that sighted users get from spacing or a connecting line must also be present as structure or text, never as visual grouping alone. Each entry carries a plain timestamp and a type cue in text or shape, not colour alone, so the feed scans without reading every entry. Loading more announces how many entries were added and keeps the person's place — never reload to the top or shift content under the reader. When new entries arrive while the feed is being read, hold them behind a count control rather than inserting them at the top and shifting content under the reader; activating the control adds the new entries at the top, announces how many were added, and keeps the reader's place. Show a relative timestamp on each entry with the exact time available on demand, and use a connecting line only where order and elapsed time carry meaning — otherwise a plain divider separates entries without implying a sequence. Entries expand in place for detail rather than forcing a jump away, and the whole feed stays keyboard-operable |
| Notification escalation | Match the interruption to the urgency | Urgency scale, badge or dot, toast, banner, notification-centre entry, modal, full-screen dialog | Map urgency to pattern and channel, one step per level: low value goes to a badge or dot in a list; informational goes to a toast or a digest; time-sensitive but non-blocking goes to a persistent banner; action required goes to a notification-centre entry with an email; urgent action required goes to a blocking modal with push and email; critical goes to a full-screen dialog. Nothing critical lives only in a toast — the person may look away and miss it. Never spend a high-urgency pattern on a low-urgency event: it teaches people to ignore alerts, and the ignored-alerts debt then hides the real ones. No redundant multi-channel duplication for the same event; each step up the scale adds disruption, and the scale ends with a channel the person cannot miss |
| Broadcast emergency alert | Send one urgent safety warning to every device in an area at once, with no sign-in, app, or phone number needed | Two alert tiers, full-screen modal, distinct sound and vibration, message body, acknowledgement control, notification-centre copy | Give the warning two tiers: the highest tier sounds a distinct tone and vibration and overrides silent mode and do-not-disturb settings, and cannot be turned off; the lower tier sounds and vibrates but never overrides a silence setting the person chose. The message states what the emergency is, where it is happening, how serious it is, what action to take, who issued it, and where to find more information — never a bare headline. Show it as a full-screen modal that stays until acknowledged, and once dismissed keep a copy in the notification centre, so a dismissed warning is never lost. Deliver it without registration, an app install, or a phone number, and where the platform supports it, without a working SIM card or a data connection. Keep the wording short and plain, and group repeated warnings about the same event rather than sounding each one again. Distinct from the notification escalation row, which grades in-app interruptions by urgency, and from the emergency assistance request row, which lets a person ask for help. Cross-check the notification escalation row and the emergency assistance request row |
| Confirmation | Prevent a costly mistake | Plain statement of effect, confirm and cancel actions | Destructive actions name the item and the effect; no pre-selected confirm; grade every action by reversibility: free actions (read-only or trivially reversible) need no interruption, reversible writes get a lightweight inline check with a before-and-after statement, and irreversible actions (pay, send, delete, publish) get the full confirmation ceremony, and the highest-stakes irreversible actions — deleting a whole workspace, removing every member, closing an account with data loss — ask the person to type the item's name or a confirmation word, so the action cannot be completed by habit; support standing permissions such as "always draft, never send"; never gate trivial actions or confirmations stop being read |
| Sensitive-action re-confirmation | Check intent again before a consequential change that an old sign-in must not silently approve | Fresh confirmation step, plain statement of what will change, scope statement, cancel action | Before a consequential action such as changing payment details, sharing sensitive content, or deleting shared content, ask for a fresh confirmation tied to that action itself: state plainly what will change, for whom, and whether it can be undone. Never reuse an earlier sign-in as silent approval for a new consequential action, and never pre-select the confirm control. Grade the check to the consequence — a plain restatement for a reversible change, a fresh credential or typed confirmation for an irreversible one — and keep the cancel action as easy to reach as the confirm action. A declined re-confirmation leaves everything exactly as it was, with no partial change applied. Distinct from the second-factor sign-in row, which adds a check at sign-in, and from the confirmation row, which confirms an ordinary action. Cross-check the confirmation row |
| Comparison | Choose between options | Option columns or tier cards, criterion rows in a fixed order, difference toggle, decision row | Fix the criterion order across options so rows line up; three to four columns is the workable range; label tiers by audience in plain words, not abstract names; each higher tier opens with "everything in the previous tier, plus…" so readers scan only what is new; a differences-only toggle hides identical rows; mark each row's winner gently with shading plus a text or icon cue, never colour alone; do the arithmetic for the reader ("+$40/month buys 3 extra seats and single sign-on"); state the metric direction explicitly where ambiguous ("lower is better"); keep option labels and a decision row with one action per option sticky on long matrices; spell out annual savings next to the billing toggle; choose the container by difference count — cards when only a few real differences separate the options, tabs or accordions or a grouped collapsible matrix when the feature list is long |
| Permission request | Earn access without losing trust | Pre-permission rationale screen, system dialog, graceful denial path, settings route | Never request on first launch just in case; trigger when the person taps the feature that needs the access; stage one is a custom pre-permission screen stating the concrete value ("to scan receipts and auto-categorise expenses"); if the person taps "not now", do not fire the system dialog; stage two is the system dialog only after a positive pre-prompt response; request one permission at a time — batched prompts produce combined denials; on denial, degrade gracefully into a feature-limited experience, never a dead end; for permanent denial, explain the limitations plainly and route to the app settings; ask once, then only in context; never nag within the same session after a denial; consider a single in-app screen listing all requested permissions with one-tap revocation |
| Install prompt | Offer installation of a web app | Install action, benefit statement, dismiss action, manual-install instructions | Offer installation only after the person has used the app enough to see its value — after a completed task or a return visit — never on first load, never on exit, and never repeatedly after a dismissal; a dismissal stays dismissed, with a quiet persistent route (such as a menu entry) kept instead of re-prompting. State the concrete benefit beside the action (faster access, offline use, notifications) and keep one primary action, not a banner of options. Where the platform offers no prompt event, give manual steps instead of a dead button. After installation, confirm success and hide the prompt; the installed app opens in its own window without browser chrome where supported. Cross-check the permission request row |
| Lightweight app entry | Complete one task without installing the full app | System entry card, single-task view, platform sign-in, platform payment, full-app install offer | Offer one task only — pay, order, book, or check in — and include only what that task needs, so the slice opens in seconds and never asks for the full app first. Open it from a code, tag, link, message, map listing, or search result, and let the system show a card naming the app and the task before anything runs, so the person chooses to open it knowing what it will do. Finish the task without an install and without creating an account first, using the sign-in and payment the person already has on the device where those are offered. Where the app is already installed, the same link opens the full app at the same task instead of the slice. Offer the full app once, after the task, as a single install action — never as a barrier to finishing, and a declined offer stays declined for that visit. Leave nothing behind when the slice is not reused: no home-screen icon and no leftover data after a stated window. One mobile platform ended its equivalent no-install route at the end of 2025, so promise this entry only where the platform offers it and link to the mobile web task elsewhere rather than a dead end. Distinct from the install prompt row, which offers installing a web app, and from the code scanner row, which captures a code. Cross-check the install prompt row and the code scanner row |
| In-app browser | Show a web page inside the app without losing the task | Embedded page view, address and security statement, close action, open-in-browser action, forward and back controls | Open a linked page inside the app only when the person needs to stay in the task — a help article, a document, a payment step — and use the platform's own in-app browser for pages the app does not control, so sign-in and payment stay in the person's own browser with its saved details and protections. State the page address and whether the connection is secure before anything is entered, and never present a page inside a plain embedded view as if it were part of the app. Keep the app's own chrome visible: a clear close action that returns to the exact place the person left, forward and back controls where the page leads onward, and an open-in-browser action for anyone who wants the full browser. Match the platform's navigation — swipe-back where the platform offers it, the system back control where it does not — and never trap the person in web-style navigation with no way back. Never use an embedded view for sign-in to another service: hand that to the system browser. Distinct from the lightweight app entry row, which opens one task without installing the full app, and from the share link row, which hands a link to someone else. Cross-check the lightweight app entry row and the session-expiry warning row |
| Search results | Recover from finding nothing | Query echo, carried filter chips, ordered recovery actions, facet panel | Keep the division of labour simple: search narrows by intent, filters narrow by attribute; the two controls sit close together since people loop between them; on empty results, say the combination matched nothing and echo it exactly ("no invoices match X with Status: Paid"), keep the query and filters visible, and never silently clear choices; offer recovery in order: a clickable spelling suggestion rather than silent autocorrect, a one-action scope broadening, carried filters as removable chips (an old filter is the most common hidden cause), and a create action where user content is accepted; label each widening action by what it changes; mirror applied filters as chips next to the query echo; a single reset clears filters but leaves the query intact; when applied chips overflow the available width, keep them in one horizontally scrollable row or collapse the excess into a single expandable chip showing how many are hidden, keep the reset control visible, and never reorder chips on selection — a chip stays in place so its position stays predictable; facet panels sit left on expanded screens and in a bottom sheet on compact ones, with live result counts per facet |
| Command palette | Run commands and jump to content by typing | Summon hint, query input, grouped result list, scope labels, shortcut hints | A complement to menus and buttons, never the only route to a command. Summon with one standard global shortcut; the input takes focus the moment the palette opens. Represent commands as data with verb-led names and match with forgiving subsequence matching plus synonyms, so a query in the user's words finds the right command. Group mixed results into typed sections — navigation, actions, destructive commands — so types stay distinguishable, and never truncate scope information ("Archive project" and "Archive selected files" must read differently after filtering). A blank palette shows recents and common actions; a no-match state explains and offers a next step, never a dead end. Keep selection stable while results stream in: state what happens when the query changes, and never execute whichever row occupies the first position at an uncertain instant. Do not let a broad fuzzy match outrank the clearly intended command when the consequence is costly. Full keyboard operation with a logical order, focus returned to the trigger on close, and result changes announced to assistive technology |
| Offline-first sync | Keep working without a connection | Local store, mutation queue, sync-status area, retry control, freshness indicator | The local store is the source of truth and the server converges later: mutations write to a durable queue with identifiers, timestamps, and attempt counts, and replay when connectivity returns, with a fallback where background sync is unavailable. The interface distinguishes granular states — syncing, local changes pending, stale — instead of a binary loading-versus-done split. Optimistic updates apply at once but stay marked pending until confirmed, then reconcile or roll back on rejection, never failing silently. A visible sync-status area carries the retry control and a manual refresh next to freshness indicators; failed sync gets a non-intrusive banner, never a modal. Derive the shown status from queue progress and real request outcomes, not from a connectivity signal alone: a device can reach a local network while the service itself stays unreachable. Optimistic display is for reversible edits only: where an action has lasting consequences — a booking, a payment, or an access change — keep it marked unconfirmed until the server confirms it, and never clear entered input until the local copy is durable |
| Offline download | Keep chosen content usable without a connection | Download action, size and storage statement, progress indicator, pause and resume controls, expiry statement, downloaded list, remove control | State the size and the storage the download will use before it starts, and refuse to start silently where the device lacks room — name how much room is needed. Show progress for each item with pause and resume that keep what was already fetched, never restarting from the beginning after an interruption. Where downloaded content expires, state the expiry before downloading and again in the downloaded list, and re-check it at the moment the content is opened rather than promising access the licence no longer allows. Removal frees the storage at once and says so, and a downloaded item that is no longer available states that plainly with a route to fetch it again. Distinct from the offline-first sync row, which queues changes made without a connection, and from the file upload row, which sends content rather than fetching it. Cross-check the low-bandwidth mode row |
| Sync-conflict resolution | Side-by-side version view, field-by-field merge control, merged preview, confirm control | Keep local, keep server, merge fields | When a locally queued change and a newer server version diverge, never silently overwrite either side and never pick a winner by timestamp alone. Show both versions side by side with the differing fields highlighted, and keep the local copy safe until the person resolves the conflict. Where the two sides changed different fields, merge those fields automatically and show the merged result for confirmation; where both sides changed the same field, ask the person to pick one version or edit a merged value. Treat a deletion on one side and an edit on the other as a conflict to resolve, not as a silent delete. Sensitive fields (status, price, assignee, dates) always ask, even when an automatic merge would be possible. The merged preview is the exact result that will be saved, and resolving it records what was chosen so the choice can be reviewed later. Until resolved, the item stays marked as needing attention and the local copy remains recoverable. Cross-check the offline-first sync row |
| Multi-device handoff | Continue a task on another device from an explicit resume point | Resume suggestion on the receiving device, transferred-state summary, fallback view, conflict prompt | Offer the handoff as a one-tap resume suggestion on the receiving device, never as an automatic takeover: the person chooses when to switch. Resume at the exact screen and state where the task stopped — entered input, selection, and scroll position — not merely at the home screen of the receiving app. State plainly what transferred and what did not, so a missing attachment or unsaved draft is visible before work continues. Where the receiving device lacks the app, open the same task in its browser view as a fallback rather than failing silently. Where both devices changed the same item before the switch, resolve it with the sync-conflict rules instead of silently keeping one version. A dismissed suggestion stays dismissed for that task, and a handoff never moves a task away from the device in use without an explicit action. Where the handoff starts on a screen the second device cannot reach directly, offer a scannable code on the first screen with a short typed link and an emailed link beside it, so the move never depends on scanning alone. Keep the code short-lived, state its expiry plainly, and confirm on the originating device when the second device connects, so a code seen by someone else cannot silently take over the task. Cross-check the offline-first sync row |
| File upload | Move files in reliably | Per-file queue item, progress indicator, pause, retry, and remove controls | Split large files into chunks of roughly 5–10 MB so a failed chunk retries alone with backoff instead of restarting the whole transfer; use resumable upload where available. Show each queued file as one item with name, progress, status, and error, plus per-file pause, retry, and remove controls; progress stays accurate after interruption and resume. Full keyboard operability with visible focus, and assistive-technology announcements when each file starts, completes, or fails. Where a suitable file is already held, offer it as a one-step reuse choice before asking for a fresh upload: name the file and where it came from, let the person confirm it or decline and upload a different file instead, and never reuse a held file silently. Where the held file no longer meets the requirements — format or recency — say so plainly and ask for a new one rather than failing after the fact. State the accepted formats, size limit, and file-count limit in plain text beside the control before anyone chooses a file, and check a file against those limits before the upload starts, naming the exact limit broken — never let a rejection be the first time the person learns the rule. Drag-and-drop is an enhancement only: a visible, keyboard-operable browse control always offers the same upload, so dragging is never the only path |
| Receipt capture | Turn a paper receipt into a checked expense record | Camera capture, long-receipt sections, recognised-field review beside the image, correction controls, reviewed mark, duplicate check | Offer capture from the camera beside an image-from-library route, and ask for camera access only when capture starts. Where a receipt is long or double-sided, capture it in labelled sections in order rather than forcing one unreadable frame, and keep the sections in that order. Show the recognised merchant, date, amount, and line items beside the receipt image for review, never as a silent record: every recognised field stays editable before saving, and a correction never requires re-capturing the image. Where a recognised field is uncertain, mark it for checking rather than presenting it as certain. Check a new capture against recent records for a duplicate before creating a second record, and merge or discard the duplicate with the person's confirmation rather than silently doubling the expense. Mark a checked record as reviewed, and keep the image with the record so a later question can be settled from the original. Distinct from the file upload row, which sends files without reading their contents, and from the code scanner row, which captures a code rather than a document's fields. Cross-check the file upload row |
| File preview | Inspect a file before opening or downloading it | File name, type, and size statement, excerpt or thumbnail, open and download actions, fallback statement | State the file name, type, and size before any preview, so the person can judge the file without opening it. Show a readable excerpt for text and documents and a thumbnail for images and video where the type allows one, and keep the preview to the first part of a long file rather than loading it whole. Where no preview exists for the type, say so plainly and offer the open and download actions from the same place, never a blank preview area. Keep the preview reachable by keyboard, with its text readable by assistive technology rather than only as a picture, and keep the open and download actions beside the preview so inspecting never replaces getting the file. Distinct from the file upload row, which covers sending files rather than inspecting them, and from the media viewer row, which inspects one visual in depth. Cross-check the file upload row |
| Paywall and upgrade prompt | Explain a limit and an option | Limit statement, option summary, trial offer statement, trial timeline, plan comparison, dismiss action, exit offer | Dismiss is always present and equally easy to find. The offer states explicitly whether a trial is included, its length, the price that follows, and the first charge date — never leave trial inclusion ambiguous. A control that adds or removes a trial is never the same control that confirms purchase: trial choice and purchase confirmation are separate explicit steps, so changing the trial cannot silently commit the person to an auto-renewing charge. While a trial runs, show its timeline — remaining time, what happens at its end, and a reminder before the first charge — with the cancellation path reachable before that charge. Cross-check the subscription cancellation row. Where options differ, compare them side by side on the same screen with the same price units and the total for the billing period, so the person can tell what each option includes without leaving the screen When the person dismisses the offer, at most one alternative may follow — a cheaper plan, a shorter trial, or a later reminder — shown once and never repeated in the same session; a second dismissal returns to the task with nothing further, so the exit route stays an exit rather than a second sales screen. |
| Gated feature entry | Show a feature that needs a higher tier as a visible entry point, not a hidden absence | Locked-feature entry, entitlement statement, upgrade action, dismiss or return action | Show the entry where the feature usually lives, marked in text as well as in shape as needing a higher tier, so the person can find it without guessing why it is missing. Selecting it states plainly what the higher tier adds and what it costs, with the same trial and price facts as the paywall row, and offers a dismiss or return action that goes back to the task in progress without losing entered work. Never open the upgrade flow from a control that looks as if it runs the feature itself, and never hide a gated feature only to reveal it at the moment of use without warning. Where the person declines, keep the entry visible for a later return rather than removing it or repeating the prompt in the same session. Distinct from the paywall and upgrade prompt row, which is the offer screen itself, and from the subscription plan change row, which changes a tier already held. Cross-check the paywall and upgrade prompt row |
| Trial-end reminder | Warn before a trial's first charge so the decision is not a surprise | Reminder notice, charge date statement, charge amount statement, following-plan statement, cancel action, post-cancellation access statement | Send the reminder a few days before the first charge, never only on the day itself, and state in the same notice the exact charge date, the amount, and the plan that follows, so the person can judge the charge without hunting for it. Keep the cancel route inside the notice and working before the charge, in the same channel used to sign up, and confirm cancellation in writing with the date billing stops. State plainly what access continues after cancelling — until the trial end, or ending at once — and what happens to saved work, so cancelling never silently deletes it. Show the remaining trial time and the same charge facts wherever the plan is shown, not only in the reminder, and never reset or extend the stated deadline to create urgency. Distinct from the paywall and upgrade prompt row, which offers the trial, and from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a paid plan. Cross-check the paywall and upgrade prompt row |
| Subscription cancellation | End a recurring plan without friction | Cancel action, effect statement, reason question, pause or plan-change alternatives, written confirmation, resume path | Cancel in the same channel used to sign up, labelled plainly and reachable from the billing or account area without extra menus; the flow states what ends, when access ends, what happens to saved content, and the next billing date before confirmation. Pause or a cheaper plan may be offered beside cancellation, never as a gate to reaching it, and confirmation options use plain parallel wording with no guilt-framed copy. Where the flow asks why the person is leaving, ask one short question with a small set of reasons and an optional comment, then match the single alternative to the answer — a cheaper plan where price is the reason, a pause where use has dropped, a fix or a support route where something is broken — and show that one alternative once, with declining it exactly as easy as accepting it; never repeat an offer after it has been declined, and never show the same generic offer whatever the reason given. After cancelling, confirm in writing the end date, that billing stops, and how to resume; keep the resume path visible until access ends.  Where a pause is chosen instead of cancellation, state in plain words what access continues during the pause, whether billing stops, and the exact resume date; label the paused state with that date wherever the plan is shown, and send a reminder a few days before billing resumes, so a resumed charge is never a surprise. Keep the resume action one step from the account area, without a new sign-up or a new setup, and keep the cancellation path visible from the paused view as well, so a pause never becomes a harder exit than cancellation itself. Never require a slower channel, such as a call, when sign-up was self-serve |
| Subscription pause | Pause a recurring plan for a set period instead of ending it | Pause control, duration choices, pause-start statement, resume-date statement, paused-state label, early-resume control, reminder | Offer pause from the billing or account area, in the same channel used to sign up, never hidden behind cancellation alone. State before confirming: when the pause starts, at once or at the next billing date; the exact resume date; what access continues during the pause; and whether billing stops for the paused period. Where the pause moves the next billing date, state the new date rather than leaving the old one in place. Offer a small set of fixed durations, and say plainly how often a pause may be used and how long it may last, so a pause never ends in a surprise refusal. While paused, label the plan with its resume date wherever the plan is shown, keep resuming to one action from the account area, and let the person resume early at any time, restoring the same plan without a new sign-up or new setup. Warn a few days before billing resumes, and keep the route to ending the plan visible from the paused view, so a pause never becomes a harder exit than ending the plan itself. Where a pause is not available, for example close to the next billing date, say so before the person starts, with the reason and the nearest alternative. Distinct from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a plan, and from the subscription plan change row, which moves to a different plan. Cross-check the subscription cancellation row |
| Subscription gifting | Give a subscription to another person for a set period, paid once by the giver | Gift choice, gift length choice, recipient field, gift message field, redemption route, renewal statement, decline route, expiry statement | State before purchase the gift length, what access it includes, and that the payment is one-time and does not renew on its own. Ask for the recipient separately from the buyer, so billing stays with the buyer and access goes to the recipient, and offer an optional message shown to the recipient. The recipient accepts before access starts, through a plain redemption route that states what will start and when it will end. State plainly who pays if access continues after the gift period ends, and what happens where the recipient already holds a plan, so a gift never silently replaces or extends a paid plan. Offer a plain decline route: where the recipient declines, or the gift is never accepted before its stated expiry, the giver is not charged for access never taken, and any amount already paid is returned or credited in words stated before purchase. Where the gift can be sent on a later date, state that date before purchase and offer a resend route where the recipient cannot find it. Distinct from the gift option row, which marks one order as a gift, and from the referral invitation row, which invites a person to join. Cross-check the gift option row and the referral invitation row |
| Restore purchases | Regain access to a purchase already made, on a new device or after reinstalling | Restore control, progress indicator, restored-items statement, nothing-found statement, sign-in route | Keep the restore control easy to find in the paywall and in the account or billing area, labelled plainly, so a person who already paid never has to buy again to regain access. Restoring never charges again: it checks the purchase record for the signed-in account and re-applies what is still eligible. While the check runs, show progress and keep the person on the same screen; on success, state in words what was restored and what access it gives, and return the person to the content or feature that was locked. Where nothing eligible is found, say so plainly rather than showing a bare failure, and offer the next step: a sign-in route for the account that made the purchase, and a help route where the purchase was made under a different account. Items that are spent on use, such as a consumable balance, are not restored by this control; where such a balance is held in an account record, state that it follows the account rather than the device. Distinct from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a plan, and from the billing overview row, which reports charges. Cross-check the paywall and upgrade prompt row |
| Session-expiry warning | Keep work safe when a session ends | Time-remaining warning, keep-working action, sign-out action, saved-draft restore step | Warn before the session ends with the time remaining stated plainly, in a warning that stays visible, never blocks continued work, and is announced to assistive technology; a simple keep-working action extends the session without losing entered input, and an idle limit is stated separately from an absolute limit that activity cannot extend. Never discard a draft or a form on expiry: save entered input first, and on return bring the person back to the same place with the same input, or to a saved draft with a clear restore step. Re-authentication after expiry restores the saved input rather than starting the task again; cross-checked against the draft autosave row and the timeout rules in catalog/states.md |
| Data export | Take a copy of personal data elsewhere | Scope and format choice, includes and excludes statement, progress indicator, download link, partial-failure report | Offer export in common formats the person can actually open elsewhere, and state plainly before starting what the export includes and excludes, including date range, scope, and any records left out. Show real progress for large exports with named stages and a stated download-link lifetime, and let the person leave without cancelling the export where the export runs in the background, with a way to check its status. On a partial failure, name what exported and what did not and offer a retry for the missing part only, never a silent short file; cross-checked against the file upload row |
| Data import | Bring an existing set of records into the product | File choice, field-mapping view, preview, commit control, failure report | State the expected fields and which fields are required before any file is chosen, so a person can shape the file to fit rather than discovering the rules from errors. In the mapping view, match each incoming column to one product field, state which columns are unmapped, and flag duplicate or ambiguous matches instead of guessing silently; where the same file shape is imported again, offer the previous mapping as a starting point that can still be changed. Before anything is committed, show a preview stating how many rows will be created, updated, skipped, and blocked, with a sample of mapped rows in their final form so a bad mapping is visible before it does damage. Tie every problem to its exact row and field with the original value and a plain reason, and separate blocking errors, which must be fixed, from warnings, which can be imported with review. State the commit rule before confirmation: either nothing is written until every blocking error is fixed, or valid rows are committed while failed rows are held back — never a silent half-import where the person cannot tell what went in. After the import, give a summary with the final counts and a failure report that names each failed row, field, and reason and stays reachable after leaving the flow, with a route to fix and retry only the failed rows without re-entering the rest. Distinct from the data export row, which takes records out, and from the file upload row, which sends a single file. Cross-check the file upload row and the data export row |
| Account deletion | Close an account with no surprises | Effect statement, grace-period undo action, export offer, written confirmation | Delete from the same channel used to sign up, labelled plainly and reachable from the account area. Before confirmation, state the effect in plain words: what content is removed, what content kept for other people stays, what records are kept and why, and when removal completes. Offer an export of personal data before deletion starts. After the request, sign the person out everywhere, and during a stated grace period offer a plain undo path that restores the account whole; once removal completes, confirm it in writing. Never require a slower channel, and never present deletion as instant when a grace period or a backup-retention window still applies; cross-checked against the subscription cancellation row and the confirmation row |
| Account switcher | Move between signed-in accounts without losing the current place | Current-account trigger, signed-in account list, active-account mark, add-account action, per-account sign-out action | The trigger always names the active account in text, not by an image alone, and the list marks the active account in text as well as in shape. Switching keeps the person in the same place wherever that place exists for the new account, and falls back to that account's home view where it does not. Data, drafts, and settings never carry across a switch unless the person explicitly moves them. Signed-in accounts stay signed in after a switch, and signing out of one account never signs out of the others unless that is chosen explicitly. An add-account action stays in the same list, and a switch never asks for credentials for an account that is already signed in |
| Workspace switcher | Move between workspaces a person belongs to, where every screen inherits the current workspace | Current-workspace trigger, workspace list, active-workspace mark, search or filter, create-or-join action | The trigger always names the current workspace in text, not by an image alone, and the current workspace stays legible on every screen, so an action is never taken in the wrong workspace by mistake. Switching re-scopes all content to the new workspace: lists, mentions, and billing follow the new context, and data, drafts, and settings never carry across a switch unless the person explicitly moves them. Where a person belongs to only one workspace, the control collapses to the workspace identity alone, with no empty switch affordance; where the list grows past a handful, order it by recent use, favourites, or alphabet, and add search or filtering with enough identity per row to tell similarly named workspaces apart. Create-or-join actions stay in the same list. Distinct from the account switcher row, which moves between signed-in accounts, from the profile and account menu, which holds the person's own actions, and from main navigation, which moves between pages inside one workspace. Cross-check the account switcher row |
| In-product help | Get help without leaving the task | Persistent help entry, context-aware suggestions, in-place search, escalation path, article feedback | Keep one help entry reachable from every screen, labelled the same everywhere, so help is found at the moment of confusion rather than after leaving the task. Suggestions follow the current screen and task, not a generic list: a person on a billing screen sees billing help first. Search runs inside the product and results open in place without losing entered input, and returning from help restores the exact screen and state. Each article answers first in short sentences, covers one task, and lists what to try when the first fix fails. When search finds nothing, state plainly that nothing matched, suggest the nearest topics by wording rather than a generic list or an empty page, keep the query editable in place with a route back to the full help list, and carry the failed query into the escalation path — along with the current screen and task context — so the person never retypes it, never a dead end and never a blank search page. Every article carries a simple helpful-or-not control, and repeated negative feedback marks the article for rewrite. Help panels are dismissible, keyboard-operable, and announced to assistive technology without stealing focus from the task |
| In-product feedback and problem report | Report a problem with the product without leaving the task | Report entry, problem-type choice, detail field, screenshot attachment with consent, context carry-over, follow-up statement | Keep one report entry reachable from every screen, labelled the same everywhere, so a problem is reported at the moment it appears rather than after leaving the task. Offer a plain choice of what went wrong - a fault, a confusing step, or a suggestion - with an optional detail field, and keep required fields to the few that identify the problem: what happened, what was expected, and where it happened. Where a screenshot or screen capture helps, ask for consent first, let the person review and remove parts before sending, and never attach a capture silently. Carry the current screen and task into the report so nothing is retyped, and state plainly what happens next: whether a reply follows, where to check progress, and how long a reply takes where that is known. A submitted report confirms in place with a reference the person can quote, and returning from the report restores the exact screen and state. Distinct from the content reporting row, which reports an item rather than a problem with the product, and from the rating row, which judges a single item. Cross-check the in-product help row and the confirmation row |
| In-app survey | Collect a short structured survey inside the product | Question set, question elements, survey container, progress indicator, skip and dismiss controls, confirmation | Keep the survey short: a few essential questions, each optional or skippable, so a person can finish quickly or leave at any point without losing the task they were doing. Pair a closed answer with an open field: a rating or yes-or-no answer alone says what, not why, so follow it with an optional field for the reason. Match the container to the length and disruption: a single short question sits inline beside the content it concerns; a short survey opens in a popover or sheet that leaves the task visible; a longer survey gets its own page only with an explicit opt-in that states it leaves the current workflow. Never ask mid-transaction or on every page: ask at the end of a process or after a completed task, and suppress a dismissed survey rather than re-asking at once. Where a survey spans steps, show progress and let the person move back and change an earlier answer before submitting. On submit, confirm in place and state what happens with the answers where that is known. Distinct from the in-product feedback and problem report row, which reports a problem with the product; from the rating and review submission row, which judges a single item; and from the poll row, which collects a group decision. Cross-check the review prompt row |
| Share link | Let someone else open the same item | Link field, copy action, access-scope statement, expiry statement, revoke control | Before sending, show the sender what the recipient will see: the item title or description the link opens, who can open it, and when access ends, with an edit step for scope and expiry from the same screen, so a link is never sent blind. State the access scope and the expiry before sharing: named people versus anyone with the link, and when access ends. Default to named access where the item is sensitive; an anyone-link is an explicit choice, not the default. The copy action confirms in place with a visible change and an announcement to assistive technology, then returns to its copy state so it can be used again. When copying fails, select the link text and offer a manual copy path instead of failing silently, and never let a slow network round trip consume the copy gesture before the link is ready — prepare the link first, then copy on the tap. A shared link opens at the same item and state; an expired or revoked link explains why it no longer opens and offers a request-access path. Where access is named, the owner can see who holds it and revoke it in one step |
| Second-factor sign-in | Confirm identity with a second step without locking people out | Code entry, method choice, resend control, backup-code entry, trusted-device choice | Offer the strongest available method first and list every method plainly, so the person chooses with full information rather than accepting a weak default. Code entry accepts pasted codes in full and moves focus sensibly, never breaking a paste across separate boxes. A resend control shows the wait time and states where the code was sent. A wrong code states how many attempts remain without revealing which part failed, and never locks the account on a single mistake. A backup-code path stays visible at all times, not hidden behind failed attempts: each backup code works once, and generating a new set cancels the old set. Saving backup codes is part of setup, not an afterthought — show them once, ask the person to store them somewhere safe and separate from the device, and confirm they were saved before finishing. A trusted-device choice is opt-in per device with a stated duration, and is never offered by default on a shared device. A second factor counts as enabled only after a code from it verifies successfully. At enrolment, offer every method plainly and let the person pick a default where several are enrolled; for an authenticator app, pair the QR code with the typed secret as an alternative and a text description of the code, so enrolment never depends on scanning alone. Changing the second-factor method requires a successful check with the current method first, and the old method stays active until the new one verifies, so a failed switch never locks the person out. |
