| Passkey sign-in | Sign in with a credential held on a device, unlocked by the device's own check | Passkey sign-in control, fallback route, device-check explanation, cross-device route, credential list, recovery route | Explain in plain words what the device check is before the system prompt appears, naming the check the device will ask for, so the prompt never arrives unexplained. Offer passkey sign-in beside a visible fallback route rather than as the only option, and keep the fallback working where the device has no credential or the person declines the prompt, never turning a declined prompt into an error. For signing in on another device, state the context before any code is shown — which device is signing in and which device holds the credential — and keep the route usable without a second device where one is not available. Offer creation after a successful sign-in rather than forcing it at sign-up, and prompt for enrolment only once, at a moment when signing in again is easy, never repeating the prompt after a decline. Let the person name each credential for its device, list them in one place with a revoke control for each, and keep a recovery path for a lost device that never strands the account. Distinct from the second-factor sign-in row, which adds a second check after a first, and from the account recovery row, which restores access after it is lost. Cross-check the second-factor sign-in row, the account recovery row, and the authentication layout |
| Account recovery | Regain access to an account when the usual sign-in route no longer works | Account identifier input, verification-method choice, reset link or code, new-password form, success notice | Start with one plain request screen asking only for the account identifier, and give the same public response whether or not an account matches, so the response never reveals whether an account exists. After submission, state what to look for, where to look including the spam folder, how long to wait before retrying, and a route back to sign-in plus help for someone who no longer has access to that inbox. Offer every reachable verification route rather than a dead end: a reset link or code, an alternate contact route, and a backup-code or support route, and let a failed verification fall back to another route instead of locking the account. Treat a reset link or code as a single-use key that expires: an expired or already-used link gets its own screen naming the next safe action, never a generic failure, and a newer request replaces an older one. On the new-password screen, state the actual rules before submission, allow paste from a password manager, and offer a show-or-hide control. After a successful reset, show a clear success state with a sign-in route, send a separate account notification, restore any saved work or session the person had, and state plainly what recovery does and does not restore. Cross-check the second-factor sign-in row and the session-expiry warning row |
| Consent choice | Record a preference | Plain description, equal accept and decline, detail link, preferences panel, persistent change route | Accept and decline sit on the first layer with equal size, weight, and steps: never hide decline behind a detail screen, a faint link, or extra confirmations, and never block the content until a choice is made. Closing or dismissing without choosing means no agreement: set nothing optional and do not re-ask at once. Where a detail panel offers per-category choices, keep the required category on and locked with a plain reason, keep accept-all and decline-all reachable inside the panel, and save only on an explicit save action. The choice stays changeable later from a persistent route, and re-asking happens only when the scope or the choices change. Cross-check the granular consent row |
| Granular consent | Give informed control over data use | Per-category toggles, just-in-time disclosure, consent history, revoke control | Consent is never one pre-checked box: list categories as separate toggles (required, functional, analytics, marketing) with one-sentence consequences each, and never pre-check an optional one — silence is not agreement. Disclose at the point of need: ask when the data is about to be used, with the specific purpose named, not months earlier in a blanket policy. Revocation is frictionless: one control, immediate effect, no retention of withdrawn consent, and the choice stays changeable from settings. Keep an audit trail of each decision with its time and scope, so the person can review and correct it. Cross-check the consent signals in trends/2026/machine-experience.md — high-risk automated actions always pause for human confirmation |
| Deceptive design patterns | Check an interface that asks for personal data against six named traps | Choice screens, defaults, repeated prompts, settings routes, disclosure text | Check every screen that asks for personal data against six traps: overloading — an avalanche of requests, choices, or information that pushes the person to accept everything to make it stop; skipping — a journey arranged so the person passes the data choices without noticing them; stirring — emotion, urgency, shame, or visual emphasis steering the choice toward sharing more; obstructing — making refusal, withdrawal, or finding a setting harder than acceptance; fickle — an inconsistent interface where the same control means different things, or wording changes between steps so the person cannot tell what a choice does; left in the dark — information or controls about data use hidden or missing, so the person does not know what happens to their data or what control they have. Present information and options in an objective, neutral way: no wording or visual emphasis that steers toward the more invasive option, and where a default is offered it is the least invasive option. Never ask again at once after a refusal, and never keep prompting until the person gives in; re-ask only when the scope or the choices change. Keep a plain route to the data settings from every screen that asks for personal data, with accept-all and decline-all reachable together where categories are offered. Distinct from the consent choice row, which governs a single accept-or-decline decision, and from the granular consent row, which governs per-category control. Cross-check the consent choice row and the granular consent row |
| Cookie consent banner | Ask before setting anything optional on a device | First-layer accept and reject, per-purpose choices, persistent withdraw control, consent record | Show the banner before any optional cookie or tracker is set, never after the page has already loaded them, and never block the content until a choice is made. Put accept and reject on the first layer as real controls of equal weight and equal steps: a reject offered only as a faint link in a paragraph, or placed outside the banner, does not count as a choice, and a reject control whose text is unreadable against its background is misleading. Never pre-tick an optional purpose, and never claim a basis other than consent for placing or reading cookies. Label a category as required only where it truly is, and be able to show that it is; a category that remembers a preference can be required, while analytics and advertising never are. Offer a plain way to withdraw at any time, such as a small permanently visible control, that reopens the same choices. Keep a record of what was shown, what was chosen, and when, and review the list whenever a new tool is added. Distinct from the consent choice row, which governs a single accept-or-decline decision, and from the granular consent row, which governs per-category control. Cross-check the consent choice row, the granular consent row, and the deceptive design patterns row |
| Media viewer | Inspect visual content | Stage, previous and next, caption, close | Keyboard and swipe both work; captions are text, not embedded in the visual |
| Code scanner | Capture a printed or on-screen code with the camera and act on what it encodes | Viewfinder frame, detected-code highlight, capture feedback, result preview, rescan control, image-from-library action, flash control, manual-entry route, stop control | State what scanning will do before the camera opens, and ask for camera access at that moment, never earlier. Keep the viewfinder unobstructed: a frame guide with a dimmed surround shows where the code belongs, and the essential controls sit together within reach at one edge rather than scattered over the feed. When a code is detected, highlight that code in place and confirm the capture at once with a visual mark plus a sound or vibration, never silence alone. Where several codes are visible at once, highlight each and let the person choose, never acting on the nearest one silently. Never act on a detection unseen: show a result preview that states the destination or action in plain words, with a confirm control and a scan-again control beside it. When capture fails, say what to change — move closer or further away, adjust the angle, improve the light, or turn on the flash — rather than a generic failure message. Where camera access is refused or unavailable, keep the task open through an image-from-library action and a manual-entry route, never a dead end. Stop the camera once a code is captured and offer a clear stop control at all times, so the camera never keeps running after the task ends. For codes shown to others, keep them large, high-contrast, and on a glare-free surface where possible, with a plain-text note beside the code stating what scanning it does. Distinct from the cross-device sign-in labelling in layouts/auth.md, which shows a code rather than scanning one. Cross-check the haptic feedback primitive and the permission request row |
| Document scanner | Turn a physical document into a digital file with the camera | Viewfinder with edge guide, capture control, page list, crop and rotate controls, filter choice, retake and delete controls, import-from-library action, result format choice | State what scanning will do before the camera opens, and ask for camera access at that moment, never earlier. Show the detected edges in place while framing, and let the person adjust the corners before the page is kept, so a skewed or cropped page is never accepted silently. Capture automatically only when the document is steady and fully in view, and always offer a manual capture control beside it. Keep every captured page visible as a list the person can reorder, retake, or delete before finishing, and state the page count plainly. Offer a filter choice that keeps text readable — original, contrast-enhanced, or plain two-tone — and keep the original page reachable until the result is confirmed. Before capture, guide framing in plain words: fill most of the frame with the document, hold the camera flat and straight, and move glare and shadows off the text, so the smallest needed text stays legible after compression; show a short good-versus-poor example beside the guidance rather than rules alone. Check blur, framing, and completeness before submission and name the fault to fix — blur, a cut-off edge, glare, or shadow — with a retake control, rather than rejecting after submission. Judge a capture by legibility, never by an exact file size, and never ask for a photo of a screen where a digital original is available. Where camera access is refused or unavailable, keep the task open through an import-from-library action, never a dead end. Distinct from the code scanner row, which reads a printed or on-screen code, from the document signing row, which captures a signature on a document, and from the file upload row, which moves files already held on the device. Cross-check the code scanner row and the file upload row |
| Simplified view | Reduce cognitive load on demand | Simplified-mode toggle, reading view, critical-region markers | Offer a simplified version that hides non-essential features and shortens text; keep it available, easy to enter, and easy to leave; mark content on critical paths for simplification (data-simplification attribute) so personalization tools can reduce it; extra features stay discoverable when wanted, never permanently removed |
| Neuro-inclusive design | Keep the interface usable for people with different sensory, attention, and processing needs | Predictable layout, plain-language copy, sensory controls, unhurried timing | Keep placement and wording steady: the same control stays in the same place across screens, wording stays plain and consistent, and a change to routine — a reordered section, a renamed action — is stated in advance rather than appearing silently. Limit sensory load: no autoplay of sound or movement, no flashing or rapidly changing content, and motion honours the reduced-motion setting, with a still equivalent wherever movement carries meaning. Give time without pressure: no countdown that cannot be paused or extended, and a session that is about to end warns in advance with a way to stay signed in. Break long tasks into short steps with progress shown, one decision per step, and a way to leave and return without losing work. Offer a calmer view on demand — reduced motion, fewer simultaneous panels, larger spacing — kept as a stored preference rather than a per-visit toggle. Distinct from the Simplified view row, which hides non-essential features on demand. Cross-check the Simplified view row |
| Map comparison | Compare places and changes on a map | Map canvas, markers, info pop-up or panel, layer control | Short hover notes on features avoid opening a full detail view; visited markers show which pop-ups were already opened so people stop reopening them; small multiples — same-size, same-scale maps side by side — beat toggled layers for comparing change over time; a peel-back swipe temporarily reveals the layer underneath for overlap comparison; short detail goes in a pop-up, long or interactive content in an info panel that never covers the map itself; rich markers add a key metric where colour or size alone cannot carry the information; dragging is never the only way to pan, place a stop, or reorder a route — offer directional pan controls, a tap-to-place action, an address entry field, and reorder controls, plus arrow-key panning; a documented exit returns focus to the list or the next control, and zoom, locate, and close controls carry accessible names rather than icon-only labels |
| Location selection | Choose a place from a map and list | Area search, current-location action, map canvas, result list, detail card, confirm control | Start with an area search and offer a current-location action only as a choice, asking for location access when that action is chosen. Map and list show the same places, and a selection stays identifiable in both when the map moves. Detail cards carry what decides the choice — name, address, hours, and availability — since proximity alone cannot answer it. Refreshing the area happens on request, never silently on every pan, so a selection is never lost to a redraw. When no place meets the current requirements, keep the searched area, explain what failed, and offer a changed area or a removed filter. When location access is refused or unavailable, say which case applies and keep the task open through area search, never a dead end. Confirming a place states the place and its hours plainly, so the choice is checked before it lands. Where nearby markers are grouped, a grouped marker states its count and looks distinct from a single place, expands to the individual places on zoom or on activation, and every grouped place stays reachable through the list |
| Live location sharing | Share a moving position for a bounded time | Recipient choice, duration choice, sharing indicator, stop control, extend control, expired view | Choose the recipients and the duration before sharing starts — a fixed span, until the end of the day, or until stopped — and state the end in words so an open-ended share is a deliberate choice, never a default. While sharing runs, keep a persistent indicator visible in the conversation and on the map, naming who can see the position and when it ends, so sharing is never silent. Offer a stop control that ends sharing at once for every recipient, and an extend control that adds time without sending a new share, so the conversation is not flooded with repeated pins. When sharing ends or expires, replace the live view with a plain statement that sharing has ended and show only the last shared position as a static point, never a still-moving pin. Pausing for one recipient must not silently stop sharing for the rest, and stopping for one recipient must not end sharing for the others unless that is stated. Distinct from the location selection row, which picks a place, and from the system share sheet row, which sends content once. Cross-check the location selection row and the system share sheet row |
| Chat and messaging | Hold a conversation with a person or a service | Message list, multi-line input, send control, typing and read indicators, resize control, save and share controls, voice input control | State capabilities and limitations up front; offer quick actions and suggested prompts alongside open text; streamed responses render progressively with a stop control; rich formats render inside bubbles; a persistent path to a human stays visible. Failed messages carry the error on the bubble itself: a warning mark, plain-cause microcopy, and an inline resend action; offline messages queue locally and send on reconnect behind a visible offline banner. New messages are announced through live regions; input, send, and history work by keyboard; targets are at least 44 px. In group conversations, show per-participant read indicators rather than a single seen line, and mark each participant's latest read message in place so nobody rereads the thread to check. Reply threading keeps side discussions readable: a reply cites its parent and threads collapse by default; unthreaded replies in a busy group read as interruptions. Presence states (available, away, do-not-disturb) stay honest and are never derived from tracking the person has not visibly agreed to. Mentions notify only the named people, and the input suggests names after the mention trigger so nobody relies on memory for spellings. Mentions can address a whole group as well as named people, and a group mention notifies only the members of that group. A mute marker on a message suppresses its notifications even where a person would otherwise be notified. Editing a message never sends a new mention notification, so a missed mention is added with a new message instead. A mention stays distinct in shape and text as well as colour, so it is recognisable without colour alone. Where a site offers more than one chat route, merge them into one entry point, so the person never has to choose between two chat buttons, and keep that entry point reachable on every page, carrying the conversation across pages instead of restarting it. Keep answers short through progressive disclosure, showing the answer first and the detail on request, and never scroll the person to the end of a long answer while they are reading it — let them stay where they are and move on their own. Let the person resize the chat window, and save or share the conversation content, and offer voice input beside typing where the device supports it. Cross-check with trends/2026/conversational-interfaces.md for voice-plus-screen pairing and the Voice dictation input row |
| Voice message | Send and listen to spoken messages inside a conversation | Record control, lock control, waveform scrubber, duration label, speed control, preview and discard controls, transcript offer | State the send gesture before recording starts: a held recording sends on release, while a locked recording waits for an explicit send control, so nobody sends a half-finished message by accident. Offer pause and resume while recording, and let the person listen to a locked recording before sending, with discard beside send. On playback, the waveform itself is the scrub control, with played and unplayed portions distinct in shape or text as well as colour, a duration label, and speed choices for long messages. Remember the last listened position when playback is interrupted, and resume from there rather than restarting. Where a transcript is available, offer it as a text alternative beside the player, never as a replacement for the recording itself. Keep the whole bubble keyboard-operable, and announce duration and playback state to assistive technology. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row and the Media controls primitive |
| Voice message transcript | Read a voice message as text where listening is not possible or not wanted | Transcript text, show and hide control, gap note, play-from-word control | Show the transcript beside the player, in reading order and as plain text that assistive technology can read, never as an image and never as a replacement for the recording itself. Offer a show and hide control so a long transcript does not push the conversation apart when it is not wanted. Where a passage could not be transcribed, say so in words at that point rather than skipping it silently or guessing at the wording. Choosing a word in the transcript starts playback from that point, so any passage can be checked against the recording. Keep the transcript available where sound cannot be played, such as on a muted device or in a quiet setting, rather than hiding it behind playback. Distinct from the voice message row, which sends and plays a message, from the live captions row, which follows speech as it happens, and from the transcript editing row, which corrects a transcript. Cross-check the voice message row and the live captions row |
| Voice dictation input | Enter text by speaking into a text field | Microphone control, interim transcript, editable transcript, pause and stop controls, permission request, offline state | Keep the text field visible and editable throughout: the person speaks, reads the transcript, and corrects it by typing in the same field, so voice is an added route into the field and never a replacement for typing. Show the microphone control with its state in words — idle, listening, paused, processing, ready, or error — and show interim words in a lighter treatment until they are confirmed, after which they become ordinary editable text. Ask for microphone access before listening starts, stating the reason, and keep any draft untouched when access is refused. Offer pause and resume while dictating, and a stop control that keeps what was captured; cancelling discards only unconfirmed words, never text already in the field. Keep short entries to one brief turn and allow longer dictation in bounded sessions with a stated length, restarting without losing what was captured. Where a word is uncertain, mark it in text as well as shape and let the person correct that word alone without re-speaking the whole passage. When the connection drops, stop listening, say so plainly, and keep the captured text; never present guessed words as confirmed. Distinct from the voice message row, which sends a recording, from the live captions row, which follows speech as it happens, and from the transcript editing row, which corrects a transcript against its audio. Cross-check the voice message row and the transcript editing row |
| Read-along narration | Follow spoken narration in written text, passage by passage | Narration player, written text, current-passage highlight, follow control, play-from-word control | Highlight the passage being spoken — a sentence, or a single word where the text is short — so the eye and the ear stay on the same passage, and move the highlight only to whole words, never to part of a word. Keep the highlighted passage in view as narration advances, scrolling it into view without moving keyboard focus, and offer a follow control that pauses automatic scrolling so a person can read ahead and resume following from the current spoken passage. Choosing a word in the text starts narration from that word, so any passage can be reheard against its written form, and pausing narration leaves the last spoken passage highlighted rather than clearing it. Mark the current passage in shape as well as colour — for example, a background change paired with an underline or a marker — and keep the change calm, with no flashing and no rapid colour shifts, so it stays usable with reduced motion. Keep the full text available with or without narration: narration is a layer over the text, never a replacement for it, and turning narration off leaves the text in place at the last passage. Distinct from the voice message transcript row, which offers a short message as text, from the live captions row, which follows speech as it happens, and from the transcript editing row, which corrects a transcript. Cross-check the voice message transcript row and the live captions row |
| Message reaction | Respond to a single message without sending a new one | Reaction bar, reaction cluster, full picker, reactors list | Open the bar from the message itself: long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message — never a separate compose step. Offer a small fixed set of quick reactions first, with an add control opening the full picker, so the common choice stays one tap. Tapping the reaction already given removes it, and tapping a different one replaces it, so one person holds at most one reaction on a message unless the design states otherwise. Show reactions as a compact cluster on the message, grouped by kind with a count, and let tapping the cluster open the list of who reacted, with each reaction named in words as well as by symbol. Mark the person's own reaction distinctly in shape or text as well as colour. A reaction never moves the conversation, never sends a separate message, and never notifies by default where the author already sees the thread — state plainly when it does notify. Keep the bar keyboard-operable, label each reaction in words for assistive technology, and position the bar so it never covers the message it reacts to. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Pinned message | Keep one important message in view at the top of a conversation | Pinned banner, pin and unpin controls, pinned list, jump-to-original control | Pin from the message itself, by long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message, never from a separate settings screen alone. State the scope plainly before pinning: a shared pin changes what everyone in the conversation sees, while a personal pin changes only one person's view. Show the pinned message as a banner at the top of the conversation with the sender, the time, and a short preview, and let tapping the banner jump to the original message in place, with a clear way back to the latest messages. Replacing a shared pinned message asks for confirmation first, and unpinning is one action with the banner removed at once. Where several pins are allowed, list them in pinned order, state the limit up front, and let each entry jump to its original message. A pinned message never replaces search or a saved item: pinning is for shared orientation in the conversation, saving is for one person's later return. Keep the banner keyboard-operable, label the pinned state in words as well as by symbol, and never let the banner cover the compose input. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row and the Saved-item collection row |
| Message edit and delete | Correct or remove a message already sent | Edit control, delete control, edited marker, deletion placeholder, scope choice, confirm control | Offer edit and delete from the message itself, by long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message, never from a separate settings screen alone. Editing opens the original text in the compose input with a visible editing state and a cancel control, and a saved edit keeps the message in place with an edited marker in words beside the time, so later readers know the text changed. Where a correction changes the meaning after others have read it, state the change plainly rather than silently replacing the text, and never rewrite replies that quoted the original. Deleting asks for confirmation first and states the scope before it lands: removal for one person only, or removal for everyone in the conversation, with a placeholder left in place stating that a message was deleted, so the thread still reads coherently. A deleted message never reappears on reload or on another device, and attachments follow the same scope as the text. Keep edit and delete keyboard-operable, label each action in words, and never offer deletion as the only way to correct a small slip. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row and the Pinned message row |
| Undo send | Stop a message that has just been sent, before it leaves | Send confirmation, undo control with remaining time, delay setting, draft return | Hold the message for a short delay after the send action — a few seconds, configurable where offered — and show an undo control for the whole of that window, with the remaining time visible, so the person can stop it without hunting for a recall control. Using undo returns the message to the compose view with its text, recipients, and attachments intact, ready to edit or discard, and never sends a partial copy. Once the window ends, the message goes as addressed and can no longer be stopped from this control: state that plainly, and never label the delay window as recalling a delivered message, since a delivered message cannot be pulled back by this control. Keep the undo control keyboard-operable and announced to assistive technology, and keep the delay short enough that a reply is not mistaken for silence. Distinct from the message edit and delete row, which acts on a message already delivered, and from the scheduled action row, which holds an action until a chosen later time. Cross-check the undo and trash recovery row and the draft autosave row |
| Swipe-to-reveal actions | Act on a list item without opening it, by revealing its actions with a swipe | List row, revealed action set, primary action, non-gesture route, undo offer | A partial swipe reveals a small set of named actions beside the row — at most two or three, each labelled in text as well as by icon — and tapping one acts on that row only; a full swipe commits the primary action at once, so the primary action is never destructive. Keep the revealed actions on the same side for every row, and keep the row itself readable while actions are shown, never pushing its content out of view. A swipe is never the only route: every revealed action stays reachable through a menu, a button, or a long-press route, with the same names and the same effect, and the action set is announced to assistive technology with the row it belongs to. For a destructive action, offer an undo after the action lands rather than asking for confirmation mid-swipe, and return the row to its resting state once the action is chosen or dismissed. Where a swipe conflicts with scrolling, lock the direction once movement passes a small threshold so a vertical scroll never reveals actions by accident. Cross-check the list reorder row and the undo and trash recovery row |
| Poll | Collect a quick group decision inside a conversation or a page | Question, option list, vote control, results view, close statement | State the question and the choice rule up front: one choice only, or several choices, and how many may be picked, so nobody votes twice by mistake. Offer each option as a labelled control that works by keyboard as well as by pointer, and confirm the vote at once with the chosen option marked in words as well as in shape. Where seeing early results would bias later votes, hold results until the person has voted or the poll closes, and say so before voting; where results are live, update counts and percentages in text as well as in bar length, and announce changes as a status message without moving focus. State before voting whether votes are anonymous or named, and whether a vote can be changed before the poll closes; a changed vote replaces the earlier one rather than adding a second count. State the close time up front, show the final result with counts and percentages once closed, and keep closed polls readable but no longer votable. Never let a poll replace a decision that needs a named, accountable answer. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Group scheduling poll | Find a time that works for a whole group | Candidate times or availability grid, per-person response, overlap view, best-time ranking, confirm control | Offer two forms and choose by the question: a shortlist of candidate times where a few times are already plausible, and a blank availability grid where no candidate stands out. On a shortlist, each person answers yes, no, or maybe for each time; on a grid, each person marks the hours they are free, by tap or drag, with a keyboard route to the same marks. Show the overlap as a heatmap plus counts in text, never colour alone, and rank the best windows by how many required people are free, with a view of exactly who is free and who is not for any window. Let the organiser filter the ranking to required people only, so an optional attendee never blocks a workable time. Each person sees every time in their own zone, with the organiser zone beside it, and a response can be changed before the poll closes. State the close time up front, keep a closed poll readable but no longer votable, and issue the chosen time as a calendar invitation rather than leaving it as a poll result. Distinct from the Poll row, which settles one group decision, and from the Calendar and scheduling row, which books slots rather than collecting availability. Cross-check the Poll row and the Calendar and scheduling row |
| Conversation branching | Explore an alternative direction without losing the current thread | Branch control on a message, branch label, branch list or tree, compare view | Branching starts from any message, not only the latest one: the new branch inherits the conversation up to that message, and the original thread stays unchanged and reachable. Label each branch by its intent so branches stay distinguishable; a branch list or tree shows where each branch started and which branches are active, archived, or merged back. Editing an earlier message or requesting another response for the same input creates a branch rather than overwriting history, so earlier answers stay reviewable. Branches are walked one at a time, and a side-by-side view compares two branches before anything from one is carried into the other. Keep the branch count visible and offer archiving for stalled branches, so exploration does not bury the main thread. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| List navigation | Move through long result lists | Result list, pager or load-more control, position indicator | Match the control to the intent: infinite scroll for exploratory browsing and passive consumption, pagination for goal-oriented tasks where people compare, reference, or return to specific items. Pagination gives structure — a clear start, middle, and end, a sense of place, reliable back-button behaviour — and is the more accessible and crawlable choice; infinite scroll loses the person's place, frustrates backward navigation, and grows memory without bound. When unsure, a load-more button is the safe default: the smooth feel with the person in control and the footer reachable. With infinite scroll, add a visible loading indicator, URL updates so people can return to their place, a back-to-top control, live-region announcements, and a paginated fallback for crawlers |
| View switcher | Change how one collection is presented without changing what it holds | View controls, list presentation, grid presentation, current-view indicator | Switching the view changes presentation only: the same items, in the same order, under the same sort and filters, so nothing is silently reordered, hidden, or re-filtered by the switch. Offer the switch as a labelled single-select control with one current view marked in text as well as shape, never as a bare icon pair and never as an on/off switch, since the choices are named presentations rather than a setting. Keep each item recognisable across views — same title and key facts — and keep selection, focus, and scroll position where they were, announcing the new view and the result count to assistive technology without moving focus. Remember the chosen view across sessions where the same collection is revisited, and fall back to the list presentation on narrow screens or where a grid would truncate essential facts. Distinct from the List navigation row, which pages through a long list, and from the Button group primitive, which supplies the control shape. Cross-check the filtering and sorting row |
| Sticky section headers | Keep a grouped list oriented while scrolling | Grouped list, section header, pinned header area | Pin only the header of the section currently in view, and let the next section header push it away rather than stacking headers on top of each other. Keep the pinned header compact, with an opaque background, so it never covers a large share of the viewport and never hides the focused item or the item reached by a jump link. Give every section header a heading level and a distinct label, so the current section stays clear in text as well as in position, and keep the section order in the underlying order so keyboard and assistive-technology order match the visual order. Where the list is short, or where pinning would leave too little room for content at high zoom, leave headers in the normal flow instead of pinning them. Cross-check the List navigation row |
| Tree view | Move through a nested hierarchy without losing place | Nested node list, expand and collapse controls, selection state, position statement | The tree is a single tab stop: arrow keys move within it, with up and down stepping to the previous and next visible node, right expanding a closed branch or stepping into its first child, and left collapsing an open branch or stepping back to its parent; Home and End jump to the first and last visible nodes, and type-ahead jumps to the next node starting with the typed characters. Every branch states whether it is expanded or collapsed, and every node states its level and its position among its siblings, so the structure is clear without sight. Selecting a node expands its ancestors so the selection is always visible, and focus stays on the selected node when the tree is left and re-entered. Offer expand-all and collapse-all controls only where the whole hierarchy stays usable when fully open; for large or remote hierarchies, load children on expansion, state that more children are loading, and never present a branch as empty while its children are still loading. Keep expand and collapse controls separate from selection, so opening a branch never silently selects it, and keep the row label as the selection target rather than the expand control alone. Cross-check the List navigation row |
| Machine-composed content disclosure | Show content a system composed on its own | Composed block, origin label, uncertainty note, correction control | Label every block the system composed — text, image, summary, or suggestion — as composed, never presenting it as authored by a person; state plainly what is uncertain and what data or sources grounded it; keep a visible correction path and an override that returns control to the person; a composed decision with effects outside the screen always pairs with an explicit approval step. Carry the composed label as a machine-readable mark attached to the content itself, so it survives copying, sharing, and re-posting instead of living only in the surrounding page; a label that disappears when the content is copied stops disclosing at the point it matters most. Cross-check the trust patterns in trends/2026/agentic-ux.md and the disclosure rules in trends/2026/conversational-interfaces.md |
| Agent checkpoint | Supervise a delegated task through pause points | Delegation summary, live progress, approval card, interrupt control, refinement input, decision record | The loop is delegate, monitor, approve, interrupt, refine: the person delegates a task, watches progress, and the flow pauses at checkpoints before any irreversible action. A checkpoint is a pause point, not a notification: execution waits for a decision, and the paused state stays visible with its progress intact. The approval card shows the exact payload — recipient, change, amount, and tool — in plain words, and a decision applies to that exact request only; a changed amount, recipient, or target needs a fresh decision. Decisions are approve, edit, or reject: editing the payload before approval is a first-class path, and rejection returns the flow to the person with the draft and the reason kept. Low-stakes reversible actions skip checkpoints under a stated autonomy budget, so approval fatigue never trains people to approve blindly; the budget itself is visible and changeable. While paused, the person can interrupt, redirect, or refine the plan without losing completed work, and when a flow took a plausible but wrong route, recovery restores the prior state and names what went wrong. Every decision is recorded with its time, scope, and outcome for later review. Cross-check the supervision and trust-gradient sections in ../trends/2026/agentic-ux.md |

| Notification digest | Group low-urgency updates into a scheduled summary | Digest entry, grouped items, period label, frequency control, individual open actions | Group related updates by thread, type, or time window instead of sending one interruption per event, and label the period the digest covers so nothing looks newer or older than it is. A digest holds low-urgency, repeatable updates only: never digest a critical or time-sensitive item, and never let a digest be the only place an action-required item appears. Every item inside stays individually openable, and opening one item marks only that item read; the unread count reflects unopened items, not digests delivered. The person controls the schedule — daily, weekly, or none — and the channel, and an empty period sends no digest rather than a summary of nothing. When a grouped event turns urgent, it leaves the digest and follows the notification escalation scale at once. Cross-check the notification escalation row |
| Personalized-content disclosure | Show why adapted content appears and how to change it | Adapted-block label, signal statement, unadapted-view control, change and turn-off controls | When order, content, or recommendations adapt to the person, state the signal that drove the adaptation in plain words at the point it appears — for example, recent activity or a stated preference — rather than in a separate policy page. Offer the unadapted view beside the adapted one so the person can compare, and make the adaptation reversible: change the signal or turn the adaptation off without losing saved content, and state plainly what cannot be changed as well as what can. Never adapt silently and never present adapted content as if it were the full or neutral set. Keep the disclosure attached to the content it describes, and keep the controls reachable from that same place, not only from settings. Cross-check the granular consent row |
| Calendar and scheduling | Show objects in time and book slots without ambiguity | Time-scale switch, date grid, slot list, timezone label, review step | Choose the time scale by the planning question: a month view for broad orientation, a week view for durations and conflicts, a day view for a detailed schedule, and an agenda list for sparse or crowded days. Switching scale preserves the selected date and context, the current range stays visible, and a return-to-present control is always offered. Keep a calendar distinct from a date picker: a calendar displays and manages objects in time, while a date picker collects a single value, so a full schedule never inherits a picker's interaction without review. Make a day click's effect predictable — opening detail, starting a request, or committing a booking — and never let a click unexpectedly create a shared event; a pending approval never looks like a confirmed booking. Slot states stay distinct — available, pending, booked, and unavailable — and an empty slot list caused by a loading or connection failure never reads as a closed schedule. If a chosen slot becomes unavailable, keep the person's context and offer the nearest alternative instead of resetting the view. Recurrence shows an inspectable rule, and editing states its scope — one occurrence or the whole series — before anything is applied; where regions differ, each event carries its own timezone label; cross-zone scheduling: where the organiser and an attendee sit in different zones, each event states both zones where they differ, and invitations show each attendee their own local time with the organiser zone beside it. Times around a zone transition that are ambiguous or repeated are flagged before booking, with the possible readings shown and a choice required. A recurring event is pinned to a named zone, never to a fixed offset, so clock changes never silently shift it; the recurrence preview shows how each attendee's local time changes across the next transition before the series is confirmed. Editing a recurring event states whether the change follows the series zone or the local zone before it is applied. Where a meeting crosses a date boundary for some attendees, the date each attendee sees is stated beside their time. When several zones must be compared, show them on one shared timeline with working hours marked, rather than asking anyone to convert by hand | Direct manipulation follows the same rules: dragging an event moves it, and dragging an edge changes its duration; the new time is previewed with any conflict shown before the move is committed, and a move can be undone. A drag never relies on gesture alone — the same move is reachable by keyboard or menu. In a month view a drag moves the event to another day while keeping its clock time; timed resizing belongs in a day or week view, where the edge being dragged states which end it changes.
| Timeline view | Plan and review work laid along a time axis, where durations and order matter | Time axis, item bars, milestone markers, now marker, zoom control, grouping rows, detail panel | Choose the form by the question: rows over a shared time axis for planned work with durations and dependencies, a vertical sequence for a history of milestones, and grouped or clustered bars where many items share a stretch of time. Label the time axis at every scale, keep the current range visible, and always offer a return-to-present control; changing scale changes the level of detail shown, not only the magnification, and preserves the selected item and date. Draw each item as a bar from its start to its end, and mark milestones as single points, never as bars with invented length; show a dependency as a link between items and state plainly when moving one item shifts another, rather than shifting dependents silently. Mark the present on the axis so past work, work in progress, and planned work stay distinct, and never present a planned item as completed. Where a stretch holds many items, group or cluster them and expand on demand, and let filtering and search narrow the view without losing the person's place. Keep every item reachable by keyboard, open detail in place or in a panel that returns to the same position, and never carry status by colour alone. Distinct from the Activity feed row, which lists past point events without durations, and from the Calendar and scheduling row, which books slots rather than laying durations along an axis. |
| Appointment management | Change or cancel a booked appointment without losing the original booking | Confirmation manage actions, reschedule picker, cancellation confirmation, reminder change links, policy statement | Show reschedule and cancel beside the booked time on the confirmation itself, not only in a separate list, so the person can act where the booking is stated. State the cancellation policy at booking, in plain words, rather than leaving it in terms alone. Rescheduling re-checks availability and keeps the original booking until the new slot is confirmed, then states plainly what changed — time, place, or both — and stops reminders for the old time. Cancelling asks for confirmation, states the consequence at once, frees the slot, and stops further reminders for it. Every reminder carries a change route alongside confirmation, so a change arrives with notice instead of a missed visit. Order the booking flow as service, then staff, then time, then details, then confirmation, so duration and availability are known before personal details are asked; never demand account creation before a first booking — offer the account on the confirmation instead. Open the slot picker on the next days that hold slots, leading with the first available appointment and grouping times into morning, afternoon, and evening, rather than opening on a bare month grid. Where a freed slot can be offered onward, offer it to the next waiting person rather than leaving it unfilled, and never treat a cancelled booking as still booked. Distinct from the calendar and scheduling row, which covers how times are shown and booked, and from the delivery slot picker row, which covers a checkout window. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row and the scheduled action row |
| Keyboard shortcut reference | Teach shortcuts and let people change them, without hiding any command | Searchable grouped list, key display, remap control, conflict warning, reset control | Reach the reference from help and from any screen where shortcuts matter; group entries by context — global, current screen, and text editing — and make the list searchable by action name and by key, so a person can find a shortcut from either end. Every entry shows the action, its keys, and the context where it applies; keys that do nothing in the current context are shown as unavailable rather than hidden, so the list stays honest. Show each shortcut beside its action in menus, tooltips, and the palette, so the reference teaches rather than replaces discovery. Single-character shortcuts are the exception, not the rule: they apply only while the relevant component has focus, and every such shortcut can be turned off or remapped, so spoken input and accidental presses never trigger actions. Remapping asks the person to press the new combination, warns before replacing a shortcut already in use by naming the action it would displace, and asks for confirmation before the replacement lands; a reset restores defaults for one group or for all. Changed shortcuts apply at once and persist across sessions. A shortcut is never the only route to an action: every shortcut action stays reachable by pointer, menu, or palette. Where no physical keyboard is present, the reference stays out of the way and never blocks the task. Cross-check the command palette row |
| Undo and trash recovery | Let people reverse destructive actions without fear | Undo offer, trash area, restore action, retention statement, permanent-delete step | Offer an immediate undo after every destructive action: the offer stays visible long enough to be noticed and acted on, and using it restores the item to its original place with its content intact. For actions that are easy to repeat — removing a list item, clearing a selection — undo alone is enough and no confirmation dialog is needed. For actions with wider effects — deleting shared content, revoking access, removing linked data — ask for confirmation first, and still keep the trash safety net behind it. Deleted items move to a trash area rather than vanishing: the trash lists what was deleted and when, and every item there can be restored to its original place. State the retention period plainly — for example, that items stay recoverable for a stated number of days — and never silently shorten it. Restoring a parent restores its children with it; restoring a child whose parent is still in trash waits for the parent, or states plainly why it cannot return yet. Permanent deletion is a separate step from the trash: it states exactly what is lost, including linked content, and it never runs as a side effect of emptying another area. A trashed item that other content links to keeps a visible placeholder or states that the link is broken, never silently dangling. Cross-check the confirmation row and the sync-conflict resolution row |
| Review prompt | Ask for a rating at the right moment, without pestering | Success-moment trigger, system review prompt, suppression rules, manual review entry | Ask only after a clear success moment — a completed task, a reached milestone, or a finished session without errors — never on first launch, during a task, during payment, or after an error or crash. Require repeated value before asking: several sessions or several success moments, not the first one. Use the system review prompt where one exists, so its frequency limits and opt-out are honoured; never build a custom screen that sends only satisfied people to a public review while diverting others, and never offer a reward for a rating or gate a feature behind one. Suppress the prompt while a support issue is open. Treat a suppressed or unseen prompt as a declined opportunity, not a failure to retry at once: record the attempt and wait for a later success moment. Keep a quiet manual route, such as an entry in settings or about, so a person who wants to leave a review can always reach it without waiting for a prompt. Cross-check the Install prompt row |
| Rating and review submission | Collect a structured rating and a written review that readers can trust | Rating control, written-review field, visibility statement, edit-window statement, moderation notice | Offer a structured rating with an optional written review, and make the rating control fully keyboard-operable with each step labelled in words, not by symbol alone. Before submitting, state plainly what will be shown publicly, what is held for moderation, and whether the review is shown with a name or anonymously. A person who already left a review edits that review rather than adding a second one, and the edit window is stated up front — for example, a stated number of days — with edits returning to moderation where moderation applies. A moderation decision states its reason and offers a path to revise and resubmit; a rejected review is never silently rewritten. Never gate a core feature behind leaving a rating, and never offer a reward for one. Cross-check the review prompt row and the confirmation row |
| Rating distribution summary | Show how ratings are spread, not just the average | Average score, review count, distribution bars, star-level filter | Show the average with its scale and the total number of reviews beside it, so a single average never stands alone. Show the share of reviews at each rating level as labelled bars, with each bar giving its count in words as well as its length, so the spread is readable without colour alone and without relying on bar length. Make each level a filter that narrows the review list to that level, and state the active filter in words with a clear way to clear it. Never merge scores from unrelated sources into one unexplained number, and never present selected quotations as though they were the full distribution. Cross-check the Rating and review submission row |
| Payment failure recovery | Recover a failed payment without losing the order | Failure notice, preserved order summary, retry control, method choice, status check | When a payment fails, keep the person in the same checkout with the order intact: items, amounts, delivery details, and entered input preserved, never sent back to an empty cart. State the failure in plain words with an actionable cause where it is known — for example, a declined card, incorrect details, or a lost connection — never a raw code alone. Offer a retry only where it can succeed: correct the details or choose another eligible method on the same screen, with no restart of the order. Where the outcome is unknown or pending, check the real status first and never offer a blind retry that could charge twice: one order, one charge, with the check stated before any second attempt. For a pending payment, say confirmation is still running, show how to check its status, and hold the order rather than showing failure prematurely. After a later successful payment, confirm once with the order number, amount, and delivery expectation; any follow-up route back to payment first confirms the original order was not already paid. Cross-check the checkout variants in layouts/ecommerce.md and the confirmation row |
| Page not found | Tell the person plainly that the page does not exist and offer a way forward | Plain not-found statement, kept address, way back, search offer, report route | State in plain words that the page could not be found, without a bare error code as the only message, and keep the requested address visible so the person can check it for a typing slip. Keep the page inside the site — same header, navigation, and footer — so it is clear the person has not left. Offer one clear way back, such as the home page, plus the two or three pages people most often want, and a search box so a person who knows what they were after can find it without starting over. Keep the choices few: a long list of links turns a dead end into a new maze. Offer a way to report the broken link, so dead ends the site owner does not know about can be fixed. Keep the page light and fast, with any decoration small and never in the way of the way out. Distinct from the error state rules in `catalog/states.md`, which cover failures inside a working page, and from the service status notice row, which reports a service-wide problem. Cross-check the breadcrumb navigation row and the search row |
| Content reporting and blocking | Report harmful content and stop contact from a person, without losing the reporter's place | Report action, reason choice, detail field, block control, blocked list, review path | Offer the report action from the item itself, never only from a distant settings screen, and ask for a plain reason choice with an optional detail field rather than a free-text essay. On submitting, hide the reported item from the reporter at once and state plainly what happens next: what was reported, what outcome the reporter will be told, and where to check it. Reporting and blocking stay separate actions: reporting asks for review of the item, blocking hides the blocked party everywhere at once — content, messages, and mentions — without telling the blocked party who blocked them. A blocked list in settings lets the person review and unblock, and unblocking restores visibility without re-sending past messages. Where a report decision is contested, offer a review path that states what was decided, why, and what new information would change it; a decision in error restores the item and tells the reporter. Never require a report before allowing a block, and never treat a block as a report. Cross-check the confirmation row |
| Language and locale selection | Change the language of the interface without losing work or landing on an unreadable page | Language list, scope choice, preview, fallback notice, persistent selector entry | Write every language name in its own language, never in the current language alone, and never use a flag alone to stand for a language, since flags mark countries and many languages cross borders. State the scope of the choice before it applies — the current session only, or a saved preference for later visits — and apply it to the same page, not back to a home screen, with entered input, drafts, and scroll position kept. Show the change at once as a preview the person can undo, rather than a silent reload that loses work. Where a language is only partly available, fall back item by item to the previous language and say so, never mixing languages silently and never leaving a page with no readable route back: the selector itself stays readable in every language it lists and stays reachable from the same place on every screen. Keep language choice separate from region choice where region changes formats, currency, or available content, and state that difference plainly. Cross-check the internationalization section in catalog/primitives.md |
| Scheduled action | Set an action to run later, and stay in control until it runs | Scheduled-time statement, scheduled list, edit and cancel controls, run confirmation, missed-run report | State the scheduled time in plain words with the date, time, and zone where the zone could change the meaning, and show the scheduled item in a list the person can return to, with its content and its scheduled time both visible. Until the action runs, it stays editable and cancellable: editing the content keeps the scheduled time unless the time is also changed, and changing the time keeps the content, so neither edit silently resets the other. Sending or running early stays an explicit choice from the scheduled item, never a side effect of editing it. When the action runs, confirm once with what ran and when, and never repeat the confirmation on every later visit. When a run is missed or fails — because the device was off, the connection failed, or the target changed — report it plainly with the cause where known, keep the item with its content intact, and offer a retry at a new time or a cancel path, never silently dropping it. A cancelled scheduled action keeps its draft where a draft is useful, and cancelling never sends the action. Cross-check the confirmation row and the draft autosave row |

| Limited-time offer countdown | Show how long a real offer lasts without pressuring a decision the person has not made | Countdown display, exact end statement, expiry consequence, offer terms link | Show the countdown beside the exact end date, time, and zone, and state plainly what happens when the time runs out — a price change, loss of access, or loss of a bonus — so the countdown never stands alone. Tie the timer to one real deadline: it never resets on a revisit, never restarts after expiry, and never counts down to a deadline that moves. Keep the offer terms reachable from the countdown itself, and keep the countdown beside the action it relates to rather than floating over unrelated content. For assistive technology, prefer a static end statement, and where a live countdown is shown, announce updates at most once a minute as a status message without moving focus, never every second; a purely visual ticking display stays hidden from assistive technology, with the same end information given in words nearby. When the time runs out, replace the countdown with the expired state at once and remove or disable the offer action rather than leaving a live timer on a dead offer. Distinct from the session-expiry warning row, which ends a session, and from the scheduled action row, which runs an action at a future time. Cross-check the session-expiry warning row |
| Age verification gate | Confirm a person meets an age requirement before showing restricted content | Gate statement, age input, decline route, remembered answer, challenge route | State the age requirement and why it applies before asking anything, and keep the restricted content blocked until the gate is answered, with no interaction with it in the meantime. Ask only for what the requirement needs: a threshold confirmation or an age band where that suffices, a fuller date where the rule requires it, and identity proof only where a further check is required, never collecting fuller details by default. Show a neutral gate with nothing pre-selected, so the person must make a choice rather than accept a suggested answer. Offer a plain decline route that leads somewhere useful, such as a general landing view, rather than a dead end, and state plainly what happens on each outcome: meeting the requirement continues into the content, a further check states what it asks for and why, and falling below the requirement blocks access with a plain explanation and no bypass route. Remember the answer only where that is appropriate, state how long it is remembered, and re-ask where the rule requires a fresh answer. Distinct from the consent choice row, which asks permission for data use, and from the passkey sign-in row, which proves a credential. Cross-check the consent choice row |

| Breadcrumb navigation | Show where the current page sits in a hierarchy and offer a way back up it | Trail list, step links, current-page mark, overflow control | Show the path from the home view to the current page, with each step a working link except the current page, which is marked as current in text as well as in shape, never colour alone. Omit the trail on single-level pages and in task flows where a trail would imply a hierarchy that does not exist, and use it only as secondary navigation, never as the only way to move between top-level sections. On narrow screens collapse the middle steps into an overflow control that always keeps the home view and the parent step reachable, and never remove the trail entirely or rely on horizontal scrolling alone to reveal hidden steps. Truncate long step labels only with the full text still available, keep one separator style throughout, and keep the trail to one line. Cross-check the primary navigation row |
| Annotation and comment thread | Discuss a specific part of content without losing its context | Anchor marker, thread list, reply control, resolve and reopen controls, thread overview | Anchor each thread to the specific section, sentence, or element it discusses, so the discussion travels with its context rather than sitting as a separate stream at the end of the document. Keep replies nested under the thread they answer, and let thread visibility follow the visibility of the content itself: a person who cannot see the content cannot see its threads. Resolving a thread hides it from the active view without deleting it, and reopening restores it with its history intact; an overview lists open and resolved threads so nothing is lost by resolving. Where the anchor was deleted or heavily rewritten, keep the thread with the quoted original text marked as outdated, offer resolve or reopen, and never silently delete the discussion. Distinct from the chat and messaging row, which covers conversation rather than anchored comment |
| Suggestion review | Review a proposed change to shared content before it becomes final | Mode switch, proposed-change marking, suggestion card, accept and reject controls, discussion link | Offer a proposing mode distinct from direct editing, so a change made in that mode stays a proposal until it is accepted, and never overwrites the current content silently. Mark proposed additions and removals in the text itself, in shape as well as colour, and show each proposal beside the content it changes with its author stated in text, so a reviewer can judge it without hunting for it. Let each proposal be accepted or rejected on its own, and attach a discussion thread to the proposal so a question stays with the change it concerns; where a thread is linked to a proposal, state what resolving or deleting the thread does to the proposal before the person acts, so a discussion action never changes content by surprise. Offer a view that shows the content with all proposals accepted and a view that shows it with all proposals rejected, so the effect of a batch decision is checked before it is applied, and never apply a batch accept without that preview. A rejected proposal leaves the original text untouched and stays reachable in a resolved list, so nothing is lost by rejecting. Distinct from the annotation and comment thread row, which discusses content without changing it, and from the version history and restore row, which returns to a saved state rather than deciding a pending proposal. Cross-check the annotation and comment thread row and the collaborative editing presence row |
| Theme selection | Let the person choose a light, dark, or system-following theme without losing work | Theme choice, live preview, saved preference, system-change statement | Offer three choices — light, dark, and following the system — labelled in plain words, and apply the choice at once as a preview that loses no entered input and can be undone. A saved preference survives later visits and keeps overriding the system theme until the person changes it, while a system-following choice applies a system change at once and states that it did so. Guarantee that text stays readable in every theme by checking contrast in each theme, never in one alone, and never let a theme change move, hide, or reset entered input. Distinct from the token and contrast rules in trends/2026/dark-mode.md, which describe how themes are built rather than how the choice is offered. Cross-check the language and locale selection row for the same-page, no-loss switch rule |
| Profile and account menu | Reach the current account's own actions from one place, without confusing them with other accounts or destructive actions | Account trigger, account header, action list, sign-out action, settings link | Anchor the menu to a trigger that names the active account in text, never by an image alone, and open it with the account's own actions only — view account, open settings, manage sign-in methods, and sign out — grouped so account actions stay apart from product navigation. Keep sign-out separate from destructive account actions such as closing the account, with its own spacing and wording, so a routine sign-out is never mistaken for deletion. After any menu action that does not leave the task, return the person to the same place with the same input; actions that do leave the task say where they go before they run. Keep the menu short: infrequent account actions belong on the full account or settings page, not in the menu, and the menu never hides the only route to sign-out. Distinct from the account switcher row, which moves between signed-in accounts, and from the settings layout, which is a full page rather than a menu. Cross-check the account switcher row |
| Profile photo upload and crop | Choose and crop a photo shown to others | Photo choice, crop preview in the shown shape, size and format statement, remove control, keyboard crop alternative | State the size and format limit beside the control before anyone chooses a photo, so a choice is never rejected after the effort of cropping. Preview the crop in exactly the shape shown to others — circle, square, or rounded shape — including at the smallest size it appears, so nothing essential sits outside the visible area. Let the person move and resize the crop, and offer a keyboard-operable crop with a stated alternative where cropping by drag is not possible, rather than leaving keyboard users with a fixed crop they cannot adjust. A remove control restores a neutral placeholder at once, without deleting the original photo from the device. Never apply a new photo silently after cropping: confirm the preview shown, and keep the previous photo until that confirmation. Distinct from the file upload row, which moves files in reliably, and from the profile and account menu row, which lists account actions. Cross-check the profile and account menu row |
| Recently viewed | Return to items opened before, without searching again | Recents list, item entry, last-opened label, clear-all control, missing-item notice | List items the person opened, newest first, with each entry naming the item and when it was last opened in plain words, so the list reads as a history rather than a set of recommendations. State the scope plainly — what kinds of items are remembered, on this device only or across devices, and for how long — and offer a clear-all control plus removal of single entries, since a history the person cannot prune becomes a record they did not choose. An entry whose item no longer exists says so in place and offers removal, never a dead link and never a silent swap to a different item. Keep recents distinct from saved items: recents are recorded automatically on opening, saved items are chosen deliberately, and neither list feeds the other without an explicit action. On shared devices, recents stay behind sign-in and clear on sign-out unless the person chose otherwise. Cross-check the saved-item collection row |
| Invitation response | Answer an invitation with a clear effect for each reply, and change the answer without creating a duplicate | Invitation summary, accept, decline, and tentative actions, reply-change control, guest-count control, withdrawn-or-expired notice | State in the invitation itself what is asked, who sent it, and when an answer is needed, with the three replies offered side by side in plain words: accept, decline, and tentative, each stating its effect before it is chosen — accept confirms attendance and blocks the time, tentative holds the time without confirming, decline frees the time and removes the item from the calendar. A changed reply updates the organiser and the attendees in place without creating a duplicate item, and the current reply stays visible on the invitation so the person never answers twice by mistake. Where a proposed time is offered instead, it keeps the original time visible until the organiser accepts it, and the invitation states that the proposal is not yet confirmed. A withdrawn or expired invitation states plainly that it no longer applies, with no reply actions offered and no silent disappearance from the list. Where guests may be brought, state the allowed number beside the reply before it is chosen, and collect a name for each additional guest rather than a bare count, so seating and check-in can be planned. A changed guest count updates the total headcount in place, and declining removes the additional guests as well as the person's own reply, with the updated total confirmed on the invitation. Distinct from the calendar and scheduling row, which covers how times are shown across zones. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row |
| Modal dialog | Ask one decision in a layer above the current task, without losing the task underneath | Dialog layer, title, decision actions, dismiss control, focus return | Ask exactly one decision in the dialog and keep the rest of the task on the page underneath, so closing the dialog returns the person to the same place with the same input. Take focus into the dialog on opening and return it to the control that opened it on closing, trap focus inside while the dialog is open, and offer a visible dismiss path besides the decision actions — a close control and a keyboard escape — so the dialog is never the only way forward. Never nest a second dialog inside the first: a second layer of decisions becomes a new view, and long content that needs scrolling belongs on a full page rather than in a dialog. On narrow screens the dialog becomes a full-width sheet with its actions in thumb reach, and a dialog that blocks the task states plainly why it cannot be dismissed until the decision is made. Cross-check the confirmation row and the no-nesting rule in the layout rules of the same file |
| Bottom sheet choice | Choose one option from a sheet that rises from the lower edge, without confusing selection with application | Sheet heading, option list, pending marker, named apply control, close control | Give the sheet one short task connected to the view underneath, such as choosing which place to browse, and keep the background partly visible so the connection stays clear; a task needing several steps, long reading, or repeated return belongs on its own page, not in a sheet. Pick one behaviour and keep it everywhere: either tapping an option applies it at once, or tapping only marks it as pending and a named apply control — stating the result, such as "Use this place" — applies it and closes the sheet. Never mix the two: never show an apply control for a choice that already applied, and never apply silently where an apply control is shown. While a choice is only pending, the view underneath keeps the previously applied value, and the heading and the pending marker stay visible so the two values are never confused. Every way out follows the same rule: a close control, a swipe, a backdrop tap, and the platform Back action all discard a pending choice when closing without applying, and reopening the sheet shows the applied value, never an abandoned pending one. A drag handle is a cue only, never the sole way out: always offer a control that can be activated without dragging. Keep the apply control visible without covering the last row, add padding at the end of the scrolling area, and keep the heading and close route understandable when the list scrolls or expands, never presenting a clipped row as the complete set. Where the sheet holds a search field, check the layout with the on-screen keyboard open, so the field, the results, and the action stay usable, and define what Back does while the keyboard is open rather than leaving it implicit. Distinct from the sheet primitive, which describes the component parts, from the modal dialog row, which asks one decision in a layer, from the filtering and sorting row, which applies several facet choices at once, and from the selecting an option row, which matches the control to the length of the set. Cross-check the modal dialog row, the filtering and sorting row, and the location selection row |
| Image gallery viewer | View a set of images one at a time with a zoomed view | Thumbnail list, viewer layer, navigation controls, zoom control, close control | Each thumbnail names what it selects, and the active thumbnail is marked as current in text as well as in shape, so the position in the set stays clear without relying on colour alone. Opening the viewer moves focus into it and closing returns focus to the thumbnail that opened it, with keyboard navigation between images and a keyboard close that always works. The viewer controls — close, previous, next, and thumbnails — stay reachable without horizontal scrolling at high magnification, even where the zoomed image itself needs panning in two directions. Where a zoomed view is offered inline on the page instead of a separate viewer layer, no focus containment is needed, since focus never leaves the page context; choose one route and keep its keyboard and focus rules consistent. Cross-check the modal dialog row |
| Guest checkout | Complete a purchase without creating an account, and keep the order if an account is created later | Guest path, order summary, post-purchase account offer, order-lookup link | Offer guest checkout beside sign-in rather than behind it, and ask only for what fulfilment needs — contact, delivery, and payment details — with no password field before the order is placed. State the difference from signed-in checkout plainly before payment: a guest order has no saved details or order history, and the receipt link is the route to track, change, or return the order.
