After the order is confirmed, offer account creation as a single optional step that carries the order and its details into the new account, never asking the person to re-enter them, and never forcing the step to finish the purchase. Where an account is genuinely required — for a recurring plan or a regulated purchase — say why before checkout starts and preserve the order if sign-in interrupts it. Distinct from the checkout variants in layouts/ecommerce.md, which describe page shapes rather than the guest path. Cross-check the payment failure recovery row |
| Time-zone picker | Choose a time zone by place, with the current local time shown, without knowing an offset | Search field, place list, current-time label, offset label, detected-zone suggestion | List zones by place name with the country shown, and show the current local time and the offset for each entry, so the person recognises their zone by place rather than by a number they may not know. Offer a search field that matches place, zone name, and offset, and suggest the detected zone at the top of the list with a plain statement that it was detected and can be changed. Order the list by current time or by place rather than by offset alone, group by region where the list is long, and state plainly whether the chosen zone follows clock changes, with the next change date shown where one is near. Confirm the choice by showing an example time in the chosen zone beside the person's own time, so a wrong choice is visible before it is saved. Distinct from the cross-zone scheduling rules, which cover how booked times are shown to attendees. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row |
| Accessibility preferences | Let the person adjust text size, contrast, motion, and captions in one place, with a live preview, without hunting through separate settings | Preference list, live preview, reset control, system-preference statement | Gather the display and motion preferences that change readability into one reachable place — text size, bold text, increased contrast, reduced motion, reduced transparency, and captions — each labelled in plain words with what it changes, rather than scattered across settings or left only to the system. Apply each change at once to a live preview beside the control, so the effect is visible before it is saved, and keep entered input and place untouched while previewing. State plainly where a choice follows the system and where it overrides it, and apply the system value on first use where no saved choice exists. Offer a single reset that returns every preference to its starting value, and keep the preference saved across visits. Never use a preference as the only route to a readable screen: the starting state must already meet contrast and target-size rules, and a preference change must never hide content or break the layout. Distinct from theme selection, which chooses light, dark, or system-following colours rather than size, contrast, motion, and captions. Cross-check the theme selection row and the settings scope rules in layouts/settings.md |

| Long-document navigation | Move within a long page without losing place | Section outline, current-section marker, jump links, return-to-top control | Show a section outline built from the page headings, labelled as the page outline so it is never confused with site navigation. Mark the current section in text as well as in shape, and keep the outline reachable while scrolling — a sticky outline on wide screens, a single collapsed outline control on narrow screens. Jump links move keyboard focus to the section heading, never only the view, and the heading stays visible below any sticky header after the jump. Collapse the outline to main sections only, with deeper levels on request, so the outline never grows longer than the page it summarises. A return-to-top control returns to the page start without losing a form: in a form, it returns to the form start or keeps entered input intact. Where the page is short or has a single section, omit the outline rather than adding clutter. Cross-check the breadcrumb navigation row and the in-product help row |
| Reading progress indicator | Show how far through a long piece of reading the person has come, without replacing navigation | Slim bar or ring, tracked content scope, optional remaining-time label | Track only the main reading content, never the whole page including headers, footers, and unrelated sections, so the fill matches what remains to read; where the page is short enough to read without scrolling, omit the indicator rather than showing a full bar. Keep it decorative for assistive technology where no action depends on it, and never announce every percentage change, so reading is not interrupted. Honour reduced-motion settings by filling without animation, keep the fill visible against its track, and pair it with the section outline for jumping, never as the only way to move within the page. Where a remaining-time label is shown, base it on the tracked content and update it only when the position changes materially, not on every scroll step. Distinct from the long-document navigation row, which moves within a page, and from the background-task progress row, which follows a task rather than reading. Cross-check the long-document navigation row |
| Print-friendly view | Prepare a page for printing or saving as a document | Print view control, page preview, page count statement | Offer a reading view that removes navigation, banners, and interactive controls before printing, and state the page count before the person commits to print. Keep headings with the content that follows them, repeat table headers on each printed page, and let wide tables wrap rather than cutting off columns. Print links with their destinations in text where the destination matters, keep charts readable without colour alone, and keep the printed copy in the same order as the screen version. Distinct from the long-document navigation row, which moves within a screen rather than preparing it for print. Cross-check the long-document navigation row |
| In-app update prompt | Get the person to a newer version without interrupting a task | Flexible prompt, blocking prompt, deferral control, resume step | Grade the prompt by impact: a flexible prompt downloads in the background and lets the person keep working, while a blocking prompt is reserved for versions that can no longer work safely, and states plainly why the block exists. Never prompt during a task or a payment, and never prompt on first launch before the person has seen any value. A flexible prompt states a deferral limit and a deadline — how many times it may be deferred and the date after which it becomes blocking — and a deferral is honoured until that limit. After a flexible download finishes, offer one restart step at a moment the person chooses, and resume the interrupted task after an interrupted update rather than restarting it. A failed or cancelled update leaves the current version working with a plain retry path, never a dead end. Distinct from the what's-new announcement row, which reports changes rather than requiring an update. Cross-check the install prompt row |
| Pull-to-refresh | Re-read list content with a gesture and a matching control | Pull gesture, refresh indicator, refresh control, result statement | Offer pull-to-refresh only on lists whose content changes over time, at the top of the list, and always beside a visible refresh control for keyboard and assistive-technology users — the gesture is never the only route. While pulling, show a progress indicator that grows with the pull and states what is refreshing; releasing before the threshold cancels without fetching, and releasing past it starts the refresh. During refresh, keep the existing list visible and never hide it behind a spinner, and announce the refresh state to assistive technology. After refresh, state the result plainly — new items added, no change, or failure with a retry — and keep the person's place, never jumping to the top unless new items were added above and the jump is stated. A failed refresh keeps the old list with a retry control. Distinct from the offline-first sync row, which covers queued changes rather than re-reading content. Cross-check the list navigation row |
| Scrolling tab bar | Move between sections of one view when the section list overflows the available width | Scrollable tab strip, current-tab marker, overflow hint, tab panels | Keep the strip to a single row and scroll it instead of wrapping it into a second row, so the current row is never ambiguous; show a hint that more tabs exist beyond the edge — a partly visible next tab or a fade — and snap scrolling so a tab never rests half-cut at the edge. Mark the current tab in text as well as in shape, with the marker on the edge that touches the panel, and move it by sliding rather than blinking. Let arrow keys move between tabs, with Home and End reaching the first and last, and bring the newly selected tab fully into view. Mirror the scroll direction for right-to-left layouts. When the set grows so large that finding a tab by scrolling takes longer than scanning a list, replace the strip with a menu that lists every section and keeps the current section as its label. Distinct from the primary navigation row, which moves between top-level sections, and from the bottom tab bar, which is a fixed control rather than a scrolling list. Cross-check the primary navigation row |
| System share sheet | Share content through the platform share sheet | Share trigger, content preview, target list, cancel control, fallback route | Show a preview of exactly what will be shared — title, text, link, or file — before the sheet opens, so the person confirms the payload rather than the trigger. Offer recent targets first, then the full target list, and never reorder targets while the sheet is open. A cancel path shares nothing and returns to the same place with input intact; treat dismissal as a normal outcome, never an error. Where no share sheet exists, offer a fallback route — copy link or download — without presenting it as a failure. Share only what the preview showed; never add extra content or parameters silently. Distinct from the share link row, which covers link scope and expiry rather than the sheet itself. Cross-check the share link row |
| Find-in-page | Search within the current long page or document | Query field, match count, next and previous controls, match highlights, dismiss control | Keep the query persistent while stepping through matches; show the match position as a count (for example, 3 of 12) and move focus to the matched text, scrolling it into view below any sticky header. Highlight every match, with the current match distinct in shape as well as colour. Next and previous wrap or state the end plainly; a query with no matches says so in place and offers to clear or widen the query rather than a dead end. Dismissing returns to the previous place with highlights cleared. Where the content spans pages or unloaded sections, state the search scope plainly — searching only loaded content must never look like searching everything. Distinct from the search row, which finds content across the product rather than within the current page. Cross-check the search row and the long-document navigation row |
| Text-selection action menu | Act on selected text without leaving the place | Selection handles, floating action menu, secondary actions menu, dismiss gesture | Show the menu beside the selection, never covering the selected text, and keep the selection visible while the menu is open; choosing an action never replaces the selection itself. Offer the frequent actions first — copy, share, and define — with further actions behind a single secondary step, and label every action in words rather than by an icon alone. On touch surfaces, place a handle at each end of the selection so its bounds can be adjusted, and expand the selection in steps (word, then passage, then all) on repeated taps inside it. Dismiss the menu by tapping elsewhere or pressing escape, and return focus to the text without moving the view. Where the selection is editable text, include cut and paste alongside copy; where it is read-only, leave cut and paste out rather than showing actions that cannot run. Distinct from the inline assistant actions row, which acts on a selected object rather than on selected text, and from the bulk selection row, which acts on chosen items. Cross-check the find-in-page row |
| Trigger-character suggestion menu | Insert an item into text by typing a trigger character | Trigger character, floating suggestion list, filter behaviour, insertion point, dismiss control | Typing a trigger character (such as @ for a person or / for a block) opens a floating list beside the caret, filtered by the characters typed after the trigger; the list never covers the caret or the text just typed. Each row names the item in words with one secondary detail, and arrow keys move through the list while Enter inserts the chosen item at the caret and returns typing to the text. Escape closes the list without inserting anything and leaves the typed trigger text in place for editing, and deleting back past the trigger closes the list. Announce the list as a list of suggestions to assistive technology, with the count and the current item, and offer a pointer route alongside the keyboard route so the list never requires a keyboard alone. Where no item matches, say so plainly and offer no insertion rather than inserting the closest match unasked. Distinct from the search row, which finds content rather than inserting into text, and from the text-selection action menu row, which acts on selected text rather than inserting at the caret. Cross-check the search row |
| Collaborative editing presence | Show who else is present in a shared document and which part each person is editing, without letting two people overwrite the same field unknowingly | Presence list, field highlight, field hold indicator, editing-mode cue | Show who is present in the document with a presence list that names each person in text as well as in colour, and mark the field each person is editing with a visible hold so a second editor sees it before typing. A held field is not a hard block: where two edits would collide, warn before overwriting and offer the sync-conflict rules instead of silently keeping one version. Presence is a short-lived claim about an open session, never proof that a person saw, understood, or approved a change: keep read and approval signals separate from presence, and drop a presence mark promptly when the session ends or goes idle. Show typing or selection cues only for the field in view, so presence never distracts from the text being read. Keep presence, cursor motion, and document state separate: a cursor packet never implies a saved edit, and a saved edit never implies presence. After a disconnect, treat the roster as uncertain — dim it or label it reconnecting — and replace it from a fresh snapshot on reconnect rather than merging, so a departed person never lingers. Undo reverses only your own edits, never a collaborator's. Cross-check the sync-conflict resolution row and the annotation and comment thread row |
| Draft autosave and recovery | Keep unfinished work safe across interruption, and restore it with a clear review step | Saved indicator, draft restore prompt, discard control, draft list | Save drafts continuously with a visible saved indicator that states when the draft was last saved, so a person never wonders whether work is safe. After a crash, a closed tab, or an interrupted session, restore the latest draft with a clear review step: show what was restored and let the person keep it, edit it, or discard it — never restore silently over newer work. A restored draft is not a sent message or a saved record: keep draft and delivery states separate, and clear the draft once the work is submitted or explicitly discarded. Offer an explicit discard path, and state the draft's scope plainly — this device only, or across devices — and how long it is kept. Where several drafts exist, list them newest first, each with a title or opening lines, its destination or context, and its last-saved time, so the right draft can be found without opening each one. A draft shown as a preview inside a parent list is labelled as a draft, never presented as sent content, and opening any draft returns to editing rather than sending or publishing it. Each draft has its own discard control, and discarding one draft never removes the others; an empty draft list states plainly that no drafts are kept. Cross-check the wizard save-and-resume rules and the session-expiry warning row |
| Saved-filter view | Return to a filter combination in one action, without rebuilding it on each visit | Saved-view list, save-view control, rename and delete controls, stale-view marker | A named view stores a filter combination — filters, sort order, and column choices — for one-tap return, and saves the definition rather than a frozen result, so the view stays current as data changes. Offer rename and delete controls for every saved view, and state its scope plainly — private or shared — before anyone else can see it. When a saved view no longer matches the current data — because a filter value was removed or renamed — mark it visibly and offer a repair step rather than silently showing a different result. Keep the query text out of a saved view unless the person chose to include it, and keep a saved view distinct from a single saved item: a view re-runs its filters, a saved item returns one chosen thing. Cross-check the filtering and sorting row and the saved-item collection row |
| Toast feedback | Confirm a completed low-stakes action without blocking the person | Toast container, message text, dismiss control, duration timer | Keep the message to one short line naming what completed, and place every toast in one consistent spot so the person learns where to look. Show it only after an action completes, never for an error, a failure, or anything that needs a decision — those belong to a persistent alert or a confirmation step. Scale the visible duration to the length of the message so it can be read comfortably, always offer a manual dismiss control, and pause the timer while the toast has focus or the pointer rests on it. Announce the message politely to assistive technology without moving focus, show no more than one toast at a time with further messages queued, and never use a toast for promotion. Cross-check the notification escalation row and the confirmation row |
| List reorder | Let the person change the order of items in a list | Drag handle, drop preview, move-up and move-down controls, position announcement | Offer drag with a visible drop preview showing exactly where the item will land, and never rely on drag alone: provide move-up and move-down controls and a keyboard path that work for every item. For long lists, also offer a move-to-position control — a destination choice or a position number — so reaching a distant position never needs repeated single steps. On touch screens, offer a tap-to-select then tap-to-place route, so reordering never requires a press-hold-drag gesture. Announce the new position after each move, for example the item name with its new position out of the total, without moving focus into the announcement. Offer a cancel path that restores the original order, and apply the new order only when the person confirms or drops the item. Disable manual reordering while the list is sorted by another attribute, and keep the change visible in the reading order, not only on screen. Cross-check the data table row and the drag-between-panes rules in layouts/tablet.md |
| Date-range picker | Collect a start date and an end date as one span in a single control | Range field, calendar surface, preset list, apply and cancel controls | Treat the range as one linked decision, not two separate dates: the first pick sets the start, hovering previews the span as it forms, and the second pick completes it, with both ends visible in one surface. Offer preset spans for common cases — today, last seven days, this month — that set both ends in one action, and show two months side by side on wide screens so a range crossing a month boundary needs no extra navigation. Prevent an end before the start, or swap the ends with a plain statement of what changed, and state plainly whether both ends are included in the span. Show the chosen span in plain words before it applies, allow typed entry alongside the calendar where a known or distant date is faster to type, and keep the calendar open until the person applies or cancels. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row and the time-zone picker row |
| Accordion disclosure | Reveal secondary content in place under a heading, without leaving the page | Heading button, expanded-state marker, collapsible panel, expand-all control where offered | Use one button per section, wrapped in a real heading so the section stays findable by heading navigation, with the expanded or collapsed state stated in text as well as in shape and announced when it changes. Toggling keeps focus on the header, and the panel content stays in reading order below it, so a person never loses their place. Sections open independently unless the design states that only one may be open at a time, and that rule is stated before anyone opens a second section. Keep a single reveal a simple disclosure rather than an accordion, and use tabs instead where sections are peers and only one should ever show. Never hide the only route to a task inside a collapsed panel, and never require opening every section to complete a task. Where an expand-all control is offered, it expands every section and returns to a collapse-all state. Cross-check the scrolling tab bar row and the tooltip primitive |
| Carousel | Present a rotating set of slides or cards in one place | Slide container, previous and next controls, slide-picker controls, pause control, position indicator | Offer previous, next, and slide-picker controls that name each slide, and state the current position in plain words, such as slide 2 of 5, so the set stays navigable without relying on shape alone. Where slides rotate on their own, provide a visible pause control whose label states what pressing it will do next, stop rotation while focus is inside the set or the pointer rests on it, and never restart rotation after the person paused it deliberately. Start stopped where a reduced-motion preference is set, and never auto-rotate content that carries the only route to a task or a decision. Keep a keyboard route and a pointer route for every slide change, never drag or swipe alone, and keep slide content readable at its own pace once rotation stops. Use a carousel only where the slides are peers of equal weight; where one message matters most, show it directly instead of burying it in rotation. Cross-check the media viewer row and the image gallery viewer row |
| App lock | Keep a signed-in app closed to anyone holding an unlocked device | Lock setting, unlock check, inactivity timer, fallback route, content-hiding setting | Offer locking as an opt-in setting, never forced at first sign-in, with a stated inactivity period after which the lock engages and a lock-on-leaving option for locking at once. Unlock with the device's own check where available, with a sign-in fallback that never depends on that check alone, and keep the fallback visible rather than hidden behind repeated failures. After a set number of failed attempts, pause further attempts and state the wait plainly, without locking the account itself. Hide sensitive content in previews and notifications while locked, and state plainly what the lock does and does not protect: it bars casual opening, it does not replace sign-in or device encryption. A change to the lock setting requires the current unlock check first, and turning the lock off states that the app will open without a check. Distinct from the second-factor sign-in row, which confirms identity at sign-in, and from the session-expiry warning row, which warns before a session ends. Cross-check the second-factor sign-in row, the passkey sign-in row, and the session-expiry warning row |
| Low-bandwidth mode | Keep a service fully usable when data is limited or the connection is slow | Mode toggle, mode statement, media placeholders, return control | Offer the mode as a person-controlled choice, and honour it wherever the system already signals a data-saving preference, so the choice never needs repeating. State plainly what the mode changes — lighter media, deferred non-essential content, fewer background requests — and keep text, navigation, forms, and actions fully usable in it, with a one-action return to the full view. Load visible content first, defer the rest until it is scrolled to or requested, and replace heavy media with a labelled placeholder and a load-on-request control rather than an empty gap. Never hide a task, a price, or an error behind the mode, and never let the mode silently change what a person entered or submitted. Cross-check the offline-first sync row and the simplified view row |
| What's-new announcement | Report changes after they land, graded by impact, without interrupting a task | Announcement list, impact-graded surface, persistent entry point, seen state, direct route to the capability | Grade visibility by impact: minor changes stay in the changelog alone; workflow changes get a dismissible in-place announcement near the changed surface; changes that block a task get a prominent notice shown once, never on every visit. Lead with what the person can do now, not the change name, and give one direct route to the new capability from the announcement itself. Keep a persistent entry point — a changelog or updates list reachable without the announcement — so a dismissed item can be reviewed later, and record a seen state so a dismissed announcement is never shown again. Never use an announcement for promotion, never stack several announcements at once, and never show one before the person has seen any value on first use. Match the surface to the impact: a badge with an unread count on the updates entry for ongoing news the person can check when they choose; a slim top bar with one action for a change that affects most people, dismissed after it has been seen once; a corner card for a single feature worth discovering, never blocking the primary surface; a modal only for a change that blocks a task or changes terms, shown once and only at a natural pause, never in the middle of a task. Show each announcement only to the people it affects, and show it at most once per person. Distinct from onboarding, which teaches first use; from the notification digest, which groups unrelated updates; and from the in-app update prompt, which requires a newer version rather than reporting a change. Cross-check the in-app update prompt row and the notification digest row |
| Version history and restore | Return content to an earlier saved state without losing newer work | Version list, change preview, compare view, restore control, named-version control | Keep a dated list of saved versions, newest first, each with its time and what changed, and fold rapid saves from one session into one entry so the list stays readable, while milestone saves — publishing and restoring — always get their own entry. Let the person preview a version and compare it with the current one, with additions and removals marked in text as well as in shape, before any restore. Restoring saves the current version first, then makes the restored version the new current one, so restore is never a one-way door; state plainly what restore brings back and what it does not — content, and settings or linked items only where stated. Where only part of an old version is needed, offer copying that part instead of restoring the whole version. State the retention rule plainly — how many versions are kept and for how long — and keep the earliest version reachable where a return to the start matters. Cross-check the draft autosave and recovery row and the sync-conflict resolution row |
| External-link departure | Leave the current service for a stated destination, with a return route to the task left behind | Link marker, destination statement, departure notice, continue and stay controls, return route | Mark every link that leaves the service in text or shape as well as in colour, and state the destination in plain words where the link text alone does not name it. Where leaving would interrupt unfinished work, show a departure notice before leaving that states the destination, states that it opens elsewhere, and states what happens to the unfinished work — saved, kept in place, or lost — and offer both a continue control and a stay control, never a continue control alone. Keep a return route after departure: the back action, or a saved place the person can return to, so leaving is never a one-way door. Never use a departure notice for links that stay inside the service, and never open an outside destination without warning where the person is mid-task. Distinct from the share link row, which sends the person's own item elsewhere, and from the in-product help row, which opens help in place. Cross-check the share link row and the in-product help row |
| Described media | Offer narration of visual information that dialogue alone does not convey | Description track choice, described-version link, no-description statement, playback controls | Offer a description track as a named choice beside captions, never buried in a settings menu alone, and keep the description separate from the dialogue so neither drowns the other. Describe the actions, scene changes, and on-screen text a person needs to follow the content, fitted into natural pauses in the dialogue; where the pauses are too short, offer an extended version that pauses playback for the narration. State plainly where no description exists, with a text alternative offered instead, rather than leaving the choice absent without explanation. Keep the description choice available throughout playback, not only at the start, and remember the choice across sessions where the person returns to the same content. Where the dialogue already conveys every meaningful visual, no separate track is needed — but say so, so the absence reads as a decision rather than an omission. Distinct from the media controls primitive, which operates playback, and from the captions rule, which transcribes speech. Cross-check the media controls primitive and the captions rule |
| Saved-cart recovery | Return to a saved cart with every change stated plainly | Saved cart list, availability and price-change notices, removal control, recalculated order summary | On return to a saved cart, state what is still available, what changed in price or availability, and what expired, before any checkout step — never let the person discover a change at payment. Keep the rest of the order intact: offer a one-action removal for each unavailable or expired item, and never silently drop an item or silently swap it for a different one; where an alternative exists, offer it as an explicit choice beside the removed item. Recalculate totals after every change and show the new total before checkout, with each price change marked beside the item it affects. State plainly how long a saved cart is kept, and warn before that period ends where the cart still holds items. Distinct from the saved-item collection row, which saves single items, and from the payment failure recovery row, which preserves an order after a failed payment. Cross-check the saved-item collection row, the payment failure recovery row, and the commerce layout |
| Picture-in-picture | Keep a video playing in a small floating window while the person does something else | Floating window, play/pause control, restore control, close control, move and resize gestures | Move the video into the floating window when the person leaves it for another task, and return it to full size when they choose restore; never start the small window unprompted while the person is still watching, and never leave playback with no visible control. A tap on the window reveals a small set of named controls — play/pause, restore to full size, and close — and a double-tap toggles between the small and large window sizes. Let the person drag the window to a corner, stash it at an edge, and resize it with a pinch, without covering the action controls of the task underneath. Closing or swiping the window away stops playback; it never keeps playing with no way to stop it. Keep playback state — position, captions, and speed — identical when moving between the full player and the floating window, and offer the window only where the content stays followable at a small size. Distinct from the media controls primitive, which operates playback, and from the split-window rules, which divide the screen between tasks. Cross-check the media controls primitive and the split-window rules |
| Colour picker | Choose a colour value that stays readable and identifiable without relying on hue alone | Named swatch grid, typed value field, contrast statement, preview on the target surface | Name every swatch in words as well as showing it, and always state the chosen value in text (name plus code) wherever the value matters, never by colour alone. Offer a typed value entry beside the visual control, kept in sync both ways, and make the picker fully keyboard-operable: arrow keys move across swatches, typed values validate on entry, and an invalid value keeps the previous choice and names the problem in text. Check contrast against the surface the colour will sit on and state the result plainly; where the colour carries text, warn or block a choice that falls below the readable threshold and offer the nearest passing alternative. Keep recent and custom choices reachable in one step, and never let a saved choice silently change meaning when the palette changes. Cross-check the filtering and sorting row and the theme selection row |
| Guest-to-account conversion | Turn a completed guest order into an account without re-asking for anything already given | Single-step creation control, benefit statement, order-link confirmation, existing-account route | Offer the step only after the order is confirmed, as one optional action that carries the order and its details into the new account; never re-ask for contact or delivery details just entered, and never make the step a condition of completing the purchase. State plainly what the account adds — order history, saved details, faster next checkout — and what it does not change about the order just placed. Where the address already belongs to an existing account, offer a sign-in and link route instead of creating a duplicate. Confirm in place that the order is now linked, and keep the guest order-lookup route working for anyone who declines. Cross-check the guest checkout row and the account deletion row |
| Sensitive-content preview | Let the person choose whether to view content that may distress or surprise | Preview card, content-type statement, reveal control, hide-again control, per-type preference | Show a plain preview first that states what kind of content follows and why it is flagged, in words, without showing the content itself. Reveal only on an explicit action — never on scroll, hover, or focus — and let the person hide the content again afterwards. Remember the choice per content type, not as one blanket switch, and keep it changeable in settings. A visual blur alone is not protection for material that must stay private: keep such material out of the delivered page until it is revealed. Keep any caption, transcript, or description available from the preview so the person can judge without revealing the content. Cross-check the consent choice row and the notification grouping rules |
| Order tracking | Follow an order from placement to delivery without chasing updates | Staged progress view, delivery window statement, delay notice, support route, per-shipment stages, per-shipment item list, live position view, recalculated arrival estimate, proof-of-delivery record | Show the order as named stages in plain words — placed, confirmed, prepared, shipped, out for delivery, delivered — with the current stage marked and earlier stages kept visible, never a bare tracking code alone. Where a live position is shown, keep the stage, the delivery window, and the arrival estimate in words as the primary view, with the map as a supplement rather than the only source of progress, so the order stays followable without the map. State when the position was last updated, and where the position is stale, say so plainly and fall back to the last confirmed stage and window rather than leaving a frozen position standing as current. Recalculate the arrival estimate as the position changes, and where the estimate moves, state the revised estimate beside the stage rather than silently replacing it. On delivery, attach the proof to the delivered stage — a photo, a signature, or a confirmation code — with a route at once where the proof does not match the order received. State the delivery window beside the current stage, and where the window moves, state the revised window and the reason in the same place, before the person notices the slip. Send an update at each stage that changes what the person should expect — shipped, out for delivery, delayed, delivered, failed — and keep every update on the tracking view itself so the person never has to hunt across messages to understand the order. Where an order splits into several shipments, state the split as soon as it is known — at confirmation or at the first dispatch — rather than leaving the person to discover it when the first parcel arrives, and never show a single order-level progress bar for a split order, since one bar cannot represent several parcels at different stages. Label each shipment separately with its own stages and window, list the items in each shipment beside that shipment, and keep the overall order state as a summary of its shipments — for example, partly delivered — rather than a stage of its own. Attach a support route to delays, failed deliveries, and missing items, with the order details already carried into the request so nothing is re-entered. Keep the tracking view readable on a small screen, with the current stage, window, and next action at the top. Distinct from the saved-cart recovery row, which revisits an unfinished cart, and from the notification escalation row, which routes urgent updates. Cross-check the guest checkout row, the saved-cart recovery row, and the commerce layout |
| Order cancellation | Cancel an order that has not yet been fulfilled, without contacting support | Cancel action on the order, cancellable-state statement, confirmation control, refund statement, cancelled-order record | Offer cancellation from the order itself while the order can still be stopped — placed or confirmed, before preparation or shipping — and state plainly on the order whether cancellation is still possible and until when, so nobody starts a request that cannot succeed. Where part of the order has already shipped, cancel only the unfulfilled part and say plainly which items stay on their way. Confirm with a plain statement of what is cancelled, what refund follows and when it lands, and what happens to any discount or stored credit used on the order, and never present cancellation as instant where a short processing step still stands between request and refund. Once cancelled, keep the order visible as cancelled with its items, total, and refund state, rather than deleting it, and carry the cancellation into the tracking view so the order never looks merely delayed. Where cancellation is no longer possible, say so at once with the reason and offer the return route instead, rather than a dead end. Distinct from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a recurring plan, and from the order tracking row, which follows an order forward. Cross-check the order tracking row and the confirmation row |
| Order editing after placement | Change items, quantities, or delivery details on a placed order before fulfilment starts | Edit action on the order, edit-window statement, editable-field list, changed-order summary, price-difference statement, confirmation control, updated-order record | Offer editing from the order itself while the order can still be changed, and state plainly on the order what can still be changed and until when, so nobody starts an edit that cannot succeed. Limit what can change by stage: items and quantities only before preparation, delivery address and window while the order is still unshipped, and nothing once shipping has started except through the return route. Show the changed order as a full summary before confirmation, with the price difference stated as an extra charge or a refund and the route for that difference, and never apply a change silently. Where an item in the edited order is no longer available, state that at once and offer a replacement or removal, rather than dropping it quietly. Once confirmed, keep the original order visible with the change recorded against it, and carry the change into the tracking view so the order never looks unchanged. Distinct from the order cancellation row, which ends an order, and from the return and exchange request row, which acts after delivery. Cross-check the order cancellation row and the order tracking row |
| Reorder past order | Repeat a previous order without rebuilding it item by item | Reorder control on the order, per-item reorder control, cart review step, unavailable-item statement | Offer a reorder control on each past order and on its detail view, and a per-item control for repeating one item alone, so a whole order and a single item are both repeatable without re-entering choices. Before payment, show the repeated items as a cart to review, with current prices and any changed options stated, rather than charging the old total silently. Where an item is no longer available, state which item and why, keep the rest of the order repeatable, and offer the nearest available alternative where one exists rather than blocking the whole reorder. Keep items already in the cart when reorder items are added, and state plainly what was added and what was left out. Where a past order used a discount or stored value, state whether it still applies rather than assuming it does. Distinct from the saved-cart recovery row, which revisits an unfinished cart, and from the order tracking row, which follows an order forward. Cross-check the order tracking row and the saved-cart recovery row |
| Time picker | Choose a single time of day without ambiguity about format or zone | Typed time field, visual picker, preset list, format indicator, zone statement | Offer typed entry beside the visual picker, kept in sync both ways, so a known time is typed rather than stepped to; accept the common typed forms for the locale, validate on entry, and where a typed value is invalid keep the previous choice and name the problem in text rather than silently converting it. Offer preset times for common choices, and state the format plainly: a 12-hour display follows the person's locale setting with the period visible and checkable, and a 24-hour display never hides an AM/PM choice. Where the time matters across zones, state the zone inline beside the time, and show the equivalent in the other relevant zone where people span zones, rather than leaving the translation to mental arithmetic. Never silently default a missing time to midnight, since that can shift the effective day across a zone boundary; where no time is set, say so. Confirm the chosen time in plain words before it applies where the time books or schedules something. Distinct from the date-range picker row, which collects a span of dates, and from the time-zone picker row, which chooses a zone by place. Cross-check the date-range picker row, the time-zone picker row, and the calendar and scheduling row |
| Verification-code entry | Enter a short code sent to a separate channel, without getting trapped by the entry control | Single code field, paste and autofill support, expiry statement, resend control, alternate route | Prefer a single field that accepts a pasted code and platform autofill, with a numeric keyboard on small screens; where per-digit boxes are used, a pasted or autofilled code must spread across every box, and backspace on an empty box must step back rather than trap focus. State the expiry plainly — how long the code stays valid — and offer a resend route with the wait before resending stated, enforced the same way for every request. Submit only on an explicit action or, where auto-submit on completion is used, state that checking is in progress; never leave the person guessing whether the code was sent. Where a code fails, name the problem in text — wrong code, expired code, or too many attempts — without locking the person out on the first failure, and always offer an alternate route, such as another channel or a different sign-in method. Never ask for the same code twice, and never display the code anywhere it does not belong. Distinct from the second-factor sign-in row, which chooses the factor, and from the passkey sign-in row, which avoids codes entirely. Cross-check the second-factor sign-in row, the passkey sign-in row, and the authentication layout |
| Human verification challenge | Prove a person is human without locking anyone out | Background check, visible challenge, alternative route, audio alternative, retry control | Prefer a check that runs in the background, so most people never see a challenge at all, and keep it silent about how it decides. Where a visible challenge is needed, state its purpose in plain words before it appears, and never make a puzzle the only route: always offer an alternative path, such as a one-time code or a link sent to an email address, that reaches the same outcome. Prefer object recognition — picking the pictures that match a named thing — over distorted text, arithmetic, or memory tasks, which exclude people who cannot see, hear, or recall the challenge; where a visual challenge is kept, offer an audio alternative beside it. Never impose a tight time limit on completing the challenge, and keep the challenge fully keyboard-operable with its state announced to assistive technology. A failed attempt names the problem in plain words and offers a fresh challenge at once, without clearing anything the person already entered, and never locks the account on a single failure. Distinct from the verification-code entry row, which checks a code already sent, and from the passkey sign-in row, which uses a device-held credential instead of a challenge. Cross-check the verification-code entry row, the passkey sign-in row, and the authentication layout |
| Background-task progress | Follow a long-running task that continues away from its starting view | Task list or activity view, progress indicator, pause and cancel controls, completion and failure notices, return route | Name the task state in plain words — queued, running, waiting for input, completed, completed with errors, or failed — so a stalled task never reads as a frozen screen. Put one plain detail line beside the indicator: what is happening, how much is done where that is known, whether the person may leave, and what happens next. Let the task continue away from its starting view and stay findable in a task or activity list, with its last update time shown. Offer pause and cancel only where safe, and state plainly what cancel keeps and what it discards, confirming where cancelling loses work. Report completion or failure in place, with a return route to the result and a retry that never re-asks for input already given. Where the share of work done is unknown, use an indeterminate indicator and never invent a percentage; where progress has not updated for a while, say the task is still running rather than leaving a frozen number on screen. Distinct from the wizard row, which steps through a task in view, and from the toast feedback row, which confirms a task already finished. Cross-check the file upload row and the toast feedback row |
| Live broadcast chat overlay | Let viewers follow a live conversation laid over a moving picture without losing the picture or the message | Overlay message list, readability treatment, message limit, highlight control, hide control | Keep the overlay to a small number of recent messages with a short display time, so the picture stays visible and each message can be read before it leaves. Use high-contrast text with a shadow, outline, or semi-opaque backing, tested on both light and dark scenes, and keep sender names distinguishable in text or shape as well as colour, with a font large enough for a small player. Keep the overlay clear of captions, labels, and other on-screen controls, and give a one-action hide control for moments of abuse or overload. Showing a message on screen republishes it: never show private messages, moderator notes, or messages held for review, and remember that a deleted message may still remain visible in a recording, so filter blocked terms and commands before display. A highlighted message shows one message at a time, larger, for a few seconds, then clears on its own or on an explicit dismiss action. Distinct from the Chat and messaging row, which covers a conversation in its own view rather than a layer over a live picture. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row and the Pinned message row |

| Kanban board | Move work items through visible stages without losing track of what is in progress | Stage columns, item cards, column limit indicator, lane grouping, move control, card detail view | Let each column stand for one real stage of the work, in the order work actually moves, and never invent a stage the team does not use. Show each column with its item count, and where a limit on work in progress is set, show the count against the limit and warn plainly when the limit is passed, rather than silently refusing a move. Move a card by dragging it and also by a keyboard or menu move control that names the destination column, so moving never depends on dragging alone; confirm the move in place and offer an undo for an accidental move. Group cards into horizontal lanes only where a second grouping genuinely helps — for example blocked work or urgent work kept apart from the ordinary flow — and keep lane labels in words, never by shading alone. Keep every card scannable at a glance: a title, the current stage, and any owner or due date in text, with fuller detail one step away in the card view. Collapse a column to its header on narrow screens while keeping its count visible, and announce moves and limit warnings to assistive technology so the board state stays clear without sight. Distinct from the list reorder row, which reorders items inside one list, whereas the board moves items between stages. Cross-check the list reorder row |
| Automated-to-human support handoff | Hand a conversation from automated help to a person without making the person repeat anything | Handoff triggers, transfer summary, queue position statement, waiting route, context carry-over | Offer a visible route to a person at all times, not only after automated help has failed. Fire the handoff on an explicit request for a person, on repeated unhelpful answers, and wherever the next step needs authority that automated help does not hold — refunds, exceptions, account changes, or sensitive cases. Before announcing the transfer, save the case: the stated issue, the steps already tried, their outcomes, and any details already given, so the receiving person starts from that summary and never re-asks for it. State what happens next in plain words: that a person is being brought in, the queue or waiting position where known, and what the person can do while waiting. Where no person is available, say so plainly instead of pretending a transfer happened, and give a stated next step — leave a message, request a callback, or return later — with the wait stated. Keep the conversation in the same channel where possible; where a channel change is unavoidable, explain it and carry the context across it. Review handoffs regularly: repeated handoffs on one topic point at missing help content, not at people asking wrongly. Distinct from the in-product help row, which opens help in place, and from the agent checkpoint row, which approves an automated task. Cross-check the in-product help row and the confirmation row |
| Subscription plan change | Change a subscription tier with price, entitlements, and effective date stated before confirmation | Before-and-after comparison, effective-date statement, prorated charge statement, change-back route | Show the change as a before-and-after comparison before confirmation: price, entitlements, billing period, and the date the change takes effect, each stated in plain words. Let an upgrade take effect at once with the prorated charge stated on the same screen, so the person never discovers the charge on the next invoice. Let a downgrade take effect at the next renewal with the higher tier kept until then, and state that plainly — a downgrade is not a refund for time already paid. Where a downgrade removes an entitlement currently in use, name what is lost and when, and offer a route to keep or export it before confirming. Keep a stated route back: a pending downgrade stays cancellable until it takes effect, and the change is confirmed in writing with the new price, the new entitlements, and the next billing date. Where a billing-period change also moves the renewal date, state the new date rather than leaving the old one in place. Distinct from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a subscription, and from the paywall row, which first offers a paid tier. Cross-check the subscription cancellation row and the confirmation row |
| Subscription billing-date change | Move a recurring charge to a different day of the cycle with the effect stated before confirmation | Date choice, current-period effect statement, next-charge statement, prorated amount statement, pending-change control | Offer the change from the billing or account area, in the same channel used to sign up. Before confirming, state in plain words whether the change moves only the next charge or shifts the whole cycle from the new date, the new next-charge date, and any prorated credit or extra charge for the lengthened or shortened current period, so the next invoice never surprises. Where a change is not available, for example during a trial, in the final cycle, or close to the next charge, say so before the person starts, with the reason and the nearest alternative. Keep a pending change cancellable until it takes effect, and confirm it in writing with the new next-charge date and any prorated amount. Where the plan is shown, label it with the new next-charge date rather than leaving the old one in place. Distinct from the subscription plan change row, which changes what is paid for, and from the subscription pause row, which suspends billing for a set period. Cross-check the subscription plan change row and the subscription pause row |
| Promo-code entry | Apply a discount code at checkout with the result shown as a line item beside the recalculated total | Collapsed code entry, applied-code line item, failure reason statement, removal control | Keep the entry collapsed so checkout stays uncluttered, expanding to a single field with an apply action; trim pasted whitespace and accept the code exactly as shown, without forcing a case the person cannot see. On success, show the discount at once as a line item beside the recalculated total, so the saving is visible before payment rather than promised after it. On failure, name the reason in plain words — expired, already used, below the qualifying spend, not matching the items, or unable to combine with an offer already applied — never a bare rejection that leaves the person guessing. Where only one code may apply, state that before entry, and name the code already applied when a second code is tried. Offer a one-action removal that restores the total at once, and keep the code applied when the person moves between checkout steps, re-checking eligibility only if the items change. Distinct from the guest checkout row, which collects payment details, and from the payment failure recovery row, which preserves an order after a failed payment. Cross-check the guest checkout row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Gift card and stored-credit redemption | Spend stored value at checkout, as a payment line rather than a discount | Code entry, applied-value line item, remaining-balance statement, balance-check route, expiry statement, split-payment statement | Keep the code entry separate from any discount-code entry, so stored value is never mistaken for a discount, and trim pasted whitespace without forcing a case the person cannot see. On success, show the applied amount at once as a payment line beside the recalculated total, and state the remaining balance where any value is left, so the person knows what stays for a later order. Where the stored value covers only part of the total, apply it first and charge the remainder to another payment method in the same checkout, stating both amounts before payment, rather than declining the order or forcing the person to cover the whole total another way. Offer a balance check outside checkout, from the account area, that states the current balance and the expiry rule before any code is applied — whether the value expires on a stated date or never expires once held as account credit — and state that rule again at the point of entry. On failure, name the reason in plain words — expired, already spent, not yet active, or unable to combine with the items — never a bare rejection. Offer a one-action removal that restores the total at once, and keep the applied value when the person moves between checkout steps, re-checking only if the items change. Distinct from the promo-code entry row, which applies a discount rather than spending stored value, and from the gift option row, which marks an order as a gift rather than paying for it. Cross-check the promo-code entry row and the order tracking row |
| Gift card purchase | Buy stored value as a gift, through its own purchase path rather than an ordinary product order | Gift card landing page, format and design choice, value choice, delivery method and date choice, recipient fields, gift message field, balance-check route, purchase confirmation | Give gift cards their own purchase path, separate from the ordinary product flow: the steps differ — choose a format and design, choose a value, choose how and when it is delivered, give the recipient's details and an optional message, then pay — so never force the card through a product page and cart built for physical goods. Offer preset values with a custom amount choice, and state any minimum or maximum before the value is chosen. Offer the delivery choices plainly: email to the recipient, email to the buyer to forward or print, or a physical card by post, with the delivery date stated before payment where sending can be scheduled, and a resend route where the recipient cannot find it. Take the recipient's name and address separately from the buyer's billing details, so billing stays with the buyer and the card reaches the recipient; keep an optional message with its length limit stated before typing. Keep a balance check reachable from the gift card page and the account area, so a held card's value and expiry are known before it is spent. Confirm with the value, the recipient, the delivery method and date, and the route to check the balance. Never let a card's value buy another card. Distinct from the gift card and stored-credit redemption row, which spends stored value at checkout, and from the subscription gifting row, which gives access to a subscription for a set period. Cross-check the gift card and stored-credit redemption row and the subscription gifting row |
| Master-detail | Pair a list with the detail of its selected item | Item list, selection indicator, detail pane, empty detail placeholder, back control | On wide screens, show the list beside its detail, with the selected item stated in words as well as in shape, so the pairing stays clear without colour alone. Changing the selection updates the detail without losing the list position or scroll, and a detail that is still loading keeps the list usable rather than blocking it. On narrow screens, stack the route as list then detail, with a clear back step that returns to the same list position, never to the top of the list. Before anything is selected, show a stated empty detail placeholder naming what to pick, never a blank pane and never the detail of a previously selected item. Where the detail is editable, state whether edits save at once or need a confirm action, and keep unsaved edits visible when the selection changes rather than discarding them silently. Distinct from the detail view row, which inspects one item without a sibling list, and from the data table row, which compares rows rather than pairing a list with its detail. Cross-check the detail view row and the tablet layout |
| Threaded message reply | Answer one message inside a busy conversation without breaking the main flow | Reply control, thread view, original-message anchor, reply count, unread count, return control | Offer the reply control on the message itself, by long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message, so the reply stays anchored to the message it answers. Keep the original message visible at the top of the thread view, with replies in time order beneath it, so a reply never reads without its context. Show a reply count on the original message, and an unread count per thread stated in text, so new replies are found without opening every thread. A reply posted in a thread stays in that thread unless the person explicitly chooses to also post it to the main conversation, so side discussions never flood the main flow. Offer a clear return route from the thread to the main conversation at the same position, and keep nesting shallow: a reply to a reply stays in the same thread rather than opening a deeper thread. Announce new thread replies as a status message without moving focus, and keep the whole thread keyboard-operable. Distinct from the annotation and comment thread row, which anchors discussion to document content, and from the message edit and delete row, which changes a message rather than answering it. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Quote reply | Answer one message in place, inside the main conversation, without opening a separate thread | Reply control, quoted preview, jump control, compose input | Offer the reply control on the message itself, by long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message, so the reply stays attached to the message it answers. Show a short preview of the quoted message directly above the new message, naming its sender and carrying its text, so the reply reads with its context without leaving the stream. Tapping or activating the preview jumps to the original message at its position in the conversation, and returns the person to the reply afterwards. Keep the quoted text as it stood when the reply was sent: an edit to the original never rewrites the quote, and where the original was deleted, the preview stays in place marked as deleted in words with the jump disabled, so the conversation still reads coherently. Keep the preview short, with longer originals trimmed and the full text reachable through the jump, and keep the whole reply keyboard-operable. Distinct from the threaded message reply row, which opens a separate thread view for side discussion, and from the message edit and delete row, which changes a message rather than answering it. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Message forwarding | Send a message received in one conversation on to another conversation or person | Forward control on the message, preview of the message to send, recipient choice, edit route, send confirmation | Offer forwarding from the message itself, by long press on touch, a hover action on pointer, or a keyboard action on the message, never from a separate settings screen alone. Before sending, show a preview of exactly what will be sent — the text, any media, and any link — naming the original sender, so the sender checks the payload and the recipient sees where it came from. Let the recipient choice state plainly where the message will land: an existing conversation, a new message to one person, or a group, and state whether replies from the recipient return to the sender or stay in the receiving conversation, so a forward never silently opens a new thread the sender did not choose. Offer an edit route before sending, so a note can be added or the text trimmed without retyping the whole message, and confirm the send in place with the receiving conversation named. Forwarded media and links travel with the message where the receiving conversation supports them; where a format cannot travel, state what was left out before sending rather than dropping it silently, and where a link opens elsewhere, state its destination. A forwarded message carries only the chosen message, never the surrounding conversation history, and never reveals the original conversation to the recipient beyond the forwarded message itself. Keep the whole forward flow keyboard-operable, and label each control in words. Distinct from the quote reply row, which answers in place, and from the threaded message reply row, which answers inside a thread. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Conversation catch-up summary | Catch up on unread messages in a conversation without reading every message | Catch-up summary entry, covered-range statement, key-points list, jump-to-first-unread control, dismiss control | Offer the summary only where there are unread messages worth condensing — for a handful of messages, show the messages themselves instead of a summary. State plainly which messages the summary covers: the count and the point from which they are unread, so nothing looks read that was only summarised. Keep the summary distinct from ordinary messages, placed where the unread messages begin, and never let it replace the messages: every point in the summary links or jumps to the message it came from, and a jump-to-first-unread control returns the person to the full stream at that point. Surface anything that needs a decision or a reply separately from the general points, so an action is never buried inside a condensed list, and mark the summary as read separately from the messages themselves — reading the summary never marks the underlying messages read. Offer a dismiss control that removes the summary without marking anything read, and never show a summary for an empty stretch or for messages the person has already read. Distinct from the notification digest row, which groups unrelated updates on a schedule, and from the threaded message reply row, which answers inside a thread. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row and the notification digest row |
| Conversation mute | Silence notifications from one conversation without leaving it | Mute control, mute-length choice, muted mark, unmute control, muted list | Offer mute from the conversation list and from inside the conversation, never from a separate settings screen alone. Let the person choose a stated length — one hour, eight hours, one week, or until unmuted — and state plainly when alerts return, so a mute never looks permanent when it is timed and never looks timed when it is open-ended. Show a plain muted mark beside the conversation in the list, in words or shape as well as colour, so the silenced state is clear without opening it, and keep the muted conversation in place rather than hiding it, so it is found again without a search. While muted, messages still arrive and stay readable in place, with no sound, vibration, or banner, and reading them never unmutes the conversation by surprise. State plainly whether a direct mention still alerts while muted, and never let a mention silently override a mute the person chose. Unmute is one action from the conversation itself and from the list, takes effect at once, and restores the previous alert behaviour without re-asking for notification permission; a timed mute ends by itself with a short note that notifications are back. Keep a muted list where every muted conversation can be reviewed and unmuted, so a silenced conversation is never lost behind its own silence. Where the account is signed in on more than one device, carry the mute across them, so silencing on one device does not leave the other one sounding. Muting one conversation never silences another. Distinct from the notification preferences row, which sets alerts for all conversations, from the focus mode schedule row, which silences delivery across the whole device, and from the archived conversation row, which removes a conversation from the list. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row, the notification preferences row, the focus mode schedule row, and the archived conversation row |
| Pinned conversation | Keep a few chosen conversations fixed at the top of the conversation list | Pinned section, pin and unpin controls, pinned mark, pin limit statement, pinned order control | Offer pinning from the conversation list row itself, by long press on touch, a menu action, or a keyboard action on the row, never from a separate settings screen alone. Pinned conversations sit in a separate pinned section above the chronological list, in pinned order, each with a plain pinned mark in words or shape as well as colour, so the pinned state is clear without opening the conversation. State the pin limit up front — a small handful only — and when the limit is reached say so plainly and offer unpinning one conversation before pinning another, rather than silently refusing or silently replacing a pin. Pinning changes position only, never alert behaviour: a pinned conversation keeps its existing mute and notification rules, and a new message in a pinned conversation never promotes it above other pinned conversations. Unpinning is one action from the list row or from inside the conversation, and returns the conversation at once to its place by latest message. Keep the pinned section in the same order on every device, and never let it hide new or unread conversations below it: state how many conversations are pinned and offer a way to view the full list. Distinct from the pinned message row, which keeps one message in view inside a single conversation, and from the conversation mute row, which silences alerts. Cross-check the chat and messaging row and the pinned message row |
| Group conversation participant management | Add, remove, and change the role of people in a group conversation, with every change visible to everyone in it | Member list, role labels, add control, invite, remove control, role change control, ownership transfer control, membership-change message | Keep the member list in the conversation details, with each person named and their role stated in words beside the name, so a role is never carried by colour or symbol alone. Show management controls only to those allowed to use them, and never offer a control that leads to a refusal after it is chosen. Adding people invites them by name or from the contact list, shows who is already in the group so nobody is invited twice, and states before sending whether a new joiner sees past messages or only messages from joining onward, so the history rule is agreed before the invite lands. Removing a person asks for confirmation first, states plainly what the person loses — the conversation and its future messages — and leaves the conversation readable to them where they could already read it, rather than erasing what they saw. Announce every membership change inside the conversation itself, naming who joined, left, was added, or was removed and by whom, so the member list never changes silently. Changing a role states the new role and what it allows before it lands, and taking a role away asks for confirmation; keep at least one person able to manage the group, and offer ownership transfer where the last manager leaves, rather than leaving the group unmanaged. Leaving is one action with a confirmation, and states plainly that messages stop arriving while the conversation stays readable. Keep the member list searchable where the group is large, paged rather than cut off, and keyboard-operable, with every action labelled in words. Distinct from the conversation mute row, which silences notifications without changing membership, and from the pinned conversation row, which pins conversations to the top of the list without changing who is in them. Cross-check the Chat and messaging row |
| Gift option | Mark an order as a gift at checkout so the recipient sees a message, not prices | Gift toggle, gift message field, recipient address fields, price-hiding statement, per-item gift choice | Offer the gift choice in the cart or at checkout before payment, never only after the order is placed, and state plainly what marking an order as a gift changes. Where only some items are gifts, let the gift choice apply per item, so a mixed order never marks everything as a gift by accident. Hide prices from the recipient on the packing slip and any receipt that travels with the parcel, while the buyer still sees the full totals and charges before payment. Offer an optional gift message shown to the recipient, with its length limit stated before typing and a preview where the message will be printed. Take a separate delivery address for the gift that never overwrites the buyer's own address, and keep billing details with the buyer. Where gift wrapping is offered, state its cost before payment and what it covers, and state plainly where wrapping is unavailable for an item. After the order, state the gift status in the confirmation without exposing the buyer's payment details. Distinct from the guest checkout row, which completes a purchase without an account, and from the saved-cart recovery row, which revisits an unfinished cart. Cross-check the guest checkout row and the order tracking row |
| Gift receipt | Give the recipient of a gift a receipt that proves the purchase for exchange, without showing what was paid | Price-free receipt, item list, purchase date, exchange and return route, order reference | Omit every price, discount, total, and payment detail from the gift receipt, while keeping item descriptions, quantities, and the purchase date, so the recipient can identify the item without learning what was spent. State the exchange and return window and route on the receipt itself, and keep a reference that links the receipt to the purchase for exchange. Offer the gift receipt at checkout and again from the order afterwards, and never send it to the buyer in place of the full receipt. Distinct from the gift option row, which marks an order as a gift with a message, and from the order receipt row, which keeps the buyer's full priced record. Cross-check the gift option row and the order receipt row |
| Saved address selection | Choose one saved address at checkout without retyping it | Saved address list, default marker, change action, add-new action, chosen-address statement | Show saved addresses as a named list with the default marked in words as well as in shape, so the default is clear without relying on position alone. Preselect the default at checkout and let the person choose another saved address in one action, with a search or short list where many addresses are saved, and always offer adding a new address without discarding the saved list. Editing one saved address must never overwrite another saved address, and a change made for one order must not silently become the new default unless the person confirms it. Before payment, state plainly which address receives the order, in full, so the choice is confirmed rather than assumed. Where no default exists, say that no address is selected and require an explicit choice or a new address before proceeding. Distinct from the location selection row, which picks a place on a map, and from the gift option row, which takes a separate recipient address for one order. Cross-check the guest checkout row and the gift option row |
| Address entry | Collect a postal address a person can check before it is used | Address fields, suggestion list, manual entry route, chosen-address statement | Ask only for the fields the delivery or record actually needs, in the order the person expects for their locale, and label each field for what it holds in that locale — postal-code wording follows the locale, and a postal code is optional where not every address has one. Use a single name field rather than forcing a two-part name, and hide a second address line behind a reveal control instead of presenting a confusing blank field, since not every address has one. Offer suggestions as the person types, and let a suggestion fill the fields while keeping every filled field editable, with a manual entry route that never depends on a suggestion being chosen; a suggestion that cannot be verified is still entered as typed rather than rejected, and checked again at the point of use. Before the address is used, state it in full in one place so the person can check it, and keep editing one field from changing any other field. Where a check after entry finds a close standardized match, show the entered address beside the suggested address with the changes highlighted, and let the person accept the suggestion or keep the address as entered; accepting the suggestion fills the fields without discarding edits made elsewhere in the form. Where no close match is found, warn plainly that the address could not be verified and still let the person continue with the address as entered, rather than blocking the task. Distinct from the saved address selection row, which chooses an address already stored, and from the location selection row, which picks a place on a map. Cross-check the form flow row and the saved address selection row |
| Delivery slot picker | Choose a delivery window at checkout that the fulfilment side can actually keep | Date picker, time-slot list, availability markers, cut-off statement, chosen-window statement | Offer the date first and then only the time windows still open for that date, so a window is never chosen for a day that cannot serve it. Mark each unavailable window as unavailable with its reason in words — full, past the cut-off, or outside the service day — rather than removing it silently, so the person understands why the choice narrowed. State the cut-off time for each window plainly, in the person's own zone, and state how far ahead booking stays open. Re-check availability at confirmation, and where a chosen window fills before payment completes, say so at once and offer the nearest open window rather than failing the order. Carry the chosen window into the order confirmation and into any later change step, with an edit route that re-checks availability before accepting a new window.
