Distinct from the order tracking row, which states a window after placement, and from the scheduled action row, which runs an action at a future time. Cross-check the order tracking row and the calendar and scheduling row |
| Delivery address validation | Check an entered delivery address before the order is placed | Entered-address statement, suggested-correction statement, missing-detail prompt, keep-original choice, re-check at confirmation | When an entered address looks incomplete or mismatched, show the suggested correction beside the entered address for confirmation, and never silently rewrite what was entered. Let the person accept the suggestion or keep the original where they insist on it, and where a unit, suite, or apartment number looks missing, prompt for it before the order proceeds rather than guessing it. Where the result is uncertain but the address may still be genuine, warn and let the person proceed; block only an address that is clearly unusable, and state the reason in words. Re-check the address at confirmation, and carry the confirmed address into the order confirmation unchanged. Distinct from the address entry row, which collects an address, and from the saved address selection row, which reuses a stored address. Cross-check the address entry row and the saved address selection row |
| Delivery instructions | Tell the carrier where to leave an order and what access it needs, in words the carrier can act on | Instruction field, drop-off choice, access-note field, chosen-instruction statement, change control | Offer a place at checkout to state where an order should be left and any access note the carrier needs — entrance, gate or intercom detail, or a named safe place — kept short and actionable rather than a free-form paragraph. Carry the instruction into the delivery record and show it back to the person before payment, so a mistake is caught while it can still be fixed. Keep a plain route to change it while the order can still be changed, and state plainly when an instruction cannot be honoured, such as a drop-off request on an order that needs a signature, rather than accepting it silently. Where the address already implies the access detail, prefill it and let the person correct it, never asking twice for the same detail. Distinct from the delivery slot picker row, which chooses when a delivery arrives, and from the saved address selection row, which chooses a stored address. Cross-check the order tracking row |
| Product variant selection | Choose one buyable combination of a product's options without reaching a dead end | Option groups, option controls, price statement, availability statement, chosen-combination statement | Present each option group — such as size, colour, or material — as one control, and update the price, image, and availability together the moment a combination is complete, so the person never wonders which option changed what. Mark unavailable combinations as unavailable in place, with the reason stated where known, rather than letting them vanish from the list, and where a choice rules out options in another group, mark those at once instead of waiting for a failed add-to-cart step. Never let a partially chosen set look like a complete choice: keep the add action disabled with a plain statement of what is still missing until every group has a choice. Where a chosen combination cannot be supplied, name the nearest available combination and offer a clear route to it, and where the person arrived with a specific combination in mind, preselect exactly that combination rather than a default that quietly substitutes another. Build each option group as a named single-choice group, and make every option a real named control rather than a styled shape alone, so the chosen value is stated in words as well as in shape, the selected state is exposed beyond colour or border alone, keyboard focus stays visible, and pale swatches keep a visible border against the background. Distinct from the filtering and sorting row, which narrows a list of products, and from the data table row, which compares separate items. Cross-check the filtering and sorting row and the saved-item collection row |
| Product configurator | Build a made-to-order product from compatible options, with a live preview and a price that follows the build | Guided questions, option groups, live preview, running summary, price breakdown, review step | Begin with questions about intended use, and narrow the options to those that fit the answers, rather than presenting the full option list at once. Update the preview, price, and availability together on every choice, and state at once when a choice rules out a later option, instead of letting the build reach a dead end. Keep a running summary of every choice beside the preview, and let a choice be changed from the summary without losing later choices that still fit; where a change invalidates a later choice, name it plainly and ask for a replacement rather than dropping it silently. End with a review of the full build — every choice, the price breakdown, and availability or lead time — before the order or quote request, and state plainly whether the next step places an order or only asks for a quote. Where the preview cannot show the exact finished item, say what it omits. Distinct from the product variant selection row, which picks one combination of a stocked product's options, and from the wizard row, which steps through a generic task. Cross-check the product variant selection row and the wizard row |
| Saved payment method selection | Choose one saved payment method at checkout without retyping its details | Saved method list, default marker, add-new action, edit and removal controls, chosen-method statement | Show saved methods as a named list, each identified by its type and last few digits only, never by full details, with the default marked in words as well as in shape, so the default is clear without relying on position alone. Preselect the default at checkout and let the person choose another saved method in one action, always offering a new method without discarding the saved list. Before payment, state plainly which method will be charged, in the same identifying words, so the charge is confirmed rather than assumed. Where a saved method has expired or is no longer usable, mark it as unusable with its reason in words and keep it out of the preselection, rather than letting it fail at payment. Editing or removing a saved method must never expose full details, and removal asks for confirmation and states at once whether a replacement default is needed. Where no default exists, say that no method is selected and require an explicit choice or a new method before proceeding. Distinct from the payment failure recovery row, which preserves an order after a failed payment, and from the guest checkout row, which completes a purchase without a saved account. Cross-check the payment failure recovery row and the saved address selection row |
| Split payment | Divide one order total across several payment parts, paid by one person or shared between people | Split method choice, share list, amount per share, running total, remaining-balance statement, per-share status, individual receipt route | Offer the split choices up front: equal shares, shares by item, or custom amounts, and state plainly whether each share is paid by a different method of one person or by a different person, since the two cases need different confirmation routes. Show every share with its amount and its payer or method in words, keep a running total beside the order total, and never let the shares be confirmed while their sum differs from the total — state the shortfall or excess in plain figures and block confirmation until it matches. Where shares are paid by different people, send each person only their own share and their own link or request, never the full order or another person's payment details, and keep the order in a pending state, with each paid and unpaid share marked, until every share is paid or the split is cancelled. Where one person pays all shares with several methods, charge each method only for its stated share and confirm each charge separately, so a failure on one share never charges another share twice or leaves the order half-paid without a statement. After the full total is paid, confirm once with the total and each share, and give each payer their own receipt for their share. Distinct from the gift card and stored-credit redemption row, which spends stored value as one payment line, and from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a single method for the whole order. Cross-check the gift card and stored-credit redemption row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Shared expense splitting | Share ongoing group costs across members and settle the resulting balances | Group, expense list, per-expense participants, split method choice, balances view, settle-up plan | Offer the split method on each expense, not only on the group: equal shares, shares by item, agreed shares or percentages, or custom amounts, and let each expense name only the members who shared it, so a member who missed an outing is never charged for it. Where items differ, split shared charges in proportion to what each person ordered, and keep every expense total exact: shares must sum to the expense total, with any rounding remainder stated plainly on one share rather than silently dropped. Show a running balance for each member and a who-owes-whom view, and offer a settle-up plan that uses the fewest transfers needed to clear all balances, with each repayment recorded as a payment, not as a new expense. Keep expenses editable until settlement, and restate every affected balance at once when an expense is corrected or removed, so no stale balance stands after a change. Distinct from the split payment row, which divides one order total at checkout, and from the shared cart row, which collects items for one checkout. Cross-check the split payment row and the shared cart row |
| Media chapters | Jump between sections of long media without scrubbing blindly | Chapter markers on the seek bar, chapter list, chapter titles and durations, current-chapter statement, transcript link | Mark every chapter on the seek bar and in a named chapter list, each entry giving its title, start time, and duration, so a section is found by name rather than by guessing a position. Choosing a chapter seeks to its start at once and announces the chapter title and new position to assistive technology, and the current chapter is stated in words as well as shown on the bar, so position is clear without colour alone. Keep the chapter list keyboard-operable and reachable from the player controls, and keep chapter markers distinct from the played and unplayed portions of the bar. Place the transcript beside the player rather than inside the player container, so it stays available when the player goes fullscreen, and announce only confirmed changes — playback starting or pausing, a chapter change, or a seek landing — rather than routine time updates. Where no chapters exist for long media, say so in the chapter list instead of showing an empty menu. Distinct from the media controls primitive, which operates playback, and from the described media row, which narrates visual information. Cross-check the media controls primitive and the described media row |
| Playback queue | Play items in order from a list, with the item now playing marked | Ordered list, current-item mark, add, remove, and reorder controls, shuffle control, repeat control, end-of-queue statement | Mark the item now playing in text as well as in shape, so the current item stays clear without relying on colour alone. Add, remove, and reorder controls never interrupt the item now playing, and removing the item now playing moves playback to the next item without stopping the list. State the shuffle and repeat modes in words wherever their controls appear: shuffle plays each item once before any item repeats, repeat-all loops the whole list, repeat-one repeats the current item, and repeat-off stops at the end of the list. State plainly what plays when the queue ends — silence, a related item, or a return to the previous view — so the end never arrives as a surprise stop. Keep the queue visible from the player and keep a jump to any item to one action, so choosing what plays next never requires leaving playback. Distinct from the media chapters row, which marks sections inside one item, and from the list reorder row, which reorders a list without playback. Cross-check the media chapters row |
| Sleep timer | Stop long-form playback on its own after a chosen time, so listening does not run on unheard | Timer control, duration choices, end-of-chapter choice, remaining-time statement, extend and cancel controls, fade-out | Offer the timer from the player itself, with fixed durations and an end-of-current-chapter choice, so setting it never requires leaving playback. State the chosen stop point in words before playback continues, and keep the remaining time visible from the player, so the stop never arrives as a surprise. Fade the sound out rather than cutting it off at the stop point, and remember the exact stop position, resuming from there — with a short step back for context — rather than restarting the item. Let the timer be extended or cancelled in one action from the player without stopping playback, and state plainly when a timer is already running before a new one is set. Where playback is interrupted before the timer ends, keep the timer choice for the next session rather than silently dropping it. Distinct from the media chapters row, which marks sections inside one item, and from the playback queue row, which orders items. Cross-check the media chapters row and the media controls primitive |

| Waitlist | Join a queue for a full item, place, or service and take a later opening when one is offered | Full-capacity statement, join action, place confirmation, offer notice with a deadline, leave action | State plainly when capacity is full and offer joining the list as the next step, never a silent refusal and never a promise of a place. On joining, confirm the place at once and state what happens next: whether a position in the queue is shown, whether joining guarantees nothing, and how the person will be told when an opening appears. Where a position is shown, keep it honest and update it when it changes, and where no position is tracked, say so rather than inventing a number. When an opening appears, offer it with a stated deadline and a clear accept step, and never enrol the person without that acceptance; when an offer expires unanswered, release it and offer it onward to the next person in the queue, stating that the expired offer does not keep the place. Offer a leave action at any time that removes the entry at once and confirms the removal, and let a person who declined or missed an offer rejoin without penalty where places remain. Distinct from the appointment management row, which changes a booking already held, and from the notification escalation row, which raises urgency rather than holding a queue place. Cross-check the appointment management row |
| Loyalty rewards | Earn and spend a points balance with a visible next reward | Balance summary, pending balance, next-reward progress, earning rules, redemption routes, history | Show the available balance beside its value in plain currency terms wherever the balance appears, so a points number never stands alone without what it buys. Keep pending points separate from available points, with the reason they are pending and when they become available stated, so a balance never promises value that is not yet spendable. Pair the balance with the nearest attainable reward and the gap to it, rather than only the full reward list, and state the earning rule beside the balance: what earns points and what is excluded. State expiry plainly: which points expire, when, and whether spending uses the earliest-expiring points first; never let a reward disappear silently. Offer earn and redeem routes from the same view, and keep a reward that is out of stock or unavailable out of the redeemable list rather than showing it as obtainable. Keep a history of earned, spent, expired, and reversed points, and keep the balance consistent after returns and reversals, stating plainly when a return removes points. Distinct from the gift card and stored-credit redemption row, which spends stored value entered as a code, and from the promo-code entry row, which applies a discount. Cross-check the gift card and stored-credit redemption row
| Referral invitation | Invite another person with a shared link or code and a stated reward for both sides | Benefit statement for each side, qualifying-action statement, share controls, referral history, reward states | State the benefit for the inviter and for the invited person side by side before any sharing, and state the action that qualifies the reward — signing up, verifying, a first purchase, or reaching a usage threshold — so sharing alone is never implied to earn it. Keep the full conditions beside the benefit, not behind a separate terms page, and name the reward type in plain words: credit usable only for certain purchases, a discount on a future price, or cash through a stated route. Offer share controls for copying the link or code and for sending it through the person's own channels, with a copy confirmation and a plain failure message where sending fails. Track each referral through states that match events the system can establish — invited, signed up, qualified, rewarded — and never present a missing reward as a failed invitation while qualification has not yet occurred. Keep the history useful without exposing the invited person's activity beyond the referral itself, and state plainly when a reward expires or is reversed. Distinct from the loyalty rewards row, which earns points from the person's own activity, and from the promo-code entry row, which applies a code at checkout. Cross-check the loyalty rewards row and the promo-code entry row |
| Link preview | Show what a pasted link leads to before it is opened | Preview card, title, short description, thumbnail, source label, plain-link fallback, suppress control | When a link is pasted into a message, expand it into one preview card showing the destination title, a short description, a thumbnail where one is available, and the source in words, so the destination can be judged before opening it. The card acts as a single link to the destination, and the original link text stays reachable rather than being hidden behind the card alone. Where no title, description, or image is available, keep the plain link instead of showing an empty or invented card, and never invent a title, description, or image for a destination that supplies none. Show one preview per message and suppress further previews where a message holds several links, so cards never flood the conversation. Let the sender remove the preview without removing the link, and let the reader dismiss a preview that obscures the message. State the source in words as well as in any thumbnail, and announce the title and source to assistive technology, so a card is never judged by its image alone. Distinct from the system share sheet row, which previews content being sent, and from the external-link departure row, which warns before leaving a service. Cross-check the system share sheet row and the external-link departure row |

| Home-screen widget | Show one glanceable summary outside the app with a route into the matching view | Summary value, size-tier layouts, tap route, refresh statement, last-updated label, sign-out clearing | Design each size tier on its own: the smallest tier shows one value and one action at most, larger tiers add a short list or a second value, and no tier tries to reproduce the full view. Every element that shows a value taps through to the matching in-app view, so a glance never dead-ends on a summary. State the refresh cadence in plain words and show the last-updated time wherever the value could be stale, and never present a stale value as current. Where the value is loading, keep the previous value with a loading marker rather than blanking the widget; where it fails, state the failure in place with a retry route and keep the tap route working. Where there is nothing to summarise, state that plainly with one action that creates the first item, rather than showing an empty frame. Clear any personal value from the widget on sign-out or account switch, so a shared device never keeps the previous account's summary on the home screen. Distinct from the notification list row, which reports events, and from the simplified view row, which reduces a full view rather than summarising it from outside. Cross-check the notification list row and the simplified view row |
| Live activity | Follow one ongoing task from outside the app, on the lock screen or in a compact system area, without opening the app | Task label, current state in words, progress or countdown, expanded detail view, compact and minimal presentations, tap route, end control | Show one task per activity, with its current state stated in words beside any progress figure or countdown, so a glance never depends on a bar or a number alone. Keep the compact presentation to the task label and one state value, and reserve fuller detail for an expanded view that opens on a long press or tap, with every value in the expanded view also reachable in the app. Design each presentation tier on its own rather than shrinking one layout: where several activities run at once, show only a minimal marker for each, and tapping it opens the matching activity. Mark data that may be out of date as stale in words, and never present a stale state as current. End the activity as soon as the task finishes, is cancelled, or is no longer relevant, and remove it from every presentation at once, rather than leaving a finished task visible; where the person dismisses it, respect that dismissal and never restart the same activity without a new trigger. Tapping the activity always lands on the matching in-app view, and any control offered outside the app, such as pausing or ending, states its effect before it acts and confirms it in the app. Keep the activity silent by default: no repeated alerts for routine updates, only a single notice when the task reaches a state the person asked to be told about. Distinct from the home-screen widget row, which shows a summary that persists whether or not a task is running, and from the background-task progress row, which follows a task inside the app. Cross-check the home-screen widget row and the background-task progress row |
| System-mediated snippet | Complete one quick action, or confirm one, from a compact surface shown by the system without opening the app | Action summary, named primary action, cancel control, result statement, route into the app | Design the snippet as its own surface with one job — starting an action, confirming it, or reporting its result — never as a smaller copy of the app. Confirmation and result presentations have different jobs: a confirmation states what will happen, names the item and the effect, and offers one clearly labelled primary action beside a cancel; a result states what happened, with one follow-up action at most. Keep the surface compact and scannable at a glance: one action, short plain wording, no scrolling detail; where the task needs more detail, choice, or editing, route into the matching in-app view instead of overflowing the lightweight surface. Where the action has effects beyond the screen — sending, paying, deleting, or contacting someone — always confirm before acting and state the effect first; a surface that acts silently on such an action is never acceptable. Announce state changes and results to assistive technology, keep the cancel control reachable, and never trap the person on the surface. Distinct from the home-screen widget row, which shows a summary that persists whether or not a task is running, from the live activity row, which follows one ongoing task, and from the confirmation row, which stages the check inside the app. Cross-check the home-screen widget row, the live activity row, and the confirmation row |
| Deep-link landing | Land on the exact content a link from outside points to, with the surrounding navigation intact | Destination view, owning navigation selection, reconstructed back stack, unavailable-content fallback, section-root return | When a link from outside opens the app, land on the exact content it names with its owning navigation selected, so the destination reads as part of the app rather than as a floating screen. Rebuild the back stack the person would have built by tapping there normally, so pressing back leads to the parent view and then to the section root instead of leaving the app at once. Where the linked content is no longer available, show a plain fallback surface stating that the content is gone, with a return route to the section root and a search route, rather than a blank screen or a generic home screen. Where the app is not installed, the link's web surface states the destination and offers the install route while keeping the destination reachable after install. Preserve any context the link carried, such as a selected item or a filter, and drop it only where it no longer applies, stating what was dropped. Distinct from the external-link departure row, which leaves the current service, and from the share link row, which sends the person's own item elsewhere. Cross-check the external-link departure row and the share link row |
| Progressive disclosure | Reveal detail only when asked, keeping the core task visible and the expanded state stated | Collapsed preview, expand control, expanded-state label, reading-order content, retained expansion, core-task visibility | Keep everything the current task needs visible without expanding anything, and place only supporting detail behind an expand control, so a collapsed block never hides a step, a price, or a condition the decision depends on. Label the expand control with what it reveals rather than a bare symbol, and state the expanded state in text as well as in shape, so the state is clear without relying on an icon alone. When expanded, keep the content in reading order with the rest of the page, and keep the expanded state where returning matters, such as a form the person revisits, rather than collapsing it silently on every return. Never make a collapsed block the only route to a task, and never nest expanded blocks so deeply that the way back is lost. Where an anchor or a link points inside a collapsed block, open that block on arrival so the destination is visible rather than apparently empty. Distinct from the accordion disclosure row, which organises sections under headings, and from the long-document navigation row, which moves within a page. Cross-check the accordion disclosure row and the long-document navigation row |
| Billing overview | Let a current customer check what they pay, when, and for what, without contacting support | Current plan summary, next charge statement, payment method summary, invoice list, usage summary, failed-payment route | State the current plan, the next charge amount and date, and the payment method that will be charged, before any offer to change or upgrade, so a person checking a charge finds the answer first. List past invoices in date order, each entry giving its period, amount, status in words, and a one-action route to the document, and keep opening an invoice separate from downloading a receipt or editing payment details. Distinguish amounts that may still change from amounts already finalised: label a current-period total as an estimate where usage is still accruing, and treat an issued invoice as the final record for its period. Where usage-based charges apply, keep the unit and the period definition beside the value, so a total never stands alone without what it counts. Where no payment method is on file, or a payment has failed, state that plainly in place with the route to fix it, rather than showing a blank or apparently paid view. Distinct from the subscription plan change row, which changes a tier, from the subscription cancellation row, which ends a plan, and from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a method at checkout. Cross-check the subscription plan change row and the subscription cancellation row |
| Usage allowance | Show how much of a recurring allowance remains, and what happens at the limit, before the limit is reached | Used, allowance, and remaining figures, period and reset statement, threshold warnings, top-up or upgrade route | Show used, allowance, and remaining together as numbers in words beside any bar, never a bare percentage or bar alone, and state the unit and the period for each meter; where several meters exist, list each separately with its own reset date. State the reset date and what resets, and label a current-period figure as an estimate where use is still accruing. Warn in place before the limit is reached, at stated thresholds, with a route to top up or upgrade, so the limit is never first mentioned at the moment work stops. State plainly, before the limit is reached, what happens at the limit — work stops, extra use is charged, or a lower tier applies — and keep a blocked state recoverable through the top-up or upgrade route, never a dead end. Where the figure may be stale, state its freshness in words and never present a stale figure as current. Distinct from the billing overview row, which reports charges and invoices, and from the subscription plan change row, which changes a tier. Cross-check the billing overview row and the subscription plan change row |
| Password strength meter | Show how strong a chosen password is while it is typed, with one actionable next step | Password input, show-or-hide control, strength indicator, short feedback line, requirement summary | Treat the meter as guidance, not a verdict: it updates as the person types, while enforcement happens at submission with a precise reason tied to the field. Judge length first, and check the value against commonly used or compromised values; do not require a fixed mix of character types, and never teach that adding a short suffix turns a weak password into a strong one. Show one short feedback line naming the biggest improvement, and once the password is strong, stop showing unchecked suggestions for it. Never use colour alone: pair any bar with a text label, keep the label programmatically associated with the field, and announce changes to assistive technology without moving focus. Keep the feedback stable rather than flashing between states on every keystroke, keep a show-or-hide control beside the input, and allow paste and saved credentials without blocking them. Distinct from the account recovery row, which restores access after it is lost, and from the verification-code entry row, which checks a code already sent. Cross-check the form flow row |
| Emoji picker | Insert a symbol into text being composed, without leaving the compose area | Search input, category tabs, recents group, symbol grid, preview label, skin-tone control, close control | Open the picker from a labelled control beside the compose input, and keep the input visible so the person never loses the text being written. Offer search by name or keyword with a plain no-match statement, and grouped categories whose active category is stated in words as well as in shape, so a category is never icon-only. Show a recents group first, limited to a small set of recently used symbols, with a way to clear it, and keep recents distinct from the full catalogue. Where a symbol has variants, offer one skin-tone choice that persists across sessions and applies only to symbols that carry variants, stated plainly rather than applied silently. Choosing a symbol inserts it at the cursor position and leaves the text unchanged around it; closing the picker, by an explicit close control or by Escape, returns focus to the compose input. Keep every symbol labelled in words for assistive technology, make the grid navigable by arrow keys with the category tabs reachable by keyboard, and never rely on colour alone to mark the chosen category or the selected symbol. Distinct from the Message reaction row, which answers a message without sending a new one, while this picker inserts a symbol into text being composed. Cross-check the Message reaction row |
| Sticker and GIF picker | Send a picture or short animation in place of typed text, from inside the compose view | Search input, category chips, recents group, saved-items group, item grid, preview, send control, text-description label | Open the picker from a labelled control beside the compose input and keep the input visible, so the person never loses the text being written. Offer search by word or phrase with a plain no-match statement, and grouped categories with the active category stated in words as well as in shape. Show recent and saved items first, with a way to clear recents, and keep saved items distinct from search results. Give every item a plain text description, announced to assistive technology, so a picture is never sent without words for those who cannot see it. Preview the item at full size before sending, and play the animation in the preview so the person sees exactly what will arrive; never send on a single tap of a grid item where sending cannot be undone. Keep the picker usable with the keyboard, with the grid navigable by arrow keys, and honour reduced-motion settings by showing a still frame instead of a looping animation. Where a search returns nothing suitable, offer a plain statement and a way back to the compose view rather than an empty grid. Distinct from the emoji picker row, which inserts a single character, and from the message reaction row, which responds to a message already sent. Cross-check the emoji picker row and the message reaction row |
| Return and exchange request | Start a return or exchange from the order itself and follow it until it is settled | Order item list, reason choice, outcome choice, label and route statement, request status | Start the request from the order itself, naming the item and quantity, so nothing has to be retyped or looked up elsewhere. Ask for the reason in plain words from a short list, with free-text detail where the list does not fit, and offer only the outcomes that apply to that item — return, exchange, or refund — naming the replacement item or choice before an exchange is submitted. Where a label is needed, state who pays for return shipping and the route: a drop-off point or a pickup, with a stated send-by date. Confirm the request at once, stating what was requested and what happens next, and keep the request status visible on the order until the outcome is settled, naming each stage — requested, approved or declined with the reason in words, received, refunded or exchanged — keeping a declined or completed request visible rather than deleting it. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows a delivery forward, and from the saved-cart recovery row, which revisits an unfinished cart. Cross-check the order tracking row and the order cancellation row |
| Return label and drop-off | Return an item with a label and a drop-off route after the request is approved | Label route, validity statement, drop-off choice, proof of handover, refund-timing statement | State where the label lives — in the order record, by message, or as a code to show at the drop-off point — and offer a printable label beside a code-only route, so a return never depends on a printer. State how long the label stays valid and what happens after it lapses, with a plain route to a fresh label where one is available. List drop-off points with address and hours, and state what to bring: the parcel, the label or code, and any packaging rule. At handover, give proof at once — a receipt, a tracking reference, or a scanned confirmation — and keep that proof in the order record. State plainly when the refund starts: at the drop-off scan, at arrival, or after inspection, naming which stage applies, and keep the return status visible until the refund is settled. Where no label is needed, say so in place rather than showing an empty label control. Distinct from the return and exchange request row, which starts the return, and from the order tracking row, which follows a delivery forward. Cross-check the return and exchange request row and the order tracking row |
| Back-in-stock alert request | Ask to be told once when one unavailable item returns | Unavailable-item statement, alert request control, coverage statement, arrival channel choice, stop control | Where an item or a variant is unavailable, offer the alert in place of the buy action, naming exactly the item and variant the alert covers, so a restock of another variant never triggers it. State plainly how the alert arrives and that it covers one restock, not an open-ended watch, and send a single alert per restock naming the exact item and variant with a route straight to it, without added promotions. Never promise availability in the message itself: re-check availability at the moment the alert is opened and state it there, so a message that arrives after the item has sold out again says so rather than implying it can still be bought. Offer a stop route from the request itself and from the alert, removing the request at once and confirming the removal, and where the person is signed in, keep the request visible in one place so it can be reviewed or stopped later. Distinct from the notification digest row, which groups low-urgency updates, and from the in-app update prompt row, which requires an update rather than reporting availability. Cross-check the notification digest row and the waitlist row |
| Contact picker | Choose contacts for the current task without handing over the whole address book | Contact list, search field, chosen-contact confirmation, multi-selection review, scope statement, add-contact route, manual entry fallback | Prefer the system picker where one is available, so the person chooses in a familiar surface and only the chosen contacts reach the task. Present contacts as a named list with a search field matching names and numbers, and where a contact holds several numbers or addresses, ask which one the task needs rather than guessing. Carry only the chosen contacts, and only the fields the task needs, into the task, and state that scope plainly before the choice — what the contacts are used for and what is not kept. Where the task needs several contacts, allow several to be chosen in the same picker, show the chosen set for review before confirming, let each chosen contact be removed on its own, and still ask which field is needed where a chosen contact holds several. Where the contact is missing, offer a route to add it without leaving the task, and always keep a manual entry route for a contact the picker cannot supply or a field it returns empty. After choosing, show the chosen contacts in words with a change action, and never retain the rest of the address book. Distinct from the invitation response row, which answers an invitation, and from the share link row, which sends an item elsewhere. Cross-check the invitation response row and the share link row |
| Seat selection | Choose specific seats from a seating map for a booking | Seating map, orientation label, legend, seat states, selection summary, list alternative | Label the map orientation in words — which side is the front, stage, or screen — so the map reads without guessing. State each seat by row and number in text, and mark availability, selection, and unavailability in shape or text as well as colour, so no state depends on colour alone. Make every available seat keyboard-focusable and selectable, and keep unavailable seats perceivable with their reason in words rather than removing them silently. Offer a list alternative to the map with the same rows, numbers, and prices, so the choice never depends on the visual layout alone. Show the selected seats with their individual prices and the running total beside the map, and let a selected seat be removed from the summary as well as from the map. Where seats are held only for a limited time, state the hold length and what happens when it ends before payment, and re-check availability at confirmation, offering the nearest open seats when a chosen seat fills. Distinct from the calendar and scheduling row, which picks a time, and from the product variant selection row, which picks a buyable combination. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row |
| Selecting an option from a list | Choose one value, or several values, from a known set without hiding the choice | Option group, select menu or combobox, custom-value field, add-another control | Match the control to the length of the list: where the set is short, show every option at once as a visible group — checkboxes where several values may be chosen, radios where only one may — so the choice needs no opening, scrolling, or searching. Where the set is long, collapse it into a select menu or a searchable combobox: a select menu suits a set that can be scanned without heavy scrolling and where native handling helps, while a combobox suits a set where typing to filter finds the value faster, and the filtering behaviour is stated plainly since not everyone discovers it. Keep the single-or-several semantics consistent across the control: a group that allows several choices never behaves like a single choice, and a chosen value stays visible after the menu closes. Where a needed value may not be listed, put a plainly labelled custom option at the end of the group; choosing it reveals a text field for the custom value, placed directly below the control. Where several custom values may be needed, offer an add-another control below the revealed field, giving each new field its own descriptive label, and cap the number of added fields so the form stays manageable. Distinct from the filtering and sorting row, which narrows a result list, and from the select primitive, which describes the control alone. Cross-check the form flow row and the filtering and sorting row |
| Home control map | Control connected home devices from a spatial view of the home | Floor plan or room map, device markers, room and floor switcher, device-type filter, scene controls, list alternative | Place each device marker where the device sits, grouped by room rather than by device type alone, so a person finds a control by place instead of by remembering a device name. Show the current state in place, in words or shape as well as colour — on or off, brightness, temperature, locked or unlocked — so no state depends on colour alone, and state plainly where a device cannot be reached rather than showing it as off. Offer a list alternative to the map with the same rooms, devices, states, and controls, so no task depends on the spatial view alone, and make every marker keyboard-focusable with a room-and-device label in words. Let a person move between floors and rooms without losing the current place, and filter the markers by device type without hiding the rest of the home from the list view. Offer named scenes as single controls that change several devices together, stating what each scene will change before it runs, and pin the most-used devices and scenes to the top of the list view so everyday control needs no map navigation. Distinct from the location selection row, which picks a place, and from the seat selection row, which picks seats from a seating map. Cross-check the location selection row and the seat selection row |
| Follow | Keep up with new activity from a chosen topic, source, or person without searching for it again | Follow control, followed-state indicator, followed list, update stream, unfollow control, per-follow notification control | Offer follow beside the topic, source, or person itself, and mark the followed state in words as well as shape, so the state never depends on colour alone. Following adds new activity from that subject to the person's update stream, never replacing the stream with only followed activity, and each update names its subject so the person can tell why it appeared. Keep a single followed list where every followed subject can be reviewed and unfollowed in one action, with the change taking effect at once and confirmed in place. Offer a per-subject notification control separate from following itself, so a person can keep the stream entry while silencing its interruptions, and never send a notification the person did not choose for that subject. Where a subject stops publishing or becomes unavailable, state that plainly in the followed list rather than silently dropping it. Distinct from the saved-item collection row, which keeps a single item for a later return, and from the notification preferences row, which governs channels and cadence across all topics. Cross-check the saved-item collection row and the notification preferences row |
| Payment instalment plan | Spread one order total across dated payments at checkout | Plan choice, payment schedule, total-cost statement, remaining-payments view, payment-method change route | Offer the plan beside full payment at checkout, with each plan stating its number of payments, each payment's amount and date, and the total cost including any charges, before confirmation — never show only the first payment or a per-payment figure without the total. Show the full schedule before the person commits, state what happens on a missed payment in plain words, and keep the plan changeable until confirmation. Where choosing the plan starts an eligibility or approval check, say so before the choice is made, with how long the check takes and what happens if approval is not given — the order stays unplaced and full payment remains available — and where an earlier check is offered, for example on the cart view, use it so the plan is presented as a payment choice rather than a surprise application. Keep pre-checkout instalment wording tied to the actual order total and conditional, never implying approval is guaranteed. After confirmation, give a single place to view remaining payments, their dates and amounts, and to change the payment method for future payments without cancelling the plan. Where an item is returned, restate the remaining schedule at once rather than leaving the old schedule standing. Distinct from the split payment row, which divides one total across payers or methods at once, and from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a single method for the whole order. Cross-check the split payment row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Subscribe-and-save purchase option | Buy a product once, then repeat the same purchase on a schedule at a stated repeat price | One-time and recurring choice, repeat interval, repeat price, next-order date, pause, skip, change, and cancel controls | Offer the recurring choice beside the one-time purchase on the product itself, stating the repeat interval, the price that applies to repeats, and whether that price can change, before the first order is placed. State the next order date plainly and send a reminder before each repeat order, early enough to pause, skip, or change it. Keep pause, skip, change, and cancel reachable from the order itself and from the account area, taking effect before the next order's cutoff, and state on the control itself which order a change applies to when the cutoff has passed. A skipped order is never charged and the schedule resumes with the following order; a paused schedule states its return date and restarts without a new setup. Cancelling stops future orders at once and never removes past orders or their records. Distinct from the subscription plan change row, which changes a service tier, and from the reorder past order row, which repeats a past order on demand. Cross-check the subscription plan change row and the reorder past order row |
| Pickup point selection | Collect an order from a named pickup point instead of a delivery address | Pickup-point list, distance and hours statement, holding-period statement, ready notice, change-point route | Offer pickup beside delivery at checkout, listing each point with its name, address, distance, and opening hours, and state which items each point can supply before the choice is made. State the holding period at choice time and again in the ready notice, and state plainly what happens when the period ends — return, refund, or restocking — so an uncollected order is never a surprise. Send a ready notice only when the order is actually ready to collect, naming the point and what to bring, and never invite collection before readiness. Keep a plain route to change the point while the order can still be changed, restating hours and holding period for the new point, and where a point cannot supply an item, say so before checkout rather than after. Distinct from the delivery slot picker row, which chooses when a delivery arrives, and from the saved address selection row, which chooses a stored address. Cross-check the delivery slot picker row and the delivery instructions row |
| Store pickup | Collect an order from a store instead of having it delivered, with a stated ready time and handoff | Store list with stock, ready-time statement, pickup window, collector statement, check-in control, handoff code | Show stock for the chosen variant at each store before the store is chosen, and state the ready time before confirmation, never promising pickup with no location and no time attached. On readiness, notify with the store, the pickup window, and what to bring, and offer a check-in control on arrival so the order can be prepared for handoff. Allow another person to collect where authorised, stating what that person must show, and state the hold period for an uncollected order with the refund or return route that follows it. Distinct from the pickup point selection row, which collects from a third-party point, and from the in-store availability check row, which checks stock before a visit without placing an order. Cross-check the pickup point selection row and the in-store availability check row |
| Parcel locker pickup | Collect a parcel from an unattended locker with a one-time code, without a staffed handoff | Pickup notice, one-time code, QR code, locker kiosk, compartment, expiry statement, reminder, return route, identity check, easy-access compartment | Send the pickup notice only when the parcel is actually in the locker, naming the locker, the code or QR, and the exact expiry — date and time — so nobody travels on a promise. State the expiry in the notice and again on the tracking view, send a reminder before it ends, and state plainly what follows an uncollected parcel — return to the sender — with the route to extend or redirect where one exists. Accept the code or the QR at the locker kiosk, or an open-from-app control, and open only the compartment holding that parcel; where several parcels share one compartment, say so in the notice so nothing is left behind. Treat the code as single-use and as proof of access to the phone or inbox, not proof of identity: a forwarded code opens the locker, so state that sharing the code shares the parcel, and where the parcel is high-value or age-restricted, require a second check — identity shown in the app before the code is issued, or collection at a staffed counter — rather than relying on the code alone. Offer an easy-access compartment on request for a parcel collected at a low or reachable height, and keep the kiosk usable without the app, with large targets and plain-language error states when a code fails. Distinct from the pickup point selection row, which chooses a staffed or third-party point at checkout, and from the store pickup row, which hands the order over in person. Cross-check the pickup point selection row and the store pickup row |
| In-store availability check | Check whether a chosen product variant is in stock at nearby stores before a visit | Store list, distance and hours statement, stock-checked time, hold or reserve route, fallback route | Offer the check from the product page for the chosen variant, so a person never checks stock for a variant they did not choose, and ask for a location only where it is needed to find nearby stores. List each store with its name, distance, and opening hours beside the stock statement for that store, and state the time the stock was last checked, so a stale result is never presented as current. Where a store holds the item, state plainly whether it can be collected today or tomorrow, and where a hold or reserve route is offered, state how long the hold lasts and what to bring, so the visit is never wasted. Where the nearest store has none, offer the next nearest store with stock or a delivery route instead of a dead end, and never invite a visit to a store whose stock is unknown. Distinct from the pickup point selection row, which chooses where a placed order is collected, and from the delivery slot picker row, which chooses when a delivery arrives. Cross-check the pickup point selection row and the product variant selection row |
| Live captions | Follow spoken content in a live call or broadcast as text, without losing the conversation | Caption area, speaker labels, caption controls, transcript view, caption preferences | Treat captions as content, never as an overlay that other controls can cover: keep the caption area clear of controls, labels, and other overlays, and let the person move and resize it. Label each speaker in words, and state non-speech sounds in brackets, so meaning never depends on voice alone. Keep lines short with a fixed starting point and automatic scrolling, and let the person change size, contrast, and font without leaving the call. Offer captions before joining as well as during the call, alongside the other call settings, and remember the choice for the next call. Show uncertain wording plainly and correct it in place when later context clarifies it, rather than holding text back until it is certain. Keep a transcript beside the live view that can be searched, copied, and revisited after the call, with each entry carrying its speaker and time. Where a person brings their own captioning service, show its captions on the same call screen rather than on a separate screen, and let them change the size, font, position, and the colour and opacity of the text and its background without asking the host. Where a person follows the call through sign language, let them keep the interpreter visible beside the speaker, pinned in place, with video clear enough to read the signing. Distinct from the media controls primitive, which toggles captions on recorded media, and from the described media row, which narrates visual information. Cross-check the media controls primitive and the accessibility preferences row |
| Recording consent | Agree before a call or meeting is recorded | Consent prompt, persistent recording indicator, pause and stop controls, leave route, scope statement | Ask before capture starts, stating in plain words what is captured, who can access it, and how long it is kept; never start capture silently and never treat joining the call as agreement. Offer accept and leave as equal choices, and give a late joiner the same prompt before they are captured. Keep a persistent indicator visible for the whole capture, naming what is being captured, and announce every start, pause, resume, and stop to all participants, in spoken form for participants on audio only. Treat consent as ongoing: ask again when the scope widens, such as adding a written transcript to a sound capture, and when anyone objects, stop the capture or let them leave before it continues — never continue over a stated objection. Show capture and transcript states separately, so stopping one never implies the other has stopped. Distinct from the granular consent row, which governs data-use categories, and from the permission request row, which governs device access. Cross-check the live captions row and the granular consent row |
| Route planning | Plan a journey with several stops in one ordered list | Stop list, map canvas, numbered markers, start and end controls, optimise control, totals statement | Keep the stop list and the map in the same order, with numbered markers that match the list numbers, so a stop is found in either view without counting. Offer entry by typing an address with suggestions, by choosing a place from a list, and by tapping the map, and keep a typed or chosen stop editable and removable without rebuilding the route. Treat the first and last stops as fixed unless the person changes them, and state plainly when an optimise action will reorder only the stops between them. Distinguish a stop where the journey pauses from a pass-through point the route merely passes, and label each one in words. Reorder by drag and by explicit move controls, never drag alone, and recalculate the route, total distance, and total time at once after every change, stating when a stop cannot be reached in the chosen order. Distinct from the location selection row, which picks one place, and from the map comparison row, which compares places and layers. Cross-check the location selection row and the map comparison row |
| Turn-by-turn guidance | Follow a chosen route step by step | Instruction banner, trip progress, route line, recenter and overview controls | Show the current step with its direction, distance to it, and plain cue text, and show the next step where two steps fall close together so the person is never surprised by a quick second turn. Keep the map oriented to the direction of travel with the position low on the screen and the road ahead visible, drawing the travelled part and the remaining part in distinct styles so progress reads at a glance. Show trip progress as arrival time with time and distance remaining, and offer an overview at any time with a single recenter action that returns to the followed view after panning. When the person leaves the route, state the recalculation plainly and show the new route before resuming guidance, and never hide the end-navigation action. Where lane choice matters, show lane guidance before the junction, and where spoken guidance is offered, keep its words identical to the banner text. Distinct from the route planning row, which builds a multi-stop journey before travel, and from the map comparison row, which compares places and layers. Cross-check the route planning row |
| Guided route navigation | Follow a chosen route step by step to a destination | Full-screen map, current-step card, next-step preview, lane guidance, travel-estimate card, reroute notice, arrival message, voice-guidance control | Show one current step at a time with its direction, distance, and plain instruction, with the next step previewed below it, so the person never has to assemble the route from a list while moving. Keep a travel estimate visible — arrival time, time left, and distance left — and update it when the route changes. When a turn is missed, reroute at once, state that the route changed, and show the new first step rather than leaving the old instruction on screen; a calculating state says so plainly instead of showing a stale step. Give lane guidance before a complex junction, and keep spoken guidance in step with the shown step, with a control to mute it without stopping navigation. On arrival, replace directions with a plain arrival message and an end control, and where the route cannot be completed, state the failure and offer the nearest workable alternative rather than a dead end. Distinct from the route planning row, which orders stops before travel, and from the location selection row, which picks one place. Cross-check the route planning row and the location selection row |
| Pass and ticket display | Show a pass or ticket held for later use in a form ready to present | Pass list, pass detail, scannable code, validity statement, fallback route, featured actions | Show the scannable code large, on a plain high-contrast background, with nothing laid over it, so it scans on the first attempt; raise screen brightness while the code is displayed and restore the previous brightness afterwards. State the pass or ticket in words beside the code — what it admits, the date and time it covers, the seat or entry where one applies, and whether it is still valid — so the code never stands alone. Keep a saved pass or ticket available without a connection once it has been downloaded, and state plainly where it is stored and how to reach it again. Where a code cannot be scanned, offer a route at once: a typed code or reference the person can read aloud, or a staff route, never a dead end and never a demand to re-purchase. A used or expired pass states that plainly and is kept visible with its history rather than vanishing, and a changed pass replaces the old one in place with the change stated. Where the platform allows actions on the pass, show at most two relevant actions below the pass face, each labelled with what it does, and keep every action about the pass itself, never a promotion. Distinct from the code scanner row, which captures a code, and from the order tracking row, which follows an order's progress. Cross-check the code scanner row and the order tracking row |
| Travel check-in | Confirm travel for a booked trip and receive a pass ready to present | Check-in window statement, seat choice, bag choice, pass download control, offline pass, next-step statement | State the check-in window before it opens, with the opening and closing times in the trip timezone, and offer check-in from the booking itself, so the person never hunts for a separate entry point. During check-in, confirm or change the seat and declare bags in the same flow, stating any bag charge before confirmation, so seat and bag choices are settled together rather than discovered later. On confirmation, issue the pass at once with boarding time, gate or platform, seat, and bag allowance restated in words beside any scannable code, and offer a saved copy that stays reachable without a connection, with a plain route to retrieve it again. State the next step plainly after check-in — bag drop or direct boarding — and where bags are checked, state the drop-off cutoff separately from the boarding time. Where check-in cannot complete, state the reason and offer a desk or staff route at once, and never mark a trip as checked in before the pass itself is available. Distinct from the seat selection row, which chooses a seat, and from the pass and ticket display row, which presents a pass already held. Cross-check the seat selection row and the pass and ticket display row |
| Ride booking | Book a ride with a driver, follow the vehicle to pickup, and confirm identity at handoff | Pickup field, destination field, labelled pickup pin, fare estimate card, vehicle choice, live tracking view, driver identity card, contact actions, handoff code, trip receipt | Treat pickup confirmation as a separate decision, not part of the map: show the pickup point as a labelled pin with a readable street or landmark description, and ask for confirmation before vehicle choice, so an unlabelled marker never becomes the pickup. Show each vehicle choice as one card carrying its arrival estimate and fare together, with the currency beside every amount, and state the reason in words where a fare changes from the first estimate, so a changed price is never presented without explanation. After matching, turn the map into a coordination view: keep the driver identity, vehicle type, registration, and arrival estimate in a stable sheet with call and message actions, and name each stage in plain words — request sent, driver assigned, driver approaching, driver arrived, trip in progress, trip completed — with each stage changing the next action offered. At arrival, confirm identity on both sides before the trip starts: show the driver name, photo, and registration beside the vehicle, and require a short handoff code that both sides confirm, so the ride cannot begin on a wrong vehicle. Keep a plain help route visible during the trip, not buried in account settings, and after the trip show an itemised receipt with the fare breakdown and the route taken. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows a delivery, and from the location selection row, which chooses a place on a map. Cross-check the order tracking row and the verification-code entry row |
| Shared cart | Let several people add to one cart before a single checkout | Shared cart, member list, per-item contributor label, owner statement, removal rule, checkout control | Name who added each item beside the item itself, so the cart reads as a shared list rather than one person's choices. State the checkout owner plainly: one named person completes checkout, and everyone else can add, change, or remove only their own items. State the rule for removing another person's item in words before anyone acts — only the owner may remove another's item, and the removal is shown to the person who added it — so nothing disappears unexplained. Keep each person's items grouped or labelled when the list is long, and show a per-person subtotal beside the shared total where costs are shared. When a contributor leaves, state what happens to their items before they leave, and never silently keep or silently drop them. At checkout, the owner confirms the full list and total, and every contributor sees the order that resulted from their items. Distinct from the split payment row, which divides payment, and from the saved-item collection row, which keeps items for one person. Cross-check the split payment row and the saved-item collection row |
| Shared shopping list | Keep one household shopping list shared in real time, so nobody buys the same item twice | Shared list, per-item claim, bought section, duplicate warning, unseen-changes count, shopping mode, list templates | Show every addition, edit, and check-off to every member as it happens, so the list on each phone is the same list, not a stale copy. Let a member claim an item before shopping it, so nobody else buys the same thing on the way home, and show who claimed it beside the item. Warn before adding an item that is already on the list, rather than letting two copies sit there unnoticed. Move bought items into a separate section instead of deleting them, so what is already covered stays visible at a glance. Where another member changed the list since it was last opened, keep those changes visible on the next open with a count of unseen additions, edits, and check-offs, so nothing changes silently. Give each item the detail the shopper needs to get it right the first time — quantity, unit, category, a note, and a photo where the exact brand matters. Offer a shopping mode that collapses several lists into one trip list, sorted for the trip, with a quick-add that never leaves the flow, and keep a history of completed trips. Save the lists that repeat — a weekly run, a party kit — as templates, so the next list starts from the usual items instead of from nothing. Distinct from the shared cart row, which gathers items for one checkout, and from the setup checklist row, which tracks one person's tasks. Cross-check the shared cart row and the offline-first sync row |
| Group checkout | Let several people pay their share of one order at checkout, so no single person carries the whole total | Participant list, share list, amount per share, per-share payment status, combined total, organiser statement, payment deadline | State the share method up front: equal shares, shares by item, or custom amounts, and show each participant only their own share and payment route, never another person's payment details. The order is placed only when every share is paid, or when the organiser covers the remainder under a stated rule. Where a share stays unpaid past the stated deadline, say plainly whether the order is held, reduced, or cancelled, and state the refund route for shares already paid. Give each payer their own receipt for their share, and keep the combined total visible to the organiser so shares always reconcile to it. Distinct from the shared cart row, which lets several people add items before a single checkout, and from the split payment row, which divides one payment without bringing each payer into checkout. Cross-check the shared cart row and the split payment row |
| Stored payment method expiry warning | Warn before a stored payment method expires so future orders and plans keep working | Expiry warning, method identifier, expiry statement, replacement route, updated-method confirmation | Warn ahead of expiry, early enough to replace the method before the next order or charge, and state which method it is — its type and last digits — and the month it expires, so the warning is actionable rather than alarming. Put the replacement route in the warning itself: adding a new method takes effect for future orders and plans without cancelling them, and the replaced method is never left as the default. Stop warning once the method has been replaced, and never warn about a method that is no longer stored. At checkout, an expired method is never offered as a choice, and a method expiring before a scheduled charge states that plainly beside the choice. Where a replacement cannot be completed, state what will fail and when, with a route to fix it before the failure. Distinct from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a method, and from the payment failure recovery row, which recovers after a failure. Cross-check the saved payment method selection row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Pass or ticket transfer | Pass a held pass or ticket to another person | Transfer offer, recipient choice, pending state, cancel route, transferred state | Name exactly which pass or ticket is being passed, with what it admits and the date and time it covers, before the offer is sent, so neither side acts on the wrong item. The transfer moves only when the recipient accepts it: show the pending state to both sides, with the offer's expiry stated, and state plainly whether the original remains usable while the offer is pending. The sender can take the offer back before acceptance, and a declined or expired offer returns the pass or ticket to the sender at once with that outcome stated to both sides. Once accepted, the original is invalidated and leaves the sender's list, and the pass or ticket appears in the recipient's list ready to present; where a transfer is single-use, state before sending that it cannot be passed on again. Distinct from the pass and ticket display row, which shows a held pass, and from the gift option row, which marks an order as a gift at purchase. Cross-check the pass and ticket display row and the gift option row |
| Document signing | Sign a document in place and show who has signed and who still must sign | Document preview, signature capture, placement control, signer list, signing order, signed record | Offer a draw route for capturing a signature, with a clear redraw route before it is applied, and keep the document preview readable while placing it: drag to position and resize, with a keyboard or menu route that offers the same placement, so dragging is never the only path. Where the signature is a ticked box or a typed name rather than a drawn mark, state plainly before signing that it carries the same effect as a handwritten signature, and ask the signer to confirm they understand that before the signature is applied; place guidance about signing in the journey before the signing step, so confidence is built before the moment of commitment, not only at it. Before applying, show the document name and the page where the signature will land, and let the person review both before confirming; after applying, mark the document signed, record the signer name and signing date, and keep the signed copy distinct from any unsigned draft. Where more than one signer is needed, state the order plainly: in a sequential order the next signer is notified only after the previous signer has finished, while in a parallel order all signers may sign at once, and the signer list states each person's status in words — pending or signed — so nobody signs twice or waits on a finished step. A signing request names the document and the signer, and states where to sign, so the signer never hunts for the place to act. Distinct from the annotation and comment thread row, which discusses content without changing it, and from the file upload row, which sends a file rather than completing it. Cross-check the annotation and comment thread row and the file upload row |
| Transcript editing | Correct a recorded transcript against its audio without losing timing | Waveform or playback view, editable transcript text, segment boundaries, speaker labels, tentative-wording marker, playback position marker | Keep audio and text in sync in both directions: choosing a position in the audio moves the text to the matching words, and choosing words moves playback to the matching audio, so a correction is never made against the wrong passage. Keep each spoken segment as one editable unit with its start time stated, and let a segment be split or joined without shifting the timing of the segments around it. Mark wording that is still tentative in text as well as shape, and correct it in place when it is confirmed, rather than replacing a whole passage or moving following text unexpectedly. Announce only confirmed changes and playback state changes to assistive technology, never every tentative wording change, and keep speaker labels in words rather than colour alone. Offer a route to remove a sensitive passage from both the text and the audio view, with the removal confirmed, and keep the corrected transcript searchable and exportable with speaker and time for each entry. Distinct from the live captions row, which follows spoken content as it happens, and from the voice message row, which offers a transcript beside a short recording. Cross-check the live captions row and the media chapters row |
| Hotel self check-in | Check in for a booked stay at a kiosk or on a phone, and receive a room key without waiting at a desk | Pre-registration prompt, arrival instructions, reservation lookup, identity check, key activation, room directions, help route | Invite pre-registration before arrival, and state the benefit in plain terms — skip the queue and go straight to the room — in short messages that read well on a phone. Send arrival instructions on the day of arrival, so the guest knows where to go and what to have ready. At the kiosk, start with language choice and reservation lookup, then identity verification; hand document capture to the guest's own phone by scannable code rather than asking for a passport on a shared lobby screen. End with the key itself — a digital key on the guest's phone or a physical key encoded at the machine beside the kiosk — and plain directions to the room, so arrival ends with entry rather than another queue. Clear the previous guest's details from the screen at once and return to the welcome screen, so no guest sees another's stay. Where several guests arrive together, spread them across kiosks and keep a staff route visible on every screen for anything that needs a person. Distinct from the hotel booking row, which chooses a stay, and from the travel check-in row, which confirms travel and issues a pass. Cross-check the hotel booking row and the travel check-in row |

| Meeting and event creation | Create a meeting or event with attendees and resources confirmed before it is sent | Title and purpose, attendee list, availability view, time-zone statement, conflict notice, calendar route | Check attendee and resource availability before confirming, and never confirm a time that is already busy without saying so. Where a conflict exists, state it in plain words — who or what is unavailable and for which part of the time — and offer the next available time that works for everyone, rather than leaving the organiser to hunt for it. State the time zone beside every time shown, and for a recurring event state whether the series follows a local hour in a named zone or a fixed instant, so a clock change never silently moves it. Keep the confirmed event editable until it is sent, and offer a route to carry it into the person's own calendar with the same time, attendees, and zone. Distinct from the calendar and scheduling row, which shows objects in time, and from the invitation response row, which answers an invitation. Cross-check the calendar and scheduling row and the invitation response row |
| Shared resource booking | Book a shared room or item from its availability | Resource list, availability view, capacity and equipment statement, booking form, approval state, cancellation route | Start from availability, not from a form: each room or item states its capacity and equipment beside its name, and only times that are actually free can be chosen. Treat buffer time between bookings as blocked time, stated beside the booking, so setup, cleanup, or handover never creates an overlap. Where a booking needs approval before it is confirmed, say so before the person submits, name what is being approved, and keep the request visible with its pending state until it is decided. A freed booking — cancelled, ended early, or never claimed — is released at once for others, and the release is stated to anyone waiting on it. Where a bundled booking asks for several items at once and one item is unavailable, state which item blocks the booking and offer the nearest working combination instead of failing the whole request silently. Distinct from the appointment management row, which manages a person's own booked time, and from the calendar and scheduling row, which shows objects in time. Cross-check the appointment management row and the calendar and scheduling row |
| Backup payment method | Keep a second stored payment method ready for use only when the primary method fails | Primary method, backup method choice, fallback notice, failure notice, change route | Let the person choose a backup method in advance, in the same place where stored methods are managed, and state at checkout which method is primary and which is the backup, so the fallback is never a surprise. Charge the backup only after a failed attempt on the primary method, and say so plainly at that moment: name the failed method, state that the backup will now be tried, and confirm the result before the order is treated as complete. Never retry the primary method silently and never charge both methods for one order. A backup that is expired, removed, or no longer valid is never offered as a fallback; where no usable backup remains, fall back to the payment failure recovery path with the order kept intact. The person can change or remove the backup at any time, and removing the primary method never silently promotes the backup without a stated confirmation. Distinct from the stored payment method expiry warning row, which warns before expiry, and from the payment failure recovery row, which recovers after a failure. Cross-check the stored payment method expiry warning row and the payment failure recovery row |
