| Saved search alert | Watch a saved search and report new matches, without the person re-running it | Saved-search choice, alert control, criteria statement, frequency statement, delivery channel choice, pause and stop controls, alert list | State the exact criteria in the alert setup and repeat them in each alert, so the person can tell why it arrived. State the check frequency plainly — as matches arrive or as a scheduled summary — and never imply instant delivery where checks run on a schedule. Report only matches that are new since the last alert, never resend a match already reported, and send nothing when nothing new matches, with that silence stated at setup rather than left ambiguous. Let the person choose whether updates to an existing match also trigger an alert, and state that choice before enabling it. The alert follows the saved search as currently defined: when the criteria change, the next alert restates the criteria in force, so a change is visible rather than silent. Offer pause and stop from the alert itself and from the alert list, and remove the alert at once on stopping, with the removal confirmed. Where the person is signed in, keep every alert visible in one place so it can be reviewed, paused, or stopped later, and link each alert straight to the matching results with the criteria applied. Distinct from the back-in-stock alert request row, which watches one item, from the saved-filter view row, which returns to a filter combination without alerting, and from the saved-item collection row, which keeps items the person chose. Cross-check the back-in-stock alert request row, the saved-filter view row, and the notification digest row |
| Magic link sign-in | Sign in through a link sent to an email address, without typing a password | Identifier input, sent-link notice, link message, confirm control, expiry statement, resend control, alternate sign-in route | Ask only for the identifier, then state where the link was sent, how long to wait before retrying, and where to look if it does not arrive, with a route back to sign-in. In the message, put the sign-in action first, state its purpose and its stated validity plainly, and say what to do when the message was not requested. Never complete sign-in on the first fetch of the link alone: opening it shows a confirm screen naming the account identifier and the destination, and sign-in completes only on an explicit confirmation, so an automated preview or scanner cannot spend a single-use link before the person sees it. Treat each link as single-use with a stated validity: an expired or already-used link gets its own screen naming the next step and offering a fresh link, never a generic failure, and a newer request replaces an older one. Where the link opens on a different device or browser, state what still works, preserve the intended destination where supported, and always offer an alternate sign-in route, such as a typed code. Keep an alternate method visible beside this route, since it fits poorly where inbox access is shared or unreliable, or where sign-in happens many times a day. Distinct from the verification-code entry row, which checks a typed code, from the passkey sign-in row, which uses a device-held credential, and from the account recovery row, which restores access after the usual route fails. Cross-check the verification-code entry row, the passkey sign-in row, and the authentication layout |
| Household member management | Manage who belongs to a shared household and what each member can see and change | Member list, role statement, invite control, role-change control, removal control, pending-invite list | List every member with the role stated in plain words, and state what each role can see and change before anyone is invited or changed: who can manage members, who can change shared settings, and who can only view. An invite states what the new member will see and change, stays visible as pending until accepted, and offers a route to resend or withdraw it before acceptance; an invite never grants access before it is accepted. A role change states its effect before confirmation and never takes effect silently, and nobody can raise their own role through the change control. Removal states what the removed member loses access to, takes effect at once, and never removes the last member able to manage the household without first naming a replacement; items the removed member owns stay theirs where the product allows, while shared items stay with the household. Distinct from the account switcher row, which moves between signed-in accounts, and from the invitation response row, which answers a single invitation. Cross-check the account switcher row and the invitation response row |
| Shared subscription plan | Let one paying manager share a paid plan with named members, each keeping a separate account | Seat count, member list, manager statement, invite control, pending-invite list, removal control, leave control, downgrade statement | State the seat limit beside the member list, with seats used and seats left, so an invite is never sent for a seat that does not exist. Only the manager manages members and billing; members never see billing details, and the manager never sees a member's private activity, only the name and contact needed to manage the seat. Each member keeps a separate account with their own content and settings, so joining or leaving never merges one person's work into another's. An invite states the plan, that the member pays nothing where the manager pays, and what access acceptance gives, stays visible as pending until accepted, and offers a route to resend or withdraw it; an invite never grants access before it is accepted. Removal states what the member loses at once and what they keep, and moves the member to the free tier rather than deleting their account, with their own content kept. A member can leave on their own at any time with the same downgrade effect, and a vacated seat returns to the pool for a new invite. Where the plan itself is cancelled, state that every member's paid access ends at the same stated date rather than ending member by member. Distinct from the household member management row, which manages membership of a shared household rather than paid seats on one plan, and from the subscription gifting row, which gives access for a set period paid once rather than sharing an ongoing plan. Cross-check the household member management row and the subscription cancellation row |
| Checkout price-change confirmation | Confirm a changed price before payment is taken | Old total, new total, change statement, fresh confirmation control, edit-or-cancel route | When any price, fee, or discount changes between the cart view and payment, stop before charging and state the old total, the new total, and what changed and why, line by line, so the change is visible rather than buried in a revised total. Require a fresh confirmation of the new total: an earlier confirmation never carries forward across a price change, and the payment control stays inactive until the new total is confirmed. A fall in price is stated just as plainly as a rise, with the same before-and-after totals. Offer an edit-cart route and a cancel route from the confirmation itself, without discarding details already entered, so refusing the new price never costs the whole checkout. Distinct from the saved-cart recovery row, which states changes on return to a saved cart, and from the limited-time offer countdown row, which states an offer deadline. Cross-check the saved-cart recovery row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Preorder purchase | Buy an item before it is available, with the release and payment terms stated up front | Preorder badge and release date, payment choice, balance-due statement, preorder order view, delay notice, cancel route | Mark the item plainly as a preorder wherever it appears — product view, list, cart, and order — never presenting it as in stock, and state the release or shipping date beside the buy action rather than only in detail text. State before payment whether the full price or a deposit is taken now, and where a deposit is taken, state the balance, when it falls due, and how it will be charged, with the total stated beside any deposit figure. Keep the preorder visible in the order view with its release date, amount paid, and amount still due, and send a notice when the date changes, stating the new date and offering a route to cancel where cancellation remains open. Before release, offer cancellation with the refund terms stated at the point of cancelling, never only in small print elsewhere, and where a preorder converts to a regular order on release, state that change and the final charge before it is taken. Distinct from the waitlist row, which holds a place without payment, and from the back-in-stock alert request row, which only reports a return. Cross-check the waitlist row and the back-in-stock alert request row |
| Split shipment display | Show one order as its separate shipments, each with its own items, window, and tracking route | Shipment list, per-shipment item list, per-shipment delivery window, per-shipment tracking route, single order total, folded order state | State the split as soon as it is known, at confirmation or at the first dispatch, never leaving the person to discover it when the first parcel arrives. Show each shipment as its own section with the items it contains, its delivery window, and its own tracking route, and never show a single order-level progress bar for a split order, since one bar cannot represent several parcels at different stages. Charge shipping once on the order, never once per shipment, and state the single order total beside the shipment list so a split is never mistaken for several orders. Keep the overall order state as a summary folded from its shipments — for example, partly delivered — rather than a stage of its own, and never derive it from the latest event alone, since a later shipment still in transit must not be hidden by an earlier delivery. Where a shipment is delayed or fails, attach the change and the next step to that shipment only, leaving the other shipments untouched. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows one delivery, and from the order cancellation row, which stops an order. Cross-check the order tracking row and the order cancellation row |
| Price-drop alert | Watch one item for a price fall and report only the first fall past a stated trigger | Watch control, watched-price statement, trigger choice, alert message, pause and stop controls, alert list | Offer the watch control beside the price on the item view, never only in account settings, and state at setup both the price being watched and the trigger — a chosen target price or a chosen fall — so the person can tell exactly what will cause an alert. Report only the first fall past the trigger, and never repeat the alert for the same fall, since a repeated message for one price reads as noise. State the old price and the new price in the alert itself, and link straight to the item with the new price applied, so the saving is verifiable without hunting. Where the price falls again after the first alert, say at setup whether a further alert follows, rather than leaving a second message unexplained. Offer pause and stop from the alert itself and from the alert list, and remove the alert at once on stopping, with the removal confirmed. Where the item becomes unavailable before the trigger is met, say so plainly instead of leaving the watch running silently. Distinct from the saved search alert row, which watches a search for new matches, and from the back-in-stock alert request row, which watches availability. Cross-check the saved search alert row and the back-in-stock alert request row |
| Live auction bidding | Bid on a lot in a live or timed auction and know at once whether the bid stands | Lot view, current bid, next bid step, bid control, maximum-bid control, countdown, bid history, outbid notice, winner statement | Show the current bid, the next step, and the exact amount a bid will place before the bid is placed, and state plainly beside the bid control that every bid is binding, so nobody bids by accident. Offer a one-action bid for the next step and a maximum-bid control that bids in steps up to the stated cap and never past it, showing the cap and the current standing bid separately. Tie the countdown to the server clock rather than the device clock, and show the end time with its zone; where a bid in the closing minutes extends the close, state the extension rule before bidding starts and reset the countdown visibly on each late bid, so the close is never a surprise. Show bid history in time order with amounts and times, and tell an outbid bidder at once with the new current bid and a route back to the lot, never letting a person keep bidding on a stale price. When the close passes, replace the bid control with the outcome at once — won, lost, or reserve not met — with the final price and what happens next for payment, collection, or delivery. Distinct from the limited-time offer countdown row, which counts to a fixed deadline that never moves, and from the preorder purchase row, which takes payment at a fixed price before release. Cross-check the limited-time offer countdown row and the preorder purchase row |
| Guest order lookup | Find an order without signing in, using only the order reference and one identifier from the order itself | Lookup form, order reference input, order identifier input, order summary view, support route, account-link offer | Offer the lookup from the order emails, the site footer, and the help area, under a plain label such as checking an order, so a guest never has to sign in to find it. Ask only for the order reference and one identifier taken from the order itself, such as the email used at checkout, and never let a reference alone open an order, since references can be guessed. On a failed lookup, state the next step — checking the reference and identifier, or contacting support with the order details already carried in — never a bare failure, and never confirm whether a reference exists without its matching identifier. Show the order state, items, and delivery window, and keep personal details out of the result except what the person needs to confirm the order, so a lookup on a shared screen exposes as little as possible. Create no account as a side effect of looking up an order, and offer account creation only as a separate optional step after the order is shown, keeping the lookup route working for anyone who declines. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows a signed-in order, and from the guest checkout row, which buys without an account. Cross-check the order tracking row, the guest checkout row, and the guest-to-account conversion row |
| Tip selection | Add an optional gratuity to an order at checkout | Preset amount choices, custom amount field, explicit no-tip choice, updated total, change-after-order statement | Offer tipping only where a gratuity is normally expected for the service, and never block checkout behind the choice: the no-tip choice sits beside the presets at the same size and emphasis, never hidden in a submenu. Show preset amounts and percentages together, offer a custom amount field for any other value, and update the order total at once when the choice changes, so the amount confirmed is the amount charged. State before confirmation whether the tip can be changed after the order is placed, and if it can, give the route and the deadline; if it cannot, say so plainly rather than leaving the choice silent. Distinct from the payment instalment plan row, which spreads a total, and from the promo-code entry row, which reduces a total. Cross-check the payment instalment plan row and the promo-code entry row |
| Donation at checkout | Add an optional charitable contribution to an order at checkout | Beneficiary statement, preset amount choices, custom amount field, round-up choice, explicit decline choice, separate line item, updated total, receipt statement | Keep the contribution optional and never preselected: the decline choice sits beside the presets at the same size and emphasis, never hidden in a submenu, and checkout never blocks behind the choice. Name the beneficiary and state the amount beside the control in plain words, so the person knows who receives the gift and what it costs before confirming. Offer a small set of preset amounts with a custom amount field, and where a round-up choice is offered, state the exact amount it will add for this order rather than only the rule. Show the contribution as a separate line item beside the order total, update the total at once when the choice changes, and carry the contribution onto the receipt as its own line. State before confirmation whether the contribution can be changed or refunded after the order is placed, and give the route where it can; where it cannot, say so plainly. Distinct from the tip selection row, which rewards the service provider, and from the gift card and stored-credit redemption row, which spends stored value. Cross-check the tip selection row and the gift card and stored-credit redemption row |
| Checkout upsell and cross-sell offer | Offer one relevant addition to an order at a stated point in the purchase, without blocking checkout | Offer placement, offer statement, add control, decline control, updated total, added-item removal control | State which kind of offer it is: an upsell offers more of, or a better version of, the item already chosen, while a cross-sell offers a different item that completes it, and never present one as the other. Offer one addition per surface and never stack several offers on one surface: a variant or quantity upsell sits beside the buy action on the product view; a complementary cross-sell sits below the cart items and above the checkout action; a single small order addition sits between shipping and payment at checkout; and one one-action offer follows payment, before the thank-you screen. Base the offer on what is already chosen — items bought together, not a generic bestseller list — keep it cheaper than the main item, a small share of the order total, and make it addable in one action with no variant or quantity choice at that moment. Never preselect or pre-tick a paid extra: the decline choice sits beside the offer at the same size and emphasis, and declining never blocks checkout. Never interrupt payment with a separate accept-or-reject step that must be answered before payment can continue; an offer that needs a decision belongs before checkout or after payment. Show the added item as its own line with its price, update the total at once when it is added, and offer removal of the added item from the cart or order view without undoing the rest of the order. Distinct from the tip selection row, which rewards the service provider, and from the donation at checkout row, which is a charitable contribution, and from the product variant selection row, which chooses the item itself rather than adding to it. Cross-check the tip selection row and the donation at checkout row |
| Signed-in device management | Review and end sessions on devices signed in to an account | Device and session list, current-device marker, last-activity statement, per-device sign-out control, sign-out-everywhere control, security follow-up route | List every device and session signed in to the account, each with a name the person recognises, the kind of device, and when it was last active, and mark the device in use so the person never signs out of the device they are holding by mistake. Keep one row per session where one device holds several sessions, and state plainly where a listed entry may lag behind real activity. Offer sign-out for one device or session from its own row, and a sign-out-everywhere control that keeps the current device signed in unless the person chooses otherwise, with a plain statement of what each sign-out ends and what it does not end, such as items stored on the device itself. After signing out an unfamiliar device, offer a direct route to change the password and to review recent activity, rather than leaving the person to find those steps. Distinct from the account switcher row, which moves between signed-in accounts, and from the second-factor sign-in row, which adds a check at sign-in. Cross-check the profile and account menu row and the account recovery row |
| Live call controls | Control microphone, camera, sharing, and leaving during a live call | Control bar, microphone toggle, camera toggle, share control, participant list, raise-hand control, leave control, connection indicator | Keep the core controls — microphone, camera, share, participants, and leave — in one fixed bar that stays reachable without hunting through menus, and keep the bar visible or recallable at all times rather than hiding it behind a gesture alone. State each toggle in words as well as in shape, so muted and camera-off are never colour alone, and confirm every toggle at once with a pressed state and a short status label. Keep leaving distinct from ending: leaving removes only the person, while ending closes the call for everyone, and state which one the control does before it is used. Before sharing, show exactly what will be shared — one window or the whole screen — and offer a pause or stop control that stays visible while sharing; never share a private window by default. Mark the current speaker in words, show a connection-trouble indicator on the affected tile with a plain explanation, and keep a raise-hand control that queues speakers in order and clears when the person speaks or lowers it. Offer keyboard shortcuts for microphone, camera, and share, including a hold-to-talk key that unmutes only while held, and keep a pointer path for every shortcut so no control depends on a key alone. Distinct from the live captions row, which renders spoken content as text, and from the chat and messaging row, which carries written conversation. Cross-check the live captions row |
| Shared presentation control | Let another participant advance shared slides or operate shared content without taking over the whole share | Share session, control grant control, holder list, revoke control, take-back control | Offer control only while content is being shared, and grant it to named participants one at a time or several at once, stating plainly what the grant covers — advancing slides, or operating the shared window — so a grant never reads as full control of the presenter's device. Show the grant to the recipient with the controls placed where they can be reached without hunting, and keep the grant visible to the presenter as a holder list or a count of holders. A grant lasts only for the current sharing session: stopping sharing ends it, and leaving presentation mode hides the recipient controls until presenting resumes. The presenter can revoke one holder or all holders at any time, and can take back control at once without asking the holder to release it. Never grant control by default, and never let a holder's control reach beyond the shared content into other windows. Keep the grant, revoke, and take-back controls keyboard-operable, and announce each grant and revocation to assistive technology. Distinct from the live call controls row, which starts and stops sharing, and from the meeting and event creation row, which arranges the meeting before it starts. Cross-check the live call controls row |
| Service status notice | Tell people what is broken, what still works, and when they will hear next | Status notice, affected-parts list, unaffected-parts statement, workaround, update cadence statement, next-update time, resolution statement, status route outside the affected service | State at the top which parts of the service are affected and, in the same notice, what the person can and cannot do right now, naming the affected task in plain words rather than an internal component name, and state what still works so the person is not left guessing. Update on a stated cadence and give the next update time in every update, even when nothing is new, so silence never reads as abandonment; never invent a return time, and where no return time is known, say so plainly. Offer a workaround where one exists, with its limits stated, and add a plain cause once it is known, without blame or internal detail. When service returns, state when it returned, what happens next for work interrupted by the outage, and whether anything needs repeating, and keep the notice visible until that resolution statement is read. Keep a status route reachable outside the affected service, linked from the notice and from help, so the notice stays reachable when sign-in or the main service fails. Distinct from the notification escalation row, which grades interruption by urgency, and from the in-app update prompt row, which requires an update rather than reporting an outage. Cross-check the notification escalation row |
| Security incident notice | Tell people plainly when account or personal data may have been exposed, and what to do next | Incident statement, data-involved statement, ordered action list, protection-in-place statement, dated update route | State plainly what happened, what data was involved, and what was not involved, in the same notice, so the person can judge the risk without hunting for a second message. List the actions in the order that matters, matched to the data involved: change the affected credentials and anywhere the same credential was reused where credentials were involved, turn on a second check where one is offered, and watch statements for unfamiliar activity where financial data was involved. State what is already being done — what has been closed, reset, or put in place — separately from what the person must do, and never present a step still in progress as finished. Keep the notice reachable inside the service itself, and never make acting on it depend on a link inside a message, since a copied notice is a common deception route. Offer a dated update route as facts change, and update the original notice rather than sending a fresh notice that contradicts it. Distinct from the service status notice row, which reports availability, and from the account recovery row, which restores access. Cross-check the service status notice row |
| Order substitution choice | Let the person decide, item by item, what happens when an ordered item cannot be supplied | Per-item preference control, substitute statement, price-difference statement, dietary and allergen guard, refund statement | Set the preference before fulfilment, per item: accept a named substitute, ask first, or remove the item, and keep the default as ask first where no preference was given, so a substitute is never imposed silently. State any substitute beside the original with its price difference before the final total is charged, and never substitute across a dietary or allergen boundary without explicit approval for that item. Where the substitute costs less, refund the price difference without being asked, and state that refund beside the substitute; where it costs more, charge the difference only after approval. Where no suitable substitute exists and the preference is ask first, hold the item out of the order and state the effect on the total and on delivery, rather than swapping in a nearest item. Suggest substitutes from the person's past purchases first, and match the quality that matters most for the category — flavour where items differ by taste, brand where they differ by maker — since a substitute that matches what the person already buys is accepted far more often. Make a stock-out visible at once with a suggested replacement, rather than hiding it until after a purchase attempt; a substitution is not a neutral fix, so where the substitute does not fit, refund or removal stays the easy alternative. Distinct from the back-in-stock alert request row, which watches availability, and from the product variant selection row, which chooses a variant at purchase. Cross-check the back-in-stock alert request row |
| Commercial content disclosure | Label paid, sponsored, and affiliate content where it is recommended, in plain words | Disclosure label, earnings statement, recommendation placement, shared-content label | Label paid, sponsored, and affiliate content at the point of recommendation, in plain words stating what the publisher earns — a fee, a commission on a purchase through the link, or a free item — so the label needs no interpretation. Place the label with the recommendation itself, before the recommendation is acted on, rather than only on a separate policy page, and never rely on a vague tag alone. Keep the label with the content when it is shared or quoted, so a copied recommendation still carries its disclosure, and repeat the label where a long recommendation is split across pages or screens. Where a recommendation mixes paid and unpaid items, label each paid item on its own rather than labelling the whole list once. Distinct from the personalized-content disclosure row, which states the signal behind an adaptation to the person, and from the machine-composed content disclosure row, which labels content the system composed. Cross-check the personalized-content disclosure row |
| Device pairing | Connect a new physical device to an account and network for the first time | Discovery list, pairing-mode instruction, network choice, progress statement, name and place assignment, failure fallback, retry control | Begin with how to put the device into pairing mode, in words and with the indicator described — for example a blinking light or a sound — so the step never depends on a picture alone. Offer nearby discovery first, listing each found device by a recognisable name, and where discovery finds nothing, offer a manual entry route and a code route at once rather than a dead end. Ask for the network once, naming the network before its details are sent, and show progress by stage — found, connecting, and registered — so a stall names the stage it reached. On failure, state the plain cause, keep everything already entered, and offer both a retry and a different method, so a failed first method never forces a restart from the beginning. After connection, ask for a recognisable name and a place for the device, confirm it appears in its control list, and offer one test control before leaving setup, so the person leaves knowing the device works. Where a device already belongs to another account, state that plainly with a route to release it rather than failing silently. When a device that was already set up drops off the network and must connect again, repeat the same guided flow as first-time setup — the same steps, the same visual guidance, and the same expected device responses — rather than assuming the person remembers the steps from the first time. Distinct from the home control map row, which controls devices already connected, and from the setup checklist row, which tracks multi-step setup in general. Cross-check the home control map row and the setup checklist row |
| Hands-busy step guidance | Follow a sequence of steps while hands are busy or the screen cannot be touched | Full-screen step view, large step text, step progress, per-step item list, labelled step timers, voice and gesture step controls, keep-awake display, pause and resume controls | Show one step at a time in large text readable at a distance, with only the items needed for that step beside it, so the person never hunts through a full list while working. Keep the screen awake for the session, and offer a pause that holds the current step, so an interruption never loses the place. State progress in words as well as shape, such as step three of eight, and let the person move back to an earlier step without losing completed steps. Where a step has a duration, offer a timer labelled for that step, let several timers run at once with each labelled, and announce expiry in sound and in words, so a finished timer is never missed in silence. Offer step movement by voice and by a large on-screen control as well as by gesture, so no route depends on touching a small target with busy hands, and confirm each move so an accidental gesture never skips a step unnoticed. On leaving, keep the place and the running timers, and on return, show the current step first rather than the start of the sequence. Distinct from the wizard row, which collects entered data across steps, from the guided tour row, which points at elements of the interface itself, and from the guided route navigation row, which directs travel. Cross-check the wizard row and the guided route navigation row |
| Guardian approval request | Let a child ask a guardian to approve a purchase, a contact, or a feature, with the decision made on the guardian's own device | Request summary, pending state, guardian decision control, decision notice, guardian-only settings area | State exactly what is being asked in the request itself — the item and its cost where there is one, the person to contact, or the feature to open — so the guardian can decide without opening the child's device. Keep the requested thing blocked until the decision arrives, with no partial access in the meantime, and show the child a pending state naming who the request went to and offering a way to withdraw it. Put the decision on the guardian's own device, as a plain approve or decline, and where an approval covers more than one occasion, state its scope plainly rather than stretching a single approval silently. Gate any guardian-only area behind a step a child cannot pass by guessing or by trying again, and never let the child's device confirm its own approval. On decline, tell the child plainly with no bypass route offered; on approval, open only what was approved and say so. Let the guardian change or withdraw an approval later from the guardian-only area, and state what that change takes away. Distinct from the age verification gate row, which confirms an age threshold, and from the sensitive-action re-confirmation row, which checks the same person's intent again. Cross-check the age verification gate row |
| Dual approval | Require a second, different person to approve a sensitive change before it takes effect | Request summary, second-approver choice, pending state, decision record, expiry statement | State the exact change in the request itself — what will change, for whom, and what effect it has — so the second person decides on the change as written, never on a summary alone. The initiator never approves their own request, and approval on one device never confirms itself: the second approver is a different person with authority for that change, deciding on their own device or account. While a request is pending, nothing takes effect and no partial change is applied, and the initiator sees a pending state naming who the request went to, with a way to withdraw it. An approval covers only the request as written: where the change is edited after approval, the edited change needs a fresh approval, and an approval is used once, never reused for a later change. State the expiry plainly, and where a request expires, is declined, or is withdrawn, leave everything exactly as it was and say so. Where the named approver is unavailable, route the request to another eligible approver rather than lowering the threshold, and keep a decision record naming who requested, who approved or declined, and when, so the change can be checked later. Distinct from the guardian approval request row, which lets one person approve for another, and from the sensitive-action re-confirmation row, which checks the same person's intent again. Cross-check the sensitive-action re-confirmation row |
| Live poll | Gather a live audience vote on one question and show the result as it forms | Question, answer options, vote control, vote confirmation, live results view, voting state and close control | Keep the question short with a small set of answers, and make voting one action with a plain confirmation, so a person knows the vote landed. Show live results as labelled bars with counts and percentages, never colour alone, and announce result changes to assistive technology without moving focus or shifting the voting controls. Let the presenter choose whether results stay hidden until voting closes, and state plainly while voting is open, closed, or paused, with the close time shown where a deadline applies. State before voting whether votes are anonymous or tied to the voter, and whether a vote can be changed, so a person never votes under a false assumption about either. Keep results available after voting closes for anyone who missed the live moment, and never require sign-in where anonymous voting was promised. Distinct from the Live broadcast chat overlay row, which shows messages over a live picture. |
| Delivery photo proof | Show a photo of a completed delivery beside its delivered stage, with a route where the photo does not match the order | Photo thumbnail, delivered-stage link, mismatch report control, retention statement, visibility statement | Attach the photo to the delivered stage in the tracking view, so the person finds it where the delivery is already followed, and state when the photo was taken beside it rather than leaving a dateless image. Show the photo large enough to check the parcel and its placement, with a text alternative stating what the photo shows for anyone who cannot see it, and never use the photo alone as the delivery confirmation where a signature or handover was also recorded. Offer a mismatch route at once from the photo itself — report a wrong item, a damaged parcel, or a delivery to the wrong place — carrying the order and the photo into the report so nothing is re-entered, and state what happens next and how long a reply takes. State plainly how long the photo is kept and who can see it, and remove the photo when that period ends rather than keeping it indefinitely. Where no photo was taken, say so plainly instead of leaving an empty frame, and keep the delivered stage and its support route reachable. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows the delivery, and from the delivery instructions row, which states where an order should be left. Cross-check the order tracking row |
| Paired handover record | Record an item's condition at handover and again at return, so any change is shown by comparison rather than by memory | Baseline record, return record, side-by-side comparison, timestamped photos, meter readings, countersign, absence notice | Record the same fields at both ends, in the same order and to the same standard, so the two records can be compared directly: a return record with no baseline proves nothing about when a change happened, and a baseline with no return record leaves the handover open. Cover the same areas each time — exterior and glass, wheels, lights, interior, and every accessory handed over — with a fixed walkaround order so nothing is skipped when time is short. Capture timestamped photos at both ends, with a close-up for every mark, and record meter readings twice where they decide a charge: an entered value plus a photo of the reading, so a disputed figure can be checked against the image. At handover, let the person receiving the item review the baseline and countersign it before use begins; at return, offer the same review and countersign, and where the person is absent, record the absence, send the return record at once, and keep that notice in the booking history. Present only the differences between the two records as new, each carrying its photo and time, and state plainly what each difference means for charges before anything is billed, rather than presenting a fresh full report that hides what changed. Distinct from the delivery photo proof row, which attaches one photo to a delivered stage, and from the return label and drop-off row, which routes a return rather than comparing its condition. Cross-check the delivery photo proof row |
| Warranty claim | Claim a repair, replacement, or refund for a faulty item under its cover | Claim entry from order or item, proof checklist, claim states, remedy options with timeframes, return route, decision notice | Start the claim from the order or item it covers, carrying its details into the claim so the person never retypes them, and state up front exactly what proof is needed — proof of purchase, the fault described in plain words, and photos where they help — with a stated route where a proof item is missing. Use claim states that match events the service can establish — received, under review, approved, declined with a reason — and show the current state with what happens next and when, never a vague in-progress label alone. State each remedy on offer — repair, replacement, or refund — with its timeframe before the person chooses, and where the item must be sent back, give the return route, the label, and the send-by date at the same step. On decision, state the reason in plain words and the next step, including a route to challenge a decline, and keep the claim visible on the order after it closes. Distinct from the return and exchange request row, which returns an item by choice, and from the order cancellation row, which cancels before fulfilment. Cross-check the return and exchange request row |
| Overflow menu | Gather secondary actions that do not fit the primary surface in one menu anchored to its trigger | Trigger control, menu list, group separators, destructive-action separation, dismiss behaviour | Gather only secondary actions in the menu, keeping primary actions on the surface itself and destructive actions out unless a destructive action is plainly separated at the end with its own confirmation, so the menu never becomes a hiding place for a task the person needs. Name the trigger for what it opens — for example, more actions for this item — rather than leaving a bare symbol to be guessed, and keep the menu anchored to its trigger with every item a short verb-first label. Close the menu on selection, on dismiss, and when focus leaves it, and return focus to the trigger on closing, so keyboard and touch routes match. Keep the same actions in the same order wherever the menu appears, group related actions with separators where the groups are not obvious, and never nest a second menu inside the first where a new view would serve better. Distinct from the profile and account menu row, which lists account actions, and from the command palette row, which executes typed commands. Cross-check the profile and account menu row |
| Translated content | Read content written in another language, with the original still reachable | Translation control, translated label, source-language statement, original view control, untranslated-parts note | State beside the content that it is translated and name the language it came from, so a translated passage is never mistaken for the original wording. Offer the original beside the translation, reachable in one action, and keep the reader's place when switching between the two. Where only part of a page is translated, name what was left untranslated — such as images, video, or embedded text — rather than leaving a silent mix. Where a translated text carries legal or safety-critical meaning, state which version controls when the two differ and give a route to a reviewed version, never presenting the translation as settled on its own. Mark each translated passage with the language it is written in, so reading tools and assistive technology handle it correctly. Distinct from the language and locale selection row, which chooses the interface language, and from the live captions row, which renders spoken content as text. Cross-check the language and locale selection row |
| Message translation | Read a message written in another language without leaving the conversation | Per-message translate action, whole-conversation translate control, translation bar, source-language statement, original view control, language choice | Offer translation from the message itself, as an action in the message menu, so one message is translated without leaving the conversation or translating the whole thread. Show the translation beside the original, labelled as a translation with the language it came from, and keep the original one action away, with the reader's place kept when switching between the two. Where a conversation is mostly in another language, offer a translation bar at the top of the conversation that translates the whole thread, including messages as they arrive, and let the person dismiss it for that conversation alone. Remember the chosen language for the next message and the next conversation, and let the person change it without re-entering it each time. Where translation is only available for some languages, say so before the person asks for it, rather than failing silently on an unsupported language. Where a message is sent in another language, let the writer check the translated wording before sending, and keep the original wording visible to the writer afterwards. Distinct from the translated content row, which covers translated passages on a page, and from the voice message transcript row, which turns speech into text. Cross-check the translated content row and the chat and messaging row |
| Converted price display | Show a price in a second currency without hiding what will actually be charged | Original price, converted amount, conversion rate statement, rate date, charging-currency statement | Show the converted amount beside the original price, never in place of it, so the person always sees the price the seller set. State the conversion rate and the date it was set beside the converted amount, and state the rounding rule where rounding changes the total, since a converted total built from rounded unit prices can drift from a converted order total. Convert every price in the same view — unit prices, fees, discounts, and the total — with the same rate, so no line contradicts another. At checkout, never replace the original price with the converted figure: where the charge lands in a different currency from the display, re-state the final charge in the charging currency before payment, with its own rate and date where they differ from the displayed ones, and where the rate moved since browsing, name the new final charge plainly rather than letting the total change silently. Distinct from the locale-aware formatting rules in catalog/primitives.md, which format a price, and from the promo-code entry row, which reduces a total. Cross-check the language and locale selection row and the checkout price-change confirmation row |
| Scheduled message send | Compose a message now and send it later, staying in control until it sends | Scheduled-time statement, scheduled message list, edit and cancel controls, send confirmation, failed-send report | State the scheduled time in plain words with the date, time, and zone where the zone could change the meaning, and show the scheduled message in a list the person can return to, with its recipients, its content, and its scheduled time all visible. Until the message sends, it stays editable and cancellable: editing the content keeps the scheduled time unless the time is also changed, and cancelling never sends the message. Offer sending early as an explicit choice from the scheduled item, never as a side effect of editing it. Before sending, re-check anything that may have changed since scheduling — recipients who left, content that no longer applies, and a conversation that already moved on — and where the message no longer fits, hold it with a plain reason and a review path rather than sending it stale. When the message sends, confirm once with what was sent and when, and never repeat the confirmation on every later visit. When a send fails, report it plainly with the cause where known, keep the message with its content intact, and offer a retry at a new time or a cancel path, never silently dropping it. Distinct from the scheduled action row, which schedules a general action, and from the draft autosave row, which saves unfinished work. Cross-check the scheduled action row and the draft autosave row |
| Saved reply | Insert a reusable message template into a conversation or reply without retyping it | Template library, trigger or picker, editable inserted text, placeholder values, send control | Offer templates from the compose view, by a short trigger or a searchable picker, so the writer never leaves the conversation to find one. Inserting a template puts its text into the compose view as editable text, never sending it at once, so the writer can adapt it to the conversation before sending. Where a template holds placeholders, fill each one from the current conversation where a value exists, and flag any placeholder left empty before sending, rather than sending a template with blank gaps. Keep templates named by their purpose and searchable by name and by content, grouped where the library grows, so the right template can be found without opening each one. State the scope of each template plainly — where it can be used and who can change it — and keep a changed template from altering messages already sent. Distinct from the draft autosave and recovery row, which saves unfinished work, and from the scheduled message send row, which sends a composed message later. Cross-check the draft autosave and recovery row and the scheduled message send row |
| Order receipt | Keep a record of a purchase that can be checked later, even after delivery | Order summary, itemised charges, payment method statement, delivery or pickup details, print and download routes, correction route | State every item with its quantity and unit price, then the subtotal, discounts, taxes, shipping, and the final total, so the total can be checked line by line and no charge appears only on the receipt that was not shown before payment. State the payment method used in masked form, never with full details, together with the date of payment and the order reference. State the delivery address and window, or the pickup place and its collection window, beside the items they belong to, so a split order never leaves a parcel without its own delivery details. Keep the receipt reachable from the order itself after delivery, and offer print and download routes that keep the same itemised charges, so the kept copy matches what was paid. Where a detail is wrong, offer a correction route from the receipt itself that states what will change and whether a refund or a further charge follows, before anything is confirmed, rather than sending the person back to checkout to start again. Distinct from the order tracking row, which follows a delivery, and from the billing overview row, which summarises recurring charges. Cross-check the order tracking row and the billing overview row |
| Card freeze | Stop a card from paying at once without cancelling it, and restore it just as fast | Card list, freeze control, frozen-state statement, unfreeze control, replacement route, pending-payment statement | Offer the freeze control on the card view itself, never only in settings, so it is found in the moment it is needed, and confirm the freeze at once with a plain statement of what stops: new payments on that card, including recurring charges where they are also stopped, and what keeps working, such as refunds coming back to the card. Show the frozen state wherever the card appears — in the card list, at checkout, and beside its transactions — so a declined payment is never a surprise, and keep the unfreeze control in the same place as the freeze control, working at once without a waiting period. State plainly what happens to payments already in progress when the freeze starts, and never change a frozen card back to active as a side effect of another action. Where the card is lost rather than only misplaced, offer the replacement route from the frozen card view, keeping the freeze in place until the replacement arrives, and state whether the replacement keeps the same number or a new one. Distinct from the stored payment method expiry warning row, which warns before a stored method stops working, and from the payment failure recovery row, which recovers a payment that already failed. Cross-check the stored payment method selection row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Travel disruption rebooking | Replace a cancelled or badly delayed booking without losing the original terms or accepting a worse journey unseen | Disruption notice, original itinerary, replacement options, side-by-side comparison, confirm-change control, refund and later-date routes, updated documents | State the disruption plainly with its reason where known, and keep the original itinerary visible beside every replacement offered, so the change can be judged rather than trusted. Compare the whole journey, not only the departure time: final arrival, number of connections, change of departure or arrival place, and any price difference, each stated before confirmation, with a price rise or fall shown as a line beside the new total. Hold the original booking until the replacement is confirmed, and never treat viewing an option as accepting it; where an offered replacement is accepted, issue the updated documents at once and state what carries over — baggage, seats, and onward bookings — and what does not. Where no suitable replacement exists, offer the refund route and a later-date route in the same view, with the same comparison facts, rather than a dead end, and keep a record of the disruption, the options offered, and the option accepted for later checking. Distinct from the appointment management row, which moves a single appointment, and from the route planning row, which orders stops before travel. Cross-check the appointment management row and the route planning row |
| Size guide and fit recommendation | Help a person choose a size before buying, using measurements and fit preference rather than the label alone | Size chart, measurement-method statement, unit switch, fit recommendation control, fit preference choice, recommendation statement, exchange route | Place the size guide beside the size selector, never only on a separate page, and state plainly whether each measurement describes the garment or the body it is meant to fit, with the measurement method and the units beside the values, and a switch between units that converts every value in the chart at once. Offer a recommendation only where the person has given measurements or a fit preference, and state what the recommendation rests on — the measurements entered, the fit preference chosen, and the chart used — rather than presenting it as certain, since a label alone never fixes a size across different product families. Keep the recommendation editable: changing a measurement or a preference recalculates it at once, and choosing a different size never hides the guide. Where the size still proves wrong, state the exchange route from the product view before purchase, so a wrong size is a swap rather than a loss. Distinct from the product variant selection row, which chooses among option groups, and from the return and exchange request row, which handles a return after purchase. Cross-check the product variant selection row and the return and exchange request row |
| Virtual try-on | Try a garment, accessory, or cosmetic on the body or face before buying, using the camera or a stored likeness | Try-on entry on the product view, tryable-item marker, live preview, size and colour choice, add-to-cart control, camera permission step, no-camera fallback, in-store mirror mode | Mark every item that can be tried on, and put the try-on entry on the product view where it is found without scrolling; starting a session applies the item at once, never behind a second confirmation. Keep size and colour choice and the add-to-cart control on the try-on screen, so buying never requires leaving the preview to find the product again. Lay out the items to try as a grid rather than a long single strip, and keep instruction text dark on a light ground. Explain the trade before asking for the camera — a photo unlocks a fitted preview — and ask only when the person starts a try-on; where the camera is refused or unavailable, offer manual measurements or a size chart instead of a dead end. In a store mirror, keep the catalogue and the fitting view separate: choose on one side or screen and see the result on the other, one task at a time, with interface chrome at the edges and never over the body or the garment. Keep mid-air gestures low and brief — controls at or below shoulder height, a hold of a couple of seconds to confirm — and offer a touch or phone route beside gestures for people who cannot or do not want to gesture. Mark the starting point on the floor or on screen and show short on-screen guidance, so a first-time user knows where to stand and what to do without staff help. Treat measurement as private: do it in a covered space where one is offered, or let the person measure at home and carry the result into the store, never in an open shop window where passers-by watch. Distinct from the size guide and fit recommendation row, which chooses a size from measurements, and from the code scanner row, which captures a code with the camera. Cross-check the size guide and fit recommendation row, the permission request row, and the code scanner row |
| Shared item access | Let named people open and change a shared item under stated roles | Access list, invite control, role control, pending-invitation state, removal control | Invite people or groups by name to a single item, and state each person's role in plain words — what they can view, add notes to, or change — before the invitation is sent. Show inherited access plainly where a parent container already grants access, so a listed role is never mistaken for the whole access picture. A pending invitation states that it is waiting, offers resend and withdrawal, and grants nothing until accepted. Changing a role states its effect before confirmation, and removing a person states what they lose access to, with the change taking effect at once and confirmed in place. Keep at least one holder able to manage access at all times, and never leave an item with no one able to restore access. Distinct from the share link row, which opens an item through a link rather than a named invitation, and from the household member management row, which manages membership of a household rather than access to one item. Cross-check the share link row and the household member management row |
| Transaction dispute | Dispute a charge from its detail view, with the outcome stated plainly | Dispute action on the transaction, amount and date statement, reason choice, evidence upload, provisional state, outcome notice | Offer the dispute action on the transaction detail itself, never only through a help article, and state the disputed amount and date at the top so the person can confirm they picked the right charge. Offer reason choices in plain words — unrecognised, wrong amount, charged twice, goods or service not received — with a place to add evidence such as a receipt or message, and confirm receipt at once with a reference the person can quote later. While the dispute is reviewed, mark the transaction as disputed and state plainly whether the amount is held, provisionally returned, or still due, with the next step and its timing. Close with a final notice stating what was refunded or upheld and why, and keep the disputed transaction visible with its outcome rather than removing it. Never ask the person to re-prove a dispute already accepted, and never leave a disputed charge looking like an ordinary settled charge. Distinct from the payment failure recovery row, which recovers a payment that already failed, and from the card freeze row, which stops a card from paying. Cross-check the payment failure recovery row and the card freeze row |
| Transaction history | Show every movement of money in and out of an account as a checkable record | Newest-first entry list, per-entry state in words, merchant and amount, search, state and date filters, detail view, export control | List entries newest first, each naming the merchant or counterpart, the date, and the amount in plain words, with the state stated in words — pending, posted, reversed, or disputed — never by colour alone, and keep pending entries visibly separate from settled ones so a balance check never mistakes a charge still clearing for money already moved. Offer search by merchant name, amount, or date, with filters for state, type, and account, and keep the applied filters visible so a narrowed list never reads as the whole record. Open a detail view from any entry showing the payment method, a reference the person can quote, and the dispute route, without crowding the list itself. Mask account and card numbers by default, revealing them only through a deliberate action, and never show a full number in a shared or exported view. Where an export is offered, export exactly the entries the current filters show, in the same order, so the exported record matches what the person checked on screen. Distinct from the transaction dispute row, which disputes a charge already paid, and from the billing overview row, which summarises recurring charges across a period, and from the receipt capture row, which turns a paper receipt into a checked record. Cross-check the transaction dispute row and the billing overview row |
| Habit streak | Show consecutive days a habit was kept, with a humane rule for a missed day | Current count, longest count, calendar of kept days, break-rule statement, recovery control, pause control | Show the current count and the longest count together, with a calendar marking kept days, so a broken run never erases the record of the best run. State the break rule in plain words before the first miss — what counts as keeping the habit, and what breaks the run — and state it again at the moment a run breaks, with the restart offered as a fresh count rather than a failure notice. Offer a recovery route where one exists, such as a single missed-day pass or a pause that holds the count without counting a miss, and state its limit plainly — one pass per week, or a pause of a stated length — so the recovery never reads as unlimited. Never use guilt wording, never hide the pause or recovery control until after the run has broken, and never count a day the person could not act because the service itself was unavailable. Distinct from the activity feed row, which lists past activity, and from the setup checklist row, which tracks one-off setup steps. Cross-check the activity feed row |
| Contactless payment confirmation | Confirm a tap payment at the terminal, with a clear fallback when the tap fails | Amount statement, ready-to-tap cue, success signal, failure signal, receipt route, fallback method choice | Show the amount and the method before the tap, and keep the ready cue visible until the terminal responds, so the person never taps before the amount is entered. Confirm success at once with a signal in more than one channel — a screen statement naming the amount and method, plus a sound or vibration where the terminal offers one — and offer the receipt route immediately, never only in a later statement. When the tap fails, name the likely cause in plain words — moved too soon, reader not ready, method not accepted — and state the next step: hold steady and try again, or switch to another method, without repeating a tap that was already declined. Where the terminal is uncertain, check the transaction state before starting again, so one purchase never becomes two charges. Distinct from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a stored method at checkout, and from the payment failure recovery row, which recovers a failed online payment. Cross-check the saved payment method selection row and the payment failure recovery row |
| PIN entry | Enter a short numeric code at a terminal or on a device without showing or speaking it | Fixed numeric keypad, masked digit indicators, cancel and confirm controls, assisted-entry mode, audio and haptic feedback | Keep the keypad fixed in the telephone layout — 1, 2, 3 / 4, 5, 6 / 7, 8, 9 / Cancel, 0, OK — centred and unmoving, so the layout can be learned by touch and never shifts between attempts. Where the hardware allows, offer a tactile keypad as an alternative, with a raised marker on 5. Never display or announce any digit of the code or the completed code itself; confirm each entered digit only by count, with haptic or audio feedback that marks progress without varying in a way that could identify a digit, and give 5 a distinct feedback so it stands in for the raised marker. State the amount before entry begins. Offer an assisted-entry mode that can be switched on in several ways — a physical button, a double-tap gesture, an on-screen symbol, plugging in headphones, or a stored preference — and that reads the amount, progress, and outcome aloud, offers a repeat of any spoken message, and allows zoom, larger text, higher contrast, and stronger haptic feedback; the mode stays on for the whole transaction and switches off by itself afterwards. Keep entry independent: nobody should have to share the code with another person to complete a payment. Distinct from the verification-code entry row, which handles codes sent to the person, and from the second-factor sign-in row, which covers the wider sign-in step. Cross-check the contactless payment confirmation row and the verification-code entry row |
| Scan-to-pay | Pay by scanning a code shown by the seller, or by showing a personal code for the seller to scan | Scan action, presented-code view, recipient statement, amount entry, review step, fee and total lines, receipt view, full-screen code control | State which mode applies before anything opens: scanning a code the seller shows, or showing a personal code for the seller to scan. Where the code carries a fixed amount, show that amount with the recipient before any confirmation; where it carries none, the person enters the amount after scanning, with the recipient shown first so the amount is never entered blind. Confirm the recipient by name or account before the amount is entered, never after the payment is sent. Show a review step before confirming, with the amount, any fee, and the total as separate lines, and confirm only from that review. Treat the receipt as a distinct state after confirmation, repeating the recipient, amount, fee, and total, so the record survives the review step. A code shown to others stays large and high-contrast on a plain background, and offers a tap to enlarge it to full screen where the display is small, so a small screen never makes the code unreadable. Do not force sign-in before the scan where the payment can be completed without an account; where sign-in is required, say so before the scan starts. Distinct from the code scanner row, which covers capturing any code, and from the contactless payment confirmation row, which confirms a tap at a terminal. Cross-check the code scanner row and the contactless payment confirmation row |
| Fare-capped transit payment | Pay for transit by tapping a card or device, with the fare capped so repeated rides stop charging once the cap for the period is reached | Tap-on cue, tap-off cue, reader success signal, fare statement, cap statement, same-method rule, cap-reached state, missed tap-off warning, payment history route, inspection route | State the cap and the period it covers — a day cap, a week cap — beside the single-ride fare before the first tap, so the person can tell what each tap costs and when rides become free. Charge the single-ride fare on each tap and stop charging once the cap for the period is reached; rides after the cap are free and still need a tap, so each journey is recorded. Require the same card or device for every tap in the period: a different card, or the same card held in a wallet instead of the physical card, counts as a different method and earns no cap or transfer. Where the fare depends on where the journey ends, ask for a tap when leaving as well as when boarding, and state where the tap-off reader stands — by the door, on the platform — before the journey ends. A missed tap-off charges the highest fare for the route and does not count towards the cap; say so plainly and offer a route to correct it. Confirm each tap at once with a signal in more than one channel — a screen statement naming the fare, plus a sound or vibration where the reader offers one — and show the fare charged on the reader after the tap. Where a reduced fare applies, say plainly that tapping charges the full fare and offer the reduced-fare route instead. Keep a payment history the person can check, with each charge and the progress towards the cap, and state that charges appear on the bank or card statement, with any temporary hold explained. Where an inspector checks the journey, the same card or device tapped on the reader proves the fare was paid. Distinct from the contactless payment confirmation row, which confirms a single tap payment at a terminal, and from the pass and ticket display row, which shows a pass already held. Cross-check the contactless payment confirmation row and the pass and ticket display row |
| Automation routine builder | Build a rule that acts on its own when a stated event happens and stated conditions hold | Rule name, trigger list, condition list, all-or-any choice, action list, enable switch, test run, last-run record | Keep triggers, conditions, and actions in three labelled lists, never merged into one list: a trigger is the event that starts the rule, a condition is a fact that must already be true at that moment, and an action is what then happens. Show the whole rule as one plain sentence — when this happens, and while this holds, do this — kept in step with the lists, so a rule is read and checked without opening each field. Where several conditions apply, state plainly whether all of them must hold or any one suffices, with all as the default, and where several actions apply, show their order and whether each runs only if the one before it succeeded. Offer a test run that shows what would happen without acting, and an enable switch that pauses a rule without deleting it, so a seasonal rule can rest and return. Before a rule is enabled, state everything it will affect and flag any action that cannot be undone, such as sending a message to others or changing a lock, and warn where an action could fire its own trigger again, so a loop is caught before it runs. Keep a last-run record naming when the rule last fired, which trigger fired it, whether each condition passed, and which actions ran or failed, so a rule that never fires or fires wrongly can be diagnosed from the record rather than guessed at. Distinct from the home control map row, which controls devices in the moment, and from the saved search alert row, which watches one search without a rule builder. Cross-check the home control map row and the saved search alert row |
| Download manager | Manage a list of files being fetched or already fetched, in one place | Download list, per-item progress, state statement, pause and resume controls, cancel control, retry control, open action, remove control | List every download in one place, newest first, each entry naming the file, its size, and its state in words — waiting, downloading, paused, finished, or failed — with progress shown as a percentage and a size fetched so far, never as a bare bar. Offer pause and resume on each downloading item, keeping what was already fetched, never restarting from the beginning after a pause or an interruption, and state plainly where resumption is not possible so the person is not left expecting it. Offer cancel on an in-progress item and remove on a finished one, with removal freeing storage at once and saying so, and keep a cancelled item out of the finished list. For a failed download, name the cause in plain words where it is known and offer a retry for that item alone, without re-queueing the whole list. A finished download offers an open action from its own entry and stays listed until removed, so a file fetched earlier can be found again without re-fetching. Where several downloads run at once, keep each item's controls with its own entry and state the overall queue order, so pausing one never pauses another by mistake. Distinct from the offline download row, which keeps content for use without a connection, and from the file preview row, which inspects a file before opening it. Cross-check the offline download row and the file preview row |
| Product recall notice | Tell people plainly about a product already bought that must be returned, repaired, or stopped from use, with a remedy kept on record | Affected-item check, risk statement, remedy choice, remedy record, return or disposal route | State which items are affected and how a person checks one — by purchase record, serial or batch identifier, or date range — so nobody guesses whether their item is included. State the risk in plain words, what to do at once, and the remedy offered at no extra cost, with its route and timeframe, and keep a record of the remedy against the order or item. Never bury the notice in a general announcement: reach people who bought the affected item directly where that is possible, and keep the notice reachable from the order itself. Where the scope changes, update the original notice rather than sending a fresh notice that contradicts it. Distinct from the service status notice row, which reports availability, and from the warranty claim row, which starts a claim for a fault. Cross-check the service status notice row and the warranty claim row |
| Account merge | Bring two accounts held by the same person into one, with a preview of what moves, what is kept, and what is lost | Account choice, preview of moves and losses, conflict choice, undo window statement, sign-in continuity statement | Show a preview before confirmation naming what moves from each account, what is kept from each where both hold the same kind of record, and what is lost, so nothing disappears by surprise. Where the same kind of record conflicts, offer a choice per kind rather than picking silently, and state which sign-in continues afterwards and which stops working. Keep both accounts usable until the merge is confirmed, and state plainly where the merge can still be undone and where it cannot. After the merge, confirm what moved and what was lost in one place, and keep a route back where undo is offered. Distinct from the account switcher row, which moves between accounts, and from the account deletion row, which closes an account. Cross-check the account switcher row and the account deletion row |
| Loyalty points expiry warning | Warn before points expire, with a route to use them from the warning itself | Amount at risk, exact expiry date, smallest saving use, use-from-warning action, balance-view repeat | State the amount at risk and the exact expiry date in the warning itself, together with the smallest use that saves the points, and offer a route to use them from the warning without hunting for it. Repeat the warning in the balance view, and state the rule plainly at enrolment — whether points expire after a set time or after inactivity, and whether activity restarts the countdown. Send the warning early enough to act on, and stop warning once the points are used or have expired, rather than repeating a warning for points already gone. Distinct from the loyalty rewards row, which earns and spends points, and from the trial-end reminder row, which warns before a charge. Cross-check the loyalty rewards row and the trial-end reminder row |
| High-demand queue | Hold a place in line when demand exceeds capacity, and enter in turn when a place opens | Queue position, wait estimate, place-kept statement, leave action, entry window | State the person's position in line and a wait estimate that is revised as the line moves, never a fixed time that cannot be kept. Keep the place through a brief interruption, such as a dropped connection or a page reload, and state how long the place is held. Offer a plain route out at any point, and release the place at once when the person leaves, so the next person in line is not kept waiting. When a place opens, state the entry window plainly and what happens when it passes, and do not move a person backward in line without stating why. Distinct from the waitlist row, which watches one item for a later opening, and from the appointment management row, which manages a booked time. |
| QR sign-in | Sign in on a screen with awkward input by approving on a phone already signed in | Code or QR display, approval prompt on the phone, typed-code fallback, expiry statement, cancel control | Show the sign-in choice on the awkward-input screen beside a typed sign-in route, never as the only option, and state in plain words what scanning or entering the code will do before any code appears. On the approving phone, name the account and the device asking to sign in, and require an explicit approval there, so a code shown to the wrong person or on a shared screen never signs anyone in by itself. Offer a typed short code beside the QR code for a phone without a camera or where scanning fails, and state the code's expiry plainly, with a fresh code on request and a cancel control that ends the attempt on both screens. Never sign in on the first scan alone where approval is still pending, and never reuse a code after it has been used or has expired. Where the approving phone is not signed in, offer its own sign-in first rather than failing the attempt. Distinct from the passkey sign-in row, which signs in with a device-held credential, and from the code scanner row, which reads codes for content rather than for sign-in. Cross-check the passkey sign-in row and the second-factor sign-in row |
| Price history display | Show past prices for one item, with their dates, beside the current price | History chart, date-range choice, price-change list, data table alternative, sale label rule, history-range statement | Show past prices in date order for one item only, never mixing different variants or conditions in a single line, so a cheaper price for a different condition never makes the item look cheaper than it is. Offer a date-range choice and state plainly how far back the history reaches and where it is incomplete, rather than drawing a flat line through a gap as if the price had stayed steady. Offer the same prices as a table beside any chart, with each change dated and the current price marked, so the history can be checked without reading a chart. Mark the current price as a sale only where a higher price genuinely applied before, naming that earlier price and the dates it applied, never a price that was never charged. Distinct from the price-drop alert row, which watches for a future fall, and from the converted-price display row, which restates a price in a second currency. Cross-check the price-drop alert row and the data visualisation row |
