| Delivery reschedule | Change the delivery window of a placed order from the order itself | Reschedule action on the order, window list, cut-off statement, fee-difference statement, confirmation control, updated tracking view | Offer rescheduling from the order itself while the order can still change, and state plainly on the order the cut-off after which the window can no longer change, so nobody starts a change that cannot succeed. Offer only windows still open for the order, re-checked at confirmation, and where a chosen window fills before confirmation completes, say so at once and offer the nearest open window rather than failing the change. State any fee difference before confirmation, as an extra charge or a refund with its route, and never apply a change silently. Once confirmed, carry the new window into the tracking view at once, with the old window kept visible as a changed record, so the order never looks unchanged. Where rescheduling is no longer possible, state that plainly on the order with the reason and the nearest alternative, rather than hiding the control. Distinct from the delivery slot picker row, which chooses a window at checkout, and from the order editing row, which changes the order itself. Cross-check the delivery slot picker row and the order tracking row |
| Loyalty tier progress | Show the current tier and the progress toward the next tier, with the next tier benefits beside the gap | Current-tier statement, progress figure, gap figure, next-tier benefit list, keep-or-lose rule, tier-change notice | State the current tier in words, with progress toward the next tier in plain figures — what has been counted and how much remains — never as a bar or colour alone. List the next tier benefits beside the gap, so the value of closing it is plain before any further action, and state the rule for reaching the tier, including whether progress counts over a fixed period or a rolling window. State plainly how the tier is kept or lost — what must be done by when, and what happens on falling short — and warn before a tier is lost, with the remaining requirement and its deadline in the warning itself. When a tier changes, state the new tier, when it takes effect, and which benefits change, rather than silently swapping the label. Distinct from the loyalty rewards row, which earns and spends a points balance, and from the loyalty points expiry warning row, which warns before points expire. Cross-check the loyalty rewards row and the loyalty points expiry warning row |
| Recipe serving scaling | Change how many servings a recipe makes and have every ingredient amount adjust to match | Serving-count control, original-serving statement, scaled ingredient list, scale-factor statement, pinned-quantity mark, print route | State the original serving count beside the control, and show the chosen count and the factor applied, so a scaled amount can be checked against the original at a glance. Multiply every scalable quantity by the chosen count divided by the original count, and round the result to a usable kitchen measure, marking any rounded amount so a rounded value is never mistaken for an exact one. Keep quantities that must not scale — a fixed item, a whole piece of equipment, or a stated count — unchanged, and mark each one as unchanged in words, so the list never looks fully scaled when part of it is not. State plainly where seasoning and leavening do not scale in proportion, and advise tasting and adjusting rather than applying the full scaled amount blindly. Carry the chosen serving count into the printed list, so the printed amounts match the amounts shown on screen, and offer a one-action return to the original count. Distinct from the product variant selection row, which picks a buyable combination. Cross-check the product variant selection row |
| Cooking mode | Follow a recipe step by step while cooking, with hands busy and attention divided | Single active step, large type, visible progress, previous and next controls, quantities beside the instruction, labelled timers, wake control, persisted step and timer state | Show one step at a time in large type readable at a distance, with progress visible and previous and next controls that always work, so the cook never loses their place. Keep each quantity in the instruction itself or one tap away, and support several timers at once, each labelled with what it times. Keep the screen awake only while cooking is in progress and say so plainly, so the benefit is clear. Save the current step and every timer on the device, and restore them after the app is backgrounded or the device sleeps, so an interruption never restarts the recipe. Where voice control is offered, confirm before any voice action changes a timer or moves a step, and always keep a touch route for the same action. Distinct from the recipe serving scaling row, which adjusts ingredient amounts for a different serving count. Cross-check the recipe serving scaling row |
| Reduced-motion preference | Honour a stated preference for less movement on every screen | Preference signal, reduced-motion variant of each animated element, feedback-preserving state change, autoplay control | Read the preference from the system setting where one is available, and offer an in-product control beside it where the product carries its own motion settings, so the preference is set once and respected everywhere without re-asking on each screen. Replace large, continuous, or self-starting movement — parallax, full-screen slides, zooming transitions, spinning indicators, and autoplaying loops — with instant or faded changes, and keep brief state feedback so an action still shows it happened: a control still changes visibly, a message still arrives with a readable notice, and a panel still opens in its final state. Never carry essential information by motion alone: a status shown only by a pulsing or sliding element gets a word or shape beside it. Apply the preference mid-session when it changes, not only at load, and keep smooth scrolling off for those who asked for less movement, jumping straight to the target instead. Where movement is essential to the information itself, offer a still alternative that states the same result in words. Distinct from the functional motion trend file, which describes when motion helps, and from the accessibility preferences row, which gathers preference controls in one place. Cross-check the functional motion trend file and the accessibility preferences row |
| Archive conversation | Move a finished conversation out of the main list without deleting it | Archive control, archive list, restore control, new-message rule statement, search inclusion | Offer archiving from the conversation list and from inside the conversation, and confirm in place where the conversation went, naming the archive location, so a conversation that leaves the list never looks deleted. Keep every message, attachment, and read state intact in the archive, and keep archived conversations findable by search, so the archive is a filing place, not a second deletion. State plainly what a new message does: whether it returns the conversation to the main list at once or leaves it archived with a notice, and never let a new message silently change the rule. Offer restore as one action from the archive itself, returning the conversation to its place in the main list ordered by its latest message, with nothing changed inside it. Where bulk archiving is offered, state how many conversations it covers and offer the same bulk restore, so a tidy-up never strands a batch. Distinct from the message edit and delete row, which changes or removes single messages, and from the saved-item collection row, which keeps chosen items. Cross-check the message edit and delete row and the chat and messaging row |
| In-conversation message search | Find a message inside one conversation without losing the conversation | Search control in the conversation header, match counter, previous and next controls, highlighted matches, latest-message route | Offer search from the conversation header or its menu, so the search stays scoped to the conversation in view and never silently widens to other conversations. Search the whole conversation, not only the messages already loaded, loading earlier messages as the search runs, so an older message is never missed because it was off screen. Show the match count as the current match over the total, highlight every match, and mark the current match in words or shape as well as colour, so it reads without colour alone. Previous and next controls step through matches in order and are inactive at the ends, with the end stated plainly rather than wrapping silently. Offer a plain route back to the latest message that also clears the search, and closing the search returns to the position held before searching, never jumping to the latest message uninvited. A search with no matches states the query and offers a changed query or a clear action, never a blank list. Distinct from the find-in-page row, which searches a page or document, and from the search history row, which returns to a past query. Cross-check the find-in-page row and the chat and messaging row |
| Expiring payment method replacement | Replace a stored payment method before it expires, from the warning itself, carrying the replacement into every use | Warning with replacement action, replacement method form, affected-uses list, effective-date statement, cannot-move statement, replacement confirmation | Offer replacement from the warning itself, so the warning is an action rather than a notice that leaves the person hunting for the control. Before confirming, list every subscription and saved checkout that used the old method, and let the person confirm the replacement for each one, so nothing is carried over silently and nothing is missed. State the date from which the new method is charged, and keep the old method usable until that date, never removing it before the replacement takes effect. Where a payment cannot move — for example a charge already in progress — state that plainly beside the affected item with its own route, rather than claiming the replacement covers everything. On confirmation, list what moved and what did not, and stop warning about the replaced method at once. Distinct from the stored payment method expiry warning row, which warns before expiry, and from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a method at checkout. Cross-check the stored payment method expiry warning row and the saved payment method selection row |
| Supervised usage limits | Let a guardian set daily time limits and quiet hours for a supervised account, with essential apps always available | Daily limit control, per-app or category limit, downtime schedule, always-allowed list, remaining-time display, limit-reached state, extension request | Set limits as a daily total and as per-app or per-category totals, and count a per-app limit inside the daily total, never on top of it, so the two together never promise more time than the day allows. State a downtime schedule by day, with the always-allowed apps named before downtime starts, so essentials stay reachable while the rest is paused. Show remaining time in words in the supervised account's own view, warn before a limit is reached, and at the limit block only the limited apps, never the route to help or to the guardian. When a limit is reached, offer one extension request that states the extra time asked for, and let the guardian's decision state its scope — once, or until a stated time — rather than stretching silently. Keep limit and downtime changes in the guardian-only area, and state when a change takes effect. Distinct from the usage allowance row, which tracks a recurring service allowance, and from the guardian approval request row, which approves one item at a time. Cross-check the usage allowance row and the guardian approval request row |
| Stored payment method label | Give each stored payment method a plain name chosen by the person, shown wherever the method is listed | Label edit control, method list display, type and last-digits display, duplicate-label note, checkout method choice | Offer naming where stored methods are managed, and show the chosen label beside the method type and last digits wherever methods appear — in the stored list, at checkout, and in failure notices — so two methods of the same type are told apart without opening either. Until the person names a method, show its type and last digits as the label, never a blank or a bare number alone. Renaming changes only the label, never the method itself, and takes effect at once everywhere the method is listed, without asking for the details again. Where two methods share a label, keep the type and last digits beside the label so the pair stays distinct, and say so plainly rather than forcing a unique name. In a failure notice, name the method by its label so the person knows at once which method failed and which one to fix or replace. Distinct from the saved payment method selection row, which chooses a method at checkout, and from the expiring payment method replacement row, which carries a replacement into every use. Cross-check the saved payment method selection row and the expiring payment method replacement row |
| Stored payment method removal | Remove a stored payment method with the effect on every use stated before confirming | Removal control, affected-uses list, re-point choice, confirmation naming the method, removal result statement | Before confirming, list every subscription and saved checkout that used the method, and state plainly for each what happens — kept on another method, re-pointed to a chosen replacement, or left without a method — so nothing is left to fail silently at the next charge. Name the method in the confirmation by its label with its type and last digits, never by full details, and require an explicit confirmation, since removal cannot be undone by re-adding the same details alone where a fresh setup is required. Where the method is the only method for a subscription, require a replacement or a plain choice to end automatic renewal before removal completes, rather than leaving the subscription to fail at renewal. On removal, state at once what was removed, what was re-pointed, and what was left without a method, and stop showing the method in checkout and in saved lists. Where removal is blocked because a charge is in progress, state that plainly with a route to retry after the charge settles, rather than silently keeping the method. Distinct from the expiring payment method replacement row, which swaps a method before expiry, and from the backup payment method row, which keeps a second method ready for a failed charge. Cross-check the expiring payment method replacement row and the saved payment method selection row |
| Conversation export | Take a copy of one conversation out as a file, with the scope stated before it starts | Export control, scope choice, media choice, format choice, size warning, progress indicator, download link, sensitivity warning | State the scope before starting: one conversation or a date range within it, and what the copy includes and leaves out — text, media, voice messages, files, and any messages already deleted or already expired, which never appear. Offer a choice to include media or leave it out, with a size warning where media makes the file large, and where media is left out, mark each omitted attachment in place rather than silently dropping it. The export is a copy, not a move: the conversation stays where it was and keeps receiving new messages, and the file is a snapshot of the moment it was made, never updated later. Warn plainly that the exported file is a plain readable copy, not protected in the same way as the conversation inside the product, so the person keeps it somewhere safe and shares it only deliberately. On a partial failure, name what exported and what did not and offer a retry for the missing part only, never a silent short file. Distinct from the data export row, which exports account data in bulk, and from the transcript editing row, which corrects a recorded transcript. Cross-check the data export row |
| Appointment reminder | Remind before a booked appointment with everything needed to attend or change it | Reminder schedule, reminder content, confirm control, reschedule control, cancel control, quiet-hours rule, stale-reminder suppression | Carry the time with its timezone, the place or join link, the duration, and anything to bring or prepare, in the reminder itself, with one primary action — confirm, reschedule, or cancel — so the person never has to hunt for the booking to act on it. Send ahead of the appointment, typically a day before and again a few hours before, adjusted to the lead time: never send a reminder whose moment has already passed at booking time, and never send a reminder for a booking that has since been rescheduled or cancelled — re-check that the booking is current before sending, and stop reminders for the old time at once when it changes. Respect quiet hours in the person's timezone, holding a reminder until morning or skipping it where the appointment starts before then, and keep sensitive reasons for the visit out of the message where the service is sensitive. Rescheduling from a reminder keeps the original booking until the new slot is confirmed, and cancelling from a reminder states the consequence at once and stops further reminders. After a missed appointment, offer a rebooking route rather than another reminder for the missed time. Distinct from the appointment management row, which changes a booking already held, and from the notification digest row, which groups low-urgency updates. Cross-check the appointment management row |
| Appointment preparation checklist | Show what to bring, do, and expect before a booked appointment, in one list with the booking and again in the reminder | Preparation list, per-item state, time-gated item statement, progress count, blocked-step route, completed-items view | Show one short list with the booking and again in the reminder, grouping what to bring, what to do, and what to expect, so the person never has to gather the same preparation from several messages. Each item is checkable, with its state — done, remaining, or not yet available — stated in words as well as in shape, and a count of done items against the total so progress is plain at a glance. Items that cannot be done yet stay visible but greyed out, with the date they become available stated beside them, rather than vanishing until the day arrives. Where a step cannot be completed — a form that will not submit, a document that will not upload — state the reason plainly and offer a route to finish it another way, such as completing it on arrival, rather than leaving the appointment blocked. Completed items collapse into a view that can be reopened, so the list stays short while nothing is lost. Send the list soon after booking and repeat the remaining items in the reminder, with reminders growing more frequent as the appointment nears for anything still undone. Distinct from the Appointment reminder row, which reminds before one booked time, and from the Travel document checklist row, which checks documents before travel. Cross-check the Appointment reminder row and the Setup checklist row |
| Appointment check-in | Confirm arrival for a booked appointment from the booking or its reminder | Check-in control, arrival window statement, queue position display, late-arrival route, next-step statement | Offer check-in from the booking itself and from its reminder, never from a separate screen alone, and state the arrival window separately from the service start, so arriving early or late is judged against the window, not the start time. On check-in, confirm at once with the service, place, and start time restated, and show a visible queue position or wait estimate where a queue applies, updating it as the position changes rather than leaving a stale number on screen. State early, on-time, and late arrival plainly: an early arrival waits until the window opens or the provider accepts it, an on-time arrival holds its place ahead of walk-ins for the same service, and a late arrival is marked late with a plain choice — wait in a standby place, reschedule, or ask staff — never silently dropped and never silently moved ahead of others. After check-in, give one plain next step — where to wait, what to bring, or what happens next — and keep a route back to the booking for changes. Where check-in fails, state the reason and offer a staff route at once, never a dead end. Distinct from the appointment management row, which changes a booking already held, and from the appointment reminder row, which reminds before the appointment. Cross-check the appointment management row and the appointment reminder row |
| Shoppable video | Buy a product shown in a video without leaving the player | Timed product tags, in-player product card, variant choice, add-to-cart action, availability statement, post-play product list | Show a tag only while its product is on screen, and name the product and its price in text, never by shape or colour alone. Tapping a tag opens the product card over or beside the player without leaving the video: playback pauses while the card is open and resumes at the same point when it closes, so the person never loses their place. The card shows the image, name, price, variant groups, and availability together, and adding to the cart confirms in place while the person stays in the video. Where a variant sells out during viewing, state that in the card before the add action is used, never after a failed add, and offer the nearest available variant where one exists. After the video ends, list every tagged product in one place rather than starting another purchase prompt on its own. Make every tag reachable by keyboard in the order it appears, each named for its product, and keep the card and the post-play list readable by assistive technology. Distinct from the product variant selection row, which chooses a combination on a product page, from the live broadcast chat overlay row, which shows messages over a live picture, and from the carousel row, which rotates peer slides. Cross-check the product variant selection row and the live broadcast chat overlay row |
| Aggregate rating display | Show a read-only rating summary for one item at a glance | Average score, review count, rating scale, inclusion statement, verified-rating filter | Show the average with its scale and the total number of ratings beside it, so an average never stands alone. State plainly which ratings count — verified purchases only, all ratings, or a stated date range — and keep that scope visible wherever the summary appears, so the same item never shows different averages without explanation. Where a verified mark exists, offer a verified-only view beside the full view, with the count for each stated, and never merge ratings from different items or unrelated sources into one unexplained number. Where no ratings exist yet, say so plainly rather than showing a zero average that reads as a poor rating. Keep the summary read-only: collecting a rating belongs to the submission row, and the spread across levels belongs to the breakdown row. Distinct from the Rating distribution summary row, which breaks the spread down by rating level, and from the Rating and review submission row, which collects a rating. Cross-check the Rating distribution summary row |
| Accessibility labels on a store listing | Show which accessibility features an app supports, on its store listing, before download | Label set, fixed label wording and order, per-device support statement, undeclared-support statement, accessibility details link, search relevance | List each supported feature by name in plain words — screen reader, voice control, larger text, dark interface, distinction without colour alone, sufficient contrast, reduced motion, captions, and audio descriptions — so the list reads as features, not as badges. Claim a feature only where a person can complete every common task with it on: the app's main tasks, first launch, sign-in, purchase, and settings, on that device. Declare support per device, since a feature offered on one device may not be offered on another, and show the labels for the device viewing the listing. Where support has not been declared, say so plainly rather than leaving the section blank, and state plainly where the app supports none of the listed features. Link from the section to one page that explains the app's accessibility features, how to turn them on, and which parts of the app they do not cover. Keep the labels accurate and current: a claim that does not match the app is corrected at once, and a change in support is reflected in the labels without delay. Treat the labels as part of search: a person searching by a feature should find apps that declare it. Use one fixed set of labels with the same wording and order on every listing, so the same feature is always found under the same name and labels can be compared across listings. Claim a label only against the published criteria for that feature, tested on every device the app supports, and keep the claim honest: a feature that works only on some screens is not claimed. Where a feature is not relevant to what the app does, do not claim it, and where support is partial, say which parts it does not cover on the linked accessibility page. Distinct from the accessibility preferences row, which sets display and motion options inside the app, and from the aggregate rating display row, which summarises ratings on the same listing. Cross-check the accessibility preferences row |
| Recurring appointment series | Book and change a repeating appointment as one series | Series rule statement, upcoming occurrence list, edit-scope choice, changed-occurrence mark, pause control, end-series control | State the series rule in plain words — every Tuesday, monthly on the third Thursday, ending after a stated count or on a stated date — and list the upcoming occurrences, with any changed occurrence marked as changed so it is never mistaken for a regular one. On any edit or cancellation, ask plainly what the change covers: this occurrence only, this occurrence and all future ones, or the whole series, and confirm the scope before applying it. Changing one occurrence never rewrites the others, and changing the series never silently overwrites a changed occurrence; where a series change conflicts with a changed occurrence, state the conflict and ask which wins. Past and completed occurrences never change. Pausing means skipping a stated date range while the series continues after it; ending a series stops future occurrences only and keeps past ones. Offer a restore route that returns a changed occurrence to the series rule. Distinct from the Appointment management row, which changes a single booking, and from the Appointment reminder row, which reminds before one appointment. Cross-check the Appointment management row |
| Travel document checklist | Check before travel that every document the trip needs is ready | Document list per traveller and destination, expiry check per document, renewal route, blocking-warning statement, offline copy | List every document the trip needs for each traveller and each country on the route, including countries passed through in transit, and check each document against the trip dates rather than against today alone. Flag any document that expires during the trip or inside the validity margin the destination requires, and offer the renewal or replacement route from the flagged item with its processing time stated, so a slow renewal is caught early. Where a missing document blocks boarding, say so plainly on the item itself, never as a silent missing tick. Keep the checked list reachable without a connection, and re-check close to departure, since entry rules change and a document that was valid at booking may no longer suffice. Never present a document as a guarantee of entry: it permits travel to the border, where entry itself is decided. Distinct from the Travel check-in row, which confirms travel for a booked trip, and from the Pass and ticket display row, which presents a pass already held. Cross-check the Travel check-in row |
| Travel itinerary view | Show a trip day by day, with each day's bookings, places, and travel legs in order | Day headings, ordered day list, booking cards, travel legs, overnight-stay marker, change notice, offline copy | Group the trip by day in the order it will be lived, with each day's bookings, places, and travel legs in time order and each time shown in the local time of its place with its zone stated. Put the current day first with the next item at the top, followed by the rest of that day, then a short look at tomorrow, and mark the overnight stay at the end of each day so the day's route ends where the person sleeps. Where a change affects one day, state the change plainly on that day and name every affected booking, never silently moving a booking to another day. Days with nothing planned say so plainly rather than vanishing. Keep the whole itinerary reachable without a connection, since coverage fails on the road. Distinct from the Travel document checklist row, which checks documents before travel, and from the Travel check-in row, which confirms travel for a booked trip. Cross-check the Travel check-in row |
| Bill explanation | Explain one bill line by line so every charge can be checked | Charge lines with dates, quantity and unit price, discount lines, credit lines, fee lines, subtotal and total, per-line dispute route | Give every charge its own line, named in plain words and dated, with quantity and unit price where they apply, so a total never stands unexplained. Show any discount, credit, or fee as its own line, never folded silently into another line, and make the lines add up visibly to the subtotal and the total. Where a line differs from what was agreed, explain the difference on the line itself. Offer a dispute route from any line that looks wrong, carrying that line's name, date, and amount into the dispute so nothing is retyped, and issue a corrected bill when a dispute is upheld rather than quietly changing the original. Distinct from the Billing overview row, which summarises recurring charges across a period, and from the Transaction dispute row, which disputes a charge already paid. Cross-check the Billing overview row |
| Appointment late-cancellation and no-show policy | State, before booking, what late cancellation and a missed appointment cost and how to cancel late | Cancellation deadline statement, late-cancellation fee, no-show fee, late-cancel route, confirmation repeat, prepayment statement | State the cancellation deadline, the fee for a late cancellation, and the fee for a missed appointment before booking, in the same numbers and words everywhere they appear, and repeat the policy at confirmation and in the reminder. Keep the late-cancel route working after the deadline, with the fee stated before confirming, so a late cancellation is never a dead end. State plainly what happens to any prepayment on a late cancellation and on a no-show — kept, moved to a new booking, or refunded — and offer a rebooking route after either outcome. Where a fee is waived, state the waiver and its reason rather than silently dropping the charge. Distinct from the Appointment management row, which changes or cancels a booking, and from the Appointment reminder row, which reminds before one appointment. Cross-check the Appointment management row |
| Shared trip itinerary | Share one trip plan with travelling companions so every person sees the same days, bookings, and changes | Shared trip view, per-person access level, change notice, proposal state, stop-sharing control, personal copy | State each person's access before sharing: view-only, which lets a person see the trip without changing it, or editable, which lets a person change days, bookings, and picks, while only the trip owner changes who can see the trip. Show the same live trip to every companion, with the current day first and the next item at the top, and when one person changes a day, state the change on that day and name every affected booking, so no companion keeps reading an old plan. Where a suggestion is not yet decided, mark it as a proposal with who suggested it and what joining it costs that person, and only count a person toward a shared cost for the proposals they joined, never toward a group average. Keep sensitive details out of a view-only link: show dates, places, and links, but hide confirmation numbers and live status unless the recipient is a named companion. Offer a stop-sharing control that takes effect at once, states what each person keeps, and leaves every companion with their own copy of the trip as it stood when sharing stopped, rather than deleting the trip from their view without warning. Distinct from the Travel itinerary view row, which shows one person's trip, and from the Saved collection sharing row, which shares saved items rather than a trip. Cross-check the Travel itinerary view row and the Saved collection sharing row |
| Emergency assistance request | Ask for help fast from wherever the person is, with the request itself carrying what responders need | One-action request control, help-type choice, shared location, cancel window, receipt statement, stage states, detail-after-request route | Offer the request as one action reachable from wherever the person is, including from a locked screen where the platform allows it, and never hide it behind sign-in, a menu, or a form. The request states what kind of help is needed — emergency, medical, or family — and shares the person's location at once, so nothing essential waits for a second step. Give a short cancel window after sending, so an accidental request is stopped before it reaches anyone, and state plainly what happens in that window. Once sent, show a receipt stating what was sent, to whom, and when, and show each stage separately — sent, received, accepted — since a sent request is not a received one and a received one is not an accepted one. Let the person add essential details after the request, such as what happened or who is with them, rather than asking before it. Where the request cannot be sent, say so at once with a stated retry route and keep the request visible, never showing an empty or silent state. Keep the control large, labelled in words as well as in shape, and operable without precise aiming or reading small text. Distinct from the automated-to-human support handoff row, which moves a support conversation to a person, and from the service status notice row, which reports an outage. Cross-check the automated-to-human support handoff row and the location selection row |
| Booking deposit | Take a deposit at booking that counts toward the price, with the refund rule stated before payment | Deposit amount, secured-item statement, refund-rule statement, remaining-balance statement, due-date statement, deposit receipt | State the deposit amount, what it secures, and the refund rule for on-time cancellation, late cancellation, and a no-show before payment is taken, using one term throughout — deposit, booking fee, or retainer — and define it in plain words where it first appears. Say plainly that the deposit counts toward the price and is not an extra charge on top of it. Show the remaining balance and its due date beside the deposit at booking and again on the confirmation, so the person never has to work out what is still owed. Where a cancellation arrives in time, move the deposit to a new date or refund it and say which; where it arrives late or the person does not attend, state that the deposit is kept and say so in the same place the rule was shown. Keep one cancellation window and apply it the same way every time, and where a refund is sent, state that it is on its way and how long it takes to appear. Distinct from the Appointment late-cancellation and no-show policy row, which states fees, and from the Payment instalment plan row, which spreads a total. Cross-check the Appointment late-cancellation and no-show policy row and the Payment instalment plan row |
| Deposit refund status | Show plainly where a deposit refund stands after a cancellation | Refund amount, destination statement, status statement, expected-arrival statement, delay route | State the refund amount, where it is going — back to the original payment method or into stored credit — and how long it takes to appear, in the same place the cancellation was confirmed, so the person never has to ask. Show the status in plain words — requested, sent, or received — and update it as the refund moves, rather than leaving one unchanging label. Where only part of the deposit returns, state the amount refunded and the amount kept, with the reason. Where the refund is delayed past the stated window, say so at once with the reason where known and the next step — wait longer, check the statement, or contact support — rather than leaving the person to guess whether it failed. Where a refund fails, state the reason plainly and offer the retry from the same place, and once a refund is complete, disable the refund action so it cannot be sent twice, while keeping the record visible with its amount, destination, and date. Distinct from the Booking deposit row, which states the refund rule before payment, and from the Transaction dispute row, which disputes a charge already paid. Cross-check the Booking deposit row and the Transaction dispute row |
| Appointment standby list | Hold a place for a booking when no slot is free, and offer the next freed slot in turn | Standby join control, place statement, offer window, one-action accept, expiry statement, leave-list control | Offer the standby list where the booking flow would otherwise end with no slot free, and join the person from that same flow without asking them to start again. State the person's place in the list and what it means — earliest joined is offered first — and state the order rule before anyone joins, so nobody has to guess why another person was offered a slot first. Ask for the details a match needs at joining: the service, the place, the practitioner where one matters, the days and times that work, and how much notice the person needs, so an offer never goes to someone who cannot take it. When a slot opens, offer it to the first matching person with a stated window to accept — long enough to check a message and reply, short enough that the slot does not sit empty — and book the slot with one action from the offer itself. Where the window passes without an answer, state plainly that the offer expired, keep the person on the list for the next slot, and never close the request after one declined or expired offer. Let the person leave the list at any time and say what leaving means for any pending offer. Distinct from the Waitlist row, which watches one item for restock, and from the Appointment management row, which changes a booked time. Cross-check the Waitlist row and the Appointment management row |
| Medication reminder | Remind the person to take each dose on time, in plain words, and keep a visible record of what was taken and missed | Dose reminder, plain-word dose name, schedule, snooze control, taken and missed marks, adherence history, schedule-change route, sharing rule | Name each dose in plain words with its amount and time — the name as written on the prescription, the amount, and when to take it — and never use a code or abbreviation the person has to decode. Offer a stated schedule that fits real days: chosen weekdays, different times on different days, and a route for as-needed doses, with a snooze that delays the reminder rather than cancelling it. Let the person mark a dose taken or missed from the reminder itself in one action, and keep a visible history of taken and missed doses so a missed dose is plain to see and a double dose is never taken by mistake. Where a dose is missed, say what to do next — take it, skip it, or check the written instructions — and never suggest taking two doses at once. Offer a plain route to change the schedule without losing the history, and state the rule for what is shared and with whom before anything leaves the device. Keep reminders quiet enough to live with: let the person choose the channel, the tone, and the frequency, and never use guilt or pressure to push adherence. Distinct from the Appointment reminder row, which reminds before one booked time, and from the Habit streak row, which counts consecutive days. Cross-check the Appointment reminder row and the Habit streak row |
| Medication refill request | Ask for a refill before the supply runs out, stating how much is left and when it runs out | Refillable prescription list, remaining-supply statement, run-out date, multi-select, code scan, delivery-or-pickup choice, confirm step, request state, delivery tracking, refill history | Show only prescriptions that can still be refilled in the request list, and keep the ones that cannot be refilled visible in the history with the reason stated plainly, so the person is never left guessing why a refill is refused. State how much is left and the date the supply runs out beside each prescription, and offer the request in good time before that date, allowing for processing. Let the person choose several prescriptions at once, with a select-all control, or identify one by scanning the code on its label. Ask for a delivery or pickup choice with the request, and confirm the request in a separate step that states what was requested and where it will go. Give every request a plain state — sent, confirmed, or ready — and show delivery tracking where delivery was chosen, so the person can tell whether the request is still being processed or is ready to collect. Keep a history of past requests with their outcomes, and never present a submitted request as ready until the supply is confirmed as available. Distinct from the Medication reminder row, which reminds the person to take a dose, and from the Reorder past order row, which repeats a purchase already made. Cross-check the Medication reminder row and the Reorder past order row |
| Recurring payment setup | Authorise future payments on a stated schedule, kept apart from a one-time payment | One-time and recurring choice, amount and date statement, payment-method statement, authorisation summary, pause, change, and cancel controls, pre-charge notice, failed-charge notice, method-change rule | Keep the recurring setup in its own flow, separate from the one-time payment, so the two are never confused and a one-time payment never silently becomes a recurring one. Before authorisation, state the amount, the date of the first charge and the repeat schedule, and the method that will be charged, in plain words on one summary the person confirms. Offer pause, change, and cancel from the setup itself and from the account area, taking effect before the next charge's cutoff, and state on each control which charge it applies to where the cutoff has passed. Send a plain notice before each charge and another after a failed charge, naming the amount, the date, and the next step, so a charge is never a surprise and a failure never goes unnoticed. Where the payment method changes or expires, state plainly whether future charges move to the new method or stop, and never let a charge fail silently for want of an updated method. A cancelled setup stops future charges at once and keeps the record of past charges visible. Distinct from the Billing overview row, which summarises recurring charges, and from the Subscription plan change row, which changes what is paid for. Cross-check the Billing overview row and the Subscription plan change row |
| Verified account badge | Show that an account has been checked, and say what was checked | Badge beside the name, checked-item statement, verification detail view, request route, removal rule | Show the badge only once the check is confirmed, never before and never on a cached or self-declared claim, so an unverified account never carries the mark. Keep the badge small beside the name, in the same place everywhere the name appears, and never let the person add it to their own account — only the check itself earns it. Opening the badge states in plain words what was checked and when, and where there are levels of check, the badge names the level rather than showing one mark for every level. Never use the badge to imply a guarantee, an endorsement, or a promise about future behaviour; it states only that the check was passed. Where the check expires, state the expiry before it lapses and offer a renewal route, and where the check no longer holds, remove the badge at once rather than leaving a stale mark. Distinct from the profile and account menu row, which shows who is signed in, and from the second-factor sign-in row, which proves the person at sign-in rather than marking the account. Cross-check the profile and account menu row |
| Virtual background in video calls | Hide or replace what is behind the person on a video call | Background choice, blur control, replacement image choice, preview, per-device setting, fallback | Offer background blur as the plain default: it hides detail without a fake scene and works in most light. Preview the effect before joining, showing the whole frame, so the person sees exactly what others will see. State plainly that blur hides detail, not movement or readable text, and that edges can glitch — move truly sensitive items out of frame rather than trusting the effect to hide them. Offer a still replacement image over a moving one for long calls, keep busy or high-contrast images out of serious calls, and never apply a replacement the person did not choose. Keep the choice per device, since a setting on one device does not carry to another, and keep it changeable mid-call with one action. Where the device cannot run the effect, say so at once and offer camera-off instead of a broken effect. Distinct from the live call controls row, which governs microphone, camera, and sharing toggles, and from the recording consent row, which governs capture. Cross-check the live call controls row |
| World clock | Show the time in several places at once, with the day and working hours plain | City list, local time and date, offset statement, day-or-night mark, working-hours band, overlap finder, share control | Show each place with its local time and its date, and the offset from the person's own time in plain figures, so no mental arithmetic is needed and a date change across the line is never missed. Mark day or night in words as well as in shade, and show working-hours bands on a shared timeline so a call time can be judged at a glance. Offer an overlap finder that names the shared window for the chosen places, and let the person slide through the day while every local time updates together, rather than converting one place at a time. State the next change to the gap where clocks move, and keep the list grouped and ordered by the person, with reorder and removal controls, so the places that matter stay first. Offer a share control that states every local time for a chosen moment in one summary, so a proposed time is never quoted in one zone alone. Distinct from the time-zone picker row, which picks one zone for a single event, and from the calendar and scheduling row, which books the event. Cross-check the time-zone picker row |
| Expense claim submission | Build a claim from receipts and send it for approval, with a plain state for every claim | Receipt capture, item list with amount, date, and category, approval-route statement, claim state, returned-with-reason notice, payout statement | Build the claim from its items, each stating its amount, date, and category, with a receipt attached to every item that needs one, and refuse submission plainly where a required receipt or field is missing, naming what is missing rather than failing silently. State the approval route before submission — who reviews it and in what order — so the claim never disappears into an unnamed queue. Give every claim one plain state — draft, submitted, approved, paid, or returned with a reason — and keep returned claims editable with the reason beside the item it concerns, so the fix is obvious and resubmission is one action. Keep a draft saveable at any point, and let a submitted claim be withdrawn while it is still awaiting review. Where a claim is paid, state the amount, the payout method, and the date together; where only part of a claim is approved, state which items were approved and which were not, with the reason for each. Distinct from the document scanner row, which captures a document, and from the order receipt row, which keeps a receipt after a purchase. Cross-check the document scanner row and the order receipt row |
| Read receipt | Show whether a sent message was delivered and read, without turning reading into pressure | Sending, sent, delivered, and read states, reader list, privacy control, mark-as-unread control | Show the state of the person's own messages only, in plain steps — sending, sent, delivered, read — with a shape as well as a colour change at each step, and show the read state on the latest message rather than repeating it on every line. In a group, name who has read the message on request rather than showing a bare count, and never show a read state for a message nobody has opened. Offer a privacy control that stops sending read states, and state plainly where it is mutual — turning it off also hides other people's read states — and where group conversations keep read states on regardless. Mark a conversation read only when the person actually reaches the latest message with it in front, and offer a mark-as-unread control that moves the read position back to a chosen message, so a message can be returned to later. Never treat a read state as a promise of a reply, and never nudge or escalate on the basis of a read state alone. Distinct from the message reaction row, which responds to one message, and from the threaded message reply row, which answers one message inside a conversation. Cross-check the message reaction row and the threaded message reply row |
| Typing indicator | Show that someone is composing a reply, so the conversation feels live without promising a message | Typing state, typer name, aggregated multi-typer statement, idle expiry, reduced-motion variant | Show the indicator only while someone is actually composing, and stop it a short time after they stop, so a stale indicator never promises a message that is not coming. Name who is typing in words — one name for one typer, an aggregated statement such as several people typing where more than one is composing — and place the indicator above the compose area where it does not shift the message list. In a busy channel, hide the indicator rather than letting it flicker, and never use it as the only sign that a reply is expected. Expire the indicator at once when the typer disconnects, and never show a typing state for a reply that was already sent or discarded. Keep the motion gentle and honour reduced-motion settings with a still label instead of animation, and announce the change to assistive technology without repeating it on every keystroke. Distinct from the live activity row, which follows one ongoing task from outside the conversation, and from the collaborative editing presence row, which shows who is editing a shared document. Cross-check the live activity row and the collaborative editing presence row |
| Product questions and answers | Let shoppers ask questions about one item and read answers from the seller or other shoppers | Question list, ask-a-question form, answer display, helpfulness votes, moderation notice, answered-state mark | Keep the section on the product page itself, separate from the review list, so a question about fit, contents, or compatibility is not buried among ratings. Show only questions that have passed review, with the newest first or the most helpful first, and state the sort plainly; a question awaiting review is visible only to the person who asked it, marked as awaiting review with the wait stated, never shown to everyone as unanswered. Offer an ask-a-question form beside the list: a short question field, an optional contact for the reply, and optional images where they help, with a plain statement that the question will be checked before it appears. Mark answers from the seller distinctly from answers by other shoppers, and keep the most helpful answers first, with a helpfulness control on each answer that records one vote per person. Where a question has no answer yet, say so plainly and answer it promptly: a visible unanswered question that lingers reads as neglect, so work the queue daily and never leave an old unanswered question on show. Allow a private question where offered, stating plainly that it stays between the asker and the seller and is never shown publicly. Distinct from the Rating and review submission row, which collects judgements after purchase, and from the Accordion disclosure row, which organises sections under headings. Cross-check the Rating and review submission row |
| Shared whiteboard | Let several people draw, write, and arrange ideas together on one shared canvas at the same time | Infinite canvas, sticky notes, shapes and connectors, frames and sections, presence cursors, follow control, voting, templates, access levels, export | Start from a prepared layout or a template — retrospective, mind map, flowchart — rather than a blank board, so the first minutes are spent adding ideas, not organising space. Offer an infinite canvas with pan and zoom, and never make dragging the only way to move: provide pan controls, zoom controls, and a jump to a named frame or section. Keep connectors attached when a shape moves, and show alignment guides so a diagram stays tidy without manual nudging. Show each person's cursor with their name in text as well as in colour, and offer a follow control that keeps everyone on the facilitator's view, with a bring-everyone-here action that returns the whole group to one spot. During silent brainstorming, hide other people's notes until the reveal, so early ideas do not anchor the group, and offer voting to rank what was gathered. Let comments and mentions attach to the object they concern, and keep who can draw separate from who can only view, with guests able to join without a long setup. Offer export of the finished board as an image or a document, so the result survives the session. Distinct from the collaborative editing presence row, which shows who is editing which part of a document, and from the annotation and comment thread row, which discusses content without changing it. Cross-check the collaborative editing presence row and the annotation and comment thread row |
| Video-call waiting room | Hold people who join before the host, and let the host admit each one by name | Holding view, wait statement, name entry, waiting list, admit control, decline control, admit-all control | People who open the link wait in a holding view and cannot see or hear the call, and nobody in the call can see or hear them, until the host admits them. The holding view states plainly what happens next and that the host must let them in, shows the name as the person entered it, and offers a way to leave the wait at any time. The host sees a waiting list with each entered name, admits or declines each person, and admits promptly — never admit a name that cannot be placed. When the call is at its limit, later joiners ask to join instead, and the host admits or declines each request, with an admit-all control for a known group. Once everyone expected has arrived, the host may switch the waiting room off so late joiners enter without interrupting, and switch it back on when late arrivals are possible and unexpected. Distinct from the live call controls row, which governs microphone, camera, and sharing toggles once inside the call. |
| Raise hand in video calls | Ask to speak without interrupting, in the order hands were raised | Raise-hand control, raised-hand mark on the tile, raised-hands list in the participants panel, moderator lower controls, gesture-detection setting | Offer one raise-hand control in the call controls and in the participants panel, so it is reachable wherever the person is looking, and keep it operable by keyboard as well as by pointer. Mark a raised hand on the person's tile and in the participants list, in words as well as in shape, and keep the queue in the order hands were raised, visible to everyone in the call, so the speaking order is never a guess. Let the moderator see the queue in one place, lower one hand or all hands, and never let a lowered hand reappear without the person raising it again. Lower a hand at once when the person speaks, and offer a plain cancel step that keeps the hand raised where the person was only testing the control or was called on by mistake. Where gesture detection is offered, keep it off by default and state plainly that a raised physical hand raises the virtual one, so nobody is queued by accident. Keep the control available while the microphone is muted — raising a hand is how a muted person asks to speak — and never use a raised hand as a substitute for a mute state. Distinct from the live call controls row, which governs microphone, camera, and sharing toggles, and from the video-call waiting room row, which holds people before they join. Cross-check the live call controls row and the video-call waiting room row |
| Screen sharing in video calls | Share one window or the whole screen with everyone in the call, while keeping private content hidden | Share control, scope picker, live preview, sharing indicator, stop control, permission request | State what will be visible before sharing starts, and offer a choice between one window, one tab, and the whole screen, with one window as the safer default — a whole-screen share shows every notification and open window. Show a preview of exactly what others will see and ask for confirmation before the share goes live, so the wrong window is caught before anyone sees it. Keep a visible sharing state for the whole share, with a stop control that ends it in one action from the call controls and from the shared view, and stop the share before opening private accounts or entering passwords, codes, or payment details. Silence notifications while sharing, or keep them outside the shared view, and state plainly that a recording or a second device can keep a copy of what was shared. Where guests can share, the host allows each one by name and can take the permission back at any time. If sharing cannot start, say so in place and leave the call unaffected, so the person can try again. Distinct from the live call controls row, which governs microphone, camera, and sharing toggles once inside the call, and from the virtual background row, which hides what is behind the person rather than what is on their screen. Cross-check the live call controls row and the video-call waiting room row |
| Disappearing messages | Let messages delete themselves after a stated time, so a conversation keeps no permanent record unless the people in it choose one | Per-conversation timer setting, lifetime picker, countdown mark, change notice, screenshot notice | Offer the timer as a per-conversation setting with a stated lifetime, from seconds to weeks, and state plainly whether the time runs from when the message is sent or from when it is seen — a message sent overnight to someone asleep stays until they open it when the count starts on viewing. Mark disappearing messages with a distinct style and a visible countdown, so nobody mistakes a vanishing message for a permanent one. Put a notice in the conversation whenever the setting changes, naming who changed it and the new lifetime, and apply the new rule only to messages sent after the change — messages already sent keep the rule they were sent under. State plainly, at the moment the setting is turned on, that a screenshot, a forward, a copy, or a photo taken with a second device can keep a copy, and never present disappearing messages as proof that no copy exists. Keep a short window to report a message after it has disappeared, so a harmful message cannot rely on vanishing before anyone can act. Distinct from the message edit and delete row, which removes one message on request, and from the conversation export row, which keeps a copy of the whole conversation. Cross-check the message edit and delete row and the conversation export row |
| Call recording notice | Tell everyone in the call, before recording starts, that a recording is being kept, and keep the recording state visible until it stops | Recording notice, consent control, recording indicator, stop control, recording location and retention statement | Show a plain statement before recording starts — what is being kept, why, and where it is kept and for how long — shown to everyone in the call, including people who join after recording started, so nobody is recorded without knowing. Let only the host or a named role start and stop recording, and state plainly who can do each. Keep a visible recording state for the whole recording — a mark in words as well as in shape, with the time running — that never hides behind a menu. Offer a plain way to object or to leave before recording starts, and stop the recording at once when consent is withdrawn, keeping what was already recorded only where the stated rule allows it. When a recording is kept, say where it lives, who can open it, and when it is deleted, and never keep a recording longer than the stated period without a fresh statement. Where a recording is shared, state who receives it before it is sent. Distinct from the screen sharing row, which shares a live view rather than keeping a copy, and from the live call controls row, which governs microphone, camera, and sharing toggles. Cross-check the screen sharing row and the live call controls row |
| Incoming call | Let the person answer, decline, or reply to a call without unlocking the device, and say plainly who is calling | Caller identity, accept control, decline control, quick-reply control, silence control, second-call controls | State the caller's identity plainly — name where known, number where not, and whether the number is saved — with the call type shown before any action is taken, so the person never answers blind. Offer accept and decline controls that work without unlocking the device, large enough to hit without looking, with accept and decline kept apart so one is never hit by mistake. Offer a quick reply for a declined call, with a few prepared messages and a custom option, and send the reply at once so the caller is not left guessing. Offer a silence control that stops the sound without declining, so the call can still be answered. Where a second call arrives during a call, offer the same accept and decline, and state plainly that accepting puts the current call on hold. When the call ends without an answer, leave a missed-call entry with the caller's identity and the time. Never show a decline control only on an unlocked device, and never let an incoming call hide a task in progress without a way back. Distinct from the live call controls row, which governs a call once it has started, and from the video-call waiting room row, which holds people before the host admits them. Cross-check the live call controls row and the missed call row |
| Missed call | Keep a clear entry for every call that was not answered, with the caller's identity, the time, and a one-action call back | Missed-call entry, caller identity, call time, call-back control, called-back mark, clear control | Keep a missed-call entry in the call list for every call that rang and was not answered, with the caller's identity and the time of the call, grouped where the same caller rang more than once so one caller never fills the list. Offer a one-action call back from the entry itself, and state plainly whether calling back uses voice or video. Mark an entry as called back once a return call connects, but keep the entry in the list, so the record of the missed call survives the return call. A missed call never clears itself: only the person clears it, one entry at a time or the whole list, with a plain confirmation for clearing all. Where a message was left, link the entry to it, and never mark a missed call as handled only because a message arrived. Where the caller is unknown, show the number plainly and offer a block control from the same entry. Distinct from the incoming call row, which handles a call while it is ringing, and from the notification list row, which gathers alerts rather than calls. Cross-check the incoming call row and the notification list row |
| Launch screen | Show a brief opening screen while the app loads, so the wait reads as the app appearing rather than a blank delay | Opening screen, brand mark or first-screen mirror, plain background, optional entry animation, transition to the first screen | Keep the screen only as long as loading genuinely takes — never add an artificial delay to extend it, and never use it to show loading progress or data: dismiss it and use skeleton content for anything still loading. Keep it minimal: one mark, or a mirror of the first screen, with no text, no complex imagery, and no advertising — text is not read in the second or two the screen is visible. Where platform guidance favours mirroring, match the first screen's layout and colours with static placeholders for its bars, so the app appears rather than loads; where it favours a branded opening, keep it to a centred mark on a plain background with an optional short entry animation. Dismiss the screen only when the first screen is visually stable and ready for interaction, and never show it again on a warm start where the app is already in memory. Provide light and dark variants that match the first screen, and keep the contrast between mark and background sufficient to read at a glance. Distinct from the onboarding row, which teaches the essential path on first use, and from the loading-state rules, which govern progress and skeleton content once the app is open. Cross-check the onboarding row |
| Call waiting | Handle a second incoming call during a live call without losing the first call | Second-call notice, caller identity, hold-and-accept control, end-and-accept control, decline control, swap control, merge control, held-call state | When a second call arrives during a live call, show the new caller's identity plainly — name where known, number where not — in a notice that stays visible without covering the controls for the current call, and sound a short waiting tone rather than a full ring, so the first call is never interrupted by surprise. Offer three plain choices, each stating its effect before it is used: hold and accept puts the current call on hold and connects the new call; end and accept ends the current call and connects the new call; decline sends the new caller to a missed-call note and keeps the current call untouched. Warn plainly before any action that would drop the first call, and never let one tap end a call by accident. While one call is held, show it with a stated hold state and a one-action return, and offer a swap control that moves between the two calls without dropping either. Offer a merge control only where the call supports joining both calls into one, and state plainly once merged that both callers can hear each other. Where a third call arrives while one call is held, state that one call must end before it can be answered, and offer decline or end-and-answer rather than a silent refusal. Distinct from the incoming call row, which handles a call while the device is idle, and from the live call controls row, which governs toggles once inside a call. Cross-check the incoming call row and the missed call row |
| Outgoing call | Show each stage of a call being placed, and say plainly what happened when it does not connect | Dialled number or contact, dialling state, ringing state, connecting state, busy state, no-answer state, failed state, cancel control, retry control, message control | Show the number or contact being called before dialling starts, and state each stage plainly in words: dialling while the call is being placed, ringing while the other side is being alerted, and connected once answered, with the call duration shown only after connection. Keep a cancel control visible while the call is ringing, so a ringing call can be ended in one action without waiting for an answer. When the line is busy, say so plainly and offer a retry or a message instead of redialling at once; when nobody answers, state that the call was not answered and offer a message or a call back later, and leave a record in the call list. When a call fails, state the cause where it is known — a wrong or disconnected number, no signal, or a blocked route — and offer a retry with the number shown for checking, or a message instead of redialling blindly. Never present a failed call as if it connected, and never redial automatically without the person choosing to. Where the call reaches a recorded message service, state that a message is being recorded and keep an end control reachable. Distinct from the incoming call row, which handles a call while it is ringing in, and from the missed call row, which records a call that was not answered. Cross-check the incoming call row and the live call controls row |
| Call quality and reconnection | Warn plainly when a call weakens, keep it alive where possible, and say what was lost and kept after a drop | Connection-quality mark, poor-connection warning, audio-only fallback control, reconnecting state, reconnection-failed state, kept-and-lost statement | When a connection weakens, show a plain warning on the affected tile — what is happening and what to do about it — in words as well as in shape, and show it only while the trouble lasts, so a warning that never clears is never mistaken for a live one. Warn the person whose own connection is poor, since they are the one who can act on it, and keep other participants' trouble visible only as a mark on their tile. Offer an audio-only fallback that keeps the call alive where video cannot, with the switch stated plainly and reversible in one action, so dropping video never ends the call by surprise. When the connection drops entirely, reconnect automatically where possible and show a reconnecting state that keeps the participants visible during a brief interruption, with a fuller reconnecting view only when the drop lasts. If reconnection fails, say so plainly and state what was lost and what was kept — whether messages, shared content, or the call record survive — and offer a one-action return to the call or a message to the other side. Never present a frozen picture or silent audio as a live call, and never drop a person from a call without a stated reason and a way back. Distinct from the live call controls row, which shows a connection-trouble indicator, and from the background-task progress row, which reports work that continues out of sight. Cross-check the live call controls row and the offline-first sync row |
| Visual voicemail | Show recorded messages as a list the person can read, play, save, or delete in any order, instead of dialling in and listening in sequence | Message list, caller identity, message time and length, play controls, pause and scrub controls, written transcript, heard mark, save control, share control, delete control, unheard count | Show every message as one list entry with the caller's identity, the time it was left, and its length, newest first, so the person can choose which message to open without listening to the others first. Let each message be played in any order with pause and scrub controls, and mark a message as heard only after it has been played or its transcript has been read — never by opening the list. Where a written transcript is available, label it plainly as a transcript and state that it may be wrong on exact details such as names, times, and numbers, so the person listens before acting on one. Show a count of unheard messages on the voicemail entry point, and clear it only as messages are heard. Let a message be saved, shared, or deleted one at a time, with deletion confirmed before it happens, and say plainly where a saved message is kept. Where a message arrives while it is still being left, show the words as they arrive and offer a one-action pick-up that joins the call at once. State up front where the list is unavailable — carrier, region, or device limits — instead of showing an empty list. Where the mailbox is full, say so plainly before new messages are refused, and offer a way to free space. Distinct from the missed call row, which records a call that was not answered, and from the voice message row, which carries a recorded message inside a conversation. Cross-check the missed call row and the voice message row |
| Voicemail greeting | Record the greeting callers hear before they leave a message | Default greeting, custom greeting, record control, playback before saving, re-record control, length limit, return-to-default control | Offer a default greeting and a custom one, and let the person record with playback before saving, so they hear exactly what callers will hear. Let a greeting be re-recorded without losing the previous one until the new one saves, so a failed recording never leaves the mailbox silent. State the length limit before recording starts, and offer a plain return to the default greeting. Distinct from the visual voicemail row, which lists messages already left, and from the call screening row, which asks an unknown caller who they are before the person answers. Cross-check the visual voicemail row and the call screening row |
| Safety check-in | Let a trusted contact know the person arrived safely, and alert them only if the person does not check in | Reason choice, duration or destination choice, trusted-contact choice, data-sharing level choice, start notice, check-in prompt, response window, add-time control, stop control, missed-check-in alert | Before starting, let the person choose the reason and either a destination or a duration, and choose which trusted contacts are told — never a whole contact list by default. State up front what is shared if the check-in is missed, and offer two levels: a limited level sharing only position, battery level, and signal strength, and a fuller level sharing the route and the last places the device was used, so the person chooses how much to share before anything is sent. Offer to tell the contacts when the check-in starts, and let the person add time while it runs without starting a new check-in. At the end of the chosen time, prompt the person to confirm they are safe, and give a short stated window to answer before anyone is alerted, so a slow reply never raises a false alarm. If there is no answer inside that window, tell the chosen contacts at once with the last known position and the chosen level of detail, and keep a plain route to call for help and to share the live position from the same screen. Let the person stop the check-in at any time with one action, and confirm the stop to the contacts so they are not left waiting. Never start a check-in silently, never share a live position before the check-in is missed unless the person asked for it, and never treat a missed check-in as proof of danger — the message says only that the person has not checked in. Distinct from the live location sharing row, which shares a moving position from the start, and from the emergency assistance request row, which asks for help at once. Cross-check the live location sharing row and the emergency assistance request row |
| Breakout rooms in video calls | Split one call into smaller rooms for focused discussion, with a clear return to the main call | Room list, room size, assignment controls, task and return-time statement, return control, broadcast message, countdown, help request, recording statement | Let the host create rooms with a stated size and a stated purpose, and show the assignment to every person before rooms open — automatic, manual, or chosen by each person — so nobody lands in a room by surprise. Put the task and the return time in writing inside every room, and keep a visible countdown before rooms close, so groups can finish and return without being cut off mid-sentence. State plainly who can move between rooms: the host may visit any room, and participants move between rooms only where the host allows it, with the return to the main call always open unless the host stated otherwise at the start. Let anyone ask the host for help from inside a room without leaving it. Assign late joiners on arrival, or keep them in the main call with a short briefing where less than a couple of minutes remain. Let the host send one message to all rooms at once, and state plainly before rooms open whether anything inside them is recorded or streamed; the default is that nothing is kept, and a recording inside a room is announced to everyone in that room before it starts. Distinct from the video-call waiting room row, which holds people before they join, and from the group conversation participant management row, which manages who is in a text conversation. Cross-check the video-call waiting room row |
| Call transfer | Move a live call to another person or team without dropping the caller | Transfer control, destination picker, hold state, consult call, introduction, completion control, cancel-and-return control, failure notice | Offer two kinds of transfer and state which one is being used before it happens: a direct transfer sends the caller straight through, and an announced transfer briefs the next person first while the caller waits on hold. Use a direct transfer only for a simple request with a clear destination; use an announced transfer wherever the next person needs context. Tell the caller who they are being passed to and why before the move, and pass the caller's name, the reason for the call, and what has already been tried, so the caller never repeats what they already said. While the caller waits on hold, say what is happening and keep the wait short, with a plain message where the wait runs long. If the next person does not answer or declines, return to the caller with the next option — another person, a message, or a call back — rather than leaving them in silence. If the transfer fails, say so plainly and offer to stay on the line, retry, or take a message, and never present a failed transfer as if it connected. Where the call supports it, offer a three-way introduction first, so the handover is heard by everyone before the first person leaves. Distinct from the automated-to-human support handoff row, which moves a conversation from automated help to a person, and from the pass or ticket transfer row, which passes a held item to another person. Cross-check the incoming call row and the call waiting row |
