| Call forwarding | Send incoming calls to another number when the person cannot or does not want to take them | Forwarding rules, per-rule destination, always/busy/unanswered/unreachable conditions, active-rules statement, per-rule turn-off control, voicemail-override warning | Offer forwarding always, or only when busy, unanswered, or unreachable, with a separate destination number for each rule, so one rule never silently inherits another rule's destination. State the active rules with their destinations shown, and offer a one-action turn-off for each rule, so forwarding never stays on by accident. Warn plainly where forwarding overrides voicemail, before the rule is saved, so a caller is never sent somewhere the person did not expect. Distinct from the call transfer row, which moves a live call to another person, and from the visual voicemail row, which holds messages left after a missed call. Cross-check the call transfer row and the visual voicemail row |
| Conference call | Merge two or more live calls into one call, with a named list of everyone on the line | Add-call control, merge control, participant list, drop-one control, speak-privately control, participant limit statement, host-leaving warning, failure notice | Start from a live call and add the next person by dialling privately first, so the person can explain who is already on the line before the merge. Show the merge control only once the second call has connected, and say plainly what happens to the first call while the second is dialled — it stays on hold, and it is never dropped. Keep a participant list naming everyone on the line in text, reachable from the call screen in one action. Offer a drop-one control beside each participant that ends that person's call without ending the call for the rest, and a speak-privately control that holds the others while one person talks, with a single merge action to bring everyone back. State the participant limit before more people are added, and mark the add control unavailable with a plain reason at the limit rather than letting the merge fail silently. Where the host leaving ends the call for everyone, warn before the host ends it, so nobody is dropped without warning. Where merging is not supported, say so plainly with the next option — a group call on a shared link or a separate call — rather than a missing control. Distinct from the call waiting row, which handles a second incoming call during a live call, and from the breakout rooms row, which splits one call into smaller rooms. Cross-check the call waiting row and the live call controls row |
| Suspected spam call warning | Warn plainly when a call looks like spam or a scam, and let the person decide without pressure | Warning label, reason statement, verification mark, caller identity, answer control, decline control, silence control, block-and-report control, mark-as-not-unwanted control | Show the warning on the incoming-call screen in plain words, with the reason where it is known — the number matches reported scam patterns, or the caller's identity could not be verified — and keep it visible until the call is answered or declined, without hiding the caller identity or the answer control. State what the warning does not prove: a flagged call can still be genuine, and an unflagged call can still be a scam, so the warning guides the decision and never makes it. Where the reason can be spoken as well as shown, speak it, so people who cannot see the screen get the same warning at the same moment. Never silence or block a flagged call without the person's choice: offer answer, decline, and block-and-report from the same screen, with a silence control that stops the sound without declining. Offer a mark-as-not-unwanted control from the same entry, so a wrongly flagged caller is corrected at once and future calls from them arrive plainly. Where a number is blocked, say plainly where its calls go, so a blocked call never simply vanishes. Where the person is unsure, offer an independent check — calling back on a number they already have — rather than trusting the identity shown on the screen. Distinct from the incoming call row, which handles an ordinary call while it is ringing in, and from the content reporting and blocking row, which reports content rather than calls. Cross-check the incoming call row and the missed call row |
| Call screening | Let the phone ask an unknown caller who they are and why they are calling, and show the answer as text before the person answers | Screen-call control, screening prompt, live transcript, suggested replies, accept control, decline control, block-and-report control, screening notice, screening settings | Offer a screen control on the incoming-call screen for a number that is not saved, and offer automatic screening for unknown or suspicious numbers as a setting the person controls. When screening runs, the caller is asked for a name and a reason, and the spoken answer appears as text on the incoming-call screen as it arrives, so the person can judge the call without answering it. Keep accept, decline, and block-and-report on the same screen as the transcript, and let the person ask the caller one more question from the transcript before deciding. A screened call is never answered silently: the person is always shown that screening ran and what the caller said, and nothing is accepted on their behalf without their choice. Where screening ends the call on its own, leave a plain entry saying so, with the transcript kept where the person can read it. Saved contacts always ring through without screening. Distinct from the suspected spam call warning row, which labels a call from known patterns without asking the caller anything, and from the visual voicemail row, which records a message after the call is missed. Cross-check the incoming call row and the suspected spam call warning row |
| Digital identity wallet presentation | Present verified identity details held in a wallet to a requesting service, sharing only what the request needs | Request summary, per-item disclosure choice, consent control, unlock authorisation, confirmation statement, error route, scannable code | Before anything is shared, show who is asking, exactly what they are asking for, and why, in plain words, so the person can judge the request before deciding. List each requested item separately, marking which items are required and which are optional; optional items start unshared, and the person chooses each one, so only what the request needs is ever sent and never the whole credential by default. Where a yes-or-no fact would satisfy the request, offer that instead of the fuller detail, so a date or an address is never shared when a threshold answer would do. Ask for explicit consent to the exact set shown; a declined or cancelled request shares nothing and leaves the wallet unchanged. After consent, require the wallet's own unlock step before sending, so a request is never completed by a stray tap. State the outcome at once: a successful presentation says what was shared and with whom, and a failed one states the reason with a retry or help route, never a silent return to the list. Where the presentation happens in person, show a scannable code the other side reads, keeping the shared items visible beside it until the scan completes, and offer a typed or staff route where scanning fails. Keep held details readable on any screen size, and name them in plain words rather than technical terms, so the person always knows what they hold and what they are about to share. Distinct from the age verification gate row, which confirms a threshold without presenting a credential, and from the pass and ticket display row, which shows a pass already held without a per-item disclosure choice. Cross-check the age verification gate row and the pass and ticket display row |
| Reading view | Read a page without navigation, banners, and interactive controls getting in the way | Reading-view control, text-size control, line-spacing control, background choice, reading-position marker, exit control | Offer a reading view for pages whose main content is text, and enter it with one action from the page itself. Remove navigation, banners, advertisements, and interactive controls, and keep headings, links, and essential images in the same order as the full page, so nothing the person needs to read is lost. Offer text size, line spacing, and light-or-dark background controls inside the view, and keep the person's choices for the next visit. Set the text column to a comfortable reading measure, roughly fifty to seventy-five characters per line, with line spacing near one and a half times the text size, and never let the text run the full width of a wide screen. State the reading position and keep it when the person leaves and returns, so a long page resumes where it stopped. Offer a one-action exit back to the full page, and never trap the person in the view. Distinct from the simplified view row, which reduces cognitive load across the whole product, and from the print-friendly view row, which prepares a page for printing rather than for reading on screen. Cross-check the simplified view row and the print-friendly view row |
| Add people to a live call | Invite others into a call already in progress, from the call itself | Add-person control, contact picker, dial pad, private-call state, merge control, participant list, join announcement, remove control | Offer an add-person control on the live-call screen, and let the person choose from contacts or dial a number without leaving the call. Dial the new person privately first, so the host can explain who is already on the line before the merge, and say plainly what happens to the current call while the new one is dialled — it stays on hold and is never dropped. Show the merge control only once the new call has connected, and bring everyone together with one action. Announce each person by name as they join, and keep a participant list naming everyone on the line, reachable from the call screen in one action. Offer a remove control beside each participant that ends that person's call without ending the call for the rest, so someone added by mistake can be dropped at once. State the participant limit before more people are added, and mark the add control unavailable with a plain reason at the limit rather than letting the merge fail silently. Where the host leaving ends the call for everyone, warn before the host ends it. Distinct from the video-call waiting room row, which holds people before the host admits them, and from the group conversation participant management row, which manages who is in a text conversation. Cross-check the conference call row and the live call controls row |
| Call history | Review past calls and call back in one action | Chronological list, call-type marks, filter by type, call-back control, per-entry delete, clear-all control, retention statement, call detail view | List calls in chronological order, newest first, grouped by day, with each entry naming the caller, the call type, the time, and the duration. Mark incoming, outgoing, and missed calls in text or shape as well as colour, so the type is plain without relying on colour alone. Offer a filter by call type, and a one-action call back or redial from each entry, with a detail view naming the time, duration, and outcome of that call. Group repeated missed calls from the same number into one entry with a count, rather than repeating the same number down the list. Offer a delete control on each entry and a clear-all control for the whole list, with a confirmation before the list is cleared, and state plainly how long entries are kept and whether the list lives on this device only or follows sign-in. A missed call never clears itself without the person's choice: an unreviewed missed call stays marked until the person reviews or removes it. Distinct from the missed call row, which records a single missed call, and from the recently viewed row, which lists opened items rather than calls. Cross-check the missed call row and the incoming call row |
| Parking session payment | Pay for a timed parking stay and manage it until it ends | Zone entry, map of nearby zones, vehicle choice, duration choice, rate and total cost, live countdown, expiry reminder, extend control, maximum-stay statement, end-early control, receipt | Start the session by entering the zone number shown on the sign or choosing the zone on a map, then choose the vehicle and the duration. Show the rate and the total cost before payment, and state the maximum stay for the zone before the person pays, so a session is never bought that cannot be extended to cover the visit. Show the remaining time live with a countdown, and send a reminder before expiry that extends the session from the notice itself, without opening the app. Where extension is not possible — outside the zone hours, or a restriction on the stay — say so plainly before the person tries, with the reason. Offer an end-early control that stops the session and refunds unused time where the operator allows it, and keep a receipt for the session with the zone, the times, and the amount paid. Distinct from the ride booking row, which books a trip rather than a timed stay, and from the order tracking row, which follows a delivery rather than a session. Cross-check the ride booking row and the order tracking row |
| Screenshot markup | Mark up a captured image so someone else can follow it, without leaking private details | Crop control, arrow, shape, text label, highlight, numbered steps, blur or solid cover, undo, original capture | Crop first to the part that matters, keeping enough context to recognise the screen, and remove the rest. Keep one idea per capture, at most three callouts, one colour for all of them, and a consistent stroke width, so the marks stay readable. Start each arrow in empty space and end it touching the target, never hovering nearby, and use numbered steps where the capture must show a sequence. Cover private details — names, addresses, tokens, amounts, faces — with a solid cover or a proper pixelation before sharing, never a translucent wash that can be reversed, and crop the detail out entirely where it sits at the edge. Keep every mark editable, movable, or removable after it is placed, and keep the original capture unchanged; share the flattened export, not the editable project. Distinct from the media viewer row, which shows an image without changing it, and from the document scanner row, which captures a page rather than explaining a capture. Cross-check the media viewer row and the document scanner row |
| Group chat administration | Govern who runs a group chat and what each role may do | Owner, admin, and member roles, per-permission toggles, admin list, ownership transfer, blocked and removed member lists, route back | Give the group one owner with full control, and let the owner appoint admins who hold only the permissions granted to them, set with a toggle for each one: edit group details, send messages, add members, approve new members, and remove members. Name the new owner before an ownership transfer happens, and confirm it, since the transfer hands over full control. Keep an admin list where the owner can promote and demote, and let demotion return a person to member without removing them from the group. Keep blocked and removed member lists with a stated route back for each, so nobody simply vanishes. Suggest a second trusted admin early, so the group is never run by one person alone. Distinct from the group conversation participant management row, which manages who is in a conversation without the role and permission rules, and from the invitation response row, which answers one invitation rather than governing a group. Cross-check the group conversation participant management row and the invitation response row |
| Hotel booking | Choose a stay by comparing rooms and rates with their full costs and terms, not by a headline nightly price | Destination, date and guest search, stay results, room card, rate cards, cancellation terms, payment timing, inclusions, total price, price hold, booking reference | Search by destination, dates, and guests, and compare stays by the full cost including taxes and fees rather than the nightly headline. Show the total price including taxes and fees beside the per-night price, and never show a price that excludes mandatory charges. Keep the rate options inline under the room card, so the guest never leaves the room to compare its rates. Name each rate by what the guest gets, with the concrete cancellation deadline in the label, not by an internal rate-plan name, and show the same attributes in the same order on every rate card: price, cancellation terms, payment timing, and inclusions. State the price difference between rates as a trade-off, so the guest can judge what the extra money buys. Put cancellation and refundability on the results list and the room card, not only behind a link, and make free cancellation a filter. In the date picker, disable sold-out dates visibly, distinguish closed-to-arrival dates from sold-out dates, and state minimum-stay rules when a date is selected. Hold the price with a stated expiry while payment is entered, and confirm with a booking reference, a full price breakdown, and a plain cancellation route, restating the chosen rate's cancellation deadline, payment timing, and inclusions in plain language. Distinct from the travel check-in row, which handles arrival rather than booking, and from the travel itinerary view row, which shows a booked trip rather than choosing a stay. Cross-check the travel check-in row and the travel itinerary view row |
| Electric vehicle charging session | Charge a vehicle at a public charge point and pay for the energy delivered, with the price known before the session starts | Charger finder, live availability, connector choice, power rating, price per unit, contactless payment, pre-authorisation hold, live session view, stop control, session summary, receipt, helpline | Find a charger with live availability, connector type, and price before driving there, and let the person pick the charge point and connector first, showing connector type, maximum power, and live availability for each. Show the total price per unit of energy before the session starts, including any fixed connection fee, and never raise the price once charging has begun. Offer contactless card payment with no account, contract, or app download required, and never make an app or membership the only way to pay. Explain any pre-authorisation hold before payment: the amount, that it is a hold and not a charge, and when it is released. During the session show, live and together, the energy delivered, the cost accrued so far, the current power, and the estimated time to the target charge level. Provide a clear stop control that ends the session at any time from the same screen, and a session summary at the end with energy delivered, total cost, and duration, with a receipt the person can keep. Advertise a free, staffed telephone helpline at the charge point and in the session screen, for faults and failed payments. Design the on-device screen and payment terminal for seated and standing use, with controls and screens within reach range, high contrast, and plain-language error states when a session fails to start. Distinct from the parking session payment row, which pays for a timed stay rather than energy delivered, and from the contactless payment confirmation row, which confirms one payment without a live session. Cross-check the parking session payment row and the contactless payment confirmation row |
| Smart home dashboard | See and control the devices in a home, organised by room, with scenes that set several devices at once | Home view, room list, zone grouping, device tile, device state, scene, automation, guest access, admin view | Organise by room first and by device type second, and order rooms as a walk through the home rather than alphabetically. Put scenes above individual device controls, so the person chooses an activity rather than a list of devices. Show state before controls: whether doors are locked, what is still on, and the current temperature, at the top of the home view. Give every device tile its own battery, updating, and error states, and an offline or unreachable state with a plain explanation and a retry action, never a dead toggle with no reason. Use plain, household language for device and room names, let names be customised, and avoid technical terms and acronyms. Provide separate views per context: a phone view for one-handed, one-task use, a glanceable wall view readable from across the room, and an admin view for diagnostics that other household members never see. Scope access by person, so guests and helpers see only the rooms and devices they need, with time-limited access where relevant. Distinct from the home control map row, which places devices on a floor plan rather than in room lists, and from the device pairing row, which connects a new device rather than controlling the home. Cross-check the home control map row and the device pairing row |
| Sleep and rest data | Show how a person slept without turning one night into a verdict | Morning view, weekly trend, sleep-stage breakdown, bedtime and wake times, rest goal, one suggested change, missing-night statement, share and export controls | Lead with the week, not the night: a single night is mostly noise, so make the seven-day pattern the headline and keep each night's detail one tap away. Never lead with a single grade or a single number that reads as a verdict, and never use judgement words such as poor, failed, or bad night, nor a red mark that reads as an alarm. State plainly what was measured — time asleep, time in bed, waking, and the stages the device can tell apart — and state the limits: a sensor estimate is not a clinical reading, and a night with no data is stated as missing, never filled in or smoothed over. Show bedtime and wake time beside the trend, keep the person's rest goal visible with the trend against it, and offer at most one suggested change, in plain words, tied to the pattern rather than to one night. Offer a share or export route that sends the trend and its limits together, never a bare number. Distinct from the focus mode schedule row, which silences interruptions for sleep, and from the sleep timer row, which stops playback after a chosen time. Cross-check the data visualisation row and the focus mode schedule row |
| Telehealth video consultation | Consult a clinician over video with identity, privacy, and a stated next step | Pre-call device and connection check, clinician identity, patient identity check, privacy confirmation, large labelled call controls, phone fallback, post-call summary, safety-netting contact | Run a device and connection check (camera, microphone, bandwidth) before the call and say what to do if it fails, so a paid consultation never starts broken. At the start, show the clinician's name and role visibly and have them introduce themselves, and verify the patient's identity by name and date of birth. Confirm the patient is in a private place and ask who else is in the room on both sides. Keep the call controls large, labelled, and always visible: mute, camera on and off, end call, and a way to ask for help. State the phone fallback before the call and switch to it if video drops, rather than leaving the patient stranded. End with a written summary naming the next step — prescription, referral, or follow-up booking — and who to contact if symptoms change. Build the whole flow to WCAG 2.2 AA: keyboard-operable controls, captions or live transcription where available, and no time limit that cuts a patient off mid-call. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books the time, and from the video-call waiting room row, which holds people before admission. Cross-check the appointment management row and the video-call waiting room row |
| Museum audio guide | Listen to per-exhibit audio with text and signed alternatives, in a set order or freestyle | Language choice, track list and map and scan entry, tour order and freestyle modes, player with speed control, transcript, audio description, signed-language video, offline state | Ask for language first, before any content plays, and remember the choice for the whole visit. Give every audio track a matching word-for-word transcript in the same language, beside the player. Provide a separate audio-description track that describes what can be seen, for visitors who cannot see the exhibit, and signed-language video as a parallel track, toggleable independently of captions. Keep tracks short, around one to two minutes per stop, and let the visitor pause, replay, and skip. Offer both a set tour order and freestyle exploration by stop number or code, always showing where the visitor is in the tour and what comes next. Keep tracks available without a connection where the visit is indoors, with a clear offline state stated before the visitor enters a dead zone. Use the visitor's own device settings — text size, screen reader, volume — rather than rebuilding them inside the guide. Distinct from the guided tour row, which points out elements inside the product itself, and from the media chapters row, which marks sections inside one item. Cross-check the guided tour row and the media chapters row |
| QR code payment at a till | Pay at a till by scanning the merchant's code with an authenticated payment app | Merchant-presented code, consumer-presented code, mode statement, merchant and amount and currency verification, dynamic code, printed-code placement, both-sides confirmation, failure route | State in words which mode applies: the merchant shows a code and the customer scans it with their payment app, or the customer shows a code for the merchant to scan — never leave the mode to an icon alone. Before the customer confirms, show the merchant name, amount, and currency exactly as encoded in the code, so the payer can verify them. Prefer a dynamic code carrying the exact amount for that transaction over a static printed code that needs the amount typed in, which invites error. Keep a printed code large and high-contrast, dark on light, with a clear quiet zone around it, placed where it scans from a comfortable standing distance in good light, and label it in plain text beside the code. Confirm success or failure on both sides of the till, with the amount, merchant, and a reference the customer can keep. On failure, say what happened and offer another payment method rather than looping the scan. Never send the customer to an unauthenticated web page to pay; the scan must open inside their authenticated payment app. Distinct from the scan-to-pay row, which covers the in-app flow around a scanned code, and from the code scanner row, which covers capturing any code. Cross-check the scan-to-pay row and the code scanner row |
| Crowdfunding campaign page | Ask for donations to a cause with the purpose and the full cost stated before payment | Purpose statement, organiser identity and registration, target amount and time limit, fee and tip disclosure, donation amounts, question route, complaints route, progress statement, reward tiers, fulfilment plan, backer updates | State the purpose of the appeal and what donations support in plain words before asking for money. Show the organiser's name and registration number on the page where one applies. Disclose all platform fees and optional tips before the donor enters payment details, and state what happens to expenses and deductions before the donor gives. State the target amount and the time limit up front, with the progress toward the target shown in plain figures. Keep the reward tiers few and comparable — no more than five for a first campaign — with each tier stating what it includes, what it costs, and when it is expected to arrive, and treat every delivery date as an estimate, not a promise. Explain the fulfilment plan on the page, including how setbacks will be handled, and collect shipping details only when delivery approaches. Update backers regularly and at the first sign of a roadblock: state the current status, the cause of the delay, what is being done, the new timeline, and the next steps, and never leave a delay undisclosed. Where a promised reward changes, compare the replacement with the original tier line by line and give backers a clear route to respond. Provide a visible way for donors to ask questions and a complaints route. Never mislead by omission, exaggeration, or ambiguity about how money will be used. Distinct from the donation at checkout row, which adds a donation to a purchase already in progress. Cross-check the donation at checkout row |
| Shared bike and scooter rental | Unlock, ride, and end a shared vehicle session with the cost and parking rules stated up front | Vehicle map, availability and battery or range, price before unlock, unlock control, live ride view, designated parking, end-of-ride confirmation, ride summary, fault report | Show vehicle availability, battery or range, and price — unlock fee plus per-minute rate — before unlocking, and make unlocking one clear step with a visible failure state and retry. Show live ride time, distance, and running cost during the session. Show designated parking areas on the map before the ride ends, close to trip ends, and confirm the end of a ride only once the vehicle is in an authorised parking area; say plainly if it is not, and what the charge consequence is. End with a summary of the route, duration, and cost, and a way to report a faulty vehicle or bad parking. Keep the app and website usable with assistive technology, with audio cues and large-print options. Distinct from the ride booking row, which books a ride with a driver, and from the parking session payment row, which pays for a timed stay. Cross-check the ride booking row and the parking session payment row |
| Keyless shared-car pickup | Pick up a shared or rented car with a phone, with no counter visit and no physical key | Booking with unlock method, check-in window, unlock control, offline state, condition check, fault report, extra-driver activation, return and lock confirmation, trip summary | State on the booking which way the car opens — by phone or by a card — before the person leaves home, so nothing is a surprise at the kerb. Open check-in a stated time before the booking starts and keep the unlock control in one clear step with a visible failure state and a retry. Let the car lock and unlock without a signal, so a basement car park or a rural road never strands the person. Ask for a short condition check before driving off — guided photos of the outside and the inside — with a fault report attached to the booking, so damage found later is never blamed on the wrong driver. Let each extra driver activate access on their own phone, within the same check-in window. End the trip with a lock confirmation and a summary of time, distance, and cost, and state plainly what to do when the phone battery dies or the phone is lost. Distinct from the digital car key sharing row, which shares a key held in a wallet, and from the shared bike and scooter rental row, which covers short sessions on two-wheeled vehicles. Cross-check the digital car key sharing row and the shared bike and scooter rental row |
| Digital car key sharing | Share a vehicle key held in a wallet with another person, with the access level and expiry stated before sending | Key in wallet, share control, recipient choice, access-level choice, expiry choice, reshare control, activation step, shared-key list, revoke control, removal statement | Share from the wallet that holds the key, never by handing over an account password. Name the recipient and state the access level before sending: a guest who may unlock and drive until a stated expiry, a co-owner with full access including sharing further, or a service role limited to the work at hand and unable to share onward. State the expiry with the guest role and whether the recipient may share the key further; a key shared by a scannable code carries preset access that cannot be changed, so say so before the code is shown. Tell the recipient what they need before they accept: a compatible device, and an activation step such as a code or the physical key on first use. Let the owner remove any shared key at any time and let each recipient remove only the key shared with them; state plainly when removal takes effect, since some vehicles apply it only when another key is next used. Distinguish a key that only unlocks from one that also starts the vehicle, and show which one the recipient received. Distinct from the pass or ticket transfer row, which hands over a single-use entitlement rather than ongoing access, and from the digital identity wallet presentation row, which shares selected details for one check rather than access over time. Cross-check the pass or ticket transfer row and the digital identity wallet presentation row |
| Mobile plan setup (eSIM) | Activate, transfer, or switch a mobile plan on a built-in SIM, without a physical card | Activation-route choice, plan list, line label, default-line choice, transfer confirmation, activation progress, signal check, porting-code entry, removal control | Name the activation route before starting and offer every route the carrier supports: a code from the carrier, the carrier's app, a plan assigned by the carrier, a transfer from a nearby phone, or typed details — never present a code as the only route where another exists, and pair a scannable code with typed details for a phone without a working camera or where scanning fails. Check first that the phone supports a built-in SIM and is unlocked for the new carrier; where it is locked, say so plainly and point to the unlock route rather than failing mid-setup. Distinguish switching carriers from moving the same plan to a new phone: a switch always takes a fresh plan from the new carrier and the old one is removed after, while a transfer moves the same number and deactivates it on the old phone. Where the person keeps their number on a switch, ask for the porting code before cancelling anything — cancelling the old plan first can end the number. Keep the old plan or physical card active until the new plan shows signal; activation can take minutes or up to a day, so show progress and say what to do if it stalls rather than leaving a blank screen. Where several plans live on one phone, let each be labelled in plain words — personal, work, travel — and let the person choose which line is the default for calls and for data, changeable later. Confirm a transfer on the old phone as well as the new one, so a plan cannot move without the owner's knowledge, and state plainly when the old phone stops working. Removing a plan needs a confirmation, and state that a removed plan needs the carrier to issue a new one — it cannot be restored from the phone alone. Distinct from the QR sign-in row, which approves a sign-in on another screen, and from the second-factor sign-in row, which enrols a code for sign-in. Cross-check the QR sign-in row and the code scanner row |
| Guest mode | Lend a device to someone else for a short session without signing in, with nothing of the owner visible and nothing of the guest kept | Guest entry point, scope statement, session timer, sign-out control, session warning, data-clear statement, named-profile route | Start the guest session in one action from the lock or profile screen, and show a short statement of what the guest can and cannot reach before anything else opens: no access to the owner's accounts, messages, photos, or files. Keep the owner's profile behind its own lock, so the guest cannot step sideways into it. State plainly that nothing the guest does is kept: history, downloads, installed items, and any signed-in state are cleared when the session ends, and say so again at sign-out. Give the session a visible end: a sign-out control that is always reachable, a stated maximum length, and a warning shortly before the session ends, with an idle timeout that ends an abandoned session rather than leaving it open. Where the same person borrows often, offer a named profile instead of a temporary guest session, so their own material persists separately from the owner's. Never present a guest session as private from the device owner: state that activity on the device can still be seen by whoever manages it. Distinct from the household member management row, which gives a named person persistent access, and from the guest checkout row, which covers one purchase without an account. Cross-check the household member management row and the guest checkout row |
| Transit card top-up | Add stored value to a travel card from a phone or a station machine, and always know the balance before paying | Balance and fare statement, preset and custom amounts, minimum and card-limit statement, auto top-up control, receipt with new balance | Show the balance and the fare for the next journey before paying, so the person can tell whether a top-up is needed at all. Offer preset amounts and a custom amount, and state the smallest top-up and the most the card can hold; refuse an amount that would pass the card limit before payment, not after. Offer an auto top-up that adds a stated amount when the balance falls below a stated threshold, with the threshold and amount shown together, a stated wait before the value reaches the card, and a plain way to stop it at any time; the first auto top-up takes payment at once. Show the new balance on the reader, in the phone wallet, and on the receipt after every top-up, so the balance is never guessed. Distinct from the fare-capped transit payment row, which charges each ride as it happens, and from the gift card and stored-credit redemption row, which covers merchant credit rather than fare value. Cross-check the fare-capped transit payment row and the gift card and stored-credit redemption row |
| In-flight entertainment | Watch, listen, and read on a seatback screen for one passenger, with the same content open to everyone on board | Welcome and language step, browse and search over onboard content, playback controls, caption and audio-description controls, nonvisual control option, announcements display | Set language first at the welcome step, before any catalogue is shown, so every later label, caption, and announcement follows the choice. Browse and search over content held on board, and mark which titles carry captions, audio description, and a signed alternative, with a filter that shows only titles carrying the feature the person needs. Offer captions on every video that carries them and audio description where the provider supplies it, with caption size and colour the person can change, and show important announcements on the screen as text as well as sound, so nothing spoken is lost. Offer a nonvisual way to work the controls — a spoken readout of each on-screen item, or a control that does not depend on sight — so the screen can be used without seeing it. Where the seatback screen cannot be used, offer an alternative device with the same content. Keep playback on the aircraft: content plays from the aircraft's own store without a connection, and playback resumes where it stopped after a restart. State plainly when live channels carry no captions, and where a title has no described version. Distinct from the television layout, which assumes a room screen and a shared remote, and from the media chapters row, which marks sections inside one item. Cross-check the live captions row and the media chapters row |
| Tenant portal | Pay rent, follow a repair, read notices, and message the property contact from one tenancy record | Balance and due date, payment and autopay controls, receipt list, maintenance request form, request status timeline, document list, message thread, emergency route | Show the balance owed, the due date, and the receipt for every payment in one place, so the rent position is never guessed. Offer payment by card or bank transfer with the amount and the date shown before confirmation, and keep autopay as a stated choice with the date and amount it will take, changeable or stoppable at any time. Let a repair be reported with a category, a description, and photos, and show its state from report to fix — received, scheduled, in progress, fixed — with the expected response time stated at the report step and the next visit time shown once booked, so the resident never has to ask for an update. Keep the agreement, notices, and past statements in a document list the resident can open at any time, with each document named by what it is and when it arrived. Offer a message route to the property contact from the same record, with the reply kept beside the request or payment it concerns. Show an emergency route on every screen for urgent repairs, and never hide it behind sign-in. Show only the resident's own tenancy: no other resident's payments, requests, or documents are ever visible. Distinct from the payment rows, which cover one payment without the tenancy record, and from the order tracking row, which follows one delivery without the tenancy context. Cross-check the saved payment method selection row and the appointment management row |
| QR table ordering | Order and pay from a table by scanning its code, without waiting for staff | Table code, menu with the table already known, basket, order status, call-staff action, pay and tip controls, receipt, genuine-code check | Give each table its own code, so scanning it opens the menu with the table already known — the guest never types a table number. Open the menu in the browser with no app to install and no account needed before ordering. Name the table on the kitchen ticket, so the order reaches the right table without the guest flagging staff down. Keep a call-staff action on the same screen as the menu, so help, allergies, and substitutions stay one action away. State on the table card how to tell the code is genuine — the name it should open and a plain instruction to ask staff if it opens anything else — and never ask the guest to pay through a code that opens an unfamiliar page. State plainly whether a submitted order can still be changed, and where it cannot, keep the change route with staff. Where the connection fails, staff take the order and the guest still eats; keep a paper menu and a person available at every table, so the code is an option and never the gate. Serve the menu as text with real headings and prices, never as an image or a document a screen reader cannot read. Let everyone at the table order from their own phone onto one shared check, split it where needed, and pay at once or at the end with the tip shown before payment and a receipt after it. Distinct from the QR code payment at a till row, which pays a fixed amount at a counter, and from the store pickup row, which hands an order over in person. Cross-check the QR code payment at a till row and the order tracking row |
| Screen time dashboard | See how time was spent on the device and set limits the person controls | Usage chart by app and category, daily and weekly views, unlock and notification counts, per-app limit control, limit warning, downtime schedule, focus preset, change and remove route | Show how much time was spent in each app or category over a stated period — today and the past week — with the total in plain figures and the busiest times of day named, so the person sees the habit rather than a single number. Show unlocks and notifications received beside the time, since both explain where the time went. Let the person set a daily limit per app or category, and warn before the limit is reached, with the time left stated. When a limit is reached, pause the app and say so plainly, stating the limit and when it resets, with a plain route to change or remove the limit from the same screen — never a silent block and never a limit that cannot be undone. Offer a scheduled downtime that leaves calls and chosen essentials working, and focus presets for work, rest, and sleep that silence the rest. Where limits are set by a guardian, state who set them and what they cover, so the person is never surprised by a block. Keep the report free of judgement: state what happened and offer the next step, rather than grading the person. Distinct from the usage allowance row, which meters a paid allowance rather than time, and from the focus mode schedule row, which silences interruptions rather than reporting use. Cross-check the usage allowance row and the focus mode schedule row |
| Health record sharing | Share a health record with a clinician or a carer, with the scope, the expiry, and the way back all stated before sharing | Recipient choice, scope choice, expiry choice, share link or code, passcode, shared-record list, revoke control, access record | State the scope before sharing: which sections go, and whether the share covers only what is there now or also what is added later, so a new result is never shared by surprise. State the expiry with the share — a date after which access ends on its own — and offer a short-lived share for a single visit beside a longer share for ongoing care. Where a link or code carries the record, protect it with a passcode shared separately from the link, and state that anyone holding the link alone cannot open it. Keep a shared-record list where the owner reviews every recipient, what they can see, and until when, and offer a revoke control on each recipient that ends access at once; revocation never deletes the record of the share, so who had access, from when to when, and who ended it stays answerable. Show the recipient what they need before they accept: no account or install beyond the link where none is needed, and a plain statement of what they will see. Distinct from the telehealth video consultation row, which holds a consultation rather than sharing a record, and from the medication refill request row, which asks for a repeat prescription. Cross-check the telehealth video consultation row and the medication refill request row |
| E-reader and reading app | Read long-form text on a phone or a dedicated reader, with the page shaped to the reader and the place kept | Reading settings, font and spacing controls, theme and margin choice, chapter list, reading position, word lookup, read-aloud control, low-glare mode | Let the reader set type size, line spacing, and margins, and keep those choices between sessions and across books, so the page is set once and stays set. Keep the reading position across devices, with the place, notes, and highlights following the reader, so opening the same title elsewhere resumes where they left off. Offer chapter navigation with the current place stated, and a plain route to look up a word without losing the page — the definition appears beside the text and closes back to the same line. Offer a low-glare reading mode for long sessions, with a warm or dark theme the reader chooses, and a read-aloud route that highlights each word as it is spoken, with the speed under the reader's control. Distinct from the reading progress indicator row, which shows progress rather than the reading surface, and from the long-document navigation row, which outlines sections rather than setting type. Cross-check the reading progress indicator row and the long-document navigation row |
| Ballot | Cast a private vote on a ballot with contests and candidates, where a wrong mark can spoil the vote | Contest list, candidate names, marking control, instructions, review screen, cast confirmation, invalid-selection notice, large-print and tactile options | Keep the ballot in sentence case and left-aligned, in one consistent readable typeface, with contest names prominent and candidate names in bold, and list the candidates in a single column with each contest clearly separated, so the ballot reads in the order it is marked. Put short instructions at the start of the ballot, in plain familiar words, in the order the tasks happen, stating what each action does before asking for it, and place the instruction for a marking just above where the marking is made. Make the marking space simple and consistent on every contest, and use one marking style throughout, so a mark is never read differently in two places. Where voters rank candidates, offer a grid or column layout where each rank is picked beside the candidate, rather than asking voters to write numbers by hand, since hand-numbered ranking is slower and more error-prone. Show a review screen before casting that lists every selection in plain words, ask the voter to check it, and offer a route back to change any choice before the vote is cast. Say plainly, before casting, when a contest has too many marks or an unusable selection, and never silently drop a mark or cast a spoiled ballot. Confirm in words once the vote is cast, and state clearly that casting is complete, so the voter never leaves unsure whether the vote counted. Offer the same ballot in large print, with a tactile template and a magnifier, so voters who cannot read the standard print can mark it independently, and keep the ballot usable with assistive technology. Distinct from the Poll row, which settles a group decision and shows results to the voters, and from the Group scheduling poll row, which collects availability rather than a private vote. Cross-check the Poll row |
| Flight disruption compensation claim | Claim fixed compensation after a cancelled or badly delayed flight, keeping the money owed separate from any refund or expenses | Disruption type choice, flight details, booking reference, eligibility bands, compensation tier display, evidence file, payment details, claim states, case reference, escalation route | Start from the disruption — delay, cancellation, or denied boarding — and show the eligibility bands before the claim is sent, with the fixed amount for each distance band stated beside the band, so the person knows what the claim is worth before entering details. Carry the flight number, route, date, and booking reference from the booking into the claim, so the person never retypes them. Keep compensation, expenses, and refund as three separate amounts with separate states, never merged into one figure, and state plainly which of the three the claim covers. Let the person add evidence to the claim after sending — boarding pass, receipts, messages from the carrier — and keep the evidence file visible beside the claim, with each item named and dated. Show claim states that match events the carrier can establish — sent, under review, approved, declined with a reason, paid — with what happens next and when, never a vague in-progress label alone. On sending, give a case reference at once and a written confirmation, so the claim can be followed even if the confirmation message never arrives. Where the carrier does not answer within the stated window, offer the route to the national enforcement body from the claim itself, carrying the claim details across rather than asking for them again. Distinct from the travel disruption rebooking row, which finds a new flight rather than claiming money, and from the warranty claim row, which claims on a product rather than a journey. Cross-check the travel disruption rebooking row and the warranty claim row |
| Self-checkout grocery kiosk | Check out and pay for a shop at a kiosk in the store, without a staffed till | Item scanner, running total, bagging area, item removal control, age-restricted item check, payment terminal, help call control, receipt choice, error and assistance states | Scan items one at a time with the running total always visible, and state the price of each scanned item as it is added, so the total is never a surprise at payment. Let the person remove an item or correct a quantity before paying, with the total updated at once, and never charge for an item that was removed. For an age-restricted item, hold that item aside and call for a check without blocking the rest of the shop. Offer a plain help control on every screen that calls a staff member and states the kiosk number, so help arrives at the right machine. Never end the session silently: warn before any time limit in more than one sense, and let the person extend it. Offer spoken guidance for every step with a private listening route, and tactile keys where keys are present, so the kiosk works without vision. Keep the payment step on the same screen as the total, show the amount before the person pays, and confirm success or failure at once with a receipt choice. When a scanned item cannot be priced, or the bagging area reports an unexpected item, state the problem in plain words and offer a retry or staff help rather than restarting the shop. Distinct from the QR code payment at a till row, which covers paying by scanning a code, and from the parcel locker pickup row, which collects an order rather than paying for a shop. Cross-check the QR code payment at a till row and the age verification gate row |
| School parent portal | Follow a child's school life from one place: attendance, grades, messages from teachers, permission slips, and payments | Child switcher, attendance record, grade summary, teacher messages, permission request list, payment list, summary settings, unsubscribe and removal controls | Keep each child separate where several children attend, with a clear switcher naming the child, so one child's record is never mistaken for another's. Reach the guardian only by invitation from the school, accepted within a stated window, and let the guardian choose daily or weekly summaries, or none at all. Show missing work, upcoming work, and class activities in the summary; where the summary carries no grades, say so plainly and give a route to ask the teacher. Keep permission requests as a list with the answer due date, a plain approve or decline for each, and a record of what was answered and when. Show payments owed and paid with receipts, and never take a payment without showing the amount and date first. Let the guardian unsubscribe or remove themselves at any time, and state what removal takes away. Never show another family's child, and never let the guardian into the child's own classroom view. Distinct from the guardian approval request row, which answers one approval rather than holding the whole relationship, and from the tenant portal row, which gathers one tenancy rather than a school record. Cross-check the guardian approval request row and the tenant portal row |
| Live translated captions | Follow spoken content in another language as translated text, live, without losing the conversation | Caption area, source-language statement, translation language choice, live caption toggle, live translation toggle, transcript view, caption preferences | Generate captions from the speech as it is spoken and translate them into the person's chosen language, keeping the translated text beside the speaker's words rather than replacing them without a trace. State the source language and the target language plainly, and offer the original-language captions alongside the translation where the person wants to check a word. Process captions on the device where the platform offers it, and state plainly when translated text is sent elsewhere for translation, so the person knows what leaves the device. Never store the caption text unless the person asks for a transcript, and say so where the captions disappear when the call or media ends. Show uncertain wording plainly and correct it in place when later context clarifies it, rather than holding text back until it is certain. Keep the caption area clear of controls and let the person move, resize, and restyle it without leaving the call. Offer captions and translation before joining as well as during, alongside the other call settings, and remember the choice for the next call. Never rely on translated captions where exact wording matters, such as medical, legal, or assessed settings, and say so, offering a human captioner route instead. Distinct from the live captions row, which renders speech as text in the same language, and from the translated content row, which translates written content rather than live speech. Cross-check the live captions row and the translated content row |
| Real-time departure board | Read the next departures from a shared board in a station or stop, at a glance, without studying it | Line and destination list, departure time or countdown, platform, disruption notice, transport-kind grouping, delay marker, static layout | Keep the board static: never rotate content away, so every departure stays visible at a glance. Group departures by transport kind — bus, tram, local train, long-distance train — so the eye lands in the right section before reading rows. Put line, direction, and departure time first in each row, with the platform beside them, and keep delays and disruptions prominent with a plain statement of what changed. Show bus and tram times as a countdown in minutes and train times as clock times, and state a delay as added minutes rather than a rewritten time. Mark delays and disruptions in shape and words as well as colour, so nothing depends on colour alone, and keep text large enough to read from a few steps back with strong contrast. Add a small transport-kind symbol beside each group to anchor the eye where groups change. Distinct from the route planning row, which orders stops before travel, and from the travel disruption rebooking row, which replaces a booking after a disruption. Cross-check the route planning row and the travel disruption rebooking row |
| Care decision card | Tell a person which care to seek, from whom, and how quickly, for a symptom or situation | Urgency tier, action heading, symptom list, contact route, nearest-service link | Offer three tiers — routine, urgent, emergency — each with one action in its heading, such as "Speak to a clinician if:" or "Call the emergency number now if:", followed by a short bullet list of the symptoms or situations that belong to it. State the urgency in words in the heading, never by colour alone, so the tier is clear without seeing the colour and clear to someone acting under stress. Keep each list short and specific: name concrete symptoms, use exact measures instead of vague words, and add a final catch-all bullet for any other worrying symptom in the urgent tier. Signpost to one service in the heading where possible, at most two, and put any further services at the end of the body; in an emergency card include only the emergency route and, where it helps, a link to find the nearest emergency department, with no other links. Order cards on a page by likelihood and seriousness for the condition at hand, and use more than one card only where different severities need different care, keeping the total small so the page reassures rather than alarms. Do not use the card for general information, for a warning, to stop a journey unless the point is to seek help now, or to point to another organisation; use a warning callout, an interruption page, or a plain link for those. Distinct from the emergency assistance request row, which sends a request for help, and from the telehealth video consultation row, which holds a consultation; this card only decides which care to seek. Cross-check the emergency assistance request row and the telehealth video consultation row |
| Recurring donation | Give repeatedly on a stated interval, with the terms and the way out stated up front | One-time and recurring choice, amount, interval, first-payment date, cancel and amend route, receipt | Before asking for money, answer the two questions donors ask first on the same page: what the work is, and how donations are used — most sites tested answered neither on their homepage. Make the donation action findable from anywhere on the site with a plainly labelled link, and keep the form to one page asking only for essential information. Present the recurring choice as a genuine, equally weighted option beside the one-time gift; never hide the one-time option, repeat the monthly ask after it was declined, or make cancelling harder than signing up. State the recurring terms in full at the point of commitment: amount, date of first payment, interval, that gifts continue until the donor makes contact, and exactly how to cancel or amend. Keep the authorisation record for at least two years after cancellation, and confirm each gift and each change in writing with the new terms restated. Distinct from the subscription plan change row, which alters a service plan rather than a gift, and from the recurring payment setup row, which arranges a payment rather than a donation. Cross-check the subscription cancellation row and the payment failure recovery row |
| Indoor wayfinding | Find a destination inside a building from a directory, signs, or a map | Directory, directional signs, room identification signs, orientation map, you-are-here marker | Use one consistent name for each destination across the directory, the directional signs, and the maps, so a name learned in one place works in all of them. Give every permanent room or space an identification sign with raised characters, uppercase and sans serif, between 5/8 inch and 2 inches high, duplicated in contracted Braille directly below the text, mounted with the baseline between 48 and 60 inches above the floor on the latch side of the door, clear of the door swing. Size visual characters on directional and directory signs to the viewing distance, at least 5/8 inch at close range and larger as distance grows, mounted at least 40 inches above the floor. Use a non-glare finish with strong light-on-dark or dark-on-light contrast and plain conventional letterforms, never italic, script, or decorative type. Always show a you-are-here marker on orientation maps so the visitor can anchor the route, and keep the route drawn in the same orientation as the map is read. Distinct from the map comparison row, which compares outdoor routes, and from the guided route navigation row, which guides a journey outdoors. Cross-check the map comparison row |
| Hotel room controls | Control a guest's own room during a stay — temperature, lighting, curtains, privacy | Wall panel or phone control, thermostat, light and curtain controls, privacy setting, scene presets | Place the operable parts of room controls within reach — between 15 and 48 inches above the floor for an unobstructed forward or side approach, lower where reaching over an obstruction — with clear floor space for a forward or side approach at every control point, including bedside controls, and never block a control with furniture or a deep shelf. Make every control operable with one hand, without tight grasping, pinching, or twisting of the wrist, and with no more than 5 pounds of force; prefer push plates, levers, and flip switches over knobs that need a full-hand grip and turn. Group controls by what they do — temperature, lights, curtains, privacy — with the current state shown in words as well as in position, and name scenes by what they are for rather than by number alone. Keep a working control in the room itself even where a phone control is offered, so the room stays usable when the phone is flat or offline. Distinct from the smart home dashboard row, which organises a whole home, and from the hotel self check-in row, which covers arrival rather than the stay. Cross-check the smart home dashboard row |
| University course registration | Plan a timetable first, then register for courses without losing the plan | Course catalogue, timetable planner, saved draft, clash and prerequisite check, waitlist, confirmation | Let the student plan first and register second: build the timetable from the catalogue, then register from the saved plan, so a failed registration never loses the plan. Make every label and step self-descriptive, so a student can tell what a control does and what happens next without prior training, and keep the course listing structure consistent — the same layout, navigation, and grouping of lecture and discussion sections — so a planned timetable can be scanned and compared at a glance. Surface problems while the student is still planning: show clashes, missing prerequisites, and full sections before the registration window opens, and let the student save a draft or mock timetable to fix problems in advance. When a registration step fails, the message leads directly to the fix — an alternative section, a waitlist place, or a replacement course — rather than only naming the problem; measure recovery, not only task time, since stress rises measurably after failed attempts. Confirm the registration in writing with the final timetable, and state plainly what changes afterwards and where to change it. Distinct from the shared resource booking row, which books a shared room or item, and from the appointment management row, which books a single service slot. Cross-check the shared resource booking row |
| Veterinary appointment booking | Book a vet visit for a named animal, in person or by video, with the animal's record carried into the booking | Animal profile, service choice, vet choice, time choice, video option, pre-visit history form, fee statement, follow-up route | Start from the animal, not the person: choose the animal from the saved profile first, so the animal's record and history travel into the booking. Offer the choices in order — animal, service, vet, then time — so each choice narrows the next, and never offer a vet who cannot take the case. Offer video only for minor concerns and only where the animal already has an established care relationship; a clinician decides suitability case by case, and an emergency is never offered a video slot. State the fee when booking, before the visit is confirmed. Send a history form ahead of the visit and ask for it back in good time, so the visit starts from the animal's story rather than a blank record. Send the video link by email and text, name a second contact route in case the link fails, and advise using a phone for flexibility. Where the clinician decides the animal must be seen in person, offer the in-clinic visit as the next step and carry any prepaid video fee toward it. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books a person's own appointment, and from the telehealth video consultation row, which covers a video visit for a person rather than an animal. Cross-check the appointment management row |
| Rental property viewing and tenancy application | Find a home to rent and apply for it, with the listing, the viewing, and the decision each stated plainly | Intent-first search, listing card, photo set, viewing booking, pre-screening questions, application form, decision notice | Let the search start from intent — place, price, size, and date — with filters that behave the same on every search, so two identical searches never return different results for the same home. Make each listing card answer the questions a renter asks first: bedrooms, one specific price, included amenities, and the condition of the home, with clear photos of every room so a visit is not needed to learn the basics. Keep the listing current: check availability against the person responsible for the home at least every two days, and remove a home that is no longer available rather than leaving it listed. Ask only for what the decision needs: verifiable income and employment, references, and identification shown but never copied; a credit check only with the applicant's written approval. Judge every applicant on the same documented criteria — affordability, references, and conduct — and never refuse on a protected ground such as family status. Use personal information only for the tenancy it was given for, and say so where it is collected. Give the decision in writing, with the next step stated either way. Distinct from the tenant portal row, which covers life after the tenancy starts. Cross-check the tenant portal row |
| Insurance claim submission | Send a claim after a loss, with what happened, the evidence, and the wait all stated | Loss statement, evidence checklist, claim reference, status view, decision notice, complaint route | Ask for notification as soon as practical after the loss, and take the first statement in plain words: what happened, when, and where. At notification, state what the claim needs, how much time there is to file, how long processing will take, and whether the loss is above the excess — before the claimant gathers anything. Give a claim reference number at once, and use it on every later message so the claim can be followed without repeating the story. Acknowledge the claim within three working days of notification, and arrange any assessor visit within three working days of the claim. List the evidence the claim needs — photographs of the damage, a police or incident report number where one applies, receipts and bills — and name anything missing within a stated time rather than going quiet. Show the claim's state throughout — received, under assessment, decided — with the next step and the date it is due. State the decision and settlement times up front, with a settlement window that depends on the size of the claim. Distinct from the warranty claim row, which covers a faulty product, and from the expense claim submission row, which covers a receipted cost. Cross-check the warranty claim row |
| Tax filing and self-assessment | File a tax return one section at a time, with a review step before sending | Eligibility check, registration, section list, figures checklist, save-and-resume, review step, payment statement | Start with an eligibility check that answers, in a few questions, whether a return is needed at all, before asking anyone to register. Ask for registration or reactivation next, and state the reference the return needs before the first section opens. Gather the figures before the return starts: list what each section needs — employment, self-employment, property, and other income — so nothing is hunted for mid-section. Take it one section at a time, one question per page, saving as the person goes, so the return can be left and resumed without losing entered figures. Show progress honestly and let the person correct any mistake before submitting; the review step shows every section's figures together with the amount owed or due back and the payment date, and nothing is sent until the person confirms it. After sending, allow the return to be amended, and state how and when the amount owed is paid, including a plan to pay in instalments where one is offered. Distinct from the expense claim submission row, which covers a receipted cost, and from the document scanner row, which captures a paper record. Cross-check the expense claim submission row |
| Temporary home access code | Give a guest or a worker a time-limited code that opens one door for a stated period | Code creation, validity window, door choice, share route, revoke control, entry record | Create the code for one person and one door, with the validity window stated as a date and time range or a recurring slot, so the code never looks open-ended. State the code, the door it opens, and the expiry before it is shared, and send the code to the person it is for, stating the door and the expiry with it, so the code never arrives without its limits. Offer single-use codes for deliveries or service visits, so a code that was used once cannot open the door again. Keep a list of everyone with access and their authorised periods, with adding and removing one person as separate actions, so removing one guest never disturbs the others. Provide a revoke route that ends access at once, and revoke a code the moment it is lost or no longer trusted, stating plainly that the code no longer works. Keep a plain entry record naming when each code was used, so the household can see who entered and when without reading a technical log. Distinct from the digital car key sharing row, which shares a vehicle key, and from the guest mode row, which lends a device session rather than entry to a place. Cross-check the digital car key sharing row and the guest mode row |
| Car rental and car sharing booking | Book a vehicle for a stated period, with the full cost and the handover terms stated before payment | Pickup place, dates and class, full-cost statement, deposit statement, pickup window, condition check, fuel or charge level, damage route | Choose pickup place, dates, and vehicle class first, and show the full cost once those are entered: the headline price includes every compulsory charge, including fuel and age surcharges, out-of-hours and one-way fees, and taxes, never added late in the booking or at the desk. State the deposit and any excess clearly and prominently before payment, no more than one step from the headline price, with a plain warning to check both before completing the booking, and state when the deposit is refunded and any pre-authorisation unblocked after the rental ends. State the fuel policy during booking — whether the vehicle returns with the same level it started with — and the fuel or charge level at handover, so the return level is agreed before the keys change hands. State the pickup window and the return procedure with its inspection timescales, so the person can be present for the checks that matter. Check the vehicle's condition with the person at pickup and at return, with a written report and a diagram noting pre-existing damage that the person checks and agrees before accepting, and take photographic evidence of the state in which the vehicle is left. Where damage is found, state the dispute procedure during booking and again at the inspection, with the right to challenge, the evidence and the repair-cost calculation provided for any charge, and a reasonable time to challenge before the charge is taken from the card. Distinct from the ride booking row, which books a trip with a driver, and from the paired handover record row, which records a handover rather than booking the vehicle. Cross-check the ride booking row and the paired handover record row |
| Government document application | Apply for or renew an official document online, with identity checks, documents, payment, and tracking in one flow | Eligibility check, stepped form, document upload, photo capture guidance, identity confirmation route, fee and timescale statement, save-and-return control, application tracking view, help route | State eligibility, the fee, and the expected timescale before the first step, so nobody starts a flow they cannot finish or cannot afford. Ask one thing per page in a stepped form, and show the step count and the time the flow takes up front. Let a partially completed application be saved and returned to within a stated window, and state that window plainly. Ask for documents only when the step needs them, name each document before asking for it, and give capture guidance before the upload rather than an error after it. Confirm identity through a stated route, and where another person must confirm identity, say who can do it and what they must do. Show a review of all answers before submission, with each answer changeable in place. State before submission that the current document becomes invalid the moment the renewal is submitted, so nobody travels on a cancelled document. After submission, show tracking as named stages — submitted, identity checked, decision made, document sent — with the date of each stage, state how and when documents are returned, and where more information is needed, say what is needed and how to supply it. Distinct from the identity verification onboarding row, which covers proving identity before an account opens, and from the wizard row, which covers multi-step tasks in general without fee, timescale, or tracking rules. Cross-check the identity verification onboarding row, the wizard row, and the order tracking row |
| Mortgage application | Apply for a home loan online with affordability known early, a stated document list, and a stated decision time | Affordability estimate, stepped application, document checklist, save-and-resume control, status tracking view, refusal reasons route | Give an affordability estimate before asking for detail, based on income and regular outgoings, and show how the estimate changes if rates rise, so nobody completes a full application for a loan they cannot afford. Show the full document checklist before the first step — identity, address, income, bank statements, and deposit proof — and let each document be uploaded when its step is reached, with a stated route for documents that are not yet to hand. Break the application into steps with save and resume, and keep entered answers when the person leaves and returns. Acknowledge a complete application within a stated number of working days, and where it is incomplete, name the missing documents in the same acknowledgement. Give the decision within a stated time from receipt of all documents, or, where the decision will take longer, give the reasons and the expected timeframe. Where an application is refused, give the reasons in writing, and offer a stated route to ask for them where they were not given. Show status as named stages — received, documents checked, decision made — with the date of each stage, and never show a silent wait. Distinct from the rental property viewing and tenancy application row, which covers renting a home rather than borrowing to buy one, and from the wizard row, which covers multi-step tasks in general. Cross-check the rental property viewing and tenancy application row, the wizard row, and the order tracking row |
| Smartwatch fall detection and emergency SOS | Get help after a hard fall, or call for help by holding a control, from a wrist device | Fall alert, immobility wait, countdown display, large cancel control, manual SOS hold control, location sharing, emergency contact notice | When a hard fall is detected, tap the wrist, sound an alarm, and show an alert with two plain actions: call for help now, or dismiss it where no help is needed. Where the wearer is moving, wait for a response and never call on its own. Where the wearer stays still and does not respond, start a stated countdown with the remaining time shown in large figures, and offer one large cancel control that stops the call. When the countdown ends without a response, call emergency services on its own, share the location, and play a recorded message stating that a fall was detected. Offer a manual route as well: press and hold one control until a short countdown ends, and cancel by releasing the hold before it ends. State up front that not all falls are detected, and that a connection is needed for the call. Distinct from the emergency assistance request row, which covers a manual request from any screen, and from the safety check-in row, which covers a planned check-in with a response window. Cross-check the emergency assistance request row and the safety check-in row |
