| Pension dashboard | See every retirement income source in one place, with what it will pay in today's money and what changes it | Total balance and projected income, breakdown by income source, per-plan detail, projection calculator, lost-account finder, plain-language explanations, bar-chart comparison | Show the total saved so far beside the projected retirement income on the first screen, and state the projection in today's money, adjusted for rising prices, so a figure decades away reads as spending power rather than a sum that will buy less. Combine every income source — state, workplace, and private — into one total and one projection, and never present one provider's figure as the whole picture. Layer the detail: the first layer carries the total and the projection, the second layer breaks the total down by income source, and the third layer gives each plan's specifics, so no screen presents all three depths at once. Let the person change the retirement age, and where offered salary or hours worked, and see the projected income move at once; show optimistic, neutral, and pessimistic outcomes rather than a single figure presented as certain, and state the assumptions behind every projection in plain words. Help find accounts the person has lost track of, such as pensions left with past employers, with a stated route to locate them. Use plain words for money terms — a lump sum as a single payment, a regular pension as payments for life — and explain rising prices with a concrete comparison; prefer bar charts when amounts are compared. Where the dashboard cannot change figures itself, say so and point to where the change is made, rather than offering a control that does nothing. Keep the dashboard usable on a phone, and protect it with strong sign-in, since it gathers a person's whole retirement picture. Distinct from the billing overview row, which summarises recurring charges, and from the transaction history row, which lists money already moved, and from the data visualisation row, which covers charts in general. Cross-check the data visualisation row, the progressive disclosure row, and the billing overview row |
| Visa application | Apply for permission to enter a country | Travel purpose and dates, document checklist, biometrics appointment, decision wait, refusal and challenge route | Start from the trip itself: the purpose of travel and the planned arrival and departure dates shape the route, the documents, and the earliest application date, so ask for them before the detail that depends on them. Give a document checklist that states what each document must show, and let the person check each one off as it is gathered. Where biometrics apply, offer an appointment at a stated place and state the window for attending it, so the appointment is never a surprise at the end. State the decision wait plainly before submission, and tell the person not to book travel until the decision arrives. Track the application by named stages. Where the application is refused, state the reason in plain words and name the route to challenge it, with its time limit, rather than a bare refusal. Keep the application saved so it can be resumed, and never ask for the same document twice. Distinct from the Government document application row, which covers the shared flow for any official document, and from the identity verification onboarding row, which proves identity for a new account. Cross-check the Government document application row and the appointment management row |
| Parking fine payment and appeal | Pay or challenge a parking fine from the notice itself | Fine amount, reduced amount and deadline, evidence window, notice-reference payment, appeal route, admission rule | State the fine amount, the reduced amount and its deadline where one applies, and the evidence window all at once, on the same screen as the notice reference. Let the person pay by the notice reference and the vehicle registration, without an account, and keep the payment route and the appeal route side by side so the choice is made with both options visible. State plainly that paying counts as an admission and closes any open challenge, so payment and challenge are never offered as if both could happen. For the appeal, state what evidence helps, how long a decision takes, and what happens while the appeal is open. Where a reduced amount applies, show the deadline beside the amount, and never show the reduced amount after its deadline has passed. Keep the notice reference visible on every step, and confirm the payment or the appeal with a reference the person can keep. Distinct from the parking session payment row, which pays for a timed stay, and from the transaction dispute row, which disputes a charge already paid. Cross-check the parking session payment row and the transaction dispute row |
| Phone voice menu | Reach a person without navigating a menu | Reserved skip digit or word, plain-words options, patient timing, directed-dialog fallback, transfer to a person | Offer a reserved digit or word, such as 0 or "help", that skips the menu at any time and goes straight to a person, and name it in the first option so the person never has to discover it. Use plain words throughout, stating each option before its number, with one-word answers where a choice is needed. Wait for slow speakers: pause between options, allow extra time as a setting, and never cut off a hesitant reply. Where the menu cannot serve the task, offer a short directed exchange or a transfer to a person, and where an error persists, move the person to a human rather than looping the menu. Human help must never require navigating a complex menu first. Distinct from the voice hybrid flows in the conversational interfaces trend note, which mix voice and screen, and from the in-product help row, which covers help inside a screen. Cross-check the in-product help row |
| Mobile cheque deposit | Deposit a paper cheque with the phone camera, with the amount checked before it is sent | Restrictive endorsement, capture guidance, amount match, cut-off time, paper retention, deposit states | Ask for the restrictive endorsement on the back of the cheque before capture, so the paper cannot be deposited again through another route. Give capture guidance that keeps all four corners and the printed line legible: a dark, flat background, even light, and a retake where any corner, figure, or the printed line cannot be read. Match the entered amount against the amount read from the image and flag a mismatch before submission, so an unreadable figure never becomes a wrong deposit. State the cut-off time before the deposit is sent, and state that a deposit after the cut-off counts as received on the next working day. Tell the person to keep the paper cheque until the deposit is confirmed, and to mark or destroy it afterwards so it cannot be deposited twice. Confirm the deposit at once with a reference, and track it by named states until the funds are available. Distinct from the document scanner row, which captures a paper record without acting on its amount, and from the code scanner row, which reads a printed code rather than a payment instrument. Cross-check the document scanner row, the code scanner row, and the receipt capture row |
| Home energy usage dashboard | Show how much energy a home uses and what it costs, over time and by device | Use over time, cost beside use, per-device breakdown, previous-period comparison, threshold alert | Show use over time with the cost beside it in money as well as in units, so a change in use reads as a change in cost. Break use down by appliance or circuit where the meter supports it, and name the largest loads first so the person sees where the energy goes. Compare against a previous period on the same view, and mark high and low use beside the figures. Alert the person when use or cost passes a threshold they set, naming the cause and the next step, and never alert without a route to act. Keep the figures live where the meter reports them, and state how fresh the figures are where they lag. Distinct from the smart home dashboard row, which controls devices rather than explaining usage, and from the billing overview row, which explains one bill rather than use over time. Cross-check the smart home dashboard row, the billing overview row, and the usage allowance row |
| Benefits claim application | Apply for public financial support online, starting from the need rather than the programme name | Life-event entry, eligibility check, shared multi-programme application, plain-language questions, contextual help, progress and save, document upload, decision and payment statement, tracking view | Start from the life event or need — a job lost, a child born, a lasting health condition — rather than the name of a programme, so the person does not need to know which programme fits before starting. Let one application cover every programme the person may qualify for, sharing each answer across programmes so nothing is asked twice. Show eligibility as the answers are given, in plain words, and say at once when enrolment can happen the same day. Let anyone check eligibility and browse the flow without an account; ask for an account only when the application is submitted. Ask questions in plain language, a small set per screen, with help beside the question that needs it rather than behind a hover or on a separate page, and show progress honestly with saving at every step so the application can be left and resumed. Ask for documents only when the step needs them, and where a document is missing, let the application be sent anyway, naming what is still needed and the date it is due. State the decision wait and the first payment date before submission, and track the claim by named stages — received, decided, paid — with the next step and the date it is due. Distinct from the Government document application row, which covers applying for an official document, and from the Tax filing and self-assessment row, which covers a return rather than a claim for support. Cross-check the Government document application row, the Tax filing and self-assessment row, and the Wizard row |
| Blood donation appointment booking | Book a time to give blood, plasma, or platelets, with eligibility settled before the visit | Eligibility check, next eligible date, donation-type choice, centre choice, pre-visit questionnaire, booking, move and cancel, reminders, donation history and tally | Check eligibility first, before any date is offered, and name the next date the person can give where they are not yet eligible, so a wasted trip is never booked. Offer the donation types the centre takes — whole blood, plasma, and platelets — and let the person choose before the centre, since each type runs at different places and takes a different time. Let the person pick a centre with its opening hours and directions, and remember a preferred centre so the next booking starts there. Let the health questionnaire be filled in before arrival, in short plain blocks, so the visit itself is shorter and the time at the centre is spent giving rather than answering. Book, move, and cancel from the same place, months ahead where the centre allows, and show the booking at once with what to bring. Remind the person before the appointment and again when the next donation is due, and keep a record of past donations with the tally, so the next eligible date is always visible. Keep a walk-in route where the centre takes walk-ins, and say plainly where booking ahead is required. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books a time with a service without the eligibility gate, and from the medication refill request row, which repeats a prescription rather than booking a donation. Cross-check the appointment management row, the appointment reminder row, and the location selection row |
| Virtual court hearing | Take part in a formal hearing by video or phone, with the same rules as a hearing in person | Hearing notice and join link, connection test, waiting room, name and role, speaker view, recording rule, phone route, reconnection route | State plainly that the hearing is formal and the same rules apply as in person, so nothing about the hearing is treated as casual because it is on a screen. Send the join link with the hearing notice — from the court list, portal, or email — with the date and time in writing, and never through a link forwarded by someone unknown. Offer a test call before the hearing so the camera, microphone, and speakers are checked early, and ask the person to do it as soon as they can, so a failed check never delays the hearing itself. Join by video from a computer, tablet, or phone, or by phone where video is not possible, with the phone route given in the same notice. Hold the person in a waiting room until the court opens the hearing, and ask for their full name with their role beside it, so every party can see who is present and in what capacity, with an alias where the court provides one. Keep the microphone muted when not speaking and the camera off when not required to be seen, and keep the room focused on the device producing the most sound, so the active speaker stays prominent. State before the hearing starts whether it is recorded and by whom, and show the warning not to record at the point of joining, so the person never has to guess; taking notes in writing stays allowed. Sit in a private, quiet, well-lit space, alone unless the court has given permission for someone to be present, and dressed as for court. Stay connected during a short adjournment with the camera off and the microphone muted. Say what to do if the connection drops — who to contact and how to rejoin — and keep those details beside the person during the hearing so they can act at once. Distinct from the video-call waiting room row, which admits people to a call without the formal roles and record, and from the telehealth video consultation row, which checks identity and privacy for a consultation rather than a proceeding. Cross-check the video-call waiting room row, the live call controls row, and the telehealth video consultation row |
| Remote online notarization | Have a document witnessed and sealed over live video by a commissioned official, with the same effect as signing in person | Pre-session identity and device check, waiting room, identity check on camera, document review view, in-session signing, recording rule, sealed document delivery, audit record | State plainly that the session carries the same effect as signing in person, and that the signing happens live on video — a recording never stands in for the live session. Before the session, check identity and the device: a current photo ID, a working camera and microphone, a supported browser, and a steady connection, and offer a test run before the day so a failed check never delays the session itself. Hold the person in a waiting room until the official admits them, and ask for full name with role beside it, so every party can see who is present and in what capacity. Verify identity on camera: the person shows the photo ID and answers identity questions, and every signer and witness needs a current verification before the act can proceed. When the document is reviewed, switch the view from the video feed to the document itself, so all parties read it together, and let the person ask questions before signing. Sign only inside the session where the act requires it, by using the sign control and adding the signature while the official watches; where signing ahead is allowed, say so before the session and confirm the signature on the call. State before the session starts whether it is recorded and by whom, and take consent from everyone where recording needs it; where anyone declines, continue without a recording. After signing, let the official add the seal, then deliver the sealed document at once with the retention period for the recording and the audit record stated, so the completed document and its proof stay reachable. Keep a phone or message route beside the session for anything that fails, and say what to do if the connection drops. Distinct from the document signing row, which captures a signature on a document without a live witness, and from the virtual court hearing row, which runs a formal proceeding rather than witnessing a signature, and from the telehealth video consultation row, which checks identity and privacy for a consultation rather than a legal act. Cross-check the document signing row, the virtual court hearing row, and the video-call waiting room row |
| Proctored exam | Sit a timed assessment under supervision online | Identity check, room and desk check, numbered question list, visible timer, autosave, rule-violation warning, resume route, result notice | Check identity with a photo document before the timer starts, and check the room and desk in plain steps, stating what the camera must show and what must be moved out of view, so the checks never arrive as a surprise. Give numbered question navigation with any-order answering and autosave after every answer, so an interrupted sitting resumes where it stopped. Keep the remaining time visible at all times, with a plain warning before the final minutes, and state the result only after submission, never during the sitting. State plainly what is monitored during the sitting and how any recording is used, and warn on each detected breach, locking the exam only after a stated number of warnings, with a route to resume where the rules allow it. Keep a live route to a person for technical failure that never costs the candidate time without saying so. Distinct from the identity verification onboarding row, which verifies a person once for an account rather than for each sitting, and from the wizard row, which covers a stepped flow without the timed assessment and supervision. Cross-check the identity verification onboarding row, the wizard row, and the live call controls row |
| Restaurant online ordering | Order food from one restaurant for pickup or delivery | Readable menu, location choice, modifier groups, dietary filters, favourites and reorder, fulfilment and cost summary, guest checkout | Show the menu as readable web content, never as a file to download, with an order action at the top of every page that opens the current menu for the chosen location. Group dishes under familiar names with plain descriptions and prices shown without opening each dish, and keep the most-ordered dishes easy to find. State every choice before the dish reaches the cart: required choices with one pick, optional choices with a stated limit, and the price of each extra beside it, so the total never surprises. Offer dietary filters that work — vegetarian, vegan, gluten-free, dairy-free, and allergen-friendly — with plain tags on each dish. Settle pickup or delivery, the time, and the full cost early: items, extras, fees, taxes, and an optional tip, each labelled, with no fee appearing for the first time on the final screen. Keep a favourites list and a one-tap reorder for past orders, and let the person check out as a guest without creating an account. After ordering, show the confirmation with the pickup or delivery time, the place, and what to do if something is wrong. Distinct from the QR table ordering row, which orders at the table by scanning a code, from the delivery slot picker row, which chooses a delivery window, and from the pickup point selection row, which chooses a collection point. Cross-check the guest checkout row, the delivery slot picker row, the pickup point selection row, the QR table ordering row, and the order tracking row |
| Virtual museum tour | Explore a collection online as a walk through virtual rooms, with orientation that survives the lack of physical reference points | Room map or mini-map, you-are-here marker, visited and unvisited rooms, room contents, set tour order, freestyle mode, exhibit detail view, deep zoom, narration with pause and replay | A virtual space has no physical reference points, so disorientation is the core failure: keep a persistent map or mini-map visible at all times, showing where the visitor is, which rooms have been visited and which have not, and what each room holds. Let the visitor jump to any room from the map, so the map is a way to move, not only a picture of the place. Use colour coding and plain symbolic markers on the map to make the layout readable at a glance, and let the visitor change the map size and highlight colours where the setting is offered. Offer both a set tour order and freestyle exploration, always showing where the visitor is in the tour and what comes next. Keep each exhibit's information accurate and complete beside the work itself, with a deep zoom that keeps colours true to the work. Keep movement between rooms slow and calm, and honour the visitor's reduced-motion setting rather than playing transitions regardless. Keep the real content — titles, dates, provenance, wall text — in plain page markup behind any three-dimensional layer, so the tour stays keyboard-navigable and screen-reader-legible when the visual layer is off. Keep narration short, around one to two minutes per stop, with pause, replay, and skip. State the connection and loading needs before the visitor enters, so a slow connection never strands them mid-tour. Distinct from the museum audio guide row, which covers audio for a visit in person, and from the indoor wayfinding row, which covers signs and maps inside a physical building, and from the guided tour row, which points out elements inside the product itself. Cross-check the museum audio guide row, the indoor wayfinding row, the media viewer row, and the guided tour row |
| Job application portal | Apply for a job online through a multi-page application form | Multi-page form, progress indicator, save-and-return, labelled controls, clear error messages, review step, submitted application record | Treat a job application as a long multi-page form, and make every page of it keyboard-navigable with labelled controls and error messages placed next to the field they concern, since date pickers and option lists are the most common points of failure. Group questions logically and show progress across the whole application, so the person always knows how much remains. Let a draft be saved at any time and returned to later from the account, and state plainly before submission that a submitted application cannot be changed, with a review step before the final submit. Keep the submitted application visible in the account with its current state named. Distinct from the form flow row, which covers a form without the hiring decision, and from the wizard row, which covers a stepped flow without the application. Cross-check the form flow row and the wizard row |
| Waste collection schedule and missed bin reporting | Check when waste is collected for an address and report a missed collection | Address-personalised schedule, bin types, present-by time, collection window, evening reminder, missed-collection report, re-collection date, contamination feedback | Show the collection schedule for the address entered, with each bin type and its collection day, and state the time each bin must be out by and the window within which collection happens, so a bin left out late is never a surprise. Send a reminder the evening before collection day stating which bins go out, offered as an email or text sign-up for the address. Let a missed collection be reported only after the stated time on the due day, with the address and the bin type on the report, and acknowledge the report at once with a reference number where the service issues one and the re-collection date stated in plain words; services differ, so state the actual report and re-collection windows rather than promising one fixed time. Where a bin was not collected because it was wrongly presented or contaminated, say so plainly with what to fix before the next collection, rather than leaving the report unanswered. Keep each missed bin as a separate report where more than one bin was missed. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books a time with a service, from the order tracking row, which follows a parcel rather than a report, and from the service status notice row, which states a service state without the report flow. Cross-check the appointment management row, the order tracking row, and the service status notice row |
| Smart TV programme guide | Find what is on live television and plan viewing | Channel grid, time axis, current programme mark, now-and-next strip, programme detail, reminder, legibility | Show a channel grid against a time axis with the programme now showing marked, so the present moment is always visible. Keep a now-and-next strip over the picture for quick checking without leaving what is playing. Show the programme's start and end times with its description beside the entry before the person tunes in. Let a reminder be set on a future programme, with the reminder stated in plain words at the time it is set. Keep the guide legible at viewing distance and usable by people with visual or hearing impairments, with programme information available in the guide and while changing channels, and state how to get help using the guide. Distinct from the real-time departure board row, which shows a time grid for departures rather than programmes, and from the television layout, which covers focus navigation and ten-foot legibility without the guide grid. Cross-check the real-time departure board row and the television layout |
| Contact a GP surgery online | Ask a GP practice about a health problem online and receive the practice's reply, without assuming the outcome is an appointment | Problem description form, direct slot booking, document request, reply inbox, response time statement, urgent-care route | Offer two routes under one entry point: describe a health problem in a structured form, or book a directly bookable slot where the practice offers one; label the first route by what the person does, such as asking about a health problem, rather than by the service's internal name. Say plainly before the form is sent that the practice decides the next step — advice, a phone call, or an appointment — so nobody waits for a booking that was never made, and state when to expect a reply and where it will arrive. Deliver replies in the same place the request was made, in a messages area, and say so at the point of sending, since people expect a message rather than a phone call or a letter. Keep document requests — a fit note, a letter, or other information — in the same entry point, since people look for them alongside appointments rather than under documents. Where the practice does not offer a route, say so and name the route that is available instead, rather than showing a dead end; never show a booking route that leads nowhere. Keep an urgent-care route visible on the same screen, so a problem that needs same-day care is never trapped behind a form with a next-working-day reply. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books a slot directly, and from the telehealth video consultation row, which holds the consultation, and from the care decision card row, which decides which care to seek. Cross-check the appointment management row, the telehealth video consultation row, and the care decision card row |
| Vaccination appointment booking and digital certificate | Book a vaccination appointment and keep the record of it in one place | Eligibility check, dose schedule, appointment choice, confirmation with place and time, arrival check-in, certificate record, scannable code, paper copy, correction route | Check eligibility before offering times, and state plainly who the appointment is for and what to bring. Where a course needs more than one dose, book the next dose at the same time as the first, with the gap between doses stated, rather than leaving the person to return and start again. Keep the booking to short steps with one question each, and keep a phone route open for anyone who cannot book online, with the same times available by phone as online. Confirm the appointment at once with the place, date, and time, what to bring, and how to move or cancel it, and send a reminder carrying the same details. On arrival, check the person in and record whether they arrived early, on time, or late, with a plain next step for each. Keep the certificate beside the appointment in the same place: the record states the vaccine, the date, and the place it was given, with a scannable code prominent for fast access and the same record available on paper with the same code, so no one needs a phone to prove it. Keep the code itself unreadable to a bystander — the details behind it stay hidden until scanned — and state plainly that the record shows only what happened, never a judgement about what it permits. Where a detail is wrong, offer a correction route in the same place, and keep records for different people separate where one account holds several. Distinct from the appointment management row, which books a time with a service, from the pass and ticket display row, which shows a code for entry, and from the health record sharing row, which shares a record with a clinician. Cross-check the appointment management row, the pass and ticket display row, and the health record sharing row |
| Live-viewing app | Watch live television or a live stream in an app, and tell live content apart from recorded content at a glance | Live label or live section, programme or stream list, player, join-in-progress statement, remaining-time indicator, channel or stream switcher, browsing footer, secondary actions, captions control | Feature live content prominently, in a dedicated live section or a first tab, so watching starts in one or two actions, and mark live items with a plain live label so they are never mistaken for recorded titles. When a person joins a programme already in progress, state whether playback starts live or from the beginning, and show how much of the programme remains. Keep playback the primary action, with secondary actions — start over, record, favourite — available in the same order everywhere they appear. Let the person browse other channels or streams without leaving playback, through a footer or panel that opens and closes by the same gesture, stays legible over the picture, and marks the item now playing. Give instant visible feedback on a channel change, naming the programme now selected, so the wait for the stream to load reads as progress rather than a dead screen. Never start full-screen immersive playback on its own; let the person choose when playback starts. For long-form video, show thumbnail previews while scrubbing so a scene can be recognised before seeking. Make every player control reachable by keyboard as well as touch, including play, pause, seeking, volume, captions, and playback speed. Distinct from the Smart TV programme guide row, which maps what is on across channels rather than the act of watching, from the Live broadcast chat overlay row, which layers messages over a live picture, and from the Picture-in-picture row, which keeps a video playing while the person does something else. Cross-check the Smart TV programme guide row and the Live broadcast chat overlay row |
| Passport renewal | Renew a travel document online without giving up travel plans by surprise | Eligibility check, document and photo upload, fee total, review step, tracking view, identity-confirmation route, response window | Check eligibility before the first step, and let the answers decide the route: where the person does not qualify for the online route, say so at once and name the route that does apply, rather than letting them complete a form that cannot be accepted. State the fee total before payment, with each part of the fee named, and state the expected wait before submission, so nobody pays for a document that arrives too late for travel. Ask for the digital photo with the requirements stated before upload — framing, background, and file shape — and where a photo is rejected, state the reason in plain words with a retake route, never a bare rejection. Show a review of all answers before submission, with each answer changeable in place. State before submission that the current document becomes invalid the moment the renewal is submitted, so nobody travels on a cancelled document, and state plainly how long the person will be without a valid document. After submission, track by named stages with the date of each stage, including identity confirmation where it applies, and where more information is needed, state what is needed, how to supply it, and the deadline for replying. Keep the old document with the person rather than asking for it to be sent away, since it stays proof of identity even when expired. Where the online route cannot be rushed, say so before submission and name the faster route where one exists, rather than promising speed the route cannot give. Distinct from the Government document application row, which covers the shared flow for any official document without the renewal-specific eligibility, photo, and invalid-on-submission rules, and from the Visa application row, which applies for permission to enter a country rather than renewing a document already held. Cross-check the Government document application row, the Visa application row, and the travel document checklist row |
| Meal planning | Plan meals for the days ahead and turn the plan into one shopping list | Week view by day and meal, recipe slots, combined ingredient list, serving counts, shop-department order, shared-list handoff | Put recipes on a calendar by day and meal, with the week as the default view; moving a recipe to another day moves its ingredients with it. Generate one combined list from the whole plan in one action; merge repeated ingredients into a single line with summed amounts, converting units where they differ, and name each contributing recipe under the merged line. Carry the serving count from the plan into the combined amounts. Sort the final list by shop department so the shop is walked in one pass; keep bought items visible in a separate section rather than deleting them. Keep the plan, the list, and the shopping state linked: editing the plan after the list is made updates the list or states plainly that it does not. Hand the list to a shared household list without duplicating items already on it. Distinct from the Recipe serving scaling row, which changes the serving count for one recipe, from the Cooking mode row, which guides cooking step by step, from the Shared shopping list row, which shares one list in real time, and from the Calendar and scheduling row, which covers time display and slot booking in general. Cross-check the Recipe serving scaling row, the Cooking mode row, and the Shared shopping list row |
| Online course lesson player and progress | Follow a course lesson by lesson, knowing at every point what is done, what is mastered, and what comes next | Chunked lessons, resume position, completion and mastery indicators, progress at lesson, module, and course level, checkpoint questions, practice questions with retry, review queue, syllabus map | Keep lessons in short chunks with a resume position that returns the learner to the exact place left; replaying an earlier lesson never resets progress. State completion separately from mastery: completion records that the content was reached to the end, while mastery records demonstrated understanding per concept, with its own threshold, so a finished course never reads as a mastered one. Show progress at lesson, module, and course level in plain words beside any figure, with a reference frame — expected pace or passing threshold — so the figure can be interpreted, and pair every progress figure with one actionable next step; a dashboard that only visualises, without feedback, shows no measurable benefit. Keep video chunks short with captions on by default. Ask checkpoint questions before the next lesson unlocks, and let practice questions be tried a small fixed number of times — three is the documented shape — with immediate feedback after each attempt and a worked explanation once the answer is right or the attempts run out; keep the submitted answer visible beside its feedback and state the remaining attempts before another submission. Offer a review queue of due items ordered by predicted recall, with failed items returning sooner and new items introduced at a limited rate, and show the count due so the queue can be cleared in one session. Let the syllabus work as both a map and a progress record, showing where the learner is and what remains. Distinct from the media chapters row, which marks points inside one recording; the enrolment-wide student view is a separate pattern. Cross-check the media chapters row |
| Online voter registration and mail-in ballot tracking | Register to vote online, request a mail-in ballot, and follow it to acceptance, with the polling place beside it | Eligibility and routing questions, one-question-per-page flow, duplicate check, written confirmation, ballot request and tracking view, signature-cure route, polling-place lookup | Start with eligibility and routing questions inside the flow, and state eligibility, deadlines, and timescales before the first step, so nobody starts a registration they cannot complete. Ask one question per page, with the page heading as the question, a back link on every page, and a forward control labelled for continuing; ask each piece of information once and carry answers forward. Show only the questions the person's jurisdiction needs, with jurisdiction-specific instructions beside the related question. Prompt the person to check whether they are already registered before starting, to avoid duplicates, and point to the online option where one is offered. Supply the correct election-office address automatically rather than asking the person to find it. Give a written confirmation on submission and offer notification sign-up for registration updates. Support multiple languages and screen-reader use, allow font-size and contrast adjustment, use plain readable language, and state alternate routes — by mail or in person — where online registration is not possible. Keep the ballot request and its tracking in one flow: one sign-up covers notifications for the request, the sending, and the return. Show named stages in plain words — sent, received, accepted — with the date of each stage, and never present "received" as "counted". Send an update at each stage by email, text, or phone, as the person chose. Where a signature problem blocks acceptance, state the problem, the deadline to fix it in days, and the route to fix it, and keep the ballot tracked while the fix is pending. State plainly that tracking follows the return envelope, not the vote, and that the vote stays confidential. State the limits: ballots returned in person do not appear as "received", and where no online tracking exists, give the phone route. For the polling place, take a full address and return the assigned place with its hours and directions, plus early-voting dates, hours, and locations where offered; where the address is not found, give address-refinement help and a contact route rather than a dead end, and state that the assigned place can change and must be confirmed before each election. Distinct from the Ballot row, which covers marking and casting a ballot with a review screen and cast confirmation, from the Government document application row, which covers the shared flow for any official document without the registration, ballot-tracking, and polling-place rules, and from the Order tracking row, which follows a parcel rather than a ballot. Cross-check the Ballot row, the Government document application row, and the Order tracking row |
| Jury duty summons response | Reply to a jury summons online, ask to move the date or be excused, and check whether to report | Summons-number sign-in, reply deadline, eligibility questionnaire, deferral and excusal routes, saved answers, reporting-status check, scam warning, non-online route | Sign in with the identifiers printed on the paper summons — summons or participant number plus PIN, or number plus name fragment and date of birth — and show an example of where to find them on the letter; no account is created. State the reply deadline and the consequence of not replying before the first step. Put the eligibility questionnaire first: it must be completed before a deferral or excusal can be requested. Keep deferral and excusal as separate actions: deferral picks a new date, often with immediate confirmation and a limit within the coming months, while excusal needs reasons and supporting documents and is decided later — state the decision timing and whether silence means granted or denied. Let the person save or download their answers at the end and give on-screen confirmation, telling them to keep it, since no separate notification follows. For reporting, state the check time the evening before the date and let the person check by group or participant number: the states are report, do not report or on call, rescheduled, or excused — and never travel on the summons date alone without checking. Put a scam warning on the entry page: a genuine summons arrives by post only, and the service never asks for payment, bank details, national ID numbers, or other sensitive data by phone, email, or text, and never threatens arrest; name the single official portal and the route for reporting suspicious messages. Offer a non-online route by post or phone and a help route, and ask about access needs and accommodations at reply time. Keep the service operable by keyboard, screen reader, and speech recognition, with text that zooms without spilling. Distinct from the Virtual court hearing row, which covers taking part in a hearing by video or phone rather than replying to a summons, from the Government document application row, which covers applying for a document with a fee and identity-document upload, and from the Appointment management row, which books a service slot rather than answering a summons. Cross-check the Virtual court hearing row, the Government document application row, and the Appointment management row |

## Layout and responsive rules

- One pattern owns the primary action of a screen; supporting patterns stay visually quieter.
- On compact screens, patterns stack in task order: search, filters, results, actions.
- On expanded screens, filters may sit beside results; the result count stays with the results.
- Keep the path back: every pattern that opens a detail or a step offers a clear return.
- Do not nest dialogs; use a new view when a second layer of decisions appears.

## States

Every element and page in this group defines all seven states. Missing states are treated as incomplete work.

| State | Appearance | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Default | A pattern at rest, with full contrast and a clear label. | Ready for input or selection. |
| Hover | Pointer-only highlight: subtle background or border change. | No change on touch devices; hover never carries essential information alone. |
| Active | Pressed or selected appearance while interaction is in progress. | Feedback appears within 100 ms of input. |
| Disabled | Reduced contrast, no pointer response, still readable. | Explain why it is unavailable nearby when the reason is not obvious. |
| Loading | Skeleton blocks or a progress indicator replace live content. | Keep layout dimensions stable so content does not shift. |
| Empty | State why the area is empty and offer the action that fills it, or a reset when filters cause it. | Offer one clear next action and, where relevant, a reset for filters. |
| Error | Keep the pattern's structure, show the failure in place, and offer retry without losing input. | Keep entered data, place the message next to the problem, and offer a retry. |

## Pre-publish checklist

The canonical publishing checklist lives in `../CONTRIBUTING.md`; the checks below are specific to this file.

- [ ] Every pattern names its purpose, anatomy, and practical rules.
- [ ] Permission-request, comparison, search-results, onboarding, command-palette, notification-escalation, offline-first-sync, and file-upload rows reflect the latest rules.
- [ ] Cross-links to catalog/states.md resolve.
- [ ] All seven states appear by name: default, hover, active, disabled, loading, empty, error.
